Teddy's Library

Behavior

Maladaptive Behavior

Aggression

The client will decrease instances of Physical Aggression

Target Behavior

Decrease instances of Physical Aggression
Operational Definition

Physical aggression is defined as any attempt or act in which the client makes forceful physical contact with another person or directs an object/liquid toward a person with the potential to cause pain, injury, or significant distress. This includes:
Hitting/slapping, kicking, pushing/shoving, pinching, scratching, biting, head-butting.

Grabbing clothing/limbs, hair-pulling, body-checking.

Throwing objects at a person, spitting or spraying liquids at a person.

Does not include:
Accidental contact (e.g., jostling in a crowded area).

Age-appropriate, consensual play that follows pre-taught rules and stops when signaled.

Self-injurious behavior without contact toward others.

Property destruction not directed toward a person (tracked separately).

Measurement: Frequency count per day (each discrete act = 1), with intensity rating:
1 = Light contact/no mark; 2 = Redness/crying/brief disruption; 3 = Injury, staff medical check, or significant disruption.
 Onset/offset: Begins when movement toward aggression starts; ends after 10 consecutive seconds with no additional attempts.

Probable Function

Most likely Escape/Avoidance from task demands and/or Access to Attention or Tangibles (with possible Automatic/Sensory contribution).
 Indicators to watch:
Escape: occurs after demands, during difficult tasks, or when task length increases.

Attention: follows adult/peer attention shifts away from client; stops when attention returns.

Tangible: occurs when access is denied or items are removed.

Automatic: occurs across settings without clear triggers; persists without social consequences.

(Confirm via ABC data and brief functional analyses as appropriate.)
Proactive Strategies

Antecedent modifications
Visual schedule, first-then boards, clear rules ("safe hands"), and personal-space cues.

Choice-making (which task first, where to sit, tool to use); task interspersal/high-p sequence.

Task adjustments: right-sized difficulty, chunking, built-in help options.

Transition supports: 2-5 min warnings, timers, countdowns, transition object.

Noncontingent reinforcement (NCR): scheduled attention (e.g., 2-5 min), planned access to preferred items/activities, movement/sensory breaks.

Environment: reduce crowding/noise, defined work and calm spaces, seating away from triggers.

Teaching & prompting
Functional Communication Training (FCT) for the likely functions (see FERB below).

Emotion ID + regulation skills: "zones," breathing, squeeze ball, wall push-ups, ask for space.

Prompting hierarchy (gesture → model → brief verbal), then fade prompts.

Reinforcement: DRA for FERB (high-quality, immediate), consider DRO for intervals with zero aggression if appropriate. Use token or point system tied to clear criteria.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Teach, practice, and reinforce these with role-play and visuals:
Escape/Break Request
Client will hand a Break card or say/sign "Break, please" or "All done" within 5 seconds of feeling overwhelmed, then transition to a calm area for 2-3 minutes.

Mastery: ≥80% of identified opportunities across 2 consecutive weeks with ≤1 prompt.

Attention Request
Client will tap a cue card, raise a hand, or say "Attention please/Can we talk?" and wait up to 10 seconds.

Mastery: ≥80% of opportunities with ≤1 prompt across 2 weeks.

Tangible Request/Turn-Taking
Client will use "My turn," "Can I have ___?", or exchange icon/SGD request; will accept "wait" with a timer.

Mastery: ≥80% with ≤1 prompt across 2 weeks.

Personal-Space/Safe Hands (if sensory/impulse)
Client will use "I need space," keep hands clasped or hold a fidget when close to others.

Mastery: Observed in 80% of proximity situations with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

At first signs (muscle tension, pacing, raised voice):
Brief, neutral prompt to FERB + immediate path to success ("You can ask for a break.").

Increase space, lower voice, minimize language, remove audience/peers when feasible.

If aggression occurs:
Safety first: Positioning/blocking to protect, increase distance, clear nearby peers/objects.

Neutral affect: Avoid reprimand or long discussion in the moment; speak briefly ("Pause. Safe hands.").

Function-based response:
Attention-maintained: Limit verbal attention; once calm + FERB used, deliver high-quality attention.

Escape-maintained: Do not terminate the demand because of aggression. After calm, represent the demand in smaller steps and honor Break requests made via FERB.

Tangible-maintained: Withhold the item during aggression; provide access after FERB is used appropriately.

Automatic: Provide a safe competing sensory option and coach "safe hands."

After calm is re-established (e.g., 2-3 min of calm body/voice):
Reinforce any FERB that occurs; guide brief debrief ("What helped?"), then return to routine with a quick win.

Record ABC data (antecedent, behavior, consequence, intensity), update triggers/reinforcers.

Crisis & ethics:
Follow agency policy; do not use physical restraint unless you are trained, authorized, and it's clinically/legally permitted for imminent safety risk. Notify caregivers/admin per policy and document.

The client will decrease instances of Verbal Aggression

Target Behavior

Decrease instances of Verbal Aggression
Operational Definition

Verbal aggression is defined as spoken or vocal behavior directed at a person with the intent or effect of intimidating, threatening, demeaning, or significantly disrupting others. This includes:
Name-calling, insults, derogatory slurs.

Swearing/cursing at a person ("You're a…") or hostile yelling directed at someone.

Threats of harm to a person or their property ("I'm going to hit you," "I'll break your stuff").

Sexually explicit or harassing comments directed at a person.

Repeated hostile taunts or mockery after being cued to stop.

Does not include:
Neutral self-talk, scripting/echolalia, singing, or sound effects.

General exclamations not directed at a person (e.g., "dang it!" to self).

Playful teasing with clear mutual consent that stops immediately when signaled.

Loud voice due to environment (e.g., assembly noise) without hostile content.

Measurement:
Primary: Frequency count per day (each discrete episode = 1).

Intensity rating (mark highest level within an episode):
1 - Mild: raised voice, rude tone/word, stops with a single prompt; no target named.

2 - Moderate: directed insult/swearing or repeated hostile remarks; brief disruption.

3 - Severe: explicit threat of harm or targeted harassment; activity stops; admin/caregiver notified.

Onset/offset: Begins with the first qualifying verbalization; ends after 20 consecutive seconds without qualifying statements (or when the client uses a taught replacement and remains calm).

Probable Function

Often maintained by Attention (seeking adult/peer reaction), Escape/Avoidance (from tasks/demands), and/or Access to Tangibles (when denied). A possible Automatic/Sensory component may be present (habit, impulsivity).
 Confirm via ABC data and brief functional assessment.
Proactive Strategies

Antecedent modifications
Post and pre-teach clear expectations (e.g., "Kind words," "No threats," "Volume 2-3 indoors").

Visual supports: rules card, "Stop/Think/Say" script, feelings chart, Volume Meter (0-3).

Predictable routines + first-then boards; preview changes with timers/warnings.

Choice-making: order of tasks, seating, partner, tools; provide "help" options.

Task design: right-sized difficulty, intersperse easy items (high-p), offer breaks proactively.

Noncontingent attention: scheduled positive check-ins (e.g., every 5-7 min in high-risk times).

Reduce triggers: noise, crowding, provocative peers; assign calm workspace.

Teaching & prompting
Functional Communication Training (FCT):
Attention: "Excuse me," "Can we talk?" wait 10s.

Escape: "Break please," "All done," "Can you help?"

Tangible: "Can I have ___?" + "When?" + wait with timer.

Emotion regulation: feelings ID, 4-square breathing, count-to-10, fidgets, "I need space."

Social problem solving: I-Message ("I feel ___ when ___; I need ___"), respectful disagreement script.

Prompting hierarchy: gesture → model → brief verbal; fade quickly.

Reinforcement: DRA for replacements (specific praise + access), consider DRO for short intervals with zero verbal aggression; token system with clear exchange rates.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Teach, practice with role-plays, post visuals, and reinforce heavily:
Request Attention
Client will raise hand/tap cue card/say "Excuse me, I need you" and wait up to 10 seconds.

Mastery: ≥80% of opportunities with ≤1 prompt across 10 consecutive school days (or 2 weeks).

Request Break/Help (Escape)
Client will say/sign "Break please" or "Help please" within 5 seconds of early signs of frustration; transitions to a calm area for 2-3 minutes as taught.

Mastery: ≥80% of identified opportunities with ≤1 prompt across 2 weeks.

Request Item/Negotiate (Tangible)
Client will say "Can I have ___?" or "When can I have ___?" and accept "wait" with a timer/first-then.

Mastery: ≥80% with ≤1 prompt across 2 weeks.

Regulation & Boundaries
Client will use an I-Message or "I need space" instead of insults when upset.

Mastery: Demonstrated in 80% of conflict situations with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

At first signs (tone shift, muttering, scowling):
Brief, neutral redirection to FERB: "If you need help, ask," offer choices; lower voice, increase distance, reduce audience.

If verbal aggression occurs:
Safety & neutrality: Maintain calm tone, brief statements ("Pause. Kind words."); avoid debate or lectures.

Function-based response:
Attention-maintained: Minimize attention to the aggression (brief, neutral cue). Deliver rich attention immediately when client uses FERB or de-escalates ("Thanks for asking respectfully—what's up?").

Escape-maintained: Do not remove/shorten the task due to aggression. After calm or FERB, offer a brief, earned break, then re-present work in smaller chunks.

Tangible-maintained: Withhold item during aggression; honor polite request/waiting swiftly once used.

Automatic: Offer competing responses (chew/fidget/breathing) and coach "I need space."

Handling severe content (threats/harassment):
Stop statement calmly: "We keep others safe here; threats are not allowed."

Remove audience, follow site policy for threats/harassment; document exact language if safe.

After calm (e.g., 2-3 min of calm body/voice), complete a brief debrief/repair (who was affected, what to do next time), then return to routine with a quick success.

Reinforce recovery:
Specific praise for using FERB, tokens/points, or earned attention/tangible. Keep feedback short; teach more later, not during crisis.

Documentation & ethics:
Record ABC data with intensity; note triggers, effective reinforcers, and what worked.

Follow agency/school policy for threats or harassment; notify caregivers/admin as required.

No physical restraint for verbal behavior; prioritize dignity and safety.

Compliance

The client will reduce instances of non-compliance

Target Behavior: Reduce Non-Compliance

Operational Definition

Non-compliance is defined as the client not initiating or not completing a reasonable, clearly delivered instruction within 10 seconds (for single-step tasks) or within 30 seconds (to begin the first step of a multi-step task), when materials are available and expectations are developmentally appropriate. It includes:
Verbal refusal (e.g., "No," "You can't make me," "Later") after an instruction.

Ignoring the instruction (no movement toward the task; engaging in unrelated activity).

Leaving the area to avoid the task after an instruction is delivered.

Starting but stopping for >20 seconds without progress and without using a taught alternative (e.g., break/help request).

Arguing/negotiating beyond one respectful attempt after the instruction is given.

Non-compliance does not include:
Asking for clarification/help within 10 seconds ("What do I do first?").

Using a pre-taught break/request strategy within 10 seconds.

Delays caused by unclear instructions, lack of materials, conflicting adult directions, novel skills beyond current ability, medical/safety concerns, or sensory distress.

Behavior during a pre-scheduled/approved break.

Probable Function

Escape/Avoidance of task demands and/or need for autonomy/control. (Attention or tangibles may occasionally co-occur; monitor with ABC data.)
Proactive Strategies

Teach Replacement Behaviors (see FERB)

Start-Then-Ask: Teach "start the first step, then ask for help/choice."

Break Request: Teach use of a break card or phrase ("Break, please") as an alternative to refusal.

Help/Clarification Request: Teach concise scripts ("Can you show me?" "What's first?").

Choice/Nego­tiation: Teach "Can I do A then B?" or choosing between two acceptable options.

How to Teach

Model → Role-play → Practice in real tasks with immediate feedback.

Use visuals (First-Then board, step checklist, "How many until done?" card).

Task-analyze multi-step activities; provide exemplars of "first step."

Prompting: Least-to-most (gesture → brief verbal → model), then fade.

Behavioral momentum: 2-3 easy "high-P" tasks before a harder demand.

Reinforcement

Differential Reinforcement of Compliance (DRC): Deliver immediate, labeled praise and access to a preferred item/activity for starting within 10 seconds and finishing as assigned.

Start FR1 (every instance), then thin to VR2-3 as skills stabilize.

Token board or point system convertible to small choices/breaks.

Reinforce FERBs on the same schedule to keep them competitive with refusal.

Antecedent Modifications

Gain attention first (name, eye-level), deliver one clear, concise instruction.

Provide choice (order, materials, location) and a brief rationale ("so we can…").

Right-size tasks (80% known steps; adjust duration/quantity).

Preview transitions and use a countdown (e.g., "2 minutes… 1 minute…").

Ensure materials ready, minimize competing stimuli, offer noise/seat supports.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Break Request: Within 10 seconds of an instruction, the client will use a break card or say "Break, please," receive a ≤2-minute regulated break, and return to start the first step when the timer ends, in 80% of opportunities with ≤1 prompt.

Help/Clarification: The client will say "What do I do first?" or "Can you help me?" within 10 seconds and then begin the prompted first step, in 80% of opportunities with ≤1 prompt.

Choice/Nego­tiation: The client will use a taught script ("Can I do A then B?") within 10 seconds and comply with the agreed sequence, in 80% of opportunities with ≤1 prompt.

Start-First Step: The client will initiate the first step within 10 seconds of the instruction and continue with no pauses >20 seconds, in 80% of opportunities.

De-escalation Measures / Reactive Strategies

Neutral Re-presentation (once): Restate the instruction once in a calm, brief tone + offer two choices (path, order, tool). Provide 10-20 seconds of quiet wait time.

Prompt a FERB: Point to visuals and briefly prompt "Break or help?" then honor the FERB quickly.

When-Then Contingency: "When you start the first step, then you earn ___."

Minimize Attention to Refusal: No arguing/lecturing; keep language short; avoid power struggles.

Reduce Demand Intensity if Escalating: Shorten the task, offer guided first step, or switch to a regulation break (timed, predictable return).

Safety First: Increase distance, remove audience/items if needed; follow site crisis plan.

Reinforce the Pivot: The moment the client engages in any step toward compliance or a FERB, deliver praise and planned reinforcement.

If No Start After ~2 Minutes: Defer the task; note antecedents; reintroduce later with adjusted supports (shorter duration, more choices, stronger reinforcement).

The client will decrease instances of prompt dependency

Target Behavior: Decrease Prompt Dependency

Operational Definition

Prompt dependency is defined as the client waiting for or requiring an adult-delivered prompt (verbal, gestural, model, or physical) to begin or complete a task after a clear discriminative stimulus (SD)/instruction is given, when the client has previously demonstrated the skill. It includes:
Pausing >5 seconds after the SD without initiating the first step until a prompt is delivered.

Scanning the adult for cues, asking "What next?" before attempting, or stating "Help me" without trying.

Beginning only after high-intrusiveness prompts (e.g., model/physical) when lower prompts or natural cues are sufficient.

Repeatedly stopping until additional prompts are provided ("step-by-step coaching").

Prompt dependency does not include:
Trials during initial acquisition of a novel skill or when the SD is unclear/ambiguous.

Lack of response due to missing materials, conflicting directions, sensory distress, or safety/medical needs.

Using a taught self-prompt (visual checklist, timer, self-talk) before adult help.

Appropriate help-seeking after a reasonable independent attempt (see FERB).

Measurement: Record % of opportunities with independent initiation within 5 seconds of the SD and the most-intrusive prompt level used when independence does not occur.
Probable Function

Primary: Access to adult guidance/assurance and escape from effort or decision-making due to a history where prompts immediately preceded success/reinforcement. (Attention and tangible access may co-occur—verify with ABC data.)
Proactive Strategies

Teach Replacement Behaviors (see FERB)

Try-Then-Ask: Attempt at least one step or idea, then request help.

Self-Prompting: Use visual checklist, task strip, timer, or self-instruction script instead of adult prompts.

Error Tolerance: Teach "I'll fix it" or "I'll try again" to reduce prompt-seeking after mistakes.

Prompt-Fading & Transfer of Stimulus Control

Use constant → progressive time delay to create a window for independent responding (start 0 s for errorless, move to 3-5 s).

Employ a least-to-most prompt hierarchy with strict caps (e.g., at most one brief indirect verbal before moving to visual/gestural).

After any prompted trial, run an immediate transfer trial (re-present SD, no prompt) to confirm control has shifted to the SD.

Remove overshadowing (stand behind/side, neutral posture, avoid hovering/verbal fillers).

Reinforcement

Differential Reinforcement of Independent Responding (DRI/DRA): Deliver immediate, labeled praise + preferred item/point/token for first-try independence within 5 s.

Reinforce use of self-prompts at the same or higher value than prompted responding.

Thin from FR1 to VR2-3 as independence stabilizes; keep prompted responses on reduced reinforcement.

Antecedent Modifications

Make SDs concise and consistent (one-step phrasing, same wording).

Task-analyze multi-step tasks; provide clear start cue (e.g., "Start at #1").

Provide limited choices (materials/order) to boost autonomy without adding prompts.

Ensure materials are visible and within reach; minimize competing adult talk.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Try-Then-Ask: Within 5 s of the SD, the client attempts the first step or generates one solution, and if still needed, uses a taught script ("I tried __. Can I have a hint?"). Criterion: 80% of opportunities with ≤1 prompt across 3 sessions.

Use a Self-Prompt: The client independently accesses a visual checklist/timer/self-talk card within 5 s and proceeds without adult verbal prompts, 80% across 3 sessions.

Specific Help Request: If stuck after an attempt, the client requests a specific prompt ("Point to where to start," "One hint") rather than general coaching; accepts the prompt and returns to independent responding, 80% with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Silent Wait Window: After the SD, provide 5 s of quiet wait; avoid repeating or "coaching aloud."

One-and-Done Prompting: If no response, deliver the least intrusive single prompt, then immediately run a transfer trial (re-present SD, no prompt).

Prompt-Seeking Redirect: Briefly point to the self-prompt or FERB card ("Try, then ask for one hint") instead of supplying extra prompts.

When-Then: "When you start on your own, then you earn ___." Keep statements brief and neutral.

Error Correction: If an error occurs, model or guide once, reset materials if needed, and follow with a no-prompt transfer.

Data + Adjust: If >30% of trials reach high-intrusive prompts for two sessions, reduce task load, increase delay gradually, or strengthen reinforcement for independence.

The client will decrease instances of Disruptive Joking

Target Behavior: Decrease Disruptive Joking

Operational Definition

Disruptive joking is defined as the client making unsolicited humorous remarks, sound effects, faces/gestures, or comedic actions that are off-topic or mistimed and cause a pause or loss of instructional/working time >3 seconds for the client or others. The behavior is counted when it occurs:
During instruction, directions, or independent/group work, or while another person is speaking.

Within 10 seconds after an instruction to begin/continue work, resulting in delay or derailment.

Includes (examples):
Calling out jokes/puns, sarcastic remarks, meme/inside-joke references unrelated to the task.

Making sound effects/funny voices, pratfalls/prop gags, or prompting peers to laugh.

Texting/air-dropping/handing around humorous notes during work time.

Does not include:
Brief, on-topic humor that is acknowledged and does not pause instruction/work >3 seconds.

Humor during designated times (e.g., free time, morning meeting "share," earned "comedy minute").

Teacher-assigned tasks that invite humor (e.g., improv warm-up, creative writing prompt).

Smiling/chuckling quietly without comments or disruption.

Measurement: Event recording of frequency per period (count an episode; separate by ≥30 s of appropriate engagement). Optional: % of 10-min intervals with 0 disruptive jokes for DRO.
Probable Function

Primary: Peer and/or adult attention (drawing laughs/interaction).
 Secondary (possible): Escape/avoidance from difficult/low-interest tasks or automatic/sensory stimulation (self-entertainment). Verify with ABC data.
Proactive Strategies

Teach Replacement Behaviors (see FERB)

Save-It Strategy: Write the joke on a "Save-It" card or sticky to share later.

Raise-Hand & Relevance Check: Ask to share only if it's on-topic and brief.

Micro-Break Request: Use a break card ("1-min reset, please") instead of clowning.

Read-the-Room Routine: Quick self-check—Is it on-topic? Is this a good time? Will it help learning?

How to Teach

Model → Role-play → Feedback using real classroom scenarios (teacher talking, transitions, group work).

Post a visual: "Humor Rules—Right Time, Right Place, Right Size."

Pre-correct before high-risk moments ("During directions: Save-It or Hand up if on-topic.").

Behavioral momentum: 2-3 quick successes before longer/less preferred tasks.

Prompting & Fading: Start with a light nonverbal cue (tap to Save-It card), fade quickly.

Reinforcement

DRA: Labeled praise + token/point for using Save-It, hand-raising, or quiet on-task behavior when tempted to joke.

DRO: Deliver a token for each 10-min interval with zero disruptive jokes.

Exchange menu includes a brief "Comedy Minute," Joke Jar share, or class helper role after work is completed.

Reinforce peers for neutral responses (no laugh-amplifying) and on-task engagement.

Antecedent Modifications

Clarify expectations with examples/non-examples and posted criteria.

Provide choice (which item first, seat/partner options) and right-size task difficulty.

Use First-Then ("First 5 problems, then Comedy Minute/Save-It share").

Seat to reduce large audience effects; increase adult proximity during directions.

Build scheduled humor outlets (e.g., opener or closer share) so humor has a place.

Avoid rhetorical prompts ("Any jokes?") during work blocks.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Save-It Card: When the urge to joke occurs, the client will write it down or place a sticker on a Save-It card within 5 s and continue the task, earning later share time; 80% of opportunities with ≤1 prompt across 3 sessions.

On-Topic, Permission-Based Share: The client will raise a hand, wait to be called, and make one on-topic, ≤10-second humorous comment at appropriate times (teacher-approved), 80% with ≤1 prompt.

Micro-Break Request: The client will present a 1-minute break card instead of joking, then return and re-engage within 10 s of the timer, 80% with ≤1 prompt.

Read-the-Room Self-Check: The client will silently scan the posted 3-question check (On-topic? Good time? Helps learning?) and choose Save-It or silence when any answer is "no," 80% independence.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Nonverbal "Not Now" Signal (pre-taught) + brief redirection to Save-It or continue task; no public call-outs.

Planned Ignore + Pivot Reinforcement: Do not respond to the joke; immediately praise nearby on-task behavior; reinforce the client the moment they pivot back.

When-Then: "When you finish #3, then you can share one from your Save-It."

One Brief Redirect Only: If repeated within 30-60 s, offer two choices: "Work with me for 2 problems or take a 1-min break—your pick," then return to task.

Reduce Audience: Quiet proximity, move seat temporarily, or assign a focused role (materials runner) to cut peer attention.

Content Boundaries: If joking becomes inappropriate/targeted, end interaction, restore safety (reset task, remove audience), and follow site consequences; debrief privately later.

Post-Incident Review (2 min): Quick ABC: What was happening? What could you do next time (Save-It/Break/Hand up)? Earn small reinforcement for participating.

The client will reduce instances of task refusal

Target Behavior

Decrease instances of&nbsp;Task Refusal
Operational Definition

Task refusal is defined as the client&nbsp;not starting, stopping, or avoiding&nbsp;a reasonably assigned task&nbsp;after a clear instruction&nbsp;when materials are available and the task is within taught ability, for&nbsp;≥5 seconds, including:
Verbal "no," "I won't," bargaining that delays work, repeated "I don't know" without attempting.

Passive refusal: no response/ignoring direction for ≥10 seconds, head down, turning away, closing eyes.

Active refusal: pushing materials away, hiding materials, leaving seat/area without permission to avoid the task.

Requesting bathroom/nurse/other errand immediately after a demand&nbsp;as a pattern&nbsp;of escape (when not medically indicated).

Does not include:
Appropriate requests for&nbsp;help, break, more time, or a different option&nbsp;(taught and signaled).

Waiting for missing materials or unclear directions.

Tasks beyond current skill, unsafe tasks, or scheduled breaks.

Behaviors tracked elsewhere (property destruction, elopement, aggression, SIB).

Measurement
Primary:&nbsp;Frequency (episodes per session/day).

Secondary (recommended):
Latency to start&nbsp;(instruction → task initiation).

% On-time starts&nbsp;(initiates within&nbsp;10 seconds).

Duration&nbsp;of refusal (start of refusal → task engagement ≥10s).

Intensity (mark highest):
1-Mild:&nbsp;delayed start >10s, stays seated.

2-Moderate:&nbsp;pushes materials/bargains >1 min, brief leave from seat/area (<30s).

3-Severe:&nbsp;leaves area >30s or refusal halts activity for group/staff.

Onset/offset rules:&nbsp;Begins at the first qualifying refusal after a clear instruction; ends when the client engages with the task for&nbsp;≥10 consecutive seconds&nbsp;or uses a taught replacement that results in a planful transition (e.g., approved break).
Probable Function

Primarily&nbsp;Escape/Avoidance&nbsp;of demands; may also contact&nbsp;Attention,&nbsp;Tangible access&nbsp;(when work blocks preferred items), or&nbsp;Control/Autonomy; occasional&nbsp;Automatic&nbsp;factors (fatigue/sensory overload).
Confirm via ABC data and brief functional assessment.
Proactive Strategies

Antecedent modifications
Clarify the&nbsp;4 Ws&nbsp;of tasks:&nbsp;What to do, How much, How to know it's done, What's next&nbsp;(visuals/first-then).

Choice-making:&nbsp;order of tasks, where to sit, tools, partner; offer 2-3 bounded choices.

Right-size tasks:&nbsp;chunk steps; intersperse easy/high-p items (3-5) before harder demand.

Provide&nbsp;help options&nbsp;(word banks, examples, calculators, partial credit, worked models).

Priming/preview:&nbsp;show sample, model 1-2 items, quick success first.

Noncontingent attention&nbsp;during high-risk times; scheduled movement/sensory breaks.

Motivation & environment:&nbsp;reduce noise/crowding, ensure materials ready, use timers and end-signals, define work & calm areas.

Teaching & prompting
Functional Communication Training (FCT):&nbsp;teach and post scripts/cards for:
"Break, please," "Help, please," "More time," "Different option/order?"

Task initiation routine:&nbsp;"Read first step → get tool → start within 10s."

Tolerance for delay/denial:&nbsp;accept "wait" with a visual timer; earn brief break after X mins.

Self-management:&nbsp;checklist or start timer; tally on-time starts; goal-setting with reward menu.

Prompting hierarchy:&nbsp;time-delay → gesture/model → brief verbal;&nbsp;fade quickly.

Reinforcement:
DRA for on-time start & persistence (specific praise + access to strong reinforcers).

Consider&nbsp;DRO&nbsp;for short intervals with zero refusal (if it won't suppress communication).

Token/point system with clear exchange rates tied to&nbsp;starts&nbsp;and&nbsp;completions.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Teach, practice with role-play, post visuals, and reinforce:
Request a Break (Escape)
Client will hand a Break card or say/sign "Break, please"&nbsp;within 5s&nbsp;of early frustration; takes a&nbsp;2-3 min&nbsp;timed break in a designated area, then returns.

Mastery:&nbsp;≥80% of identified opportunities with ≤1 prompt across 2 weeks.

Request Help/Modification
Client will say/sign "Help, please" / "Which step?" / "Can you model one?" and resume within&nbsp;10s&nbsp;after help.

Mastery:&nbsp;≥80% with ≤1 prompt across 2 weeks.

Negotiate Order/Amount (Autonomy)
Client will ask "Can I do 5 now, 5 later?" or "Can I do reading before math?" using a first-then board.

Mastery:&nbsp;≥80% appropriate negotiations accepted; refusal decreases.

On-Time Start
After clear instruction, client initiates the first step within&nbsp;10 seconds&nbsp;using the posted "Start" routine.

Mastery:&nbsp;≥80% of opportunities across 10 consecutive school days (or 2 weeks).

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Early signs (sighing, looking away, pushing paper):
Brief, neutral support: "You can ask for&nbsp;break&nbsp;or&nbsp;help," offer a bounded choice, show timer/first-then.

Reduce audience/stimulation; simplify language; move closer but non-intrusive.

If refusal occurs:
Neutral affect; short prompts.&nbsp;Avoid debates/lectures.

Keep the demand&nbsp;available but right-sized: break into micro-steps; use guided start ("Do #1 together").

Provide&nbsp;limited choices&nbsp;(A/B), brief countdown, or starter hint; prompt FERB.

Do not allow refusal to be the fastest path to escape.&nbsp;When calm or FERB is used, honor break/help quickly, then re-present work in smaller chunks.

With tangible-maintained patterns: withhold item during refusal; deliver promptly after&nbsp;on-time start&nbsp;or FERB.

If leaving area / severe disruption:
Follow safety plan; reduce stimulation; once calm (e.g.,&nbsp;2-3 min&nbsp;calm body/voice), guide a&nbsp;repair&nbsp;("What helped? What next time?") and finish a&nbsp;small portion&nbsp;to contact reinforcement.

Reinforce recovery
Immediate, specific praise and access for using FERB and re-engaging ("Nice ask for help—here's your timer, then back to #2.").

Documentation & ethics
Collect ABC data with&nbsp;latency/duration, note effective antecedents/reinforcers.

Align with school/agency policy; ensure dignity; avoid coercive practices or excessive demands.

Impolite Behavior

The client will decrease instances of impolite interrupting

Target Behavior

Decrease instances of Impolite Interrupting
Operational Definition

Impolite interrupting is defined as the client taking the conversational floor while another person is speaking without a pre-taught signal or invitation, and without waiting for an appropriate pause. This includes:
Calling out comments/questions across the room or into a conversation already in progress.

Talking over a speaker, finishing their sentences uninvited, or starting a new topic mid-sentence.

Making attention-getting vocalizations (e.g., "Hey! Hey!"), clapping/snapping, or repeating the speaker's name to seize the floor.

Continuing to speak after a cue to wait or hold that thought.

Does not include:
Using a pre-taught attention signal (e.g., hand raise, "excuse me") and waiting appropriately.

Brief, non-disruptive backchannel (e.g., "mm-hmm," nodding) that doesn't take the floor.

Responding to a direct question or safety-related interruptions (e.g., "Stop!" for danger).

Scripted turn-taking in fast-paced formats where interruptions are part of the rules.

Measurement
Primary: Frequency (episodes per period/day).

Secondary (recommended): % on-time turn entries (waited for cue/pause), and average wait time before speaking.

Intensity (mark highest per episode):
1—Mild: single brief call-out; activity continues.

2—Moderate: repeated over-talking after 1 prompt; brief disruption.

3—Severe: loud/shouting over others or derails activity/meeting.

Onset/offset: Begins when the client speaks/acts to take the floor while another is speaking; ends after 15 consecutive seconds with no further interruptions or after the client uses a taught replacement and the speaker resumes.

Probable Function

Most often Attention/Access to the floor; may also contact Escape from waiting, Control/Autonomy, or Automatic/Impulsive responding.
 Verify with ABC data and brief functional assessment.
Proactive Strategies

Antecedent modifications
Post and teach simple rules: "Wait—Signal—Speak." Add a Volume/Wait visual and personal "Hold-That-Thought" card/pad.

Pre-correct before high-risk times: "Remember—hand up, wait for name."

Structure turns: speaking tokens, time box (e.g., 30-60s per turn), "parking lot" for off-topic ideas.

Increase access to the floor: frequent cold-calling/round-robin so waiting time is predictable.

Offer choices: "Share now or after two more turns?" seat near facilitator, provide fidget for impulse control.

Noncontingent attention: brief positive check-ins at set intervals to reduce attention-seeking surges.

Teaching & prompting
Teach the 3-step entry routine:
Signal (hand raise or "Excuse me").

Wait for name/gesture or a clear pause (count to 5).

Speak (max 1-2 sentences), then invite others ("Your turn").

Scripts & visuals: "I'd like to add," "Can I go next?", "Quick add—10 seconds?"

Self-management: tally "waited turns," use a timer or finger-count to pace speaking.

Prompting hierarchy: gesture → silent point to visual → brief verbal; fade quickly.

Reinforcement:
DRA for appropriate signals/waiting (specific praise + points/tokens/privileges).

Optional DRO for short intervals with zero impolite interruptions (only if it doesn't suppress polite requesting).

Group contingencies (table/team points) for collective turn-taking.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Teach, practice with role-plays, post visuals, and reinforce:
Signal for Attention & Wait
Client raises hand or says "Excuse me" and waits up to 10 seconds for acknowledgement or a pause.

Mastery: ≥80% of opportunities with ≤1 prompt across 10 consecutive days (or 2 weeks).

Request Order/Priority (Escape from waiting/Autonomy)
Client uses "May I go next?" or shows a Priority Card (limit 2-3 per day) for time-sensitive comments.

Mastery: Uses appropriately in ≥80% of opportunities; misuse decreases.

Hold-That-Thought Strategy
Client writes/draws the thought on a sticky/notepad or taps it into a device; shares when called.

Mastery: Uses in ≥80% of prompted opportunities; interruptions decline.

Brief Turn Format
Client limits contributions to 1-2 sentences or ≤10 seconds, then yields ("Go ahead").

Mastery: Observed in 80% of speaking turns with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

At first signs (leaning in, mouth open to cut in, audible inhale):
Nonverbal cue (flat palm "wait," point to visual), move closer, and give a quick path to success: "I see you—you're next."

If impolite interruption occurs:
Brief, neutral response (no debate): "Pause. Wait-Signal-Speak."

Function-based consequences:
Attention-maintained: Minimize attention to the interruption; immediately give rich attention to the first appropriate signal/wait that follows.

Escape from waiting: Acknowledge and schedule: "You're on deck after Sam," then reinforce when they wait.

Control/Autonomy: Offer a bounded choice: "Quick add (10s) now or full turn next?"

If repeated: use a speaking token or "one more chance" rule; redirect to "Hold-That-Thought."

Repair & return
After the speaker finishes, prompt a brief repair: "Sorry for cutting in—please continue." Reinforce successful repair and next appropriate turn entry.

Documentation & ethics
Track frequency + % appropriate entries; note triggers (topic, wait length).

Reinforce growth, avoid public shaming; ensure culturally responsive norms for turn-taking.

The client will decrease instances of complaining

Target Behavior: Decrease Complaining

Operational Definition

Complaining is defined as the client making negative, non-solution-focused verbalizations or vocalizations that express dissatisfaction, blame, or unwillingness and that delay work or disrupt instruction for >3 seconds. Count an occurrence when, within 5 seconds of the comment, the client does not follow up with a taught request or problem-solving step.
 Includes (examples):
"This is stupid," "I hate this," "Why do we have to do this?"

Repeated sighs/groans paired with negative statements.

Rhetorical arguing ("This is unfair") that stalls the task.

Stirring peer side comments ("Everyone knows this is dumb") during work time.

Does not include:
A taught, solution-focused request (e.g., "Can I have help?" "Can I do odds instead?").

Reporting pain/illness/safety issues, or accurate feedback about a problem with materials.

Appropriate humor or neutral venting that doesn't pause work >3 s.

Calm "I-statements" paired with a request ("I'm frustrated; can I take a 1-minute break?").

Measurement: Event recording of frequency per class/period. Define an episode as one or more complaints separated by ≥30 s of appropriate engagement or a topic/task change. Optional: % of 10-min intervals with 0 complaints (DRO), and latency to re-engage after a complaint.
Probable Function

Most likely attention (peer/adult reactions) and/or escape/avoidance of low-interest or difficult tasks; possible autonomy/control seeking. Verify with ABC data.
Proactive Strategies

Teach Replacement Behaviors (see FERB)

Complain → Request Translation: Convert "I don't want to" into a specific request (help, choice, time, break).

Two-Options Proposal: "Can I do A then B?" or "Evens or odds?"

I-Statement + Request: "I feel overwhelmed; can I get a hint?"

Micro-Break Request: Quick, timed regulation break instead of venting.

Problem Size Check: 1-5 scale; if ≤2, use coping + keep working; if ≥3, use a request.

How to Teach

Model → Role-play → Practice with common triggers (hard problems, transitions).

Post a Request Menu visual (Help, Break, Choice, Time, Clarify, Alternate method).

Pre-correct before risk moments ("If it's tough, use the Request Menu—not complaints").

Prompting: silent point to the menu/gesture cue; fade quickly.

Behavioral momentum: 2-3 easy wins before harder tasks.

Reinforcement

DRA: Immediate, labeled praise + token/point for first use of a replacement at the moment they're tempted to complain.

DRO: Earn a token for each 10-minute block with 0 complaints.

Exchange menu can include 1-minute chat/comedy minute, choice time, or class helper role after task completion.

Keep reinforcement for replacements ≥ reinforcement for on-task after complaints to make replacements "worth it."

Antecedent Modifications

Deliver clear, concise instructions with a brief rationale ("so we can ___").

Provide choices (order, materials, partner/seat) and right-size task difficulty.

Use First-Then, timers, and visual schedules; preview transitions.

Ensure materials are ready; reduce wait time and unnecessary demands.

Increase adult proximity during known hot spots; seat to minimize large audience effects.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Specific Request: Within 5 s of an urge/trigger, the client states a clear request (help/choice/time/clarify/alternate) and resumes or begins the task upon response, ≥80% of opportunities with ≤1 prompt across 3 sessions.

I-Statement + Request: The client says "I feel __; I need __." and follows the agreed plan, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Micro-Break: The client presents a 1-2 min break card instead of complaining and re-engages within 10 s of the timer, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Two-Options Proposal: The client offers two acceptable options ("Can I do #1-3, then check-in?") within 5 s and follows through, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Validate-Redirect (≤5 words): "I hear you. Use the menu." (point to Request Menu).

Planned Ignore + Pivot: Do not debate/lecture; withhold attention to the complaint. Immediately praise nearby on-task and reinforce as soon as the client switches to a FERB or task.

When-Then: "When you make a request or start #1, then you earn ___."

One Brief Reminder Only: If it continues, offer two choices (e.g., "Work with me for 2 problems or take a 1-min break").

Reduce Audience: Quiet proximity; if needed, temporary seat change or private prompt.

If Content Turns Inappropriate (insults/profanity): Stop interaction, restore safety, follow site consequences, and debrief privately later using the Complain → Request script.

Post-Incident Review (≤2 min): Identify trigger, select a better response next time, and earn a small reinforcement for completing the review.

The client will decrease the use of inappropriate language

Target Behavior: Decrease Use of Inappropriate Language

Operational Definition

"Inappropriate language" is defined as the client using words, phrases, or gestures that are profane, sexually explicit, threatening, or derogatory toward individuals or groups (e.g., profanity, insults, discriminatory remarks, threats of harm, or obscene gestures).
 This includes:
Speaking, shouting, whispering, singing, or chanting such language within earshot of others.

Writing, typing, or displaying it (notes, chat messages, whiteboards, devices).

Using offensive gestures directed at others.

An instance is counted when a prohibited word/phrase/gesture occurs. Multiple utterances within 5 seconds of each other count as a single instance; a pause of >5 seconds starts a new instance. Each discrete gesture counts as one instance. Behavior is measured across all settings (e.g., classroom/therapy room, hallways, cafeteria, transport, community).
Does not include:
Quoting content as part of instruction or assigned reading with prior staff approval.

Scripted lines in drama/role-play when pre-taught and contextually appropriate.

Neutral statements of emotion or disagreement using appropriate words (e.g., "I'm frustrated," "I disagree," "Please stop").

Probable Function

Often Attention (peer/adult reactions), Escape/Avoidance (delay/escape a task or demand), Access (to a preferred item/activity), and/or Automatic (self-stimulation/venting). Actual function to be confirmed via ABC data and team review.
Proactive Strategies

Teach Replacement Behaviors (skill building)

Functional Communication Training (FCT): Teach brief, acceptable phrases matched to the function, e.g.,
Attention: "Excuse me," "Can I talk to you?"

Escape: "I need a short break," "This is hard—please help."

Access: "Can I have ___?" or "When can I use ___?"

Emotional Regulation:
"I-statements" ("I feel ___ because ___; I need ___").

Coping menu: 3 deep breaths, count to 10, stress ball, water break, quiet corner.

Assertive Language: Phrases for setting boundaries ("Please stop," "That joke isn't okay," "Let's change the subject").

"School-Safe Words" Toolkit: Pre-agreed neutral exclamations (e.g., "wow," "shoot," "dang") as acceptable outlets.

Antecedent Modifications (preventive)

Prime & Pre-teach: Review expectations ("respectful talk," examples/non-examples) before high-risk times.

Visual Supports: Poster or card with "Say this instead…" sentence starters; traffic-light reminder for language.

Choice & Task Supports: Break tasks into chunks, offer first/then boards, and choice of order/materials.

Predictable Routines: Visual schedule; 2-5 minute transition warnings.

Noncontingent Attention (NCR): Provide brief positive attention/check-ins on a fixed schedule (e.g., every 5-7 minutes) to reduce attention-seeking bursts.

Environmental Supports: Access to calm space/noise reduction during identified triggers.

Reinforcement

DRA: Immediately praise and reinforce appropriate requests/"I-statements" and safe words.

DRO: Deliver reinforcement for intervals with zero instances (start with short intervals, thin gradually).

Specific Praise: "Thanks for saying 'I need a break'—that was respectful."

Token System: Tokens/stickers leading to preferred activities for meeting daily/interval goals.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

FCR for Breaks/Help: When frustrated, the client will say or show a pre-taught signal (e.g., "Break please," "Help please") within 5 seconds of trigger in 80% of opportunities, with ≤1 prompt, across 4/5 days.

I-Statement Use: The client will express feelings/needs using an I-statement in place of inappropriate language in 75% of probed situations, with ≤1 prompt, across 3 consecutive weeks.

Safe-Word Substitution: For surprise pain/frustration (e.g., dropping items), the client will use a pre-taught neutral exclamation instead of profanity in 90% of quick-trigger moments over 2 consecutive weeks.

Coping Strategy Selection: Upon a staff cue ("Use your strategy"), the client will choose a coping option from the menu and begin it within 30 seconds in 80% of trials.

Teaching methods: model → role-play → in-the-moment coaching → self-monitoring chart; reinforce each successful FERB immediately.
De-escalation Measures / Reactive Strategies

Low-Attention Response to Inappropriate Language (when safe): Brief, neutral affect; minimal verbal attention. Do not argue or lecture.

Differential Consequences by Function:
Attention-maintained: Withhold attention for the inappropriate language; provide attention immediately when an appropriate FCR/FERB is used.

Escape-maintained: Do not remove demands following inappropriate language; honor a break only after an FCR (use brief "work-then-break").

Access-maintained: Withhold item when inappropriate language occurs; provide item promptly for an appropriate request.

Automatic: Offer sensory alternatives (fidget, movement, quiet space); coach FERBs.

Non-verbal/Pre-taught Signals: Quiet hand signal or point to "Say this instead" card to prompt replacement without adding attention.

Prompting Hierarchy: Gesture → 1-3-word prompt ("Use your words") → model, fading quickly after success.

Safety & Threats: If language includes threats or targeted harassment, follow site policy and crisis plan immediately while maintaining calm, brief directives.

Restitution/Repair: After calm is re-established, coach a brief apology/repair statement and return to routine.

Data Collection: Tally frequency by context, note suspected function/triggers, and record FERB use and reinforcement delivered.

Elopement

The client will decrease instances of Elopement

Target Behavior

Decrease instances of Elopement
Operational Definition

Elopement is defined as the client leaving a designated area or adult supervision without permission by crossing a taught boundary (e.g., doorway, gate, cone line, taped floor line) or moving more than 10 feet from the supervising adult in community settings, in a manner that reduces supervision or creates safety risk. This includes:
Exiting seat/workspace and moving toward/through a doorway, gate, hallway, stairwell, lobby, playground exit, parking lot, or street without permission.

Hiding to avoid supervision or moving behind structures to prevent monitoring.

Bolting/running from staff during transitions or when prompted to return.

Does not include:
Scheduled transitions or errands with permission (bathroom, office run).

Movement within clearly defined boundaries (e.g., within the taped area) during free time.

Staff-directed movement (e.g., "Please take this to the bin").

Behaviors tracked separately (e.g., aggression, property destruction).

Measurement
Primary: Frequency (episodes per day/session).

Secondary (recommended): Duration (elopement start → safe return), distance from supervision, latency from demand/transition to elopement.

Intensity (mark highest per episode):
1—Attempt/near-miss: approaches boundary, hand on handle, crosses internal line but remains in same room; redirects with minimal prompt.

2—Leaves assigned area/room: remains within building or fenced area; line-of-sight maintained.

3—Exits building/secured area or loses line-of-sight (e.g., toward parking lot/street/water); activates emergency procedures.

Onset/offset: Begins when the client crosses the boundary or exceeds the 10-ft rule without permission; ends after the client is back within boundary and maintains proximity/engagement for ≥30 consecutive seconds (or follows the taught return routine).

Probable Function

Common maintaining variables include Escape/Avoidance (from tasks, demands, noise, transitions), Access to Tangibles/Activities (preferred areas/items), Attention (chase/negotiation), and/or Automatic/Sensory (seeking motion/deep pressure).
 Confirm via ABC data and brief functional assessment.
Proactive Strategies

Environmental & antecedent modifications
Secure & define boundaries: visual blocks/tape, cones, "STOP" signs on exits, door chimes/alarms (per policy), seating/activities away from exits.

Staff positioning: between client and exits; assign clear supervision zones and handoffs.

Predictable routines: visual schedules, first-then boards, transition warnings (2-5 min), countdowns, designated line spots or walking rope/handle.

Choice-making: order of tasks/centers, where to sit, which tool; offer 2-3 bounded choices.

Task design: right-size difficulty; brief, high-p warmups before harder work; embed preferred interests.

Noncontingent access: scheduled movement/sensory breaks; planned access to highly preferred areas/items to reduce "escape to get it."

Motivating operations: reduce triggers (noise, crowding, odors), provide headphones/fidgets, cool-down space that is inside boundaries.

Teaching & prompting
Functional Communication Training (FCT) aligned to function (see FERB).

Return routine: stop → turn → walk back to staff → check-in phrase ("I'm back") → resume plan. Practice as a game.

Spatial rules: "Feet stay inside the line," "Within 3 steps of adult," "Hold the rope/handle."

Safety responses: teach "Freeze/Stop" with immediate reinforcement; practice in low-stakes drills.

Prompting hierarchy: gesture/model → brief verbal → least intrusive physical guidance if needed for safety; fade quickly.

Reinforcement: DRA for staying within boundaries, on-time transitions, and using FERB (specific praise, tokens, fast access to preferred). Consider brief DRO for zero elopement during high-risk intervals (without punishing communication).

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Teach, post visuals, role-play, and reinforce heavily:
Request a Break / Different Location (Escape)
Client uses card/sign/speech: "Break please," "Different spot?" or "It's too loud." Moves to a designated calm area (inside boundaries) for 2-3 min, then returns.

Mastery: ≥80% of identified opportunities with ≤1 prompt across 2 weeks.

Request Item/Activity (Tangible)
"Can I have ___?" / "When can we go to ___?" accepts wait with timer/first-then.

Mastery: ≥80% with ≤1 prompt; elopement to the item/area declines.

Request Attention/Help
"Can you come with me?" "Walk with me?" "Help please."

Mastery: ≥80% across 2 weeks; chases/attention-driven elopement decline.

Proximity Check Skill
Client self-checks "3 steps to adult" or keeps a hand on cart/rope when moving; uses "I need space" while staying inside boundary.

Mastery: Observed in ≥80% of transitions with ≤1 prompt.

Stop/Freeze Response
On "Stop" cue, client halts within 2 steps, turns to adult, and waits for next direction.

Mastery: 4 of 5 probe trials across 3 days.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Early signs (glancing at exits, edging toward doorway, pacing, refusal before transition):
Move into intercept position (between client and exit), increase proximity, reduce language.

Offer quick path to success: prompt FERB ("Break or Help?"), show first-then/timer, present a bounded choice.

Lower demands briefly; reduce stimulation (noise, crowding); start a guided micro-step to re-engage.

If elopement occurs:
Safety first: Maintain line-of-sight; alert team per site protocol; secure doors/peers; remove hazards.

Calm, minimal language: "Stop. Come back." Avoid chasing if it increases running—use blocking angles and preferred items/people to attract back when safe.

Function-based response:
Escape-maintained: Do not end the entire demand because of elopement. After calm/return, represent the task in smaller chunks and honor Break when requested appropriately.

Tangible-maintained: Withhold the item/area during elopement; deliver promptly after appropriate request/waiting.

Attention-maintained: Minimize "chase talk"; provide rich attention immediately for return and FERB use.

Automatic/sensory: Offer movement/sensory alternative inside boundaries (wall push-ups, scooter path) and teach "movement break please."

After safe return (e.g., ≥2-3 min calm body/voice):
Reinforce the return routine and any FERB used; conduct a brief repair ("What will help next time?"), then resume routine with a quick win.

Documentation & ethics
Record ABC data with intensity, duration, distance, and what effectively prompted return.

Review environment/supervision gaps; update prevention plan.

Follow agency/school crisis policy; notify caregivers/admin as required.

Use physical guidance/restraint only if trained, authorized, and there is an imminent safety risk; prioritize dignity and safety.

Silly Behaviors

The client will decrease instances of silly behaviors

Tantrum's

The client will decrease tantrum behaviors

Target Behavior: Tantrum Behavior

Operational Definition:

Tantrum behavior is defined as a combination of verbal and physical actions that occur in response to perceived demands, limits, or undesired situations. This includes, but is not limited to:
Screaming loudly for more than 5 seconds.

Crying with tears, sobbing, or wailing that disrupts the environment.

Throwing objects (e.g., school materials, toys, personal items) with force.

Dropping to the floor and refusing to move or respond to directives.

Hitting others (peers, adults, or self) with hands or objects.

Tantrum behavior does not include:
Mild vocal protests or brief expressions of frustration (e.g., saying "I don't want to").

Crying that is self-regulated and does not escalate into disruptive behavior.

Isolated incidents of throwing a soft object without aggression or intent to harm.

Probable Function:

Escape/Avoidance of non-preferred demands or situations, or Access to a preferred item, activity, or attention.
Proactive Strategies:

1. Visual Schedules & Warnings

Use a consistent visual schedule so the learner knows what to expect.

Provide transition warnings (e.g., "In 2 minutes, we're cleaning up.").

2. Choice Making

Offer limited choices during non-preferred tasks to increase sense of control (e.g., "Do you want to use crayons or markers?").

3. Teach Emotion Regulation

Teach the child to identify and label their emotions using tools like emotion thermometers or visuals.

Introduce calm-down strategies such as deep breathing, squeezing a stress ball, or going to a calm space.

4. Task Modification

Break tasks into smaller steps and use timers or reinforcement to increase tolerance to work demands.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB):

The learner will:
Use a "Break" card, hand signal, or verbal request (e.g., "Can I take a break?") when feeling overwhelmed.

Request help appropriately by raising a hand or saying "I need help" before escalation.

Use pre-taught coping strategies (e.g., breathing, asking for space) instead of engaging in tantrum behavior.

Teaching Plan:
Model and role-play break requests and emotion strategies during calm moments.

Reinforce every use of appropriate replacement behavior with praise, tokens, or short breaks.

De-escalation Measures / Reactive Strategies:

1. Maintain Safety

Use proximity and remove unsafe items if aggression or throwing begins.

Move other students if necessary to avoid injury.

2. Minimal Attention

Limit verbal interaction during escalation to avoid reinforcing behavior.

Use neutral body language and tone; avoid power struggles.

3. Nonverbal Prompts

Point to visuals (e.g., break card, calm-down tools).

Offer access to calm-down space without verbal prompting if pre-taught.

4. Post-Episode Support

Once calm, debrief the event by reviewing what happened, what the learner can do next time, and reinforce any small attempts to use strategies.

Avoid giving access to the function (e.g., escape or item) immediately after the tantrum, unless the replacement behavior was used.

The client will reduce the duration of tantrum behaviors

Off Task Behaviors

The client will decrease instances of engaging in unexpected behaviors

The client will decrease instances of inappropriate/annoying touch to peers

Property Destruction

The client will decrease instances of property destruction

Unsafe Behaviors

The client will decrease unsafe behaviors

Behavior for Increase

Flexibility

The client will display flexibility by tolerating situations where things don't go as expected or desired

Target Behavior:
Displaying flexibility by tolerating situations where things don't go as expected or desired.
&nbsp;
Motivating Operation:
 Promoting adaptive coping skills, increasing frustration tolerance, and fostering emotional regulation.
Antecedent Procedures:
 1. Identify settings or situations where the client is likely to encounter unexpected or undesired outcomes.
 2. Teach the client coping strategies such as deep breathing, counting, or positive self-talk to use when things don't go as planned.
 3. Make the client aware that the goal is to show flexibility and tolerance when faced with undesired outcomes.
&nbsp;
Discriminative Stimulus:
 A situation occurs where things do not go as the client expected or desired, such as losing a game, not getting a preferred item, or experiencing a change in routine.
Appropriate Response:
 The client will employ coping strategies and refrain from engaging in problem behaviors such as tantrums, aggression, or withdrawal, to be considered a successful trial.
&nbsp;
Error Correction Procedures:
 1. **Prompting**: If the client shows inflexibility, provide immediate verbal or non-verbal prompts suggesting coping strategies.
 2. **Modeling**: Demonstrate how to react flexibly and cope effectively when faced with a similar situation.
 3. **Immediate Feedback**: Provide specific feedback on the importance of being flexible and tolerant.
 4. **Reinforcement**: Positive reinforcement is given when the client successfully shows flexibility.
&nbsp;
Criteria for Mastery:
 The client will display flexibility by tolerating things that don't go his way in 8 out of 10 opportunities across 3 consecutive data collection sessions.
&nbsp;
This ABA goal aims to develop the client's ability to cope with unexpected or challenging situations, which is essential for emotional and social well-being.
Instructional Notes

MO:
 Promoting adaptive coping skills, increasing frustration tolerance, and fostering emotional regulation.
 
 Antecedent Procedures:
Identify settings or situations where the client is likely to encounter unexpected or undesired outcomes.
 2. Teach the client coping strategies such as deep breathing, counting, or positive self-talk to use when things don't go as planned.
 3. Make the client aware that the goal is to show flexibility and tolerance when faced with undesired outcomes.
&nbsp;

 
 SD:
 A situation occurs where things do not go as the client expected or desired, such as losing a game, not getting a preferred item, or experiencing a change in routine.
 
 Response:
 The client will employ coping strategies and refrain from engaging in problem behaviors such as tantrums, aggression, or withdrawal, to be considered a successful trial.
 
 Prompting:
 visual prompt,verbal prompt,gestural prompt,modeling
 
 Error Correction:
 1. **Prompting**: If the client shows inflexibility, provide immediate verbal or non-verbal prompts suggesting coping strategies. 2. **Modeling**: Demonstrate how to react flexibly and cope effectively when faced with a similar situation. 3. **Immediate Feedback**: Provide specific feedback on the importance of being flexible and tolerant. 4. **Reinforcement**: Positive reinforcement is given when the client successfully shows flexibility.
 
 Reinforcement:
 differential social reinforcement,socially mediated reinforcement
 

The Client will demonstrate tolerance of frustration by responding calmly to a setback through the use of an appropriate coping strategy Once Per Session with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior: Frustration Tolerance — Calm Coping After a Setback (≤2 verbal prompts, once per session)

Operational Definition

Frustration tolerance is defined as the client responding calmly to a minor-moderate setback by initiating and completing an appropriate coping strategy and returning to expected activity, with no more than two brief verbal prompts from staff.
Minor-moderate setbacks (examples): correction/redo request, making an error, denied access/wait, losing a turn/game, unexpected change in routine, peer noise/movement that disrupts work.
 Not included: safety events, insults/bullying, property damage by others, medical issues—stop and follow safety plan.
Calm responding includes (all observable):
Voice at conversational volume; neutral or brief disappointed tone (≤10s).

Body safe: hands/feet to self; no threatening gestures.

Task/interaction resumes within the time limits below.

Appropriate coping strategies (one or more):
3-5 deep breaths (e.g., box breathing), positive self-talk ("I can try again"), brief count (to 5-10), squeeze/fidget, 5-4-3-2-1 grounding, request 30-60s micro-break, request a single hint/help, use calm-down card, "okay"/acceptance statement and resume.

Does NOT count: arguing, blaming, complaining >10s, profanity, refusal statements, negotiating new rules, elopement, property destruction, aggression, attention-seeking outbursts, or needing >2 verbal prompts or modeling/physical guidance.
Timing rules
Start of trial: Immediately upon an authentic setback (or planned mild one) with materials/expectations in place.

Success window: Within 30 seconds of the setback (or prompt), the client initiates a listed coping strategy; maintains calm for 60 seconds and re-engages in the activity for ≥60 seconds (these may overlap).

Terminate/mark "not met" if any disqualifying behavior occurs, >2 prompts are needed, or safety concerns arise.

One opportunity per session: Score the first eligible setback each session.

Measurement/criterion: Event-based (Y/N) with integrity checks. Criterion met when coping is initiated within 30s, calm is maintained 60s, and activity resumes for ≥60s, with ≤2 brief verbal prompts (e.g., "Use a strategy," "Take 3 breaths.").
Probable Function

Likely escape/avoidance of effort or error; possible attention (seeking adult mediation) and/or automatic regulation (sensory relief).
Proactive Strategies

Pre-teach coping menu (visual card) and practice daily with brief role-plays (BST: model → rehearse → feedback).

Pre-correction: "If something goes wrong, choose a strategy first."

Choice & predictability: visuals, first-then, warn of changes when possible.

Task design: intersperse easy items; allow one redo/repair attempt to reduce perfectionism.

Materials: access to quiet fidget, breathing card, timer; establish calm space (planned, time-limited).

Reinforcement: labeled praise for micro-skills ("Nice reset breath"), plus immediate small reinforcer for a successful trial; thin gradually.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Coping initiation: Client independently starts a listed strategy within 30s of a setback.

Help/break request: Uses a 3-6 word script ("Help with #2, please" / "Short break please") and returns on timer.

Acceptance & resume: States "Okay/I'll try again," and restarts the task within 10s.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Brief, neutral prompts (max two): "Strategy card," then "Breathe/choose one." Pause up to 10s after each.

Acknowledge + redirect: "That's frustrating. Use your plan."

Space & time: allow silent regulation (30-60s) if safe; avoid lectures/debates.

Protect contingencies: no access to preferred items or extended attention for problem behavior; reinforce any return-to-calm/FERB.

If escalation: follow safety plan; debrief later with quick reflect-plan ("What happened? What worked? Next time?").

Compliance

The Client will comply with therapist instructions during session

Target Behavior: Increase Instructional Compliance (Therapy Sessions)

Operational Definition

Instructional compliance is defined as the client initiating the first step within 10 seconds of a clear, developmentally appropriate instruction (SD) delivered when attention is secured and materials are available, and continuing the response without pauses >20 seconds until the step/assignment is completed as specified (or for the agreed duration). Compliance also includes using a taught alternative (FERB) within 10 seconds (e.g., "Break, please," "Can you help?") and then following the agreed plan.
Counts as compliance (examples):
Beginning the requested action (sit, start task, transition) within 10 s and finishing as assigned.

For multi-step tasks: starting step 1 within 10 s and progressing through steps with no >20 s gaps.

Using a FERB (break/help/choice) within 10 s and returning to complete the task as agreed.

Does not count as compliance:
Verbal refusal/arguing, ignoring, or off-task behavior after the SD.

Starting only after repeated prompts or high-intrusiveness prompts when lower/no prompt was sufficient.

Delays caused by unclear SDs, missing materials, conflicting directions, medical/safety issues (do not score).

Measurement:
Primary: % of trials with independent compliance (start ≤10 s, complete as assigned, 0-1 minimal prompt).

Secondary: Latency to start (s) and most-intrusive prompt level used.

Define a trial as any discrete therapist instruction; separate episodes by task change or ≥30 s.

Probable Function (for noncompliance)

Most commonly escape/avoidance of effort or low-interest tasks and/or autonomy/control seeking; attention may co-occur. Verify with ABC/data review.
Proactive Strategies

Instruction Delivery

Gain attention (name, eye level), then use one concise SD ("Open workbook to page 3").

Provide a brief rationale or outcome ("…so we can play the game after").

Use visuals (first-then, checklist, timer) and task analyze multi-step tasks.

Behavioral momentum: 2-3 easy, quick requests before harder tasks.

Environment & Antecedents

Right-size difficulty/duration (≈80% known steps; chunk longer tasks).

Offer bounded choices (order/materials/seat) to support autonomy.

Prime transitions (warnings, countdowns); have materials ready; minimize wait time.

Prompting & Fading

Start with least-to-most prompts; cap at one brief prompt before a transfer trial.

Use time-delay (0 s → 3-5 s) to create opportunities for independence.

Avoid overshadowing (no hovering, minimal extra talk); stand to the side/back.

Reinforcement

Differential Reinforcement of Compliance (DRC): Immediate, labeled praise + token/brief preferred access for starting within 10 s and finishing as assigned.

Begin FR1, then thin (VR2-3) as stable.

Reinforce FERBs at equal or higher value than prompted responding.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Start-Then-Ask: Within 10 s, client starts the first step, then requests help/clarification if needed ("Can you show me the first one?"). ≥80% of opportunities with ≤1 prompt across 3 sessions.

Break Request: Within 10 s, client presents break card/"Break, please" for ≤2 minutes and re-engages within 10 s of timer; completes task per plan, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Choice/Nego­tiation: Client uses a taught script ("Can I do A then B?" "Odds not evens?") within 10 s and follows the agreed sequence, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Specific Help Request: Client asks for a single, specific prompt ("Point to where to start") and resumes independently, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Silent Wait Window: After SD, provide 5-10 s quiet wait (no extra coaching).

One Re-presentation + Choice: Restate once + offer two bounded options ("with pencil or pen"); wait quietly.

Prompt a FERB: Point to visuals: "Break or Help?" Honor quickly; then return to task.

When-Then: "When you start #1, then you earn ___." Keep language brief, neutral.

Planned Ignore for Arguing/Side Comments; reinforce the pivot back to task immediately.

Reduce Demand Intensity if escalating (shorten chunk, guided first step, micro-break).

Safety first: Increase distance/remove audience per site plan if needed.

If no start after ~2 minutes: Log ABC, adjust task/reinforcement next trial; reintroduce with stronger antecedents.

The client will follow instructions given by the group leader in the absence of maladaptive behaviors with 2 verbal prompts

Target Behavior

Increase following instructions given by the group leader with ≤2 verbal prompts and zero maladaptive behaviors during the response window.
Operational Definition

When the group leader delivers a clear instruction (to the whole group or directly to the client), the client:
Begins the first step within 10 seconds and

Completes the expected components (e.g., gathers materials, moves to location, starts/finishes task)

Without engaging in maladaptive behaviors (e.g., refusal/arguing, verbal aggression, property destruction, elopement, noncompliance beyond allowed prompts, disruptive vocalizations).

Includes: responding to transitions ("Line up," "Come to circle"), task starts ("Open your book"), participation ("Share one idea"), clean-up.
 Does not include: unclear/contradictory directions, missing materials, directions to wait, or tasks beyond current skill/safety.
 Measurement:
Primary: % of instructions followed with ≤2 verbal prompts (or fewer).

Secondary: latency to start; prompt level used (independent → gesture/model → verbal 1 → verbal 2); duration to completion; frequency of maladaptive behaviors during instruction windows.
 Onset/offset: Onset at instruction delivery; offset when engaged for ≥10 consecutive seconds or task is completed.

Probable Function

Noncompliance/maladaptive responses typically contact Escape/Avoidance, Attention, Access to tangibles/activities, and/or Autonomy/Control; occasional Automatic/Sensory factors. (Verify via ABC data.)
Proactive Strategies

Antecedent setup
Post & pre-teach a 3-step routine: Look → Say "Okay" → Start (within 10s).

Visuals: first-then board, mini checklist ("What/How much/How know it's done/What's next"), group signals (hand up, chime).

Pre-correction before high-risk times: "Two-step directions, then choice time."

Choice-making: order of tasks, seat/partner/tools; embed preferred elements.

Task design: right-sized difficulty, chunking, model first 1-2 items, high-p momentum (3 easy, then 1 tough).

Noncontingent attention during demanding blocks; scheduled movement/sensory breaks.

Optimize environment: materials ready, minimize noise/crowding, clear supervision zones.

Teaching & prompting
Model-lead-test the 3-step routine with role-plays.

Functional Communication Training (FCT): teach "Break please," "Help please," "Which step?," "More time?"

Prompting hierarchy: gesture/model → brief verbal (Prompt 1) → brief verbal (Prompt 2); then pause for response; fade quickly.

Reinforcement: DRA for on-time starts/completions (specific praise + access/tokens). Begin dense (e.g., FR1 for starts), then thin.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Teach, post visuals, and reinforce:
Request Help/Clarification (Escape from confusion)
Says/signs "Help please / Which step?" and resumes within 10s after support.

Mastery: ≥80% of opportunities with ≤1 prompt across 2 weeks.

Request Brief Break (Escape)
"Break, please" → 2-3 min calm space (inside boundaries) → returns and starts.

Mastery: ≥80% appropriate use; refusal decreases.

Request More Time / Different Order (Autonomy)
"More time?" or "Can I do X before Y?" using first-then/timer.

Mastery: ≥80% appropriate requests accepted; on-time starts increase.

Attention Request
"Excuse me"/hand raise for help instead of calling out/arguing.

Mastery: ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Early signs (stalling, sighing, looking away):
Neutral, brief cue to routine: "Look-Okay-Start," offer A/B choice, show first-then/timer, prompt FERB. Move closer; reduce language.

If maladaptive behavior begins:
Calm & brief: "Pause. Safe voice. Break or Help?"

Function-based response:
Attention-maintained: minimize attention to arguing; deliver rich attention for first appropriate start/FERB.

Escape-maintained: do not remove task due to behavior; after calm/FERB, re-present in smaller chunks.

Tangible-maintained: withhold item until start or FERB; then deliver promptly.

If safety risk: follow crisis plan; reduce stimulation; once calm 2-3 min, return with a micro-step to quick success.

Reinforce recovery & completion:
Specific praise + token/access when the client starts within 10s and completes with ≤2 prompts and no maladaptive behavior.

The client will maintain appropriate body orientation during sessions (feet on the ground, hands on the table, and head and body facing the technician)

Target Behavior: Maintain Appropriate Body Orientation ("Ready Body")

Operational Definition

Appropriate body orientation ("Ready Body") is defined as the client, during seated instruction or work, meeting all of the following within 5 seconds of an attention cue or instruction and maintaining them through the direction/task (no pauses >3 seconds):
Feet: flat on the floor or a stable footrest.

Hands: on the table/desk or appropriately engaged with task materials (or holding an approved fidget).

Head & Body: head, shoulders, and torso generally oriented toward the technician/materials (within ~30°), eyes available for shared attention.

Counts as Ready Body (examples):
Brief shifts/scratches/stretching ≤3 seconds while returning to Ready Body.

Quiet fidget use that does not block task engagement or create noise.

Leaning slightly or adjusting seat while remaining oriented and hands/feet criteria met.

Does not count as Ready Body:
Turning the torso/head away from the technician/materials >30° for >3 seconds.

Feet tucked on the chair, on furniture, or moving around the room; kneeling on or under the table.

Hands off the table/materials for >3 seconds without a task reason (drumming, tapping peers, playing with unrelated items).

Laying head on desk, slouching fully away, or covering face/ears (unless part of a taught regulation strategy).

Measurement:
Primary: Momentary Time Sampling (MTS) every 10 seconds during instructional blocks; record "+" if all three criteria are met at the beep. Summarize as % of intervals with Ready Body.

Secondary: Latency from attention cue ("Ready body") or instruction to Ready Body (goal ≤5 s). Optional: duration of continuous Ready Body.

Probable Function

Usually a mix of escape/avoidance of effort, sensory regulation needs (movement/position change), and/or attention-seeking. Confirm with ABC data and adjust supports accordingly.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine (Skill Acquisition)

Create a 3-icon visual ("Feet-Hands-Face") and explicitly teach the sequence: "Feet down, hands ready, face the teacher."

Model → Rehearse → Role-play with quick feedback; embed in session openers ("Ready Body check").

Pair Ready Body with a brief rationale ("so we can finish quick and play ___").

Environment & Ergonomics

Ensure chair/desk fit; provide footrest if feet don't reach the floor.

Offer approved fidgets or chair bands for quiet movement (pre-teach "quiet hands/feet").

Seat to minimize distractions; place materials centrally to encourage natural orientation.

Antecedent Supports

Attention cue before instructions ("Name + Ready body").

Use First-Then, timers, visual schedules; preview transitions with brief countdowns.

Behavioral momentum: 2-3 quick, easy responses before longer tasks.

Prompting & Fading

Least-to-most: nonverbal point to visual → brief gestural cue → concise verbal ("Feet-Hands-Face").

Use a 3-5 s time delay after the cue to allow independent orientation.

After any prompted trial, run a transfer trial (re-present cue, aim for independence).

Avoid "hovering" or continuous narration; keep prompts brief and fade quickly.

Reinforcement

DRA/DRI: Labeled praise + token/point when Ready Body is shown within 5 s and at random MTS checks.

Start at FR1 (every successful check), then thin to VR2-3.

Exchange for brief, preferred items/activities or movement breaks after work chunks.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Self-Prompt Ready Body: Within 5 s of the attention cue, the client independently aligns Feet-Hands-Face and maintains through the direction, ≥80% of MTS intervals with ≤1 prompt across 3 sessions.

Micro-Break Request: When dysregulated, the client uses a 30-120 s movement/regulation break card (stand, wall push-ups, stretch) and returns to Ready Body within 10 s of timer, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Adjust-Then-Engage: Client quietly adjusts seat/footrest/fidget (≤10 s) and resumes Ready Body without staff prompting, ≥80% opportunities.

Specific Help Request: If posture is disrupted by task difficulty, client says, "Can you show me where to start?" and then returns to Ready Body, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Nonverbal Cue First: Silent point to the Feet-Hands-Face visual; wait 3-5 s.

One Brief Verbal Prompt: "Ready body." Pair with When-Then: "When Ready Body, then we start the game."

Prompt a FERB: Offer micro-break or seat/footrest adjustment; set a brief timer and cue return.
