Planned Ignore + Pivot Reinforcement: Withhold attention to off-posture behaviors; immediately praise and reinforce the first Ready Body recovery.

Reduce Demand Intensity if posture signals overwhelm (shorten chunk, provide guided first step).

Safety & Consent: Use only least intrusive prompts; avoid physical repositioning unless part of a consented plan and per site policy. If distress rises, pause instruction and follow the regulation plan.

The client will comply with the therapist's request to begin undesired task

The client will comply with tedious tasks such as reading and writing and begin with no prompting necessary

The client will comply to all adult directives aside from therapist

Task Persistence

The Client will persist in completing a non-preferred or moderately challenging task for up to 5 minutes with 2 Verbal Prompts, Once Per Session

Target Behavior: Task Persistence (5 minutes with ≤2 verbal prompts, once per session)

Operational Definition

Task Persistence&nbsp;is defined as the client actively engaging with an assigned&nbsp;non-preferred or moderately challenging task&nbsp;for&nbsp;5 consecutive minutes&nbsp;following a clear start directive, with&nbsp;no more than two brief verbal prompts&nbsp;from staff during that 5-minute interval.
Engagement includes:
Writing/typing, reading required materials, solving problems, assembling/manipulating task items.

Following task directions, answering task-related questions, or asking brief, on-topic clarification (≤10 seconds).

Quiet scanning of instructions or materials (≤10 seconds) that directly precedes the next response.

Not counted as engagement:
Inactivity or looking away from materials >10 seconds, unrelated conversation, play with non-task items, out-of-seat without task materials, refusal statements, elopement, tantrums, or behaviors requiring staff to remove the demand.

Assistance beyond two verbal prompts (e.g., modeling, physical guidance, step-by-step coaching) within the 5 minutes.

Start/Stop Rules:
Start&nbsp;timer when the client begins the first observable task action after the start directive and materials are available.

Pause/terminate&nbsp;and mark "not met" if: a) >10 seconds continuous off-task behavior occurs, b) a safety concern arises, or c) >2 verbal prompts are required.

One opportunity per session:&nbsp;Record data for the first eligible non-preferred/moderately challenging task that occurs that session (e.g., independent writing, multi-step worksheet, cleaning up, problem set). "Non-preferred" is determined by recent preference data or staff report over the last two weeks.

Measurement:&nbsp;Duration with integrity checks (task type, prompts used, off-task episodes).&nbsp;Criterion:&nbsp;5:00 minutes engaged, ≤2 verbal prompts.
Probable Function

Primary hypothesis:&nbsp;escape/avoidance&nbsp;of effortful tasks. Secondary contributors may include&nbsp;access to adult attention&nbsp;or&nbsp;tangibles&nbsp;(preferred activities) obtained by delaying/ending work.
Proactive Strategies

Antecedent adjustments
Provide a&nbsp;clear, concise start directive&nbsp;and model the first example only&nbsp;before&nbsp;timing starts.

Task shaping/chunking:&nbsp;break work into 1-3 minute chunks with visible checklist.

Choice within task:&nbsp;order, materials, seating, or writing utensil.

Interspersal/high-p sequence:&nbsp;2-3 easy items before moderate items.

Visuals & timing:&nbsp;first-then card, visual timer, and a 5-box "minute tracker."

Environmental supports:&nbsp;minimize distractions; have all materials ready; set "no help beyond 2 prompts" expectation.

Reinforcement plan
DRA for persistence:&nbsp;labeled praise every 60-90s of continuous engagement ("You're sticking with it—nice perseverance").

Immediate terminal reinforcer&nbsp;at 5:00 (token, sticker, brief preferred activity). Use Premack: "First 5 minutes, then ___."

Thin gradually (e.g., from every 60s to every 120s) once criterion is consistently met.

Instructional supports
Pre-teach&nbsp;self-monitoring&nbsp;with a minute-by-minute checkmark card.

Pre-teach&nbsp;help script&nbsp;and&nbsp;"next-step" self-talk&nbsp;("Read, underline, do #1").

Use neutral, consistent&nbsp;micro-prompts&nbsp;only when needed (max two): "Keep going," "What's next?"

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Request Help Appropriately:&nbsp;Given difficulty, the client will use a brief help script ("I need a hint") and resume within 10 seconds after support.

Request a Timed Micro-Break and Return:&nbsp;Client may request&nbsp;one 30-60s micro-break&nbsp;per task chunk (not during the timed 5-minute trial) using a break card; returns when timer signals.

Self-Monitoring for Stamina:&nbsp;Client marks each minute of engagement on a card and quietly celebrates progress at minutes 2, 3, 4.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Least attention to escape behaviors:&nbsp;brief, neutral redirection to FERB ("Use your help card"); avoid debate/lectures.

Prompt, then pause:&nbsp;deliver 1 prompt, allow up to 10s latency; if needed, deliver the 2nd and final prompt.

Contingent choice:&nbsp;"Keep working 2 more minutes&nbsp;or&nbsp;take a 30s break and then continue."

Modify demand without removing it:&nbsp;reduce to the next mastered step or provide one hint, then fade.

Protect reinforcement contingencies:&nbsp;do&nbsp;not&nbsp;grant preferred activities for problem behavior; provide reinforcement contingent on any return-to-task or on FERBs.

Safety first:&nbsp;if behavior escalates to unsafe, suspend timing, follow safety plan, and re-offer the task later with a smaller chunk.

Delayed Gratification

The Client will delay gratification by waiting up to 2 minutes for a preferred item or activity when told "wait," with 2 Verbal Prompts, Once Per Session

Target Behavior: Delayed Gratification — Wait up to 2 minutes for a preferred item/activity (≤2 verbal prompts, once per session)

Operational Definition

Delayed gratification (waiting) is defined as the client refraining from accessing a clearly identified preferred item or activity for up to 2 minutes after being told "Wait," while remaining calm and safe, with no more than two brief verbal prompts from staff during the interval.
Counts as successful waiting (all must be true):
No access to the item/activity until the timer ends and permission is given.

Calm body/voice: hands/feet to self; voice at conversational volume; neutral/brief comments only (≤5s).

Stays in designated area (e.g., within arm's length of desk or beside staff).

Acceptable quiet alternatives while waiting (if pre-taught): gentle fidget, deep breaths, counting, visual timer check, looking at a book/picture card.

Does NOT count:
Touching/attempting to take the item, bargaining/arguing >10s, repeated "when?" questions (>1x), whining >10s, profanity, threats, property destruction, aggression, elopement, or requiring >2 verbal prompts, modeling, or physical guidance.

Start/Stop rules:
Start timing within 5s of the "Wait" directive once the preferred item/activity is identified and visible/clearly available later.

Terminate/mark "not met" if any disqualifying behavior occurs, more than 2 verbal prompts are needed, or safety concerns arise.

Release at 2:00 with "Thanks for waiting—your turn now," and deliver the promised access immediately (contingent on success).

One opportunity per session: Score the first eligible waiting trial that session.

Measurement/criterion: Event-based (Y/N). Criterion met when the client completes a full 2-minute wait with ≤2 brief verbal prompts and no disqualifying behaviors.
Probable Function

Primary: tangible access (immediate item/activity).
 Secondary contributors: attention (seeking adult interaction during wait) and/or escape (from the demand to wait).
Proactive Strategies

Set-up & teaching
Pre-teach the Wait Routine (model → role-play → feedback): "Hear 'wait' → hands to self → look at timer/choose coping → earn it."

Use visual supports: "First-Then" card, Wait card, and a visual timer (countdown or sand timer).

Choice within the wait: choose a coping option (breaths, count to 20, quiet fidget, look at book) or a non-competing neutral activity (not a preferred alternative).

Predictability: preview expectations—"Sometimes you'll wait up to 2 minutes; then your turn."

Environment: place the item slightly out of reach but in sight; remove competing triggers.

Reinforcement plan
Immediate, high-quality reinforcement: deliver the promised item/activity at release, plus labeled praise ("You waited the whole time—great patience").

Add a bonus (token/sticker) for full, prompt compliance with 0-1 prompts to motivate clean trials.

If building toward 2 minutes, shape (e.g., 20s → 40s → 60s → 90s → 120s across days) while maintaining high reinforcement.

Prompting (max two during the interval)
Prompt 1 (micro-cue): "Remember: wait. Check the timer."

Prompt 2 (redirect to coping): "Use your wait plan—hands to self, quiet breaths."

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Appropriate Wait Response: On hearing "Wait," client holds hands to self, orients to timer, and begins a pre-taught coping routine within 5s.

One-time Information Request: If unsure, client asks once: "How long?" or "When timer done?" then returns to waiting.

Coping Menu Use: Deep breaths (3-5), silent counting, fidget squeeze, reading a neutral card, quiet self-talk ("I can wait").

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Brief, neutral prompts only (as above), then pause 5-10s for compliance. Avoid lectures/bargaining.

Shield the item (move slightly farther/cover) if reaching occurs; restate "Wait" once and reset only if safe/appropriate per plan.

Contingent choice (if struggling): "Wait quietly for 30s then timer starts or choose a coping tool now and timer starts."

Protect contingencies: do not deliver the preferred item/activity if the wait criteria aren't met; instead, provide a brief neutral delay and re-teach later.

Safety first: pause trial for any unsafe behavior and follow the safety plan; re-offer with a shorter interval when calm.

Effort Over Time

The Client will sustain effort on a multi-step functional task until completion, with 2 Verbal Prompts, Once Per Session

Target Behavior: Sustained Effort on a Multi-Step Functional Task (completion with ≤2 verbal prompts, once per session)

Operational Definition

Sustained effort is defined as the client initiating and completing all steps of a pre-defined multi-step functional task (e.g., pack backpack, clean work area, file/sort papers, simple snack prep, handwashing/laundry step sequence) to the task's completion criteria, with no more than two brief verbal prompts from staff across the entire task.
Completion criteria (set on a task analysis/checklist before the probe):
All required steps are performed in the correct order (or approved flexible order) and to an acceptable standard (e.g., items placed/labeled correctly; area visibly clean; materials put away).

Client transitions from one step to the next within 10 seconds without additional coaching.

Natural waiting that is part of the task (e.g., microwave timer) does not count as off-task if the client stays oriented to the task (e.g., sets timer, gathers next item).

Counts as sustained effort (examples):
Following the visual/printed checklist; gathering materials; completing each step; brief, on-topic clarification (≤10s) leading directly back to work; monitoring a built-in wait (timer).

Does NOT count:
Off-task behavior >10s (unrelated talk, play with non-task items), refusal statements, elopement, arguing/bargaining, out-of-area without task need, or any need for modeling/physical guidance/step-by-step coaching.

More than two verbal prompts total (e.g., "Keep going," "What's next?").

Start/Stop rules:
Start when materials are available and the client begins Step 1 after a clear start directive.

Terminate/mark "not met" if: a) any disqualifying behavior occurs, b) >2 verbal prompts are required, c) safety concerns arise.

One opportunity per session: Score the first eligible multi-step functional task that session.

Measurement/criterion: Event-based (Y/N) with integrity checks. Criterion met when all steps on the task analysis are completed to criterion with ≤2 brief verbal prompts and no disqualifying behaviors.
Probable Function

Primary: escape/avoidance from effort or task demands.
 Secondary: access to attention/tangibles by delaying/ending tasks.
Proactive Strategies

Antecedent setup
Provide a clear start directive and show the visual task analysis/checklist (pictures or text).

Task shaping/chunking: group steps into 2-4 mini-chunks if needed; fade chunks as success improves.

Choice within task: order of sub-steps, tool choice, workspace, or music/no-music.

Materials ready & environment simplified: minimize distractions; stage required items.

Preview "prompt rules": "You'll get at most two quick reminders; try to follow your checklist."

Teaching supports
Pre-teach how to use the checklist ("Check → Do → Check").

Teach next-step self-talk ("What's next?") and a placeholder strategy (finger/marker) to keep place.

Use a visual timer only for embedded waits (e.g., microwave) or to show overall routine length.

Reinforcement
Labeled praise at natural step boundaries ("You moved right to the next step—great persistence").

Immediate terminal reinforcer on completion (access to preferred activity, token, small edible). Thin praise density as independence stabilizes.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Brief help request: "I need a hint for step __," then resume within 10s after a single hint.

Self-prompting: Client points to/reads next step on checklist and proceeds without adult cue.

Micro-break request (outside safety issues): One 30-60s reset between mini-chunks; returns when timer signals.

Error repair: Client identifies and self-corrects a missed step within 30s without extra adult coaching.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Prompt-then-pause: deliver one brief cue ("Check your list"), wait up to 10s; if needed, deliver the second and final cue ("What's next on your list?").

Contingent choice: "Finish the last 2 steps or take a 30s reset and then finish."

Modify without removing the demand: reduce to the next actionable sub-step or provide one neutral hint; fade immediately.

Protect contingencies: no access to preferred items/extended attention for refusal; reinforce any return-to-task or use of FERBs.

Safety first: follow safety plan if escalation occurs; re-offer later with smaller chunks.

Staying Within Boundaries

The client will ask permission respectfully before leaving a session or room

Resilience

The Client will respond to a failure or setback e.g., making an error, losing a game by using a positive coping response e.g., saying "I'll try again," taking a deep breath Once Per Session with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior: Positive Coping After a Setback (≤2 verbal prompts, once per session)

Operational Definition

Positive coping after a setback is defined as the client using an approved coping response and remaining calm/safe following a minor-moderate failure (e.g., error/correction, losing a game/turn, brief denied access, small schedule change), with no more than two brief verbal prompts from staff.
Success criteria (all must be met):
Initiation: Within 30 seconds of the setback (or prompt), the client initiates one or more approved coping responses, such as:
Coping phrase: "I'll try again," "That's okay," "Good game," "I can fix it."

3-5 deep breaths, silent count to 10, brief grounding (5-4-3-2-1), calm-down card/fidget squeeze.

Appropriate help/break request (one-time, ≤6 words), then returns when cued.

Calm body/voice: conversational volume; hands/feet to self; no threats or property misuse.

Return to activity: Resumes/continues the task or social routine within 60 seconds and stays engaged ≥60 seconds without escalation.

Does NOT count: arguing/bargaining >10s, blame/complaints >10s, profanity, refusal/elopement, aggression, property destruction, repeated reassurance-seeking, or >2 verbal prompts, modeling, or physical guidance.
Start/Stop rules:
Start when a qualifying setback occurs and materials/expectations are in place.

Terminate/mark "not met" if any disqualifying behavior occurs, >2 prompts are required, or safety concerns arise.

One opportunity per session: Score the first eligible setback each session.

Measurement/criterion: Event-based (Y/N) with integrity checks. Criterion is met when coping is initiated ≤30s, calm is maintained ≥60s, and activity resumes within 60s for ≥60s, with ≤2 prompts.
Probable Function

Primary: escape/avoidance from error/effort or loss;
 Secondary: attention (seeking adult mediation) and/or tangible delay.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the skill (BST): model → rehearse → feedback for coping phrases, breathing, grounding, "good game" script, brief help/break request.
 Visuals & routines: coping menu card, feelings thermometer/zones, first-then, visual timer.
 Pre-corrections: "If it's not how you want, choose a coping strategy first."
 Error-friendly environment: allow redo/repair, erase/try-again opportunities, embed small "planned oops" during practice.
 Choice & predictability: warn of transitions; let the client choose the coping tool; define game rules and win/lose expectations beforehand.
 Reinforcement: labeled praise for micro-skills ("Nice reset breath"), plus immediate small reinforcer for a clean coping response; fade density as independence increases.
Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Coping phrase + resume: "I'll try again/Good game," then back to task within 10s.

Breathing/grounding routine: completes 3-5 breaths or a 10-count and re-engages.

One-time help/break request: "Help with #2, please," or "Short break?"—returns on cue/timer.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Acknowledge + prompt (max two): "That's frustrating. Choose a strategy," then pause 5-10s. If needed: "Use your breathing/phrase."

Neutral affect; brief language; avoid lectures/debates.

Contingent choice: "Use a coping tool now or take a 30s reset and then continue."

Protect contingencies: no preferred access/extended attention for arguing/refusal; reinforce any return-to-calm and FERBs.

Safety first: follow safety plan for escalation; re-teach later with a simpler practice scenario.

Tantrum FERB's

The client will respond appropriately and respectfully with prompting

Attention

Attending Skills

Sustaining attention to a challenging task for a duration of 5 minutes

Target Behavior: 
 Sustaining attention to a challenging task for a duration of 5 minutes.
&nbsp;
Motivating Operation:
 Enhancing focus, developing perseverance, and fostering the ability to complete tasks that require extended attention.
Antecedent Procedures:
 1. Identify tasks that are challenging but within the client's ability level.
 2. Create a distraction-free environment conducive for focused work.
 3. Make the client aware that the goal is to sustain attention for 5 minutes on a challenging task.
&nbsp;
Discriminative Stimulus:
 The initiation of a challenging task that requires focused attention.
Appropriate Response:
 The client will maintain focused attention on the challenging task for a full 5 minutes without engaging in off-task behaviors, to be considered a successful trial.
&nbsp;
Error Correction Procedures:
 1. **Prompting**: If the client becomes distracted or disengages, use verbal or non-verbal prompts to redirect their focus to the task.
 2. **Modeling**: Demonstrate sustained attention on a similar task.
 3. **Immediate Feedback**: Provide specific feedback about the importance of sustaining attention on challenging tasks.
 4. **Reinforcement**: Positive reinforcement is given when the client successfully sustains attention for 5 minutes.
&nbsp;
Criteria for Mastery: 
 The client will sustain attention on a challenging task for 5 minutes in 8 out of 10 opportunities across 3 consecutive data collection sessions.
&nbsp;
This ABA goal is designed to improve the client's ability to focus and persevere, which are critical skills for academic and life success.
Instructional Notes

MO:
 Enhancing focus, developing perseverance, and fostering the ability to complete tasks that require extended attention.
 
 Antecedent Procedures:
Identify tasks that are challenging but within the client's ability level.
 2. Create a distraction-free environment conducive for focused work.
 3. Make the client aware that the goal is to sustain attention for 5 minutes on a challenging task.
&nbsp;

 
 SD:
 The initiation of a challenging task that requires focused attention.
 
 Response:
 The client will maintain focused attention on the challenging task for a full 5 minutes without engaging in off-task behaviors, to be considered a successful trial.
 
 Prompting:
 visual prompt,verbal prompt,gestural prompt,modeling
 
 Error Correction:
 1. **Prompting**: If the client becomes distracted or disengages, use verbal or non-verbal prompts to redirect their focus to the task. 2. **Modeling**: Demonstrate sustained attention on a similar task. 3. **Immediate Feedback**: Provide specific feedback about the importance of sustaining attention on challenging tasks. 4. **Reinforcement**: Positive reinforcement is given when the client successfully sustains attention for 5 minutes.
 
 Reinforcement:
 differential social reinforcement,socially mediated reinforcement

Sustaining Attention

The Client will maintain focused attention on a non-preferred or play task for 5 minutes with 2 Verbal Prompts, Once Per Session

Selective Attention

The Client will sustain focus on a task for 5 minutes while ignoring minor distractions e.g., peer noise, movement in the room with 2 Verbal Prompts, Once Per Session

Target Behavior: Distraction-Tolerant Focus (5 minutes with ≤2 verbal prompts, once per session)

Operational Definition

Distraction-tolerant focus is defined as the client sustaining task-directed engagement for 5 consecutive minutes after a clear start directive, while ignoring minor environmental distractions, with no more than two brief verbal prompts from staff during that interval.
Counts as on-task/focused:
Writing/typing, reading assigned material, solving items, assembling/manipulating required materials.

Brief, on-topic clarification (≤10s) that leads directly back to work.

Momentary glances (≤2s) toward a distraction without stopping work.

Does NOT count:
Looking toward a distraction >2s, commenting on it, laughing at it, or orienting the body away from work.

Stopping work >3s due to a distraction, out-of-seat not required by the task, unrelated talk/noises, refusal statements.

Assistance beyond two brief prompts (e.g., modeling, step-by-step coaching, physical guidance).

Minor distractions (examples): peer whispering/tapping, chair squeaks, pencil drops, classmates walking by, door opening/closing, hallway noise, brief movement in the room.
 Not minor (exclude/stop timing): direct instruction to the client, someone addressing/touching the client, safety events, alarms, yelling, or any sustained loud noise that would reasonably require adult attention.
Start/Stop rules:
Start when materials are present and the client begins the first observable task action after the start directive.

Terminate/mark "not met" if: a) any single off-task pause >3s tied to a minor distraction occurs, b) more than 2 verbal prompts are needed, or c) a non-minor event occurs (follow safety plan).

One opportunity per session: Score the first eligible task presented that session.

Measurement/criterion: Duration with integrity checks. Criterion met when the client maintains focus for 5:00 with ≤2 prompts and no disqualifying responses to minor distractions.
Probable Function

Primary: escape/avoidance from effort + sensitivity to environmental stimuli.
 Secondary: attention (commenting on distractions draws adult/peer attention).
Proactive Strategies

Antecedent setup
Deliver a concise start directive and preview expectation: "Work for 5 minutes and ignore little noises/movement."

Seat/space optimization: position away from high-traffic areas; reduce visual clutter; have all materials ready.

Choice within task: order of items, tools, seating, or writing utensil.

Visual supports: first-then card, visual timer, and a 5-box "minute tracker."

Pre-teach the skill of ignoring: define "minor vs. major," model quick glance-and-return, and practice with 1-2 brief mock distractions.

If allowed, noise-reduction (non-music) headphones or a privacy shield as a temporary accommodation—plan to fade as tolerance improves.

Reinforcement plan
Labeled praise every 60-90s of continuous focus ("You heard the chair squeak and stayed with your work—great ignoring!").

Terminal reinforcer at 5:00 (token, sticker, brief preferred activity). Thin praise density as success stabilizes.

Instructional supports
Teach a self-talk script: "Eyes on work; hands doing; ignore it."

Teach a help script for non-minor issues: "This is too loud; can I move?"

Use neutral micro-prompts only as needed (max two): "Back to work," "Eyes on."

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Ignore Routine: On encountering a minor distraction, client silently re-orients within 2s and continues work.

Appropriate Relocation Request (for non-minor/persistent noise): "May I move to a quieter spot?" and returns to task within 30s.

Self-Monitoring: Client checks one box per focused minute and quietly re-sets attention when a distraction occurs.

Break Request (outside the 5-min probe): One 30-60s micro-break per work chunk using a break card; returns when timer signals.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Brief, neutral redirection to FERB: "Eyes on work," then pause up to 10s.

If needed, deliver the second and final prompt; avoid discussion about the distraction.

Contingent choice: "Work 2 more minutes or take a 30s reset, then finish."

If a distraction persists or is not minor, pause timing, adjust environment (move seat/headphones), and restart when appropriate.

Protect contingencies: do not provide preferred access or extended attention for commenting on distractions; reinforce any return-to-task and use of FERBs.

Safety first: follow the safety plan for any non-minor event.

Divided Attention

The Client will divide attention between two tasks e.g., listening to instructions while completing written work for 3 minutes with 2 Verbal Prompts, Once Per Session

Target Behavior: Divided Attention (dual-task) — 3 minutes with ≤2 verbal prompts, once per session

Operational Definition

Divided attention is defined as the client simultaneously listening to live instructions while continuing a primary written/manipulative task for 3 consecutive minutes after a clear start directive, with no more than two brief verbal prompts from staff during that interval.
Counts as divided-attention engagement (examples):
Writing/working while orienting to the speaker within 2 seconds of each instruction cue.

Brief pencil pause during instruction (≤3s), then resumes task within 5-10s producing work that matches the instruction.

Jotting a 1-3 word note from the instruction and returning to work.

Giving a brief, on-topic acknowledgement (e.g., nod, "ok") without stopping work flow.

Does NOT count:
Stopping work >3s for reasons other than the instruction being delivered.

Missing or ignoring instructions (no response within 10s after the instruction ends).

Off-topic comments, unrelated movement, out-of-seat (not required), or requesting repeats more than once per instruction.

Assistance beyond two brief verbal prompts (e.g., modeling, step-by-step coaching, physical guidance).

Instruction events during probe: At least 2 instruction events (e.g., "Underline key word," "Skip #3," "Start sentence 2") occur naturally or are embedded during the 3-minute interval.
Start/Stop rules:
Start timing when materials are present and the client begins the first observable task action after the start directive.

Terminate/mark "not met" if: a) any single off-task pause >3s unrelated to instruction occurs, b) >2 verbal prompts are required, c) safety/event disruptions occur.

One opportunity per session: Score the first eligible dual-task block presented that session.

Measurement/criterion: Duration with integrity checks. Criterion is met when the client sustains divided attention for 3:00, follows ≥80% of instruction events within 10s, and requires ≤2 verbal prompts.
Probable Function

Primary: escape/avoidance from increased cognitive load of dual-tasking.
 Secondary: attention (commenting to gain adult/peer attention) and/or tangibles (delaying work to access preferred items).
Proactive Strategies

Antecedent setup
Set expectation: "For 3 minutes, you'll listen and work at the same time."

Embed short, clear instructions (≤6 words) at predictable points (about every 45-60s).

Provide a visual anchor card: "Listen → Note (1-3 words) → Resume."

Seat/space optimization: fewer visual distractions; materials prepped; speaker within client's visual field.

Choice within task: order of items, writing tool, or place to start.

Use a visual timer and a simple 3-box minute tracker.

Skill teaching
Rehearse a quick orient-note-resume routine with a 30-60s practice before the probe.

Teach a help script for clarity: "Repeat once, please."

Pre-teach a placeholder strategy (dot or finger on line) to resume exactly where they paused.

Reinforcement
Labeled praise every 45-60s of dual-task success ("Nice—listened and kept writing.").

Terminal reinforcer at 3:00 (token, sticker, brief preferred activity). Fade praise density as performance stabilizes.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

One-time Repeat Request: For unclear instructions, client says "Repeat once, please," then resumes within 5-10s.

Placeholder & Resume: Client marks a dot/holds place during instruction and restarts at that point immediately after.

Micro-Note: Client writes a 1-3 word cue from the instruction (e.g., "skip #3") and continues the task.

Self-Monitoring: Client checks one box per focused minute without verbalizing.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Prompt-then-pause: brief cue ("Eyes up," then "Back to working") with up to 10s wait; maximum two prompts in the interval.

Contingent choice: "Keep going for 1 more minute or take a 20-30s reset, then finish."

Adjust density, not difficulty: if struggling, reduce instruction frequency (every ~60-90s) while keeping the task active.

Protect contingencies: do not grant preferred access for off-task talk; reinforce any return-to-task and FERBs.

Safety first: if escalation occurs, stop timing, follow safety plan, and re-offer later with a shorter dual-task block.

Communication

Functional Communication Training

Self Advocacy

The client will appropriately communicate personal needs when prompted in various settings

Target Behavior: 
 Appropriately communicating personal needs when prompted in various settings.
&nbsp;
Motivating Operation:
 Encouraging self-advocacy, fostering better understanding between the client and others, and enhancing overall well-being through clear communication of needs.
Antecedent Procedures:
 1. Create an environment that encourages open communication, such as a therapy room or a quiet space.
 2. Engage in activities or discussions that would naturally lead to the client having a need (e.g., thirst, a question, needing a break).
 3. Inform the client that they will be prompted to express any needs they might have during the activity or discussion.
&nbsp;
Discriminative Stimulus:
 The therapist or caregiver prompts, "Is there anything you need right now?" or a similar question intended to elicit the expression of needs.
Appropriate Response:
 Within 15 seconds of the SD, the client will appropriately communicate a current need, such as "I need a glass of water," or "I need to take a break," to be considered a successful trial.
&nbsp;
Error Correction Procedures:
 1. **Modeling**: If the client is unable to express their need, the therapist will model an appropriate way to communicate a need.
 2. **Prompting**: Use verbal or gestural prompts like "You can tell me if you need something," to encourage communication.
 3. **Immediate Feedback**: Provide specific feedback about any inappropriate or unclear expression of needs and how to improve.
 4. **Repetition**: Re-prompt the SD to give the client another opportunity to appropriately communicate their need.
&nbsp;
Criteria for Mastery: 
 The client will appropriately communicate their needs within 15 seconds of the SD in 8 out of 10 trials across 3 consecutive data collection sessions. Mastery should be generalized across various settings and types of needs.
&nbsp;
This ABA goal is designed to improve the client's ability to advocate for themselves by appropriately communicating their needs, which is a crucial skill for both social interactions and individual well-being.
Instructional Notes

MO:
 Encouraging self-advocacy, fostering better understanding between the client and others, and enhancing overall well-being through clear communication of needs.
 
 Antecedent Procedures:
Create an environment that encourages open communication, such as a therapy room or a quiet space.
 2. Engage in activities or discussions that would naturally lead to the client having a need (e.g., thirst, a question, needing a break).
 3. Inform the client that they will be prompted to express any needs they might have during the activity or discussion.

 
 SD:
 The therapist or caregiver prompts, "Is there anything you need right now?" or a similar question intended to elicit the expression of needs.
 
 Response:
 Within 15 seconds of the SD, the client will appropriately communicate a current need, such as "I need a glass of water," or "I need to take a break," to be considered a successful trial.
 
 Prompting:
 visual prompt,verbal prompt,gestural prompt,modeling
 
 Error Correction:
 1. **Modeling**: If the client is unable to express their need, the therapist will model an appropriate way to communicate a need. 2. **Prompting**: Use verbal or gestural prompts like "You can tell me if you need something," to encourage communication. 3. **Immediate Feedback**: Provide specific feedback about any inappropriate or unclear expression of needs and how to improve. 4. **Repetition**: Re-prompt the SD to give the client another opportunity to appropriately communicate their need.
 
 Reinforcement:
 differential social reinforcement,socially mediated reinforcement

Verbalizing discomfort or annoyance when something bothers the client in different situations

Target Behavior: 
 Verbalizing discomfort or annoyance when something bothers the client in different situations.
&nbsp;
Motivating Operation:
 Promoting emotional intelligence, self-advocacy, and effective communication to improve well-being and social relationships.
Antecedent Procedures:
 1. Create a supportive environment that encourages open expression, such as a therapy room or a familiar social setting.
 2. Engage in activities or scenarios that could potentially bother or discomfort the client, either planned or naturally occurring.
 3. Signal to the client that it is acceptable and encouraged to speak up if something bothers them.
&nbsp;
Discriminative Stimulus:
 An event, comment, or situation occurs that could reasonably be assumed to bother or discomfort the client.
Appropriate Response:
 Within 30 seconds of recognizing the discomforting event, the client will express that something is bothering them in an appropriate manner, such as saying "I don't like that," or "That bothers me," to be considered a successful trial.
&nbsp;
Error Correction Procedures:
 1. **Modeling**: If the client doesn't express discomfort, the therapist will model an appropriate way to verbalize feelings.
 2. **Prompting**: Use verbal or non-verbal cues, like "How are you feeling about this?" to encourage the client to express themselves.
 3. **Immediate Feedback**: Provide specific feedback about any missed opportunities or inappropriate ways of expressing discomfort.
 4. **Repetition**: Re-create the situation or present a new discomforting scenario to allow the client another chance to express themselves appropriately.
&nbsp;
Criteria for Mastery: 
 The client will express their discomfort appropriately within 30 seconds of the triggering event in 8 out of 10 trials across 3 consecutive data collection sessions. Mastery should be demonstrated across a variety of situations and settings.
&nbsp;
This ABA goal aims to help the client become more expressive and self-advocative, particularly when something bothers them. This skill is crucial for emotional well-being and effective interpersonal relations.
Instructional Notes

MO:
 Promoting emotional intelligence, self-advocacy, and effective communication to improve well-being and social relationships.
 
 Antecedent Procedures:
Create a supportive environment that encourages open expression, such as a therapy room or a familiar social setting.
 2. Engage in activities or scenarios that could potentially bother or discomfort the client, either planned or naturally occurring.
 3. Signal to the client that it is acceptable and encouraged to speak up if something bothers them.
&nbsp;

 
 SD:
 An event, comment, or situation occurs that could reasonably be assumed to bother or discomfort the client.
 
 Response:
 Within 30 seconds of recognizing the discomforting event, the client will express that something is bothering them in an appropriate manner, such as saying "I don't like that," or "That bothers me," to be considered a successful trial.
 
 Prompting:
 visual prompt,verbal prompt,gestural prompt,modeling
 
 Error Correction:
 1. **Modeling**: If the client doesn't express discomfort, the therapist will model an appropriate way to verbalize feelings. 2. **Prompting**: Use verbal or non-verbal cues, like "How are you feeling about this?" to encourage the client to express themselves. 3. **Immediate Feedback**: Provide specific feedback about any missed opportunities or inappropriate ways of expressing discomfort. 4. **Repetition**: Re-create the situation or present a new discomforting scenario to allow the client another chance to express themselves appropriately.
 
 Reinforcement:
 differential social reinforcement,socially mediated reinforcement

Verbalizing "no" appropriately in social situations where the client does not want to engage in a particular activity or action

Target Behavior:
Verbalizing "no" appropriately in social situations where the client does not want to engage in a particular activity or action.
Operational Definition:

The client will clearly and audibly say the word "no" when prompted with a choice or activity they do not wish to partake in. The response should be within 5 seconds of the prompt and must be made in an appropriate tone and volume.
Condition:

During structured role-play sessions with the therapist and in naturalistic settings with known adults and peers, using real or simulated choices and activities.
Measurement:

The behavior will be measured using frequency data collection, noting each instance where the client successfully verbalizes "no" within 5 seconds of a choice or activity they do not wish to partake in. Anecdotal notes will be taken to provide context and clarity.
Criteria for Mastery:

The client will appropriately verbalize "no" within 5 seconds in 8 out of 10 trials across 3 consecutive data collection sessions. The mastery should be generalized across multiple settings and with different individuals.
Instructional Notes

MO:
 Decrease in social pressure and increased self-advocacy to better align the client's actions with their own preferences.
 
 Antecedent Procedures:
Create a safe and structured environment for role-play or natural setting for real-life scenarios.
Introduce the social context where the client typically struggles to say "no."
Ensure the presence of known adults or peers to simulate the social situation.

 
 SD:
 A direct or implied request or choice is presented to the client, such as "Would you like to play this game?" or "Let's go do [Activity X]."
 
 Response:
 The client will clearly and audibly say the word "no" within 5 seconds of the SD. The response must be in an appropriate tone and volume to be considered a successful trial.
 
 Prompting:
 visual prompt,verbal prompt,gestural prompt,modeling
 
 Error Correction:
 Modeling: If the client fails to say "no" or does so inadequately, the therapist will model the correct behavior. Prompting: Use a verbal or gestural prompt to indicate the expected response. Fade prompts as the client becomes more proficient. Immediate Feedback: Provide immediate feedback for incorrect responses, clarifying what went wrong and how to correct it. Repetition: Immediately re-present the SD to offer the client another opportunity to demonstrate the correct behavior.
 
 Reinforcement:
 socially mediated reinforcement

The client will ask for help with a difficult task with 2 verbal prompts

The Client will ask for help if he feels overwhelmed by a transition with 2 verbal prompts

Target Behavior: Ask for Help With a Difficult Task (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

"Appropriate help-seeking" is defined as the client communicating a clear request for assistance verbally or via an approved modality (e.g., AAC button, "help" card, raised hand with "Help please," or "Can you show me?") during a difficult task.
A difficult task is any assigned task where one or more of the following occurs:
No progress for ≥10 seconds despite engagement, or

2 consecutive errors on the same step, or

Observable signs of frustration (e.g., sighing, fidgeting, saying "I can't") for ≥10 seconds.

A request counts when it is audible/visible to staff, directed to the available adult, and occurs independently or after ≤2 verbal prompts (e.g., Prompt 1: "Do you need help?" Prompt 2: "Ask for help."). The client should respond within 10 seconds of the prompt.
Does not include:
Indirect or unclear remarks (e.g., "Ugh," "This is stupid") without a help request.

Requests made after engaging in problem behavior to escape the task (e.g., leaving area, ripping paper).

Requests for unrelated items/activities ("I need my phone") when help is needed.

Requests that include inappropriate language.

Measurement: Each identified difficult-task episode = 1 opportunity. Record level of prompting (Independent / 1 prompt / 2 prompts / No request or problem behavior). Track latency (sec) from prompt to request.
Mastery goal for this objective: In ≥80% of difficult-task opportunities, the client will request help with ≤2 verbal prompts across 3 consecutive sessions. (Stretch goals: fade to ≤1 prompt, then independent.)
Probable Function

Primary: Escape/avoidance of task difficulty; Secondary: Access to assistance/information; possible Attention from adult. Confirm via ABC data.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Skill (FCT)

Scripted phrases: "Help please," "Can you show me?", "I need help with ___."

Modality options: help card, AAC "Help" button, hand-raise + phrase.

Three-step teaching: model → brief role-play with errorless prompts → in-task practice.

Supports & Antecedents

Prime before work: "If it's tricky, use your help card or say 'Help please.'"

Help Menu (visual): "Read again," "Show example," "Break it down," "Point to step," "Check my work."

Task design: break tasks into small steps; provide exemplars; reduce initial response effort.

Prompting hierarchy (fade quickly): gesture → visual → verbal prompt 1 → verbal prompt 2 → model.

Noncontingent check-ins during known hard parts ("I'm nearby if you need help.") to prevent escalation.

Reinforcement

Immediate, task-matched help when the client requests appropriately.

Specific praise: "Nice job asking for help the first time."

DRA: Reinforce help-seeking; do not reinforce escape following problem behavior.

Token/points for meeting interval/session targets (e.g., 4/5 opportunities).

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Help Request (primary FERB): When a task becomes difficult, the client will request help via approved modality within 10 seconds, with ≤2 verbal prompts, in 80% of opportunities across 3 sessions.

Break Request (if needed): The client will request a brief, pre-taught break ("Break please—1 minute") instead of engaging in escape behavior, with ≤2 prompts, in 70% of opportunities.

Clarification Request: The client will ask for a repeat/example ("Can you show me step 1?") with ≤2 prompts in 70% of opportunities.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Brief, neutral coaching: If signs of difficulty appear, give Prompt 1 → wait up to 10s → Prompt 2 → wait up to 10s → model if needed.

Match consequence to function: Provide assistance immediately contingent on the appropriate request; avoid removing the task solely after problem behavior.

Minimize attention to problem behavior (when safe); redirect to the help card/AAC with a calm gesture or 1-3 word cue ("Ask for help").

If escalation occurs: use few words, offer binary choice ("Ask for help or short break"), follow site safety plan.

Repair & return: After calm, practice a quick do-over (prompt → help request → assist), then praise and resume.

Data review: Analyze patterns (tasks, times, steps) and thin prompts as success increases.

The Client will verbally describe progress on his own task performance Once Per Session with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior

Increase verbally describing progress on own task performance once per session with ≤2 verbal prompts.
Operational Definition

During a designated progress check (scheduled or cued by the leader), the client gives a brief verbal update (10-30 seconds) about their own task. A qualifying update includes at least two of the elements below:
Status: what is done / percent complete / step number.

Obstacle/Need: anything in the way or help needed.

Next Step: what they will do next or by when.

Counts as success when:
The client provides the update once per session within the check window,

Requires no more than 2 verbal prompts, and

The content is about their own task.

Does not include: silence >10s after prompts; "I don't know" with no attempt; off-topic talk; updates about others' tasks; written-only responses (unless immediately read aloud).
Measurement
Primary: Success (Yes/No) per session.

Secondary: prompts used (0/1/2), latency from prompt to start (sec), and quality score (0-3):
0 = no update; 1 = includes 1 element; 2 = includes 2 elements; 3 = includes all 3.
 Onset/offset: Onset at leader's progress-check cue; offset when the update ends or 30s elapse.

Probable Function

Desired behavior is typically maintained by attention/recognition and access to support; interfering behaviors (e.g., avoidance, "I don't know," topic-shifting) often serve escape from evaluation/demands or control/autonomy. (Verify via ABC notes.)
Proactive Strategies

Antecedent setup
Predictable check: schedule a single progress check time per session (e.g., last 5 minutes); post it on a visual schedule.

Visual scaffold: a 3-part Progress Card: "I finished ___; I'm stuck on ___; Next I will ___."

Sentence starters at desk/device; optional % wheel or step tracker.

Model & rehearse the 10-30s update with exemplars; practice in pairs before whole-group.

Choice & autonomy: allow choice of order (go first/last), standing or seated, and brief/extended format.

Priming: before session, preview the moment ("We'll do one quick progress check at the end—use your card.").

Teaching & prompting
Teach a 3-step script: Status → Obstacle/Need → Next Step.

Use model-lead-test and quick role-plays ("Your turn—use the card").

Prompting hierarchy for the check:
Prompt 1 (generic): "Progress check—tell us what you've done and what's next."

Prompt 2 (scaffold): "Start with 'I finished…,' then say the next step."

Reinforcement: specific praise for elements included; token/points or access to a preferred item/role (e.g., "project lead" next session) when a 2-3 element update is given.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

If the client typically avoids or derails the check, teach these alternatives that still meet the function:
Ask for Help Briefly (escape from uncertainty)
"I need help with ___; next I will ___ after help." (Meets 'Obstacle' + 'Next Step')

Use a Rating + One Sentence (reduced demand)
"I'm at 3/5; next I will ___." (Status + Next Step)

Use a 'Pass-then-Return' Ticket (autonomy)
One per day: "Pass—come back to me after Sam." Must complete update within the same check window.

AAC/Visual-to-Verbal Bridge (communication)
Point to the 3 boxes on the card and read aloud what's indicated.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Early signs (shrugging, long pause, topic shift):
Keep tone neutral; point to Progress Card; offer A/B choices ("% complete or next step first?").

Reduce response effort: allow rating-first or yes/no scaffold ("Did you finish the outline? What's next?").

If refusal/avoidance continues after Prompt 2:
Provide a minimal success path: leader asks two closed questions to build the update:
"What's one thing you finished?" (wait) → "What's the next step?"

Deliver brief praise for completion; circle back later if needed for fuller detail.

After the check:
Reinforce recovery and completeness ("You gave status + next step—nice clarity.").

Log which scaffold worked; use it first next time

Expressing a Problem

Will express a problem to trusted adult during emotion triggering event

Comprehension

Group Instruction Follow Through

The Client will respond appropriately to a group directive by initiating the expected action within 10 seconds, Once Per Session with 2 Verbal Prompts.

Target Behavior: Group Directive Response — Initiate within 10 seconds (≤2 verbal prompts, once per session)

Operational Definition

Group directive response is defined as the client initiating the expected action within 10 seconds after a group-directed instruction (addressed to the whole class/group), with no more than two brief verbal prompts from staff.
Group directives (examples): "Line up," "Take out math," "Clean up," "Eyes on me," "Hands down," "Start page 12," "Move to centers."
Initiation = the first clear, observable step toward compliance (e.g., stands and walks to line, opens math book, begins putting items away, or orients eyes/body to speaker), and the client continues the response for ≥30 seconds or until the step is complete (e.g., reaches the line, materials are out).
Does NOT count:
No movement toward the instruction within 10s, off-topic talk, refusal/arguing, elopement, or behavior requiring task removal.

Needing more than two verbal prompts, modeling, physical guidance, or step-by-step coaching.

Instructions not addressed to the group (e.g., the client's name only), vague comments ("Let's be good"), or when materials/space are unavailable.

Timing/Scoring rules:
Start latency at the end of the group directive.

Prompts allowed (max two): brief cues such as "Do it now," "What's the direction?"

Terminate/mark 'not met' if >10s to initiate after the directive (or final prompt), disqualifying behavior occurs, or >2 prompts are used.

One opportunity per session: Score the first eligible group directive delivered in a typical context.

Measurement/criterion: Event-based Y/N + latency to initiate (s). Criterion met when initiation occurs ≤10s with ≤2 brief verbal prompts and response is maintained as defined.
Probable Function

Primary: escape/avoidance of transitions or new demands;
 Secondary: attention (seeking adult mediation) and/or processing load (difficulty tracking group cues without a direct cue).
Proactive Strategies

Antecedent supports
Pre-correction/preview: "When you hear a group direction, start within 10 seconds."

Visuals: icons for common directives (Line Up, Clean Up, Eyes On Me) on the board/desk card; first-then schedule.

Routines & signals: consistent attention signal (e.g., chime + hand up), 3-2-1 countdown into common directives.

Positioning: seat near instruction source and peer models; minimize visual/auditory distractions.

Choice within compliance: "Line up with a book or fidget, your choice," "Start with pencil or pen."

Behavioral momentum: 2-3 quick "high-P" group responses (e.g., "Show me thumbs up," "Touch your head") before a tougher directive.

Reinforcement
DRA for quick start: labeled praise for ≤10s initiation ("Nice—started right away"), token/sticker toward a small reward.

Periodic mystery motivator for zero-prompt responses to strengthen independence.

Instructional teaching
Practice the "Hear → Repeat in head → Start" routine; brief role-plays of common directives.

Teach a one-time clarification script ("Repeat, please?") and self-prompt ("What's first?").

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

One-time clarification/confirm: "What did you say?" / "Line up, right?" then initiate within 5s.

Self-prompting: Client glances at visual cue card and begins the first step without adult cue.

Transition readiness signal: Raises hand/looks to teacher and stands (for attention directives) within 10s.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Prompt-then-pause: deliver Prompt 1 ("Start now"), wait up to 5-10s; if needed, Prompt 2 ("What's the direction? Do it.").

Contingent choice: "Start now or after a 20s reset, but then go straight to line."

Reduce language, not expectation: simplify to a 2-3 word cue + gesture; avoid lectures/debates.

Protect contingencies: withhold preferred access/attention for delay/argue; reinforce any initiation/FERB.

Safety first: follow safety plan if escalation occurs; re-teach later.

The Client will respond appropriately to a group directive given during group instruction within 10 seconds, Once Per Session with 2 Verbal Prompts.

Target Behavior: Group Instruction Directive Response — Initiate within 10 seconds (≤2 verbal prompts, once per session)

Operational Definition

Group-instruction directive response is defined as the client initiating the expected action within 10 seconds of a group directive delivered during ongoing instruction (addressed to everyone), with no more than two brief verbal prompts from staff.
Examples of group directives during instruction: "Track the speaker," "Pencils down," "Open to page 34," "Get whiteboards," "Turn and talk," "Hands down," "Eyes on me," "Start number 1," "Close laptops."
Initiation = a clear, observable first step toward the directive (e.g., eyes/body orient to teacher, pencil put down, book opened, materials retrieved, partner turn begun) within 10s and continued for ≥30s or until the step is complete (e.g., materials are out).
Does NOT count:
No movement toward compliance within 10s; off-topic talk; arguing; calling out; elopement; disruption requiring task removal.

Needing >2 verbal prompts, modeling, physical guidance, or step-by-step coaching.

When the directive is not audible/visible to the client, is individualized (name only), or materials/space are unavailable.

Timing/Scoring rules:
Start latency at the end of the teacher's directive.

Prompts allowed (max two): brief cues such as "Start now," "What's the direction?"

Terminate/mark 'not met' if initiation >10s after directive (or final prompt), disqualifying behavior occurs, or >2 prompts are used.

One opportunity per session: Score the first eligible directive occurring during typical group instruction.

Measurement/criterion: Event-based Y/N + latency to initiate (s). Criterion met when initiation occurs ≤10s with ≤2 prompts and the response is maintained as defined.
Probable Function

Primary: escape/avoidance of instructional demand or transition;
 Secondary: attention (seeking adult/peer attention) and/or processing load (difficulty tracking group cues).
Proactive Strategies

Antecedent supports
Pre-correction/preview: "When we're learning as a group and I give a direction, start within 10 seconds."

Consistent attention signal (e.g., chime + hand raise) preceding directives; 3-2-1 countdown to action.

Visual icons for common directives (Track, Pencils Down, Open Book, Partner Talk) posted or on a desk card.

Seating/positioning: near instruction source and peer models; minimize distractions; materials pre-staged.

Behavioral momentum: 2-3 quick high-probability responses ("Show me your eyes," "Hands still") before tougher directives.

Choice within compliance: "Open to page 34 with a pencil or pen," "Turn-and-talk—pick partner A or B to start."

Teaching routines
Practice "Hear → Repeat in head → Start" and "Look-Hands-Do" in brief role-plays.

Teach a one-time clarification script: "Repeat, please?" then initiate within 5s.

Reinforcement
Labeled praise for fast starts ("You opened to 34 right away—nice!"), token/sticker system, occasional mystery motivator for 0-prompt responses.

Fade praise density as independence stabilizes.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Clarify once: "What was the direction?" → initiates within 5s after repeat.

Self-prompt via visual: Glances at directive icon/desk card and begins the first step.

Transition readiness signal: Eyes on teacher + materials paused (e.g., hands off keyboard) within 10s of attention directives.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Prompt-then-pause: Prompt 1 "Start now," wait 5-10s; if needed, Prompt 2 "What's the direction? Do it."

Reduce language, keep expectation: pair with a gesture/icon; avoid lectures/debates.

Contingent choice: "Begin now or take 20s to reset, then begin."

Protect contingencies: withhold preferred access/attention for delay/argue; reinforce any initiation/FERB.

Safety first: follow safety plan for escalation; re-teach later.

Functional Instructions

The Client will follow a functional instruction in a daily context within 15 seconds, Once Per Session with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior: Follow Functional Instruction in Daily Context — Initiate within 15 seconds (≤2 verbal prompts, once per session)

Operational Definition

Functional instruction response is defined as the client initiating the expected action within 15 seconds after a clear, contextually relevant directive (addressed to the client or group) and continuing the action to a logical step completion (≥30 seconds or until the first step is completed), with no more than two brief verbal prompts from staff.
Functional instruction (examples):
 "Hang up your coat." "Wash hands." "Throw the cup in the trash." "Put math folder in backpack." "Line up." "Sit at the table." "Take out notebook." "Clean up toys."
Counts as initiation:
First observable step toward compliance (stands, walks to area, reaches for item, begins the action) ≤15s after the directive ends, and continues the response for ≥30s or until the required step is completed.

Does NOT count:
No movement toward the task within 15s; off-topic talk; refusal/arguing; elopement; calling out for attention; or any need for >2 verbal prompts, modeling, physical guidance, or step-by-step coaching.

Situations where the directive was not audible/visible to the client, was ambiguous ("Be good"), or materials/space were unavailable (do not score).

Timing / Scoring rules:
Start latency timer at the end of the directive.

Prompts allowed (max two): brief cues such as "Start now," "What's the direction?" paired with a gesture.

Mark "not met" if initiation occurs >15s after the directive (or final prompt), disqualifying behavior occurs, or >2 prompts are used.

One opportunity per session: Score the first eligible functional instruction in a typical daily context.

Measurement/criterion: Event-based Y/N + latency to initiate (seconds). Criterion met when initiation occurs ≤15s, response is maintained as defined, and ≤2 brief verbal prompts are used.
Probable Function

Primary: escape/avoidance of demands or transitions.
 Secondary: attention (delaying to obtain adult/peer interaction) and/or tangibles (postponing cleanup to keep access).
Proactive Strategies

Antecedent supports
Pre-correction/preview: "When you hear a direction like 'Wash hands,' start within 15 seconds."

Visuals: first-then card; mini icons for common directives (Wash, Line Up, Clean Up) on desk/wall; visual schedules.

Routines & signals: consistent attention signal (chime/hand up) before directives; optional 3-2-1 countdown.

Environment: stage required materials (soap, towel, trash can visible); reduce distractions; seating near cues/peer models.

Behavioral momentum: 2-3 quick, easy responses ("Show me thumbs up," "Touch your nose") before tougher directives.

Instructional teaching
Rehearse the "Hear → Say in head → Start" routine with brief role-plays.

Teach a one-time clarification script: "Repeat, please?" or "Where do I put it?" followed by immediate initiation.

Teach self-prompting with a checklist or icon: point → do → check.

Reinforcement
DRA for fast starts: labeled praise ("Nice—started right away"), token/sticker, or brief preferred access after completion.

Occasional mystery motivator for zero-prompt responses; thin reinforcement as independence stabilizes.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Clarify once, then act: Client asks a single brief question (≤6 words) and initiates within 5s of the answer.

Self-prompt from visual: Client looks at the icon/first-then card and begins without adult cue.

Transition readiness: Client stops competing activity, orients to the task area, and starts the first step within 15s.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Prompt-then-pause: Prompt 1 "Start now," wait 5-10s; if needed, Prompt 2 "What's the direction? Do it."

Reduce language, keep expectation: pair with a clear gesture or point; avoid debates/lectures.

Contingent choice: "Start now or take a 20s reset and then start."

Protect contingencies: withhold preferred access/attention for delay/refusal; reinforce any initiation/FERB.

Safety first: if escalation occurs, follow safety plan and re-teach later in a simpler context.

Temporal Direction Comprehension

The Client will follow a sequenced instruction of 2-3 steps in order with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior: Follow a 2-3 Step Sequenced Instruction in Order (≤2 verbal prompts, once per session)

Operational Definition

Sequenced instruction following is defined as the client initiating and completing each step of a 2-3 step directive in the stated order after a clear instruction, with no more than two brief verbal prompts from staff across the entire sequence.
Initiation/transition timing:
Start the first step within 10 seconds of the directive ending.

Move to each next step within 10 seconds of finishing the prior step.

Short, on-task clarifications (≤5-10s) that immediately lead back to the sequence count as on task.

Examples of 2-3 step directives:
 "Get your folder, put it in your backpack, zip it." / "Throw away your cup and sit at the table." / "Open to page 12, underline the title, start number 1."
Does NOT count:
Doing steps out of order, skipping a step, or reversing steps.

Off-task >10s between steps, refusal/arguing, elopement, calling out.

Needing >2 verbal prompts, modeling, physical guidance, or step-by-step coaching during the probe.

Scoring rules:
Start latency timer at the end of the directive.

Allowed prompts (max two): brief cues like "What's next?" / "Keep going."

Mark "not met" if any disqualifying condition occurs.

One opportunity per session: Score the first eligible 2-3 step directive that session.

Measurement/criterion: Event-based Y/N + latency to start and latencies between steps. Criterion met when all steps are completed in order with ≤2 brief verbal prompts and no disqualifying behaviors.
Probable Function

Primary: escape/avoidance (effort, transitions) and/or processing load/working memory demands.
 Secondary: attention (delaying to obtain adult interaction).
Proactive Strategies

Antecedent supports
Pre-correction: "When I give 2-3 steps, start within 10 seconds and do them in order."

Visuals: 2-3 icon strip or numbers on the board; "First-Then-Then" card.

Clarity: keep language concise (≤7 words per step); number the steps ("1…2…3").

Staging: ensure materials/space are ready; seat near models/cues; reduce distractions.

Behavioral momentum: 1-2 quick high-P responses before a tougher sequence.

Teaching routines (outside the probe)
Say-Do-Check: repeat steps (aloud/subvocal) → do → check off.

Point-Say-Do with a mini checklist; finger count (1-2-3).

Rehearse common sequences; use time delay and chaining to fade prompts.

Reinforcement
Labeled praise for orderly completion and fast starts; token/sticker for 0-1 prompts.

Fade density as independence stabilizes.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

One-time repeat/confirm: "It's get folder, backpack, zip—right?" then begin within 5s.

Self-prompting: Client points to visual/holds up 1-2-3 fingers and proceeds without adult cue.

Brief help request (information only): "Where is the folder?"—then resumes sequence immediately.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Prompt-then-pause: Prompt 1 "What's next? Do it," wait 5-10s; if needed, Prompt 2 "Finish the steps in order."

Reduce language, keep expectation: pair with a point to the visual numbers.

Contingent choice: "Finish the 3 steps now or take a 20s reset and then finish."

Protect contingencies: no preferred access/extended attention for refusing; reinforce any return to sequence and FERBs.

Safety first: follow safety plan; if sequence breaks down, re-teach later with two steps and re-build.

Conversation

Maintaining Conversation Skills

The client will engage in a back-and-forth conversation with 4 exchanges

Target Behavior: 
 The client will engage in a back-and-forth conversation with 4 exchanges.
&nbsp;
Motivating Operation:
 To improve social communication skills and enhance the ability to participate in reciprocal conversations, which is crucial for both social and academic settings.
Antecedent Procedures:
 1. Training on basic conversation skills including eye contact, turn-taking, and appropriate use of language.
 2. Role-playing to practice conversation exchanges.
 3. Setting up social situations where back-and-forth conversation can occur.
&nbsp;
Discriminative Stimulus:
 A social situation where conversation is expected, such as talking to a peer, family member, or teacher.
Appropriate Response:
 The client will participate in a back-and-forth conversation consisting of at least 4 exchanges to be considered a successful trial.
&nbsp;
Error Correction Procedures:
 1. **Prompting**: If the client fails to reciprocate or continue the conversation, use a verbal or gestural prompt.
 2. **Modeling**: Demonstrate a proper back-and-forth conversation with 4 exchanges.
 3. **Immediate Feedback**: Provide immediate, specific feedback for any incorrect or incomplete attempts.
 4. **Reinforcement**: Positive reinforcement is given for successfully engaging in a 4-exchange conversation.
&nbsp;
Criteria for Mastery: 
 The client will engage in a back-and-forth conversation with 4 exchanges in 9 out of 10 opportunities across 3 consecutive data collection sessions.
&nbsp;
This ABA goal is geared toward enhancing the client's ability to have a reciprocal conversation, an essential skill for social interactions as well as for academic success.
Instructional Notes

MO:
 To improve social communication skills and enhance the ability to participate in reciprocal conversations, which is crucial for both social and academic settings.
 
 Antecedent Procedures:
Training on basic conversation skills including eye contact, turn-taking, and appropriate use of language.
 2. Role-playing to practice conversation exchanges.
 3. Setting up social situations where back-and-forth conversation can occur.
&nbsp;

 
 SD:
 A social situation where conversation is expected, such as talking to a peer, family member, or teacher.
 
 Response:
 The client will participate in a back-and-forth conversation consisting of at least 4 exchanges to be considered a successful trial.
 
 Prompting:
 visual prompt, verbal prompt ,gestural prompt,modeling
 
 Error Correction:
 1. **Prompting**: If the client fails to reciprocate or continue the conversation, use a verbal or gestural prompt. 2. **Modeling**: Demonstrate a proper back-and-forth conversation with 4 exchanges. 3. **Immediate Feedback**: Provide immediate, specific feedback for any incorrect or incomplete attempts. 4. **Reinforcement**: Positive reinforcement is given for successfully engaging in a 4-exchange conversation.
 
 Reinforcement:
 differential social reinforcement, socially mediated reinforcement

The client will speak in full sentences during conversations, avoiding 1-2 word answers

Target Behavior: 
 The client will speak in full sentences during conversations, avoiding 1-2 word answers.
&nbsp;
Motivating Operation:
 To improve expressive language skills, enrich social communication, and encourage fuller participation in both social and educational environments.
Antecedent Procedures:
 1. Train the client on sentence structure and the importance of providing complete answers.
 2. Use role-playing exercises to practice speaking in full sentences.
 3. Provide opportunities for the client to engage in conversation that encourages full sentences.
&nbsp;
Discriminative Stimulus:
 A question or conversational prompt that warrants more than a 1-2 word answer, encountered in various settings like home, school, or social situations.
Appropriate Response:
 The client will respond in a complete sentence, avoiding 1-2 word answers, to be considered a successful trial.
&nbsp;
Error Correction Procedures:
 1. **Prompting**: If the client responds with a 1-2 word answer, prompt them to expand their response.
 2. **Modeling**: Demonstrate how to appropriately respond with a full sentence.
 3. **Immediate Feedback**: Give immediate, specific feedback if the client provides an incomplete answer.
 4. **Reinforcement**: Offer positive reinforcement when the client successfully speaks in full sentences.
This goal aims to foster better communication skills by encouraging the use of full sentences. This can be particularly beneficial for engaging more meaningfully in social interactions and educational settings.
Instructional Notes

MO:
 To improve expressive language skills, enrich social communication, and encourage fuller participation in both social and educational environments.
 
 Antecedent Procedures:
Train the client on sentence structure and the importance of providing complete answers.
 2. Use role-playing exercises to practice speaking in full sentences.
 3. Provide opportunities for the client to engage in conversation that encourages full sentences.

 
 SD:
 A question or conversational prompt that warrants more than a 1-2 word answer, encountered in various settings like home, school, or social situations.
 
 Response:
 The client will respond in a complete sentence, avoiding 1-2 word answers, to be considered a successful trial.
 
 Prompting:
 visual prompt,verbal prompt,gestural prompt,modeling
 
 Error Correction:
 1. **Prompting**: If the client responds with a 1-2 word answer, prompt them to expand their response. 2. **Modeling**: Demonstrate how to appropriately respond with a full sentence. 3. **Immediate Feedback**: Give immediate, specific feedback if the client provides an incomplete answer. 4. **Reinforcement**: Offer positive reinforcement when the client successfully speaks in full sentences.
 
 Reinforcement:
 differential social reinforcement,socially mediated reinforcement

The client will stay on topic for at least 3 conversational turns

Target Behavior:
 The client will stay on topic for at least 3 conversational turns.
&nbsp;
Motivating Operation:
 To improve the quality of social interactions by enhancing conversational skills, thereby fostering better relationships and increasing social adaptability.
Antecedent Procedures:
 1. Teach the client the importance of staying on topic during conversations.
 2. Use role-play to practice staying on topic for 3 conversational turns.
 3. Introduce and practice signal or cue systems to redirect the client back to the topic if they stray.
&nbsp;
Discriminative Stimulus:
 The initiation of a conversation or the introduction of a specific topic within a conversation, either by the client or the conversational partner.
Appropriate Response:
 The client will stay on the presented topic for at least 3 conversational turns to consider it a successful trial.
&nbsp;
Error Correction Procedures:
 1. **Prompting**: If the client strays off-topic, use a verbal or non-verbal cue to guide them back to the original topic.
 2. **Modeling**: Demonstrate how to stay on topic during a conversation.
 3. **Immediate Feedback**: Provide immediate, specific feedback if the client does not stay on topic for 3 turns.
 4. **Reinforcement**: Give positive reinforcement for successfully staying on topic.
&nbsp;
Criteria for Mastery:
 The client will stay on topic for at least 3 conversational turns across 3 consecutive data collection sessions.
&nbsp;
This goal is designed to improve the client's ability to maintain focused and coherent conversations, which is a vital social skill.
Instructional Notes

MO:
 To improve the quality of social interactions by enhancing conversational skills, thereby fostering better relationships and increasing social adaptability.
 
 Antecedent Procedures:
Teach the client the importance of staying on topic during conversations.
 2. Use role-play to practice staying on topic for 3 conversational turns.
 3. Introduce and practice signal or cue systems to redirect the client back to the topic if they stray.

 
 SD:
 The initiation of a conversation or the introduction of a specific topic within a conversation, either by the client or the conversational partner.
 
 Response:
 The client will stay on the presented topic for at least 3 conversational turns to consider it a successful trial.
 
 Prompting:
 visual prompt,verbal prompt,gestural prompt,modeling
 
 Error Correction:
 1. **Prompting**: If the client strays off-topic, use a verbal or non-verbal cue to guide them back to the original topic. 2. **Modeling**: Demonstrate how to stay on topic during a conversation. 3. **Immediate Feedback**: Provide immediate, specific feedback if the client does not stay on topic for 3 turns. 4. **Reinforcement**: Give positive reinforcement for successfully staying on topic.
 
 Reinforcement:
 differential social reinforcement,socially mediated reinforcement

The Client will ask for time after or during a conversation to tell jokes such as during a lull in the conversation or after important points have been made and the discussion is at a less serious turn with 2 verbal prompts

Target Behavior: Increase Appropriate Humor Requesting & Timing (≤2 Prompts)

Operational Definition

Appropriate humor requesting is defined as the client requesting permission to tell a joke using a taught script (e.g., "Is now a good time for a joke?" / "May I share something funny?"), waiting for a yes, sharing one brief (≤10 sec) appropriate joke, and then returning to the topic—all during a lull (≥3 seconds of natural pause) or after an important point when the discussion turns less serious. The behavior may occur with up to two verbal prompts from the technician (see Prompting below).
Counts as appropriate:
Uses the request script within 5 seconds of noticing a lull/turn to casual tone or when shown a pre-taught "green light" cue.

Waits quietly for permission; shares one joke ≤10 seconds; stops when acknowledged or cued to end.

Content is school/clinic-appropriate (no insults, sensitive topics, or put-downs).

Does not count as appropriate:
Interrupting a speaker or inserting humor while the tone is serious ("red light"), during directions, or while someone else has the floor.

Telling a joke after a "not now," repeating requests after a denial, or exceeding the 10-second limit.

Humor that targets individuals, protected groups, or uses profanity.

Measurement:
Event recording of opportunities (lulls/green-light moments) and responses.
Score Independent (no prompt), Prompted (≤2 verbal prompts), or Inappropriate (interrupt/no request).

Primary metric: % of opportunities with appropriate humor requesting (independent + prompted) and % independent.

Secondary: count of inappropriate humor interjections per session.

Probable Function

Primarily social attention (seeking laughs/connection) and self-expression/engagement; occasionally escape from task difficulty. Verify with brief ABC notes.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine

Visual "Humor Traffic Light":
Green = casual chat/lull → may request.

Yellow = uncertain → use Save-It card or ask, "Is now okay?"

Red = directions/serious topic → Save-It only.

Model → Role-play → Feedback across scenarios (instruction, class discussion, peer chat).

Create a short social script: "Excuse me—Is now a good time for a quick joke?" → wait → share ≤10s → "Thanks."

Prompting (max 2 verbal prompts)

Prompt 1 (nonverbal preferred): point to traffic-light visual or tap Humor Request card.

Prompt 2 (brief verbal): "Use your humor request."

After a prompted share, run a transfer trial later (next green moment) with an expectant pause to build independence.

Reinforcement

DRA: Labeled praise + token/point for requesting at green moments and sticking to ≤10 s.

DRO: Earn a token for each 10-minute block with 0 inappropriate interjections.

Exchange menu includes "Comedy Minute," Joke Jar share, or emcee role after work blocks.

Antecedent Modifications

Schedule humor windows (e.g., end of topic = automatic green).

Use First-Then ("First discussion, then Comedy Minute").

Provide Save-It card (sticky note or phone note) to capture jokes for later.

Prime expectations before high-risk settings ("During Q&A it's yellow—ask first.").

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Permission-Based Humor Share (primary target): During green/yellow moments, the client will request permission, wait for response, and share one appropriate joke (≤10 s), then return to topic, achieving ≥80% of opportunities with ≤2 verbal prompts across 3 sessions.

Save-It Strategy: When the moment is red or uncertain, the client will write the joke on a Save-It card (≤10 s) and continue participating, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Read-the-Room Check: Client silently scans a posted 3-point check (Is it a lull? Tone casual? Respectful content?) and chooses request or Save-It, ≥80% independence.

Micro-Break Request (if urge is strong): Client uses a 1-minute break card instead of blurting; returns and logs the joke on Save-It, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Nonverbal "Not Now" (show red card/hand signal) → brief When-Then: "When we finish this point, then you can ask."

Planned Ignore for blurts, immediately reinforce the pivot to request/Save-It.

One Brief Redirect Only: "Save-It or ask." Avoid lecturing or public corrections.

Reduce Audience Effect if laughter spikes (proximity, quiet prompt); schedule a quick green window soon after to honor appropriate use.

Content Boundary: If humor is inappropriate, end share, acknowledge privately later, reteach the Respect Check; follow site policy.

The Client will demonstrate Basic Respectful Behavior, Like Listening When Others Speak, in 1 Activity Per Session

Target Behavior: Demonstrate Basic Respectful Behavior (Listening When Others Speak) — in ≥1 activity per session

Operational Definition
