"Basic Respectful Behavior—Listening When Others Speak" is defined as the client, during a designated activity (e.g., morning meeting, small-group work, class discussion, therapy circle), doing all of the following while another person has the floor:
Mouth quiet (no blurting, side comments, sound effects).

Body/eyes oriented toward the speaker or shared materials.

Waits turn: does not speak until called on or until a natural pause and an approved signal is used (e.g., hand raise, "Excuse me").

Appropriate volume/tone when it is their turn (inside-voice; respectful wording).

Counts as demonstrated when, for one pre-identified activity per session:
The client meets all four criteria in ≥80% of 30-second whole intervals across the activity and

Requires ≤2 adult prompts total (gestural/visual/verbal combined) during that activity.

Non-examples: talking over others; whispering to peers; unrelated comments; eye/body turned away from the speaker for >10 seconds; making derisive sounds; interrupting without a signal.
Measurement: Whole-interval recording (30s) for the selected activity; tally number of prompts; note any interruptions. One designated activity per session is the "data activity."
Mastery criterion: Demonstrates the behavior for 1 activity per session on 4 of 5 consecutive sessions (then increase to 2+ activities or raise interval criterion to 90%).
Probable Function

Interfering behaviors (e.g., blurting, side talk) are typically maintained by attention (peer/adult response), sometimes escape (from listening tasks), or automatic stimulation. Confirm with ABC data.
Proactive Strategies

Teach & Practice (skill building)

Active Listening Microrules: "Look-Quiet-Wait." Rehearse with brief modeling and role-play.

WAIT Rule: "Why Am I Talking?"—self-check card on desk.

3-Second Pause: Teach waiting at least 3 seconds after a speaker finishes before signaling to speak.

Paraphrase First: When called on, start with "I heard ___ say…" (brief paraphrase) before adding own point.

Antecedent Supports

Prime Expectations: Before the target activity, review the four criteria with examples/non-examples.

Visuals: Desk cue or wristband icons (👀, 🤫, ✋, 🔈).

Roles & Structure: Pre-set speaking order or "talk tokens" to reduce uncertainty about turns.

Seating/Environment: Minimize distractors; offer a quiet fidget if needed.

Predictable Signals: Teacher uses a consistent nonverbal "listening" cue (e.g., hand to ear).

Reinforcement

Specific Praise: "You waited and raised your hand—respectful listening!"

DRA: Reinforce called-on turns and appropriate signals; do not reinforce blurts.

DRO (short intervals): Earn a token/sticker for each 2-3 minutes with zero interruptions; exchange at activity end.

Group Contingency (if appropriate): Team earns a bonus when all members meet listening goals.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Signal to Speak: Client will raise hand or say "Excuse me" before speaking in 80% of opportunities during the target activity, with ≤1 prompt.

Wait Then Speak: Client will wait ≥3 seconds after another finishes before beginning to talk in 80% of observed turns, independent on 3/5 sessions.

Paraphrase-Then-Share: When called on, client will paraphrase one idea from the prior speaker before adding their own comment in 60% of turns (build to 80%).

Brief Break Request (if escape-maintained): Client will request a 1-minute listening break using a card/phrase instead of side talk in 80% of need moments, with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Low-Profile Prompts: Use gesture/visual first (tap cue card, finger to lips), then a 1-3 word prompt ("Wait," "Hand up"), then model if needed—fade quickly after success.

Planned Ignore → Immediate Reinforce: Do not respond to brief blurts; immediately call on the client after they use the correct signal next, and praise specifically.

Reset Option: If chatter persists, offer a brief reset ("30 seconds of quiet listening, then your turn").

If frustration rises: Provide binary choice ("Listen or short break"), maintain calm tone, and reduce language.

Repair & Return: After the activity, practice a quick do-over (model → client demonstrates Listen-Signal-Speak), then praise.

Data & Review: Graph % whole intervals + # prompts per session; problem-solve triggers (topic length, seating, time of day) and adjust supports.

The Client Will Engage in Simple Conversational Exchanges with the Therapist, Like Answering Questions, 3 Times Per Session with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior

Increase engaging in simple conversational exchanges with the therapist 3 times per session with ≤2 verbal prompts.
Operational Definition

A simple conversational exchange is a brief, on-topic, back-and-forth interaction between the client and therapist that includes at least one client response that is understandable (speech, sign, or AAC) and relevant to the therapist's question/comment. Examples:
Therapist: "What did you do at recess?" → Client: "Played soccer with Tim."

Therapist: "Do you like drawing or building today?" → Client: "Drawing."

Therapist: "Tell me one plan for after session." → Client: "Snack, then homework."

Counts as success when all are true:
Occurs 3 times within the session.

Client responds within 10 seconds of the therapist's cue, requiring no more than 2 verbal prompts (e.g., re-ask, scaffolded cue).

Response is on-topic and at least:
Speech/AAC/sign of ≥3 words/symbols, or a clear yes/no/choice when that fully answers the question.

Does not include: unrelated scripting/echolalia, whispering inaudibly with no repair attempt, off-topic responses after two prompts, or written-only replies not read aloud (unless AAC).
Measurement
Primary: Frequency of qualifying exchanges (goal = 3).

Secondary: Prompts used (0/1/2), latency to respond (sec), modality used (speech/AAC/sign).
 Onset/offset: Onset at therapist's question/comment; offset once the client gives a qualifying response or 10s pass without response after the last prompt.

Probable Function

Desired behavior is maintained by social attention/connection and access to information. Interfering behaviors (silence, off-topic talk, refusal) often serve escape/avoidance of social demands, control/autonomy, or reflect skill deficits (language planning, topic generation, anxiety, processing time). (Verify with ABC notes.)
Proactive Strategies

Antecedent supports
Predictable conversation slots (e.g., start, mid, end) on a visual schedule.

Topic menu or visual choice board (school, weekend, favorites, feelings).

Sentence starters: "I did…," "I like…," "My plan is…," "Because…," plus WH-strips.

Allow modality choice (speech/AAC/sign); ensure device is charged and vocab is available.

Prime before session: preview 1-2 likely questions and rehearse quick answers.

Teaching & prompting
Model-lead-test a 3-step script: Listen → Think → Answer (10s); optional + Ask/Comment.

Use least-to-most prompting with a pause after each:
Open question (natural cue).

Scaffolded cue (choice, WH-fill-in): "What did you do—outside or inside?"

Carrier phrase: "Say: 'I played ___.'"

Time delay (wait up to 5-10s) to encourage independent responding.

Reinforcement: labeled praise + access to preferred activity/token for each qualifying exchange; thin gradually (e.g., every 2nd/3rd exchange).

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

If the client is not ready to answer or needs support, teach these skillful alternatives that still meet the function:
Request Help/More Time
"Help please," "More time," or taps ⏱️ icon; therapist repeats or simplifies once.

Request a Choice/Yes-No Format
"Choices?" or "Yes/No?" to reduce response effort.

Pass-Then-Return
"Pass, come back to me," usable once per session; must answer later in the same window.

Topic Swap (Autonomy)
"Different topic: ___?" (from topic menu), then provides an on-topic answer.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Early signs (shrugging, gaze aversion, long pause):
Keep tone neutral; offer A/B choice or yes/no version; point to sentence starter/topic menu.

Reduce language load; allow up to 10s quiet think time; honor Help/More time requests.

If avoidance/refusal persists after 2 prompts:
Provide a minimal success path (closed question): "Did you play outside—yes or no?" → Reinforce, then gently expand next trial ("What game?").

Avoid debates/pressure; if anxiety rises, offer a brief calm break and try a simpler question on return.

If off-topic scripting occurs:
Acknowledge briefly, then redirect with a link and pivot: "You said 'cars'—Which car do you like most?" Reinforce on-topic reply.

The Client will maintain a conversation for 4 conversational turns with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior

Increase maintaining a conversation for 4 conversational turns with ≤2 verbal prompts.
Operational Definition

A conversational turn is one speaker's meaningful, on-topic contribution (spoken, sign, or AAC).
 A qualifying 4-turn exchange is an alternating sequence such as Therapist → Client → Therapist → Client (T-C-T-C) in which:
The client's two turns are on-topic, understandable, and related to the prior turn (answer, comment, or follow-up question).

Each client turn occurs within 10 seconds of the cue or previous speaker's turn.

The entire 4-turn exchange requires no more than 2 verbal prompts total (e.g., re-ask, model a carrier phrase).

Counts as success when: one full 4-turn exchange occurs during the session under the above conditions.
 Does not include: unrelated scripting, perseveration that ignores partner input, whispering inaudibly with no repair, repeating the same response after prompts, or written-only replies not read aloud (unless AAC).
Measurement
Primary: Number of successful 4-turn exchanges per session (goal ≥1, or specify more).

Secondary: Prompts used (0/1/2) per exchange, latency to each client turn (sec), modality (speech/AAC/sign), and quality score per client turn (0-2: 0 off-topic/none, 1 related answer/comment, 2 related answer + follow-up question/additional detail).
 Onset/offset: Onset at therapist's first turn; offset after the client's second qualifying turn (4th turn total) or after 10s of no response following the final allowed prompt.

Probable Function

Desired behavior is maintained by social attention/connection and access to information/interests. Interfering behaviors (silence, off-topic scripting, single-word replies) may serve escape/avoidance of social demands, control/autonomy, or reflect skill deficits (language formulation, topic maintenance, question-asking). (Track with ABC notes.)
Proactive Strategies

Antecedent supports
Post a simple Conversation Map visual:
Start (greeting/topic) → 2) Answer/Comment → 3) Ask/Invite ("What about you?") → 4) Add detail/Close.

Topic choice menu (school, hobbies, weekend, favorites, plans, feelings). Let the client pick first topic.

Sentence starters & WH strips: "I like…," "On the weekend…," "My favorite ___ is…," "What do you think about ___?"

Ensure AAC vocabulary or key phrases are available and pre-programmed; device charged.

Prime likely topics before session; rehearse 1 model exchange.

Teaching & prompting
Model-lead-test the Answer + Ask rule: "Answer the question, then add a question or detail."

Use least-to-most prompting with a pause (5-10s) between levels:
Natural cue (therapist turn).

Prompt 1—scaffolded cue ("Tell me one thing about it." / "Ask me what I like about it.").

Prompt 2—carrier phrase/model ("Say: 'I like Lego. What do you like?'").

Keep turns short and success-oriented (client aim: 3-8 words/symbols or a clear yes/no + one word detail).

Reinforcement: labeled praise and brief access to preferred item/point/token after each successful 4-turn exchange; thin gradually (e.g., every other exchange).

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Teach alternatives that still keep the interaction going when language load is high:
Request Help/Time
"Help please," "More time," or taps ⏱️; therapist repeats/simplifies once.

Choice/Yes-No Bridge
"Choices?" → therapist offers two topic-related choices to answer/ask about.

Topic Swap (Autonomy)
"Different topic: ___?" (chosen from menu), then complete the 4-turn exchange on that topic.

Ask-for-Meaning/Clarify
"What does ___ mean?" or "Can you say that again?"—counts as a related client turn.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Early signs (long pause, shrug, gaze away, scripting):
Neutral tone; point to Conversation Map or sentence starter; offer A/B choices; allow up to 10s think time.

If scripting occurs, link and pivot: "You said 'cars'—What car do you like?" Reinforce on-topic reply.

If engagement stalls after 2 prompts:
Provide a minimal success path: closed question → brief answer → therapist follow-up → client yes/no + one-word detail to finish the 4th turn.

If anxiety rises, allow a brief calm break and retry with an easier/preferred topic.

The client will stay on the topic of the conversation for at least 3 conversational turns with 2 verbal prompts

Target Behavior: Maintain Topic for 3 Conversational Turns (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

Topic maintenance is defined as the client producing three consecutive, on-topic turns in a back-and-forth exchange with a partner, with no more than two verbal prompts from the technician.
A client turn = a relevant comment, answer, or question (≈1-2 sentences or ≤10 seconds) that:
references the current topic (key word, detail, or related wh-question), and

alternates with the partner's turns (Client-Partner-Client-Partner-Client).

Counts as on-topic:
Answering and then asking a related wh-question.

Adding a related detail, opinion, or short story about the same topic.

Using a taught bridge phrase to link subtopics ("Speaking of dogs, have you ever…?").

Does not count:
Introducing a new/irrelevant topic, special-interest monologue, or repeating the same line after a cue.

Off-topic jokes/comments, echoing the partner without adding meaning, or interrupting.

Exchanges during instructional drills (non-conversational) or when the topic was never established.

Prompt allowance: Up to two brief verbal prompts (e.g., "Ask a related question," "Stay on [topic]"). Nonverbal/visual cues do not use up the two prompts.
Measurement:
Define an opportunity when a topic is started (by partner or client) and acknowledged.

Score:
Independent = 3 client turns, 0 verbal prompts.

Prompted = achieved with 1-2 verbal prompts.

Not met = <3 turns or >2 prompts/off-topic.

Primary metric: % of opportunities with 3-turn success (independent + prompted) and % independent per session.

Optional: latency to first on-topic turn; frequency of off-topic shifts.

Probable Function

Primarily social attention/connection and access to preferred topics; off-topic shifts may also function as escape from uncertainty/demand. Verify with brief ABC notes.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine (CQC: Comment → Question → Connect)

Mini-script card:
Comment on the partner's topic.

Ask a related Question.

Connect with a short related fact/feeling.

Role-play & video models across settings (therapy, peer chat, group).

Use topic trackers (sticky note with 1-3 key words) to anchor content.

Visuals & Tools

"On-Topic Filter" cue (What word are we talking about? Use it!).

Bridge phrases list: "Speaking of __…," "That reminds me of __ (related) …," "Also, …."

Parking Lot/Save-It card for off-topic ideas to share later.

Prompting & Fading (max 2 verbals)

Nonverbal/visual: point to topic word/CQC card; thumbs-up for "your turn."

Verbal 1: "Ask a related question."

Verbal 2: "Stay on [topic]—your comment."

After any prompted success, run a transfer trial on the next topic with longer wait time for independence.

Reinforcement

DRA: Labeled praise + token/point for each completed 3-turn chain (bonus if independent).

Start FR1, thin to VR2-3 as stable.

Exchange options: choose next topic, brief preferred chat, game minute, or sticker.

Reinforce use of Parking Lot and bridge phrases at equal or higher value than prompted responding.

Antecedent Modifications

Begin with high-interest or shared-experience topics; gradually introduce neutral topics.

Provide clear start signals: "Let's talk about ___."

Limit background distractions; seat to face partner; use timers for turn pacing.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

CQC Chain (primary target): Given a topic, the client completes Comment → Question → Connect on the same topic, achieving 3 on-topic turns with ≤2 verbal prompts in ≥80% of opportunities across 3 sessions.

Bridge to Related Topic: When wanting to shift, client uses a bridge phrase to link topics appropriately, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Parking Lot Request: If the idea is unrelated, client logs it on the Save-It card and returns to topic, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Clarify-Then-Answer: If unsure, client asks, "Do you mean ___?" before responding, then gives an on-topic turn, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Brief cue, then wait: "On-topic," point to topic word; allow 3-5 s think time.

Two-choice redirect: "Ask a related question or make a related comment."

Planned ignore of off-topic, immediately reinforce the pivot back to topic.

When-Then: "When we finish 3 turns on [topic], then you pick the next topic."

If perseveration escalates, offer Parking Lot or 30-60 s micro-break, then restart with a high-probability question.

The Client will increase the duration in which he engages in conversational exchanges for up to 1 minute, in the absence of maladaptive behaviors

Target Behavior: Increase Conversational Exchanges (up to 1 minute) without maladaptive behavior

Operational Definition

"Conversational exchange" is defined as the client participating in a reciprocal, on-topic back-and-forth with a communication partner using speech or an approved modality (AAC, sign, communication cards).
A client turn counts when the client:
Produces a contextually relevant utterance (speech ≥2 words, or AAC/sign equivalent), and

Directs it to the partner (eye/body orientation, name use, or device output), and

Responds within 5 seconds of the partner's turn or appropriately initiates a new turn.

A conversation bout starts when the client gives/returns a relevant turn and ends when any of the following occur:
Silence or no response for >10 seconds,

Topic loss (off-topic for >10 seconds),

Maladaptive behavior occurs (e.g., yelling/swearing, aggression, property destruction, elopement, self-injury, disruptive stereotypy that prevents participation),

The client appropriately ends the interaction ("All done talking," break request).

Target performance: The client will sustain a conversation for up to 1 minute (60 seconds) while remaining on-topic and without maladaptive behavior. Brief natural pauses (≤10 seconds) are permitted if the exchange resumes.
Measurement: Duration recording in seconds for each prompted/independent bout during designated practice opportunities (e.g., 2-4 trials per session). Also record # of client turns, prompt level, and occurrence of maladaptive behavior (Y/N).
Prompt allowance for data trials: Up to 2 low-intrusive prompts total (gesture/visual/verbal) across the 1-minute bout. Additional prompts end the trial.
Mastery criterion: Client sustains ≥60 seconds of conversational exchange in 3 of 4 trials per session on 4 of 5 consecutive sessions, with ≤2 prompts and 0 maladaptive behaviors.
Probable Function

Competing behaviors (e.g., escape, stereotypy, attention-seeking through disruption) may serve escape/avoidance, automatic, or attention functions. Confirm via ABC data; design supports accordingly.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Skill (explicit instruction + practice)

Conversation Micro-Skills (model → role-play → in vivo):
Open: "Hi ___," "Can we talk about ___?"

Ask: Use a wh- or choice question.

Comment: Add 1-2 relevant details.

Follow-up: Ask a related question.

Close: "Thanks for talking," or appropriate end/break.

Scripts & Supports: Topic ring, "Ask-Comment-Ask" prompt strip, visual conversation map, AAC page with quick phrases ("Tell me more," "What do you think?").

Repair Strategies: Teach "Can you repeat that?", "I don't understand," "Can you show me?"

End/Break Language: "All done talking," "I need a short break." (Prevents maladaptive escape.)

Antecedent Modifications

Prime expectations and the minute goal; preview roles/topics.

Preferred topics/partners first, then generalize.

Clear structure: timer visible; set "your turn/my turn" tokens to cue alternation.

Environment: reduce noise/distractions; provide a quiet fidget if needed.

Shaping ladder: 20s → 30s → 45s → 60s with quick wins at each step.

Reinforcement

Immediate, behavior-specific praise for each correct turn and milestone (e.g., every 15-20s).

Token/points for meeting each time rung; exchange at session end.

Natural reinforcers: access to preferred topic, partner choice, or listener attention after sustained exchanges.

Differential reinforcement: Reinforce appropriate break/end requests; withhold reinforcement following maladaptive escape.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Appropriate Break Request: When conversation feels overwhelming, client will use pre-taught phrase/sign/AAC ("Break please—1 minute") within 5 seconds of early signs, with ≤1 prompt, in 80% of opportunities.

Repair/Clarify Request: Client will request repetition/clarification instead of disengaging or using maladaptive behavior in 80% of breakdowns.

Topic Shift Request: Client will ask to change topic ("Can we talk about ___?") rather than going off-topic, in 70% of relevant moments.

Turn-Taking Signal: Client will use a hand cue or starter phrase ("My turn?") to re-enter the exchange after a pause in 80% of opportunities.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Low-profile prompts first: gesture to visual, point to script, 1-3 word cue ("Ask," "Comment," "Break?").

Planned ignore of minor off-topic (≤5s) if it doesn't derail; quickly reinforce the next on-topic turn.

If signs of escalation (voice rise, fidgeting): offer binary choice—"Keep talking 15s more or take a short break," honor immediately when chosen.

Do not allow escape from the expectation via maladaptive behavior; re-teach and practice a quick do-over (prompt → appropriate phrase → brief reinforcement).

Safety/Crisis: If maladaptive behaviors emerge, follow the site plan; after calm, run a repair trial (15-20s successful exchange) and praise.

Data review each session: graph duration and turns; thin prompts and increase topic difficulty as stable.

The client will identify 3 contrived communication breakdowns and 1 possible solution to each with 2 verbal prompts

Target Behavior

Increase identifying 3 contrived communication breakdowns and naming 1 solution for each with ≤2 verbal prompts per breakdown.
Operational Definition

During structured practice, the therapist presents brief, contrived communication breakdowns (e.g., unclear directions, background noise, interrupted turn, missing information, talking too fast, AAC/volume issue). For each scenario, the client:
Identifies the problem (names or describes what went wrong), and

States one appropriate repair (what to do next: ask to repeat, request clarification, move to quiet spot, slow down, check device/volume, rephrase, confirm understanding).

Counts as success when all are true for each scenario:
Client gives both an identification and a solution within 10 seconds of the cue and with ≤2 verbal prompts.

Three distinct scenarios are completed in the session.

Does not include: "I don't know," off-topic responses, repeating the instruction verbatim without naming the problem/solution, or solutions that don't fit the scenario.
Measurement

Primary: 3/3 successful breakdowns identified + repaired (Yes/No per scenario).

Secondary: Prompts used (0/1/2), latency to respond (sec), accuracy scores:
ID (0-1): 0 = incorrect/none; 1 = correct or functionally correct.

Solution (0-1): 0 = not appropriate; 1 = appropriate.

Onset/offset: Onset at therapist's presentation of the breakdown; offset after the client provides both parts or 30s elapse following the last allowed prompt.

Probable Function

Desired behavior is maintained by effective access to information/attention and reduced frustration. Interfering behaviors (silence, giving up, irritation) often serve escape from communicative demand or control/autonomy, and may reflect skill/processing needs.
Proactive Strategies

Antecedent supports
Teach a simple Repair Script (e.g., N.N.F.C.: Notice → Name → Fix → Check).

Post a Breakdown → Fix visual matrix (examples below).

Pre-teach 4-6 common breakdown types with brief models and video/role-play.

Ensure AAC has quick phrases: "Say that again," "Slower please," "I need more info," "Too loud," "One at a time," "What does ___ mean?"

Start with low-load scenarios; increase difficulty (noise, speed, abstract words) gradually.

Example Breakdown → Fix pairs
Too fast → "Please slow down."

Too quiet/noisy → "I can't hear—can we move/turn it down?"

Don't understand a word → "What does ___ mean?"

Missing info/ambiguous → "Can you tell me where/when/how?"

Interrupted turn → "I wasn't finished—my turn, please."

AAC/volume/page issue → "I need my ___ page / Turn up volume, please."

Teaching & Prompting

Model-Lead-Test each type with brief skits.

Least-to-most prompts (pause 5-10s between levels):
Open cue: "What went wrong? What could you do?"

Scaffold: "Was it too fast or too noisy? What should you say?"

Carrier phrase/model: "Say: 'Please say that slower.'"

Use time-delay to build independence; rotate scenarios to ensure discrimination.

Reinforcement: specific praise + token/point/access for correct ID + repair; deliver richer reinforcement for independent or first-prompt responses. Thin over time.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

When stress or confusion rises, teach and reinforce:
"More time, please."

"Help me figure it out."

"Choices?" (to reduce response load).

"Pass—come back." (Use once per session; still must complete 3 total scenarios.)

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Early signs (shrugging, long pause, frustration):
Keep tone neutral; point to the matrix; offer A/B choices (fast vs. noisy?); allow up to 10s think time.

If stuck after Prompt 2, give a minimal success path: therapist names the problem, client selects a solution from 2 options; reinforce, then retry a similar scenario later for independence.

After attempts:
Reinforce effort and correct fits ("You noticed 'too fast' and picked 'slow down'—perfect match.").

Briefly debrief mismatches ("If it's noisy, asking for slower won't help—try 'quieter place.'").

Asking Questions

The client will Initiate questions during verbal interactions in various settings

Target Behavior: 
 Initiating questions during verbal interactions in various settings.
&nbsp;
Motivating Operation:
 Enhancing curiosity, engagement, and interactive communication skills to foster better social relationships and understanding of topics discussed.
Antecedent Procedures:
 1. Engage in a typical conversational setting, whether simulated or real, involving the client and known adults or peers.
 2. Make it known that there will be opportunities for asking questions.
 3. Create a supportive environment that encourages curiosity and open-ended dialogue.
&nbsp;
Discriminative Stimulus:
 The therapist or conversation partner provides information or makes a statement that could naturally elicit a question, such as "I went to a science museum this weekend" or "The weather has been really strange lately."
Appropriate Response:
 Within 10 seconds of the SD, the client will initiate at least one relevant question related to the topic discussed, like "What did you see at the museum?" or "How has the weather been strange?" to be considered a successful trial.
&nbsp;
Error Correction Procedures:
 1. **Modeling**: If the client does not ask a question, the therapist will model an appropriate question.
 2. **Prompting**: Use a verbal or gestural prompt like, "Is there something you want to ask?" to elicit a question.
 3. **Immediate Feedback**: Provide feedback if the question is off-topic or inappropriate, specifying what should be adjusted.
 4. **Repetition**: Re-introduce the SD to give the client another chance to ask a relevant question.
&nbsp;
Criteria for Mastery: 
 The client will initiate at least one relevant question within 10 seconds of the SD in 8 out of 10 trials across 3 consecutive data collection sessions. Mastery should be demonstrated across different conversational topics and with various partners.
&nbsp;
This ABA goal is designed to encourage the client to become more inquisitive and interactive in social settings by asking questions. This is a crucial skill for effective communication and deeper understanding.
Instructional Notes

MO:
 Enhancing curiosity, engagement, and interactive communication skills to foster better social relationships and understanding of topics discussed.
 
 Antecedent Procedures:
Engage in a typical conversational setting, whether simulated or real, involving the client and known adults or peers.
 2. Make it known that there will be opportunities for asking questions.
 3. Create a supportive environment that encourages curiosity and open-ended dialogue.

 
 SD:
 The therapist or conversation partner provides information or makes a statement that could naturally elicit a question, such as "I went to a science museum this weekend" or "The weather has been really strange lately."
 
 Response:
 Within 10 seconds of the SD, the client will initiate at least one relevant question related to the topic discussed, like "What did you see at the museum?" or "How has the weather been strange?" to be considered a successful trial.
 
 Prompting:
 visual prompt,verbal prompt,gestural prompt,modeling

 differential social reinforcement,socially mediated reinforcement

Answering Questions

The client will use full sentences of at least 5-7 words to respond to questions or contribute to a conversation

Target Behavior:
 The client will use full sentences of at least 5-7 words to respond to questions or contribute to a conversation.
&nbsp;
Motivating Operation:
 To enhance expressive language skills and promote fuller, more meaningful social interactions in various environments such as home, school, and social gatherings.
Antecedent Procedures:
 1. Teach the principles of sentence structure and the elements that make a sentence complete.
 2. Use role-playing to practice responding in full sentences.
 3. Provide opportunities for structured conversations that encourage full sentences.
&nbsp;
Discriminative Stimulus:
 A question or conversational prompt that can be adequately answered or addressed with a full sentence of at least 5-7 words.
Appropriate Response:
 The client will respond to the question or contribute to the conversation using a complete sentence of at least 5-7 words.
&nbsp;
Error Correction Procedures:
 1. **Prompting**: If the client responds with fewer than 5 words, prompt them to expand their answer.
 2. **Modeling**: Demonstrate how to appropriately respond using a full sentence of at least 5-7 words.
 3. **Immediate Feedback**: Provide immediate, specific feedback for short or incomplete responses.
 4. **Reinforcement**: Use positive reinforcement when the client successfully speaks in full sentences of 5-7 words.
&nbsp;
Criteria for Mastery:
 The client will use full sentences of at least 5-7 words to respond to questions or contribute to a conversation in 9 out of 10 opportunities across 3 consecutive data collection sessions.
&nbsp;
This goal aims to enrich the client's expressive language capabilities, thereby improving their ability to engage in more nuanced and meaningful social interactions.
Instructional Notes

MO:
 To enhance expressive language skills and promote fuller, more meaningful social interactions in various environments such as home, school, and social gatherings.
 
 Antecedent Procedures:
Teach the principles of sentence structure and the elements that make a sentence complete.
 2. Use role-playing to practice responding in full sentences.
 3. Provide opportunities for structured conversations that encourage full sentences.

 
 SD:
 A question or conversational prompt that can be adequately answered or addressed with a full sentence of at least 5-7 words.
 
 Response:
 The client will respond to the question or contribute to the conversation using a complete sentence of at least 5-7 words.
 
 Prompting:
 visual prompt, verbal prompt , gestural prompt, modeling
 
 Error Correction:
 1. **Prompting**: If the client responds with fewer than 5 words, prompt them to expand their answer. 2. **Modeling**: Demonstrate how to appropriately respond using a full sentence of at least 5-7 words. 3. **Immediate Feedback**: Provide immediate, specific feedback for short or incomplete responses. 4. **Reinforcement**: Use positive reinforcement when the client successfully speaks in full sentences of 5-7 words.
 
 Reinforcement:
 differential social reinforcement, socially mediated reinforcement

Initiating

The client will initiate an appropriate conversation with a peer 3 times per session

Target Behavior: Increase Peer Conversation Initiations (3 per session)

Operational Definition

A peer conversation initiation is counted when the client, during appropriate social times, orients to a peer (body facing, within ~3-6 ft), uses an appropriate opener (spoken or AAC) such as a greeting, question, comment, compliment, or invitation, and pauses to allow a response (≥3 seconds). One initiation = one opener to one peer.
Counts as an initiation (examples):
"Hey Maya, want to play Uno?"

"Hi! What did you do at recess?"

"I like your drawing, Jacob."

Tapping AAC button: "Hi—Can I join?"

Does not count:
Initiations directed to adults instead of peers.

Occurring during instruction/"red light" moments (e.g., teacher giving directions).

After a direct verbal prompt that supplies the words (e.g., "Say 'Hi, Liam, can I play?'").

Repeating the same script to the same peer within 2 minutes without contextual change.

Off-topic, rude, or perseverative comments that do not invite interaction.

Prompt allowance for data:
 Record level each time: Independent, Gestural/visual prompt, or Verbal prompt (brief cue like "Try a greeting"). Aim to fade to independent; goal requires ≥3 total (independent or prompted) per session with an increasing % independent.
Measurement:
Event recording: tally initiations per session (+ prompt level, peer name/topic).

Optional quality checks: waited ≥3 s for response (Y/N); appropriate timing (Y/N).

Probable Function

Primarily social attention/connection and access to play/activities; initiation may be low due to skill deficits or anxiety. Confirm with ABC notes.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine (G-O-A-R-E)

Greet ("Hi, [name]") → Open with a topic/invite → Ask a related question → React to their answer → End politely ("See you later").

Use visual cue card with 3-5 sample openers tailored to the setting.

Model → Role-play → Practice with peers; incorporate video/self-modeling.

Create Opportunities

Schedule green-light windows (arrival, centers, free choice, transitions to recess).

Arrange peer partners/buddies and proximity seating; provide shared materials/games that invite an opener.

Pre-load topic banks (today's activity, shared interests).

Prompting & Fading

Time delay after a setup: 3-5 s of expectant pause.

Least-to-most: point to visual → gesture toward peer → brief verbal cue ("Try a greeting").

After any prompted success, run a transfer trial later (new opportunity, fewer prompts).

Reinforcement

DRA: Immediate, labeled praise for initiations (bonus for independent).

Token/point toward peer-preferred activities (game leader, choose partner, show-and-tell).

Start FR1, thin to VR2-3 as independence increases.

Reinforce peers for responding kindly ("Thanks for inviting me!").

Environment/Regulation Supports

Teach and allow micro-breaks if anxious; use starter scripts or topic rings.

Provide AAC access and pre-programmed opener buttons if applicable.

Minimize competing adult talk during social windows.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Name + Greeting + Opener (primary): Client faces a peer and delivers a greeting plus a topic/question/invite, then waits ≥3 s. Criterion: ≥3 per session with no more than 1 brief verbal prompt on any single initiation, across 3 consecutive sessions.

Join-Group Script: Client says/AAC "Can I join?" or "Is this spot open?" when approaching an ongoing activity, ≥80% of opportunities with ≤1 prompt.

Compliment-to-Question: Client gives a brief compliment and follows with a related question (e.g., "Cool Lego build—what are you making?"), ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Exit/Retry Strategy: If no response in 5-7 s, client uses "I'll try later" or initiates with a different peer, ≥80% independence.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

If the client hesitates, use nonverbal cue to the opener card; avoid supplying full scripts.

If ignored, coach a graceful retry ("Try Sam or use 'Is now a good time?'"), then reinforce the attempt.

For perseveration/off-topic starts, give a two-choice redirect: "Greeting or question about [shared activity]?"

Keep corrections brief/private; immediately praise the pivot to an appropriate opener.

The client will wait 2 minutes before trying to politely interrupt with 2 verbal prompts

Target Behavior: Wait 2 minutes before politely interrupting (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

When another person has the floor (e.g., teacher/peer speaking, presentation, group instruction), the client will:
Wait quietly for 2 consecutive minutes (120 seconds)—no blurting, side comments, sound effects, or attention-seeking gestures; body/eyes generally oriented to the speaker; hands/objects used quietly.

After the 2 minutes elapse, use a pre-taught polite interrupt (e.g., raised hand + "Excuse me," "May I ask a quick question?") and wait for acknowledgment before speaking.

What starts a trial:
A designated "listening" segment begins and the client indicates a desire to speak (attempts to interject, leans forward, raises hand early, or teacher announces, "This is a 2-minute wait trial").
 What ends a trial:

Client completes 120 seconds of quiet waiting and then uses the polite signal, or

Client interrupts before 120 seconds (trial = not met), or

Speaker explicitly invites open discussion before the interval ends.

Prompts allowed: Up to two brief verbal prompts total during the 2-minute wait and/or at the moment of signaling (e.g., Prompt 1: "Remember—wait," Prompt 2: "Use your signal."). A third verbal prompt ends the trial as not met.
Non-examples: blurting or whispering comments; "polite" phrase before 2 minutes; exaggerated sighing, tapping others, or waving to capture attention; speaking without acknowledgment after signaling.
Measurement: For each opportunity, record: wait duration (sec), # of verbal prompts (0-2), whether any blurt occurred (Y/N), and whether a polite signal after 2 minutes occurred (Y/N).
 Success = 120 seconds of quiet waiting and a polite interrupt, with ≤2 verbal prompts.
Mastery criterion: ≥80% successful opportunities per session across 4 of 5 consecutive sessions (then thin to ≤1 prompt, then independent).
Probable Function

Interrupting is commonly attention-maintained; may also be escape (from listening) or automatic (impulsivity). Confirm via ABC data.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Skill Chain (model → role-play → in vivo)

Chain: Notice urge → Start 2-min wait → Polite signal → Wait for acknowledgment → Speak briefly.

Scripts: "Excuse me," "May I add something?" "I have a quick question."

Role-play timing: practice with a visible 2-minute timer; rehearse both success and reset.

Antecedent Supports

Visual timer (countdown to 0) positioned where client can see it.

"Hold-That-Thought" tools: mini notepad/sticky note card to write or draw the idea while waiting.

Self-monitoring card: two boxes—"Waited 2 min" ✔ / "Used signal" ✔.

Pre-teaching & priming: before high-risk segments, review expectation and signal.

Structured opportunities: plan brief 2-3 trials per session during natural instruction.

Seating & sensory: seat with reduced distractions; offer a quiet fidget that doesn't produce sound.

Reinforcement

DRO-2:00: Deliver reinforcement when the 2-minute interval has zero interruptions.

DRA: Provide immediate attention/response when the client uses the polite signal after 2 minutes; avoid giving attention to blurts.

Behavior-specific praise: "You waited the full two minutes and used 'Excuse me'—that was respectful."

Token system: Earn a token for each successful trial; exchange for a preferred activity at session end.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Note-to-self: When the urge to speak arises, client writes/draws the idea on a card instead of speaking, in 80% of trials, ≤1 prompt.

Polite Signal After Wait: After 120s, client raises hand and says a script in 80% of trials, ≤2 prompts.

Brief Break Request (if escape-maintained): Client uses "Short listening break?" instead of interrupting, ≤1 prompt, 80% of need moments.

Turn Limit Statement: When acknowledged, client keeps the first comment to ≤10 seconds or one sentence (coach with timer/talk token).

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Low-profile cues first: gesture to timer, tap "wait" icon, then a 1-3 word prompt ("Wait," "Signal").

Planned ignore → immediate reinforce: Do not respond to quick blurts (when safe); rapidly reinforce the next correct wait or polite signal.

Reset neutrally: "Let's restart the 2-minute wait," start timer without lecture.

Offer a binary choice if agitation rises: "Write it down or short break," then return to the trial.

Consistent contingencies: Do not grant the attention/content requested following an early interruption; provide attention after the successful 2-minute wait + polite signal.

Post-event repair: Quick do-over (15-30s practice of wait → signal), praise, and rejoin activity.

The client will greet the other person when appropriate with one verbal prompt

Target Behavior

Increase greeting the other person when appropriate with ≤1 verbal prompt.
Operational Definition

A greeting is a brief, directed social opener (speech, sign, or AAC) such as "Hi," "Hello," "Good morning," optionally with the person's name (e.g., "Hi, Ms. Lee").
 A greeting counts when the client:
Delivers it within 5 seconds of an appropriate opportunity,

Aims it toward the person (eye gaze/turns body or uses their name), and

Requires no more than 1 verbal prompt (e.g., "Say hi to Ms. Lee").

Appropriate opportunities include:
First contact with a person in a setting (arrival to room, joining group, passing within ~6 ft with eye contact).

When addressed by name or someone says "Hi/Hello" to the client.

Joining or leaving an interaction ("coming to circle," "leaving session").

After receiving help/gift/feedback as a social opener to the interaction.

Does not include: greetings after the window (>5s), muttered/inaudible greetings with no repair, greetings to self, off-topic phrases, or waving only (unless that modality is pre-taught/accepted—see notes).
Measurement
Primary: % of greeting opportunities with a qualifying greeting (≤1 prompt, within 5s).

Secondary: Prompt level (0/1), latency to greet (sec), modality (speech/AAC/sign).
 Onset/offset: Onset at the moment an opportunity occurs; offset at greeting production or after 5s post-prompt.

Probable Function

Desired greeting behavior is maintained by social attention/connection. Missed greetings/avoidance may serve escape from social demand, control/autonomy, or reflect skill/anxiety/processing needs. (Track with ABC notes.)
Proactive Strategies

Antecedent supports
Post a simple S³ Greeting Routine: See → Smile → Say "Hi, [Name]".

Visual prompts at key locations (doorway, meeting area): "Greet & Go."

Prime expected greeting targets before transitions (who might you see?).

Name bank or photo ring for practice; ensure AAC has quick buttons ("Hi," "Hello," names).

Define greeting roles (door greeter, line leader) to create predictable opportunities.

Teaching & prompting
Model-Lead-Test the S³ routine with quick role-plays.

Least-to-most prompting with 1 verbal prompt max: gesture/point → verbal ("Say hi to Ms. Lee").

Provide 5-10s wait time for independent responding before prompting.

Reinforcement: specific, immediate praise ("Nice greeting with her name!") + token/point/access early (FR1), then thin.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

If verbal speech is difficult in the moment, allow pre-taught alternatives that still meet the social function:
AAC/Voice output: "Hi, [Name]."

Sign/gesture + voice: wave paired with a clear "Hi" on AAC or whispered, then repaired to audible if prompted.

Request more time/help: "One sec," "Help me say hi," then complete within the window if possible (or at next micro-opportunity).

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Early signs (hesitation, gaze down, freezing):
Neutral cue to S³ card; reduce audience; offer name scaffold ("This is Mr. Ortiz—ready?").

If no response, use the single allowed verbal prompt, then move on without pressure; practice a quick do-over later in a low-demand spot.

If client refuses or shows anxiety:
Offer modality choice (AAC/sign/shorter "Hi"), or a pass-then-return (one per session); reinforce when completed.

Using Proper Tone and Volume

The client will use the proper tone and volume for different situations with 2 verbal prompts

Target Behavior: 
 Using an average voice volume during verbal communication in various social settings.
Motivating Operation:
 Increase in social engagement and effective communication to enhance social interactions and comprehension by others.
&nbsp;Antecedent Procedures:
 1. Set up a controlled environment, like a therapy room, with minimal background noise.
 2. Establish a conversational context, which could be a role-play or an actual interaction with known peers or adults.
&nbsp;
Discriminative Stimulus:
 A question, statement, or prompt requiring verbal response is presented to the client, such as "Tell me about your day," or "What is your opinion on this?"
Response:
 The client will respond using an average voice volume, as measured by the decibel meter or judged by the clinician within 5 seconds of the SD. The response must be clear and at a volume consistent with an average speaker to be considered a successful trial.
Error Correction Procedures:
 1. Modeling: If the client responds at a low volume, the therapist will model the appropriate voice volume.
 2. Prompting: Use a gestural or verbal prompt to encourage louder speech, such as saying "A bit louder, please."
 3. Immediate Feedback: Provide constructive feedback if the voice volume is too low, specifying what needs to be adjusted.
 4. Repetition: Offer another chance to respond to the same or a different SD, giving the client another opportunity to exhibit the correct behavior.
Criteria for Mastery: 
 The client will respond with an average voice volume within 5 seconds in 8 out of 10 trials across 3 consecutive data collection sessions. The mastery should be generalized across various settings and conversational partners.
This ABA goal should provide a well-rounded strategy to help the client improve their voice volume to an average level, making it easier for them to communicate effectively in different social situations.
Instructional Notes

MO:
 Increase in social engagement and effective communication to enhance social interactions and comprehension by others.
 
 Antecedent Procedures:
Set up a controlled environment, like a therapy room, with minimal background noise.
 2. Establish a conversational context, which could be a role-play or an actual interaction with known peers or adults.
 3. Utilize a decibel meter or equivalent tool to measure voice volume objectively.

 
 SD:
 A question, statement, or prompt requiring verbal response is presented to the client, such as "Tell me about your day," or "What is your opinion on this?"
 
 Response:
 The client will respond using an average voice volume, as measured by the decibel meter or judged by the clinician within 5 seconds of the SD. The response must be clear and at a volume consistent with an average speaker to be considered a successful trial.
 
 Prompting:
 visual prompt,verbal prompt,gestural prompt,modeling
 
 Error Correction:
 1. Modeling: If the client responds at a low volume, the therapist will model the appropriate voice volume. 2. Prompting: Use a gestural or verbal prompt to encourage louder speech, such as saying "A bit louder, please." 3. Immediate Feedback: Provide constructive feedback if the voice volume is too low, specifying what needs to be adjusted. 4. Repetition: Offer another chance to respond to the same or a different SD, giving the client another opportunity to exhibit the correct behavior.
 
 Reinforcement:
 differential social reinforcement,socially mediated reinforcement

The Client will use a calm tone of voice to discuss when he thinks he was treated unfairly

Target Behavior: Calm, Assertive Self-Advocacy About "Unfairness"

Operational Definition

Calm self-advocacy is defined as the client expressing a concern about perceived unfair treatment using a calm voice and respectful, solution-focused language. A response counts when, within 60 seconds of the trigger (or after a taught micro-break), the client:
Speaks at voice level 2-3 on a 0-5 classroom scale (inside voice; no yelling/whispering).

Uses an I-statement/request script (e.g., "I feel __ when __. I need/can we __?").

Allows the other person to speak (no overlapping talk >2 seconds), and avoids insults, threats, profanity, or sarcasm.

Keeps each speaking turn ≤15 seconds unless asked to elaborate.

Does not count as calm self-advocacy:
Raising voice above level 3, interrupting repeatedly, or refusing to listen.

Complaining/arguing without a request or proposed solution.

Content that is disrespectful (name-calling, swearing) or unrelated to the issue.

Situations where the rule/expectation was unclear or safety/medical concerns are present (score "N/A").

Prompt allowance: Up to two brief verbal prompts (e.g., "Use your I-statement," "Inside voice"). Nonverbal/visual cues do not use up the two prompts.
Measurement:
Event recording of "calm advocacy episodes" per opportunity (whenever the client indicates something felt unfair). Score Independent, Prompted (≤2), or Not met.

Optional ratings each episode: Voice level (0-5), Respect (Y/N), Included a request (Y/N).

Probable Function

Primary: Autonomy/control and fairness/justice seeking; may co-occur with attention and escape from aversive interactions. Verify with brief ABC notes.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine (P-B-S-A: Pause → Breathe → State → Ask)

Pause (3-5 s) and Breathe (box breath x1).

State with the script: "I feel __ when __."

Ask: "Could we __?" "What can I do to fix it?"

Practice with modeling, role-play, and video/self-modeling using past "unfair" scenarios.

Visuals & Tools

Voice Meter (0-5) posted; "calm" = 2-3.

I-Statement card and Request Menu (clarify, redo, more time, different order, apology/repair, explanation).

Micro-break/Appeal card: "I need 1 minute, then can we talk?"

Antecedent Supports

Adults give a brief rationale with limits ("so everyone gets a turn").

Offer predictable appeal windows ("After this direction, we can discuss for 1 minute").

Provide choices (order/role/method) to reduce control struggles.

Pre-correct: "If something feels unfair, use your I-statement or Appeal card."

Reinforcement

DRA: Immediate, labeled praise + token/point for calm tone + request.

Bonus for independent use (no verbal prompts).

Exchange menu: brief problem-solving conference, choice time, role privileges.

Reinforce adults/peers for listening respectfully (models the skill).

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Calm I-Statement + Request (primary target): Client uses the script at voice level 2-3 and proposes a solution within 60 s of the trigger (or after a ≤2-min micro-break), achieving this in ≥80% of opportunities with ≤2 verbal prompts across 3 sessions.

Appeal Card/Micro-Break: Client presents card to take a 1-2 minute regulation break instead of arguing, then returns and uses the I-statement, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Clarify-First: If unsure, client asks, "Can you explain the rule?" before stating the concern, then makes a request, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Repair Option: If the client contributed to the issue, they propose a repair ("I can redo the step now"), ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Validate-then-Guide (≤7 words): "I hear you—use your I-statement."

When-Then: "When your voice is level 2-3, then we'll problem-solve."

One prompt at a time, then wait 5-10 s. Avoid lectures/power struggles.

Offer the Appeal/Micro-break if voice rises; set a timer and reconvene.

Planned ignore for disrespectful tone/content; immediately reinforce the pivot to calm voice.

If escalation continues: Reduce audience, increase space, switch to written exchange if helpful; follow site safety plan. Debrief privately using the P-B-S-A steps.

Manners

The Client Will Use Polite Phrases or Gestures, Like Saying "Thank You" or "Excuse Me," in Appropriate Situations Once Per Session

Target Behavior

Increase using polite phrases or gestures (e.g., "Thank you," "Excuse me," "Please") in appropriate situations once per session with ≤1 verbal prompt.
Operational Definition

A qualifying polite response is a brief, directed social behavior—spoken, signed, or AAC—or a pre-taught gesture paired with attention to the person (eye gaze/turning body or name). The client produces it within 5-7 seconds of an appropriate opportunity.
Examples of polite phrases/gestures
"Thank you," "Thanks," nod + smile; "You're welcome."

"Excuse me" to pass by/interrupt gently; light attention tap (if pre-taught/approved).

"Please" when making a request; "Pardon?" to ask for a repeat.

Wave/hand over heart "thanks" (if taught) or AAC button equivalents.

Appropriate opportunities include
Receiving help, an item, feedback, or a compliment (thank you/you're welcome).

Needing to pass through a space or get someone's attention (excuse me).

Making a request (please).

Accidentally bumping/interrupting (sorry/excuse me).

Does not include
Responses after the time window (>7s) without a repair, inaudible speech with no attempt to repair, gestures not previously taught to count, or phrases not directed to a person.

Measurement
Primary: Session success (Yes/No) = at least 1 qualifying polite response with ≤1 prompt.

Secondary: Prompt level (0/1), latency (s), modality (speech/AAC/sign/gesture), and context (help, pass, request, interrupt).
 Onset/offset: Onset at the moment the opportunity occurs; offset when the polite response is produced or 7s elapse after the single allowed prompt.

Probable Function

Desired behavior is maintained by social attention/affiliation and smoother access/coordination (e.g., passing, requesting). Missed responses may reflect skill/awareness gaps, processing time/anxiety, or escape from social demands. (Log ABC notes to verify.)
Proactive Strategies

Antecedent supports
Post a simple S²P Routine: See the situation → Say/Signal the polite phrase (e.g., "Thanks," "Excuse me").

Prime likely moments on the visual schedule (doorway, materials hand-off, line-up).

Ensure AAC quick buttons: Thank you • Excuse me • Please • Sorry • You're welcome.

Create contrived opportunities early in session (hand over a pencil, block a doorway briefly).

Keep opportunities predictable at first; thin unpredictability as skills grow.

Teaching & prompting
Model-Lead-Test each phrase in short role-plays (2-3 trials each).

Least-to-most prompting (max 1 verbal):
Pause/gesture toward the partner or visual cue.

Single verbal prompt: "Say thank you," or "Use excuse me."

Allow 5-7s wait time before prompting to encourage independence.

Reinforcement: immediate, labeled praise ("Nice quick 'excuse me'!") + token/point/access early (FR1), then thin.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Pre-teach acceptable alternatives that still meet the social goal:
AAC button ("Thank you," "Excuse me").

Gesture + brief word (nod + "thanks," hand raise + "excuse me").

Repair option if the moment passed: "I forgot to say thanks—thank you for helping me."

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Early signs (freeze, whisper, looking away):
Point to S²P card or AAC; reduce audience; offer A/B choice: "Thank you" or "Thanks"?

If no response after the single verbal prompt, move on without pressure; create a quick do-over later in a low-demand spot.

If anxiety/refusal appears:
Offer modality choice (AAC/sign/shorter word) or a pass-then-return (one per session); reinforce when completed.

The Client Will Accept a Differing Opinion from an Adult in a Constructive Manner Without Arguing with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior: Accept Differing Opinions/Decisions Constructively (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

Constructive acceptance is defined as the client responding to an adult's differing opinion, feedback, or decision by listening without interruption, giving a brief acknowledgment, and moving on appropriately (following the direction/transitioning or agreeing to disagree) within 10 seconds, with no arguing. Up to two brief verbal prompts may be used.
Counts as constructive acceptance (any of the following, with calm tone):
Acknowledge + comply/transition: "Okay," "Got it," then follows the direction.

Acknowledge + agree-to-disagree: "I see your point. I still feel different, but okay."

Acknowledge + one respectful request/question (max one try): "Could I try it after math?" If the adult says no, the client ends the discussion and moves on.

Does not count:
Arguing: debating after one respectful request, trying to win the point, repeated "but…" statements.

Interrupting, eye-rolling, sarcasm, sighing/groaning paired with refusal.

Escalation (raised voice, insults) or leaving without permission.

Situations with unclear directions/safety/medical issues (score N/A).

Measurement
Define an opportunity whenever an adult expresses a differing opinion/decision/limit.

Score each opportunity as Independent, Prompted (≤2), or Not Met.

Primary metric: % of opportunities with constructive acceptance (independent + prompted).

Secondary: % independent, latency to acceptance (goal ≤10 s).

Probable Function (of arguing)

Commonly autonomy/control, justice/fairness-seeking, and/or attention; sometimes escape from demands. Verify with brief ABC notes.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine (A-R-A-A)

Acknowledge the adult's view: "Okay/I hear you."

Regulate (1 breath or 10-second pause if needed).

Ask once (optional, respectful request/clarify).

Accept/Act: comply, transition, or agree-to-disagree and continue.

Use a visual card with 4 steps and 2-3 model phrases.

Model → Role-play → Rehearse with common triggers; include video/self-modeling.

Antecedent Supports

Adults give clear, brief rationale for limits when feasible.

Provide bounded choices (order/method/when) to preserve autonomy.

Preview hot spots ("If you disagree, use your A-R-A-A card").

Prompting & Fading (max 2 verbals)

Nonverbal point to the card/gesture to pause (doesn't use a prompt).

Verbal 1: "Acknowledge."

Verbal 2: "Ask once or accept—then act."

After any prompted success, run a transfer trial later (longer wait for independence).

Reinforcement

DRA: Immediate, labeled praise + token/point for constructive acceptance (bonus for independent).

DRO: Earn a token for each 10-minute block with 0 arguing.

Exchange for small, quick privileges (choice time, job role, first pick).

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Acknowledge-and-Act (primary): Client says "Okay/I hear you," then complies or transitions within 10 s, ≥80% of opportunities with ≤2 verbal prompts across 3 sessions.

Ask-Once Option: Client uses one respectful request/clarifying question, accepts the answer, then acts, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Agree-to-Disagree Script: "I see it differently, but I'll do it," then acts, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Appeal/Micro-Break Card: If dysregulated, client requests 1-2 min break and returns to Acknowledge-and-Act, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Validate → Guide (≤7 words): "I hear you—Acknowledge, then act."

When-Then: "When you accept, then we can revisit later."

One-and-done prompting: Provide at most the two brief prompts; avoid debate/lectures.

Planned ignore of attempts to argue; immediately reinforce the pivot to acceptance.

If escalation rises: offer micro-break/appeal card; reduce audience; follow site safety plan.

Brief private debrief later: identify trigger, rehearse A-R-A-A, choose a phrase for next time.

The Client Will Accept a Differing Opinion from a Peer in a Constructive Manner Without Arguing with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior: Accept a Differing Opinion Constructively—without arguing (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

When a peer expresses an opinion that differs from the client's, the client will respond in a constructive manner by completing all of the following within the interaction:
Pause at least 3 seconds (no interrupting/over-talking).

Acknowledge the peer (e.g., "I hear you," "Thanks for sharing," paraphrase 1 key point).

Respond respectfully with either:
a neutral/agreeing statement or

a respectful disagreement using an I-statement (e.g., "I see it differently because ___").

Allow the conversation to move on (asks one related question or says a neutral close like "Let's agree to disagree").

Calm body/voice (inside voice, neutral/appropriate tone; no eye-rolling, mocking, or hostile gestures).

No arguing behaviors for ≥30 seconds after the exchange or until the teacher moves on—whichever comes first.

Prompts allowed: Up to two brief verbal prompts total during the exchange (e.g., Prompt 1: "Acknowledge." Prompt 2: "Use your I-statement."). Additional prompts end the opportunity as not met. Gestural/visual cues may be used first when available.
Counts as one opportunity when a clear differing opinion is expressed during instruction, discussion, or play (planned 2-5 times per session).
 Success = all criteria above met with ≤2 verbal prompts.
Non-examples / arguing behaviors: speaking over the peer; repeating the same counter-point after being acknowledged; raising voice; sarcasm/insults; eye-rolling or scoffing directed at the peer; trying to recruit others against the peer; refusing to let the topic end.
Measurement: Event recording per opportunity—mark Success/No, # verbal prompts (0-2), latency to acknowledge (sec), and note triggers/topic.
Mastery criterion: ≥80% successful opportunities per session across 4 of 5 consecutive sessions (then fade to ≤1 prompt, then independent).
Probable Function

Arguing often produces attention (peer/adult engagement), control/influence over the conversation, or escape from cognitive dissonance/uncomfortable topics; sometimes automatic (impulse/rigidity). Confirm with ABC data.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Skill (model → role-play → in vivo)

PACE script (post at desk or AAC page): Pause → Acknowledge → Comment/I-statement → End/Agree to disagree.

Sentence stems:
Acknowledge: "I hear your point about ___." / "You think ___."

I-statement: "I see it differently because ___." / "My experience is ___."

Close/Shift: "Let's agree to disagree." / "Can we change topics?"

Perspective-taking mini-practice: paraphrase the peer's point in 1 short sentence before sharing your own.

Repair phrases: "Can you explain more?" "I'm getting frustrated—can I take a quick break?"

Antecedent Supports

Prime before known debate topics; preview the PACE steps.

Visuals: pocket card with the 4 steps; emoji cue for tone/volume.

Choice & structure: allow choice of speaking order; set time limits for turns.

Noncontingent attention: quick, scheduled check-ins to reduce attention-seeking through debate.

Flexibility warm-ups: quick activities that practice "two answers can be okay."

Reinforcement

DRA: Immediately praise and, when appropriate, reinforce with preferred social access when PACE is used.

Behavior-specific praise: "You paused and paraphrased before sharing your view—respectful!"

Token system: Earn a token for each successful opportunity; exchange at session end.

Group contingency (optional): Team earns a bonus when everyone uses respectful disagreement once.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Acknowledge + I-statement: Upon hearing a differing opinion, client uses an acknowledge stem then an I-statement within 10s, with ≤2 prompts, in 80% of opportunities.

Ask-then-Close: Client asks one clarifying question, then uses a close ("Thanks for explaining") without re-arguing in 80% of opportunities.

Agree-to-Disagree: Client states "We can agree to disagree" and lets the topic end in 80% of opportunities.

Brief Break Request (if escalation risk): Client uses "Short break please" and returns within 2 minutes, ≤1 prompt, in 80% of need moments.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Low-profile prompting: gesture to PACE card → Prompt 1 ("Acknowledge") → wait up to 5-10s → Prompt 2 ("Use your I-statement").

Do not debate or provide extra attention to arguing; reinforce immediately once the client uses any PACE step.

Limit-set neutrally: "We're ending this topic now—choose: agree to disagree or brief break."

Reset if arguing starts: "Let's redo—pause, then acknowledge," then practice a 10-20s repair exchange and praise.

Safety plan if behavior escalates; after calm, run a short successful PACE trial to close the loop.

The Client Will Express a Differing Opinion in a Constructive Manner Without Arguing with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior

Increase expressing a differing opinion in a constructive manner with ≤2 verbal prompts and zero arguing.
Operational Definition

A qualifying response occurs when, after hearing another person's statement or request, the client states a respectful disagreement using an approved script (speech, sign, or AAC) within 10 seconds, stays calm and on-topic, and does not argue (no insults, no repeated back-and-forth after the response). A constructive response includes both:
A polite frame (e.g., "I see it differently…," "I disagree," "Another idea is…"), and

One reason or alternative/compromise (e.g., "…because ___," "…could we try ___?").

Counts as success when:
Produced in an appropriate context (someone shared an opinion/plan that differs from the client's).

Occurs with ≤2 verbal prompts (e.g., "Use your disagree script," "Give one reason.").

No arguing behaviors occur (e.g., raised voice, personal attacks, repeated contradictions after a limit, interrupting).

Optional closing is allowed: "What do you think?" / "Can we agree to disagree?"

Does not include: insults/sarcasm, repeating the same disagreement more than once after a limit, off-topic complaints, loud/hostile tone, or walking away without communicating (unless using the pre-taught "I need a break").
Measurement
Primary: % of disagreement opportunities with a constructive response (goal ≥1 per session initially).

Secondary: prompts used (0/1/2), latency to respond (sec), quality score (0-3):
0 = no/hostile/arguing; 1 = polite phrase only; 2 = polite + one reason/alternative; 3 = polite + reason and invite/compromise.

Onset/offset: Onset at partner's opinion/request; offset after client's qualifying response or after 10s without response following the last allowed prompt.

Probable Function

Desired behavior is maintained by attention/recognition, autonomy/control (having a voice), and better access to compromise. Arguing/hostile responses may function for attention, escape (from non-preferred plans), or control when skills to disagree respectfully are weak. (Confirm with ABC notes.)
Proactive Strategies

Antecedent supports
Post a Respectful Disagreement Script card:
Start: "I see it differently." / "I disagree."

Reason/Alternative: "because ___" / "Could we ___ instead?"

Close: "What do you think?" / "I can do ___ as a compromise."

Pre-correct before likely triggers: "If you disagree, use your 2-step: Say it + One reason."

Provide choice architecture (A/B options, compromise menu) to lower win-lose pressure.

Teach group norms: one person speaks at a time; 'one-and-done' rule (one disagreement + one reason).

Ensure AAC has quick buttons: I disagree • Another idea • Because • Could we ___ • Agree to disagree • I need a break.

Teaching & prompting
Model-Lead-Test with short role-plays (easy topics first).

Least-to-most prompting (pause 5-10s between levels):
Gesture to script card / "Use your disagree script."

Prompt 1: "Start with 'I see it differently' and give one reason."

Prompt 2: Provide carrier phrase: "Say, 'I see it differently because ___.'"

Reinforce brief, calm statements; cap turns using a timer or token to prevent drift into argument.

Reinforcement
DRA: specific praise + token/point/access for each constructive disagreement.

Bonus for independent (0-1 prompt) or level-3 quality responses.

Thin reinforcement as fluency increases.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Request a compromise: "Can we meet in the middle with ___?"

Agree to disagree: "We see it differently—let's agree to disagree."

Ask for time/space: "I need a minute," then return and use the script.

Clarify first: "Can you explain more?" (if unsure what to disagree with).

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Early signs (voice rising, interrupting, repeating point):
Calm, brief cue: "One-and-done—use your script." Point to card, lower voice, increase space.

Offer A/B compromise choice or time-limited turn ("10 seconds to state your reason").

If arguing begins:
Neutral reset: "Pause. Try the script once, then we move on."

Function-based follow-through:
Attention-maintained: limit debate; provide rich attention for the scripted, brief statement.

Escape/control: acknowledge autonomy ("You shared your view") and provide bounded choice/compromise after scripted response.

If escalation continues, prompt break request, then re-engage with the one-and-done rule.

Repair
After calm, brief reflection: "What worked? Next time say: 'I disagree because ___.'" Reinforce repair use next opportunity.

The Client Will Show Respectful Behavior for 3 rounds of conversation, Like Listening or Nodding, When Interacting with an Adult with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior: Show Respectful Behavior for 3 Conversation Rounds (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

Respectful behavior in conversation is defined as the client demonstrating active-listening behaviors while interacting with an adult across three consecutive conversation rounds (Adult → Client → Adult → Client → Adult → Client).
 A round is counted when the client:
During the adult's turn:
Faces the speaker (listening body),

Keeps voice off and hands/feet quiet,

Uses one backchannel (nod, "okay," "mm-hmm") or waits without interrupting.

During the client's turn:
Responds with a brief, respectful turn (≤10 seconds)—a relevant comment, answer, or clarifying question—in a calm tone.

The chain is successful when the client meets the above for three consecutive rounds within a single interaction, with no more than two brief verbal prompts (e.g., "listening body," "your turn").
Includes (examples):
Nodding while the adult speaks; waiting; then answering the question briefly.

Saying "I see" / "okay" and asking one related question.

Calm body/voice, facing the adult, short relevant responses.

Does not include:
Interrupting, talking over the adult, side comments/jokes, eye-rolling/sighing.

Off-topic monologues >10 seconds, argumentative tone, turning away for >3 seconds.

Situations with unclear expectations or safety/medical issues (score N/A).

Measurement:
Each identified conversation counts as an opportunity. Score Independent (0 prompts), Prompted (1-2 verbal prompts), or Not Met (>2 prompts or chain breaks).

Primary metric: % of opportunities with a 3-round respectful chain (independent + prompted).

Secondary: % independent; # of interruptions.

Probable Function

Desired outcome is social connection and competence. Breaks in respectful behavior may function for attention, escape from uncertainty/demand, or autonomy/control. Verify with brief ABC notes.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine — "L-A-R-A"

Listen (body facing, quiet mouth/hands, nod/"mm-hmm").

Acknowledge (one backchannel or "I see").

Respond (≤10 s: answer/comment/clarify).

Ask (optional 1 related question).
 Use modeling, role-play, and quick feedback in short practice chats.

Visuals & Tools

"Listening Body" card (Eyes-Body-Hands-Voice).

Backchannel list: "okay," "got it," "I see," nod.

Turn timer or finger cue to keep client turns brief.

Antecedent Modifications

Signal start: "Let's talk for three rounds."

Begin with predictable topics (schedule, choices, plan), then broaden.

Reduce distractions; seat face-to-face; pre-teach expected length.

Prompting & Fading (max 2 verbals)

Nonverbal cue to card or gentle hand signal (does not use a prompt).

Verbal 1: "Listening body."

Verbal 2: "Your turn—keep it brief."
 After a prompted success, run a transfer trial later with longer wait for independence.

Reinforcement

DRA: Labeled praise + token/point after each completed 3-round chain (bonus for independent).

Start FR1, thin to VR2-3 as consistent.

Menu ideas: choose next topic, helper role, short preferred activity.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

3-Round Respect Chain (primary target): Client completes three consecutive rounds meeting listening + brief-response criteria with ≤2 verbal prompts in ≥80% of opportunities across 3 sessions.

Backchannel Instead of Interrupting: When wanting to speak, client uses a backchannel (nod/"okay") and waits to be cued, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Clarify-Then-Respond: If unsure, client asks a short clarifying question before giving a brief response, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Pause Card/Micro-Break: If dysregulated, client shows a 30-60 s pause card and then resumes the 3-round routine, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Brief cue, then wait (3-5 s): "Listening body."

Two-choice redirect: "Backchannel or wait your turn."

Planned ignore for minor blurts; immediately reinforce the pivot to listening.

When-Then: "When we finish three rounds, then you pick the topic."

If tone escalates, offer pause card, reduce audience, and restart with a simpler topic.

Responding

The client will respond within 3 seconds when spoken to during verbal interactions in various settings

Target Behavior: 
 Responding within 10 seconds when spoken to during verbal interactions in various settings.
&nbsp;
Motivating Operation:
 Enhancing engagement, social reciprocity, and timely communication to foster better social interactions and relationships.
Antecedent Procedures:
 1. Create a controlled environment that is conducive to verbal interactions, such as a quiet therapy room or a familiar social setting.
 2. Establish a conversational context involving the client and familiar adults or peers.
 3. Notify the client that they will be evaluated on their responsiveness during the interaction.
&nbsp;
Discriminative Stimulus:
 The therapist or conversation partner directs a question, statement, or command towards the client that naturally necessitates a response.
Appropriate Response:
 The client will respond to the SD within 10 seconds. If the client does not respond initially, one additional prompt will be provided. A coherent and contextually appropriate response within the timeframe is considered a successful trial.
&nbsp;
&nbsp;Error Correction Procedures:
 1. **Prompting**: If the client fails to respond within 10 seconds, a single verbal or gestural prompt like "Did you hear me?" is used.
 2. **Immediate Feedback**: Offer feedback if the client does not respond within the time frame, even after the prompt, specifying the need for a quicker response.
 3. **Repetition**: Re-introduce the SD or present a new one, giving the client another chance to respond within the allocated time.
&nbsp;
Criteria for Mastery: 
 The client will respond within 10 seconds to the SD in 8 out of 10 trials across 3 consecutive data collection sessions, with the allowance of one additional prompt. Mastery should be generalized across various settings and conversational partners.
&nbsp;
This ABA goal aims to improve the client's responsiveness in verbal interactions, a critical skill for effective communication and social engagement.
Instructional Notes

MO:
 Enhancing engagement, social reciprocity, and timely communication to foster better social interactions and relationships.
 
 Antecedent Procedures:
Create a controlled environment that is conducive to verbal interactions, such as a quiet therapy room or a familiar social setting.
 2. Establish a conversational context involving the client and familiar adults or peers.
 3. Notify the client that they will be evaluated on their responsiveness during the interaction.

 
 SD:
 The therapist or conversation partner directs a question, statement, or command towards the client that naturally necessitates a response.
 
 Response:
 The client will respond to the SD within 10 seconds. If the client does not respond initially, one additional prompt will be provided. A coherent and contextually appropriate response within the timeframe is considered a successful trial.
 
 Prompting:
 visual prompt,verbal prompt,gestural prompt,modeling
 
 Error Correction:
 1. **Prompting**: If the client fails to respond within 10 seconds, a single verbal or gestural prompt like "Did you hear me?" is used. 2. **Immediate Feedback**: Offer feedback if the client does not respond within the time frame, even after the prompt, specifying the need for a quicker response. 3. **Repetition**: Re-introduce the SD or present a new one, giving the client another chance to respond within the allocated time.
 
 Reinforcement:
 differential social reinforcement,socially mediated reinforcement

The Client will accept a 30-second latency from when he requests an item until he receives the item without engaging in maladaptive behaviors with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior: Accept a 30-second delay to access an item—without maladaptive behavior (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

Following an appropriate request for a preferred item/activity (e.g., "Can I have the iPad?" / AAC "iPad"), the client will wait 30 consecutive seconds before receiving it while showing calm, safe behavior.
Acceptable waiting behaviors: quiet voice (or silence), hands/feet/body calm, staying in the designated area, looking at a visual/timer or using a pre-taught fidget, and (optionally) asking once: "How much longer?"
Maladaptive behaviors (any = not met): yelling, profanity, arguing/bargaining repeatedly, crying >10s, aggression, property destruction, elopement, self-injury, disruptive stereotypy that prevents waiting.
Prompts allowed: up to two brief verbal prompts total across the 30 s (e.g., Prompt 1: "Waiting," Prompt 2: "Use your waiting plan."). Gestural/visual cues may occur without counting toward the two if they're nonverbal.
