What starts a trial: client makes an appropriate request and staff says a delay cue (e.g., "Yes—wait 30 seconds.") and starts a visible timer.
 What ends a trial: timer reaches 0 and the item is delivered immediately, or a maladaptive behavior occurs (trial = not met), or a 3rd verbal prompt is needed (not met).
Measurement (each opportunity): success (Y/N), # verbal prompts (0-2), latency to first maladaptive behavior (sec) if any, notes/context.
Mastery criterion: ≥80% successful trials per session across 4 of 5 consecutive sessions (then thin prompts to ≤1, then independent; optionally increase delay).
Probable Function

Competing behavior typically serves access to tangibles; may also contact attention (through protesting). Confirm via ABC data.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Skill (Delay/Denial Tolerance)

Pre-teach the routine: Request → "Yes, wait 30" → Watch timer/Use waiting plan → Timer ends → Receive item.

Visuals: "WAIT" card, first-then board, 30-s countdown timer in view.

Waiting plan menu: look at timer, deep breaths x3, squeeze fidget, count to 10 x3, ask once "How much longer?"

Shaping to 30 s: Start with a delay the client can do (e.g., 5-10 s), contact reinforcement, then increase by 5-10 s steps to 30 s over sessions.

Antecedent Supports

Clear, consistent cue: "Yes—wait 30 seconds." (Avoid saying "maybe" or "we'll see.")

Environment: minimize competing triggers; have fidgets ready; mark a "waiting spot."

Establish schedules: predictable access times reduce high EO (deprivation).

Choice during wait: pick a micro-task (e.g., choose a sticker, trace a shape) that doesn't compete with waiting calmly.

Reinforcement

DRA for waiting: Deliver the requested item immediately at 0 when criteria are met. Pair with behavior-specific praise ("You waited 30 seconds calmly!").

Bonuses: Tokens/points for each successful wait; exchange later.

Thin reinforcement carefully: Once stable, vary to VR-2 bonuses while keeping item delivery fixed at 0.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Appropriate request: Client will request the item with an approved modality before access in 90% of opportunities.

One follow-up check: During the wait, client may ask once "How much longer?" and accept the reminder ("When the timer ends") in 80% of trials.

Coping action: Client will use one waiting-plan action (timer/fidget/breaths) within 5 s of Prompt 1 in 80% of trials.

Brief break from demand (if relevant): Client requests "Short break" instead of protesting, ≤1 prompt, in 80% of need moments (use only when the requested item is not the break itself).

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Low-profile prompts: point to timer/WAIT card → Prompt 1 ("Waiting") → wait up to 5-10 s → Prompt 2 ("Use your waiting plan").

If maladaptive behavior starts:
Keep voice neutral; do not deliver the item during/after problem behavior.

Block unsafe behavior per site plan; withhold attention beyond brief safety statements.

Reset after a brief calm period (e.g., 5-10 s of calm): either restart the 30-s trial or drop to the last mastered delay to contact reinforcement quickly, then step back up.

Repair trial: After calm, run a quick successful wait (e.g., 10-20 s), deliver item, and praise specifically.

Consistency: The only path to the item is calm waiting; avoid negotiating/lengthy explanations.

The Client Will Acknowledge a Peer's Comment or Action with a Nod or Brief Verbal Response Once Daily

Target Behavior

Increase acknowledging a peer's comment or action with a nod or brief verbal response once per day with ≤1 verbal prompt.
Operational Definition

An acknowledgment is a short, directed signal (speech, sign, AAC, or nod) that shows the client noticed a peer's comment/action. A qualifying acknowledgment:
Occurs within 5-7 seconds of the peer's comment/action,

Is directed to the peer (turns body/eyes or uses their name), and

Requires no more than 1 verbal prompt (e.g., "Give a quick nod/'okay'").

Examples (count as success): nod + eye contact; "okay," "got it," "thanks," "cool," "nice pass," "I heard you," "good idea," thumbs-up (if pre-taught), AAC "OK/Thanks."
Appropriate opportunities include:
Peer speaks to the client (request, idea, greeting, feedback).

Peer assists/returns item or makes a team move affecting the client (e.g., pass in a game, share materials).

Peer reacts to the client's comment (laughter/response) prompting reciprocal acknowledgment.

Does not include: acknowledgments after the time window without repair, inaudible whisper with no repair, gestures not previously taught to count, or acknowledgments to self/away from peer.
Measurement
Primary: Daily success (Yes/No) = ≥1 qualifying acknowledgment with ≤1 prompt.

Secondary: Prompt level (0/1), latency (sec), modality (nod/speech/AAC/gesture), context (talk/help/play/transition).
 Onset/offset: Onset at peer comment/action toward/affecting client; offset at acknowledgment or after 7s post-prompt.

Probable Function

Desired behavior is maintained by social attention/affiliation and smoother interactions. Missed acknowledgments may reflect awareness/skill gaps, processing time/anxiety, or escape from social demand. (Track with brief ABC notes.)
Proactive Strategies

Antecedent supports
Post a 3-step cue: See → Turn → Signal ("OK/Thanks/Nod").

Prime likely moments (partner work, materials hand-off, game play).

Seat/pair strategically with supportive peers; reduce noise for clarity.

Ensure AAC quick buttons: OK • Thanks • Got it • Nice job.

Pre-teach acceptable gestures (nod, thumbs-up) that "count."

Teaching & prompting
Model-Lead-Test with 3-5 quick role-plays (peer asks, helps, passes).

Least-to-most prompts (max 1 verbal):
Gesture: point to chest then nod; tap "OK" card/AAC.

Single verbal: "Acknowledge—give a quick 'ok' or nod."

Wait 5-7s for independent responding before prompting.

Reinforcement: labeled praise ("Nice quick nod to Sam!") + token/point/access early (FR1), then thin.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

If speech is hard in the moment, teach and accept:
Nod or thumbs-up (pre-taught).

AAC 'OK/Thanks' button.

Repair after missed window: "I forgot—thanks for helping me."

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Early signs (freeze, gaze down, delayed processing):
Neutral cue to 3-step card/AAC; reduce audience; offer A/B choice ("Nod or 'ok'?").

If no response after the single prompt, move on without pressure; create a quick do-over later in a low-demand moment.

If avoidance/anxiety occurs:
Allow modality choice (gesture/AAC) or a brief pass-then-return (one per day); reinforce when completed.

The Client Will Acknowledge and Correct a False Statement with a Verbal Prompt

Target Behavior: Acknowledge & Correct a False Statement (≤1 verbal prompt)

Operational Definition

Acknowledge-and-correct is defined as the client, after making a statement that is factually inaccurate (about work, rules, recent events, or objective details), verbally acknowledging the inaccuracy and stating the correct information or retracting the claim within 10 seconds of a single, brief verbal prompt (e.g., "Check your statement," "Try your fix-it script").
 Acceptable responses include:
"You're right; I misspoke. The correct answer is 14."

"I was wrong about that—yesterday was Tuesday."

"I'm not sure; let me check," followed by checking and correcting.

Includes (examples):
Incorrect academic answers ("It's 15" when the key is 14).

False procedural statements ("We don't need to clean up").

Inaccurate personal claims ("I finished it already") discovered to be untrue.

Does not include:
Opinions/feelings ("I didn't like the game"), jokes/pretend play, or ambiguous/unknown facts.

Cases where the instruction was unclear, materials were missing, or memory is genuinely uncertain—use "not sure/let me check" instead (counts if followed by a correction).

Situations involving safety or high emotion where regulation must come first (score N/A).

Measurement

Opportunities: Any time the client's statement is shown or identified to be false/inaccurate during session.

Scoring per opportunity:
Independent = client corrects before any prompt.

Prompted = corrects after ≤1 verbal prompt within 10 s.

Not met = refuses, argues, or needs >1 prompt/ >10 s.

Primary metric: % of opportunities with acknowledge-and-correct (independent + prompted).

Secondary: % independent; latency (s) from prompt to correction.

Probable Function (for not correcting)

Commonly attention/face-saving, escape from accountability or task demands, and/or control/autonomy. Sometimes simple skill/accuracy deficit or memory error. Confirm with brief ABC notes.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine — "A-C-C" (Acknowledge → Correct → Commit)

Acknowledge: "You're right / I misspoke / Good catch."

Correct: "The correct ___ is ___."

Commit: "I'll remember next time / I'll fix it now."
 Use modeling → role-play → feedback with real examples from work and daily routines.

Visuals & Tools

"Fix-It Script" card with A-C-C sentence starters.

Error-friendly norms ("Mistakes = data; we fix and move on").

Check prompts on worksheets (✅ "Double-check before you say it").

Antecedent Supports

Give clear SDs and show where the truth source is (answer key, schedule, rule poster).

Build in self-check points before public responding.

Normalize corrections by adults modeling their own quick A-C-C.

Prompting (max 1 verbal prompt)

Prefer nonverbal cue first (point to Fix-It card or answer key).

If needed, give one brief prompt: "Use your fix-it script." Then wait 5-10 s silently.

After a prompted success, run a transfer trial later (new opportunity, no prompt) to build independence.

Reinforcement

DRA: Labeled praise + token/point for independent A-C-C; reinforce prompted A-C-C as well (slightly lower value).

Start FR1 for early practice; thin to VR2-3 as consistent.

Bonus for calm tone and quick latency (<5 s).

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Primary Target — A-C-C with ≤1 Prompt: Following identification of an inaccuracy, the client acknowledges and states the correct info (or retracts) within 10 s of one verbal prompt, in ≥80% of opportunities across 3 sessions.

Self-Correct Before Prompt: The client independently uses A-C-C upon noticing an error, ≥50% of opportunities by mastery phase.

"Not Sure—Check" Option: When uncertain, client says, "I'm not sure; let me check," verifies with an allowed source, and corrects, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Repair Statement (if others affected): "I gave the wrong info; here's the correct ___," ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Keep prompts brief/neutral; avoid "gotcha" tones.

When-Then: "When you use your fix-it script, then we move on."

Planned ignore for arguing/defensiveness; immediately reinforce the pivot to A-C-C.

Offer a 30-60 s micro-break if emotion rises; resume with the Fix-It card.

If debate persists, cap interaction ("We'll revisit later") and record ABCs.

The Client Will Respond to Requests from Adults with a Calm 'Yes' or an Appropriate Alternative Suggestion With a Verbal Prompt

Target Behavior: Respond to adult requests with a calm "Yes" or an appropriate alternative suggestion (≤1 verbal prompt)

Operational Definition

When an adult delivers a clear request/direction (e.g., "Please start your math," "Put the iPad away," "Come to the table"), the client will, within 10 seconds:
EITHER
Say a calm, respectful "Yes" (or equivalent: "Okay," "Sure") and begin the requested action within 10 seconds,

OR
 2) Offer one appropriate alternative suggestion in a calm voice (e.g., "Can I finish this page first?" "Two more minutes, then I'll start," "Can I do reading before math?").
 The suggestion must be:
Respectful (neutral tone; no sarcasm/rolling eyes),

Feasible & brief (time-bound ≤2 minutes or a small sequence change),

Functionally equivalent (still leads to the adult's goal), and

Accepts the adult's decision (if the adult declines, client says "Okay" and begins the task within 10 seconds).

Prompts allowed: Up to one brief verbal prompt total (e.g., "Answer with 'yes' or offer an alternative."). Nonverbal/visual cues (gesture to script card) may be used without counting toward the one prompt.
Non-examples: ignoring the request; arguing/bargaining after an answer; repeated "but—"; hostile tone; conditional "yes" with sarcasm; offering multiple alternatives after the adult decides; engaging in maladaptive behavior (e.g., yelling, refusal posturing, leaving area).
Measurement: Each adult request = 1 opportunity. Record:
Outcome: Calm Yes & started, Appropriate Alternative → accepted, Appropriate Alternative → declined then complied, Not met

# Verbal prompts (0/1)

Latency (sec) to initial response and to task start

Notes (topic, setting)

Mastery criterion: ≥80% successful opportunities per session across 4 of 5 consecutive sessions, with ≤1 verbal prompt. (Then fade to independent.)
Probable Function

Prior noncompliance/arguing often contacts escape/avoidance, access to tangibles/activities, and/or attention/control. Confirm with ABC data.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Skill (model → role-play → in vivo)

Two-path script (posted/visual/AAC page):
Path A: "Yes → Start"

Path B: "One Alternative → Accept decision → Start"

Sentence stems:
Yes: "Okay," "Sure, I can."

Alternative: "Can I ___ first?" "Two more minutes, then I'll ___." "Can I switch to ___ and come back to ___?"

Acceptance: "Okay," "Got it."

Rehearsal: Practice 3-5 quick scenarios daily (teacher asks → client uses Yes or Alternative → accept decision → start).

Antecedent Supports

Clear, concise requests (one-step or numbered steps).

First-Then or Checklist visible.

Transition warnings (e.g., 2-min / 30-sec).

Choice architecture: embed small choices (where to sit, which pencil, which problem first).

Behavioral momentum: 2-3 easy "high-P" actions before the big request.

Task adjustments: break into chunks; provide exemplars and needed materials.

Reinforcement

DRA: Immediate praise and access to natural reinforcement for Calm Yes or Appropriate Alternative followed by compliance.

Behavior-specific praise: "You offered one respectful alternative and started right away—nice flexibility."

Token/points: Earn a token for each success; exchange at session end.

Premack: "After you start now, you can pick music."

Honor feasible alternatives on a predictable schedule (e.g., ~50% of the time), then thin gradually.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Calm "Yes" Response: Within 10s of a request, client says "Okay/Yes" and begins within 10s, independent or ≤1 prompt, 80% of opportunities.

One Alternative Suggestion: Client offers one respectful, time-bound alternative within 10s, ≤1 prompt, 80% of opportunities.

Accept Decision & Start: After adult accepts/declines, client says "Okay" and starts within 10s, ≤1 prompt, 80% of opportunities.

Clarification/Help Request (if needed): "Can you show me?" "What's step one?" instead of arguing, ≤1 prompt, 80%.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Prompting hierarchy: gesture to script card → single verbal prompt ("Yes or one alternative") → wait up to 10s.

If arguing starts: neutral tone; do not debate. Restate choice once: "Yes now, or one respectful alternative." If client continues, end discussion and deliver a brief, clear directive with time to start.

No reinforcement for arguing/refusal: Withhold extra attention/tangibles obtained via debate.

If escalation signs appear: offer a brief, pre-taught break option ("30-second reset, then start").

Repair trial after calm: run a quick success (request → Yes/Alternative → accept → start), praise, and return to routine.

Consistency: Adult decisions are final; only one alternative per opportunity.

The client will wait 10 seconds before saying 'I don't know' in response to a question posed to him

Target Behavior

Increase waiting 10 seconds before saying "I don't know" when a question is posed.
Operational Definition

When the therapist/teacher finishes asking a question (speech, sign, or AAC), the client:
Does not say "I don't know" for at least 10 seconds, and

During that 10-second window either
 a) attempts an answer (spoken/AAC/sign, or choice selection), or
 b) uses a taught repair/request (e.g., "More time please," "Can you repeat/rephrase?" "Can I have choices?" "A clue, please."), or
 c) remains in active think posture (looks at materials/partner, silent rehearsal) while the partner holds wait time.

After 10 seconds, an IDK is acceptable and does not count against the target.
Does not include: rhetorical questions; questions beyond taught skill without supports; immediate IDK used as part of a pre-taught strategy after the 10-second wait; off-topic responses.
Measurement
Primary: % of targeted questions where latency to IDK ≥10s (or no IDK stated).

Secondary: use of alternatives (help/time/choices/clue), number of prompts used (0/1/2), and whether an attempted answer was produced.

Onset/offset: Onset = end of the question; offset = first qualifying response (answer/repair) or 30s elapse.

Probable Function

Immediate IDK often functions as escape/avoidance from effort or evaluation, anxiety reduction, or control/autonomy. Skill factors may include slow retrieval, language formulation load, or auditory processing needs. (Track with brief ABC notes.)
Proactive Strategies

Antecedent supports
Post a THINK-10 routine: Hear → Breathe → Count to 10 → Try/Ask.

Use a silent 10-second timer (sand timer, on-screen ring, or finger count) that both partners honor.

Prime with topic cues (word bank, visuals) and allow modality choice (speech/AAC/sign/point).

Right-size difficulty; start with known items and gradually increase complexity.

Build in noncontingent reassurance ("Take your time—thinking is okay.") to reduce pressure.

Teaching & prompting (least → most)
Gesture to THINK-10 card/timer; therapist models a calm 10-second wait.

Prompt 1: "Use your think time—then try or ask for help."

Prompt 2 (scaffold): "Choose one: more time / repeat / choices / clue."

Use time-delay (5 → 10 seconds) as independence grows.

Keep question wording stable during the wait; avoid rapid rephrasing that resets processing.

Reinforcement
DRA: labeled praise + token/point/access for waiting the full 10s and either attempting an answer or using a repair.

Extra reinforcement for independent waits (0-1 prompt) and for attempted answers.

Do not immediately remove the task because of a premature IDK; honor breaks when requested via the taught script.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Teach and accept these within the 10-second window:
"More time, please."

"Repeat / Rephrase it, please."

"Can I have choices?"

"A clue/hint, please."

"I'm thinking…" (card/AAC).

"Help me start." (partner gives first step, then client tries).

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

If IDK is said before 10 seconds:
Neutral reset: "Let's use THINK-10," show timer, silently wait; then re-offer the same question or a scaffold (choices/clue).

Provide a minimal success path after the wait: forced choice, yes/no, or first-sound cue to enable an attempt.

If stress rises (sighing, fidgeting, avoidance):
Lower language load; offer A/B supports ("Repeat or choices?"), reduce audience, and allow a brief calm breath without removing the question contingency.

The Client will wait until the adult pauses before he says what he wants to with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior: Wait for a Conversational Pause Before Speaking (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

Wait-for-Pause is defined as the client not beginning to speak while the adult is talking and waiting until a clear pause before stating what he wants to say. A response counts when the client:
Holds voice off until one of the following occurs:
The adult pauses for ≥1 second, or

The adult gives a handoff cue (e.g., says the client's name, "Go ahead," or gestures open hand).

Then the client begins speaking within 3 seconds of the pause/cue using a calm tone (≤10-second turn).

Includes (examples):
Quietly waiting while the adult finishes a sentence; speaking after a ≥1-second pause.

Raising a hand or touching a pre-taught "I have something" card while waiting.

Saying "Excuse me" after the pause if needed.

Does not include:
Talking over the adult or starting during a syllable/word.

Repeated "but—but—but—" overlaps or sound effects to grab the floor.

Speaking after more than 3 seconds have passed when invited (missed turn).

Safety/urgent alerts are exempt (score N/A).

Prompt allowance: Up to two brief verbal prompts (e.g., "Wait for the pause," then "Your turn now"). Nonverbal/visual cues do not use the two-prompt allowance.
Measurement
Opportunities: When the client indicates a desire to speak while an adult is talking (e.g., leans forward, raises hand) or during an adult monologue/discussion turn.

Score each as Independent (0 prompts), Prompted (1-2 prompts), or Not Met (>2 prompts/overlap/missed turn).

Primary metric: % of opportunities with successful Wait-for-Pause (independent + prompted).

Secondary: % independent; overlap count (interruptions); latency from pause to start (goal ≤3 s).

Probable Function (for interrupting)

Often attention/engagement, impulsivity, excitement about ideas, or escape from waiting/uncertainty. Confirm with brief ABC notes.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine — "S-S-W-S" (Signal → Still → Wait → Speak)

Signal you have something (hand up/"I have something").

Still body/voice (hands quiet, eyes on speaker).

Wait for the pause or name cue.

Speak briefly (≤10 s), then yield the floor.

Model → Role-play → Practice with short scripted scenes.

Use a visual (hand icon + pause symbol) on the desk.

Antecedent Supports

Adults announce turn structure ("I'll finish this point, then your turn").

Provide predictable pauses every 1-2 sentences in teaching moments to create success.

Seat the client where visual cues are easy to see; reduce background noise.

Prompting & Fading (max 2 verbals)

Nonverbal first: finger "pause" gesture, point to visual (doesn't use a prompt).

Verbal 1: "Wait for the pause."

Verbal 2 (handoff): "Your turn now."

After any prompted success, run a transfer trial later (longer silent wait) to build independence.

Reinforcement

DRA: Labeled praise + token/point for waiting and speaking after the pause (bonus for independent).

Start FR1, thin to VR2-3 as consistent.

Menu: choose next question/topic, brief preferred activity, helper role.

Environment/Skill Supports

Teach backchannels (nod, "mm-hmm") as a substitute for blurting.

Offer a "Save-It" card to jot the idea while waiting.

For impulsivity, pair with 1 slow breath as part of "Still."

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Primary — Wait-Then-Speak: When wanting to talk, the client waits until a ≥1-second adult pause or handoff cue, then speaks within 3 seconds, in ≥80% of opportunities with ≤2 verbal prompts across 3 sessions.

Signal Instead of Blurting: Client uses hand up/"I have something" while waiting, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Save-It Strategy: If the pause doesn't come quickly, client writes the idea on a Save-It card and keeps waiting, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Brief Turn: Client keeps his turn ≤10 seconds unless invited to elaborate, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Brief cue then wait (3-5 s): "Wait for the pause."

Two-choice redirect: "Hand up or Save-It."

Planned ignore of minor overlaps; immediately reinforce the pivot back to waiting.

When-Then: "When you wait for the pause, then you share first."

If urgency rises, offer micro-break (30-60 s) and restart with a clear turn plan.

The Client will wait until the peer pauses before he says what he wants to say 1 time per session with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior: Wait for a peer's pause before speaking — 1×/session (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

During a conversation or group activity, when the client wants to contribute while a peer is speaking, the client will:
Hold comments (mouth quiet; no side remarks or sound effects).

Attend to the speaker (body/eyes generally oriented).

Wait for a natural pause from the peer, defined as ≥2 seconds of silence or a clear turn-yield cue (e.g., peer looks up/makes eye contact, nods "your turn," or finishes a sentence and stops).

Use a polite entry (hand raise or brief signal such as "Excuse me," "Can I add something?") and then speak when acknowledged.

Prompts allowed: Up to two brief verbal prompts total per opportunity (e.g., Prompt 1: "Wait for the pause," Prompt 2: "Use your signal."). Gestural/visual cues may occur in addition.
Non-examples: blurting before a pause; whispering side comments; tapping/waving to capture attention; speaking without acknowledgment; speaking over the peer.
Measurement: Designate 2-4 natural conversation opportunities per session. Mark Success/No, # verbal prompts (0-2), and brief notes.
 Session criterion: At least 1 successful instance (meets all steps with ≤2 prompts) per session.
 Mastery: Success in 4 of 5 consecutive sessions (then fade to ≤1 prompt or increase to 2-3 successes/session).
Probable Function

Interrupting typically contacts attention (immediate response), sometimes escape from waiting or automatic (impulsivity). Confirm via ABC data.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Micro-Skill (model → role-play → in vivo)

Rule: "Pause-2, Signal, Speak."

Scripts: "Excuse me," "May I add something?" "I have a thought about ___."

Timing practice: Use short read-alouds; insert deliberate pauses; client practices counting "one-two" silently, then signaling.

Perspective cue: Paraphrase first: "I heard you say ___," then add the comment.

Antecedent Supports

Visual cue card on desk/wrist: 👂 Pause-2 → ✋ Signal → 🗣️ Speak.

Talk tokens/turn sticks to structure turns.

Notepad/"Hold-that-thought" card to jot ideas while waiting.

Seating & sensory: reduce distractors; quiet fidget allowed.

Reinforcement

DRA: Immediate, specific praise and attention when the client waits for the pause and signals ("You waited for the pause and used 'Excuse me'—respectful turn-taking!").

Token/points for each success; exchange at end of session.

Planned opportunities: Ensure at least 2-4 chances daily so the client can contact reinforcement.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Pause-2 then Signal: Client waits ≥2 s and signals politely in 80% of practice trials with ≤2 prompts across sessions.

Paraphrase-then-Share: When acknowledged, client paraphrases the peer's point in one short phrase before adding their own in 60-80% of turns (shape up).

Note-to-self Strategy: When urge to speak arises, client writes/draws a keyword instead of blurting in 80% of need moments (≤1 prompt).

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Low-profile cues first: gesture to cue card → whisper "Wait for the pause" → after pause, "Use your signal."

Planned ignore of brief blurts (when safe); do not reward the blurt with a response. Rapidly call on the client after the next correct pause + signal and praise.

Neutral reset: "Let's try that again—Pause-2," then run a quick do-over and reinforce.

If agitation rises: offer a binary choice ("Write it down or short 30-sec break, then share").

Consistency: Attention follows pause + signal, not blurting

The client will use a verbal cue such as "excuse me" before interjecting when others are speaking with 2 verbal prompts

Target Behavior

Increase using a verbal cue (e.g., "Excuse me," "Pardon me," "May I say something?") before interjecting while others are speaking, with ≤2 verbal prompts.
Operational Definition

During an ongoing conversation where another person is speaking, the client:
Produces a polite attention cue (speech/AAC/sign) such as "Excuse me," before taking the floor, and

Waits for an acknowledgment or a natural pause (up to 10 seconds) before speaking their message.

Counts as success when all are true:
Context: someone else is actively speaking or has the floor.

The client uses a polite cue prior to their contribution and does not talk over the speaker.

The contribution occurs after an acknowledgment/pause, within the 10-second wait.

Requires no more than 2 verbal prompts (e.g., "Use your cue," "Wait for a pause.").

Does not include:
Speaking without any cue while another is talking,

Calling out the message and then saying "excuse me" afterward,

Safety/emergency interruptions (tracked separately—these bypass the rule),

Off-topic or disruptive interjections.

Measurement
Primary: % of interjection opportunities with a qualifying cue + wait (≤2 prompts).

Secondary: prompts used (0/1/2), latency from cue to speaking, instances of talking over others (record as errors).
 Onset/offset: Onset at the moment the client attempts to enter while another is speaking; offset after the client speaks following acknowledgment/pause, or after 10s with no response.

Probable Function

Most often Attention/Access to the floor, with contributions from Escape from waiting and Autonomy/Control; sometimes impulsive/automatic responding. (Verify with ABC data.)
Proactive Strategies

Antecedent supports
Post the simple rule: Wait → Signal ("Excuse me") → Speak.

Pre-correct before high-risk times: "Remember, cue first, then wait for name or a pause."

Structure turn-taking: visible speaking tokens, timer per turn, or a "You're on deck" card.

Increase predictable access to the floor: round-robin or frequent checks-in.

Seat placement where the leader can see the cue; minimize noise and crowding.

Ensure AAC has quick buttons: Excuse me • May I add? • Can I go next?.

Teaching & prompting
Model-Lead-Test the 3-step routine with quick role-plays (easy, preferred topics first).

Prompting hierarchy (max 2 verbal prompts total):
Nonverbal: point to visual/raise hand gesture.

Verbal Prompt 1: "Use your cue."

Verbal Prompt 2: "Now wait for a pause—then speak."

Coach brief entries (≤10 seconds or 1-2 sentences) and teach yielding ("Your turn").

Reinforcement: DRA—specific praise + points/token/access for cue+wait use; bonus for independent or first-prompt responses. Consider group points for collective turn-taking.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Cue + Wait (primary): "Excuse me," hand raise, or AAC "May I add…?" then wait.

Priority Card (limit 1-2 per activity) if the message is time-sensitive: show card plus say "Excuse me."

Hold-That-Thought card/notepad—write it down while waiting.

Request order: "Can I go next?" or "Put me on deck?"

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Early signs (leaning in, mouth opening to cut in):
Silent palm "wait" + point to the visual; move closer to acknowledge ("I see you—on deck").

If the client interjects without a cue:
Brief, neutral reset: "Pause—cue first." (Counts as a prompt.) Then acknowledge quickly after the cue to reinforce the sequence.

If they struggle to wait:
Offer a bounded choice: "Quick 10-second add now, or full turn next?"

If persistence escalates, assign a speaking token for the next turn and reinforce when used.

The Client Will Respond to Feedback with a Simple Acknowledgment or Thank You Once Per Session

Target Behavior: Respond to Feedback with a Simple Acknowledgment/"Thank you" (≥1 per session)

Operational Definition

Feedback acknowledgment is defined as the client giving a brief, respectful acknowledgment within 5 seconds after an adult provides feedback (correction, suggestion, or praise). An acknowledgment may be verbal or AAC and includes:
"Okay," "Got it," "Thanks," "Thank you for the feedback," "I understand," or pressing a programmed AAC acknowledgment.

Optionally adding one clarifying question or next step (e.g., "Should I fix #3 now?").

Does not count:
Silence paired with arguing body language (eye roll, sigh) or walking away.

Arguing, debating, or blaming.

Acknowledgments given after more than one verbal prompt or beyond 5 seconds.

Situations where feedback is unsafe/inappropriate (score N/A).

Measurement
Opportunities: Any time the adult gives corrective/constructive feedback.

Score each opportunity: Independent (0 prompts), Prompted (≤1 brief verbal prompt, e.g., "Say 'okay'"), Not met (>1 prompt/arguing/latency >5 s).

Session goal: Record whether at least one acknowledgment (independent or prompted) occurred that session.

Primary metric: % of sessions meeting the ≥1 acknowledgment goal; Secondary: % independent acknowledgments.

Probable Function (when not acknowledging)

Often autonomy/control or face-saving, sometimes escape from correction or attention. Verify with brief ABC notes.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine — "H-A-D" (Hear → Acknowledge → Do)

Hear the feedback (listening body, eye on speaker/material).

Acknowledge with one short phrase: "Okay/Thanks/Got it."

Do the next step (fix it, try again, or ask one clarifying question).

Model → Role-play → Practice using common feedback scenarios.

Create a script card with 3-5 preferred phrases (and AAC buttons if used).

Antecedent Supports

Adults give brief, clear feedback + next step ("Fix #2, then you're done").

Signal feedback moments: "Feedback coming—ready?"

Offer choice in repair ("Do you want a hint or try alone?").

Prompting & Fading

Nonverbal first (point to script card/thumbs-up).

If needed, one brief verbal prompt: "Say 'Okay' or 'Thanks.'"

After a prompted success, run a transfer trial later (create another small feedback moment; wait for independence).

Reinforcement

DRA: Labeled praise + token/point for quick (≤5 s) acknowledgments (bonus for independent).

Start FR1, thin to VR2-3 as consistent.

Offer small exchange items/privileges or immediate positive consequence (e.g., "Thanks—go ahead and submit").

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Primary—Acknowledge (≤1 prompt): Following feedback, the client says one acknowledgment (or AAC) within 5 s, in ≥80% of feedback opportunities, ensuring ≥1 acknowledgment per session across 3 consecutive sessions.

Clarify-Then-Act: If unsure, client asks one clarifying question, then acknowledges ("Okay, I'll fix #2"), ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Appeal/Micro-Break Option: If upset, client requests a brief 1-2 min pause ("I need a minute"), returns, then acknowledges, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Validate → Guide (≤6 words): "I hear you—say 'okay.'"

When-Then: "When you acknowledge, then we problem-solve."

Planned ignore for arguing; reinforce the pivot the moment an acknowledgment occurs.

Offer the micro-break if tone rises; resume with the script card.

Keep corrections brief/private; avoid debates.

The Client Will Use Role-Playing to Practice Appropriate Social Responses and Behaviors in 1 Scenario Per Session

Target Behavior: Use role-playing to practice appropriate social responses/behaviors — 1 scenario per session (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

During a scheduled social-skills block, the client will complete one full role-play scenario that targets a specific social skill (e.g., greeting, asking to join, accepting "no," disagreeing respectfully, asking for help, waiting turn, coping with teasing).
 A completed scenario includes all of the following:
Instruction (30-60s): the adult states the situation and goal (e.g., "Ask to join a game").

Model (≤30s): adult/peer models the target response.

Rehearsal #1 (coached): client role-plays the response with ≤2 verbal prompts total (gestural/visual prompts allowed and don't count).

Rehearsal #2 (probe): client repeats the scenario with the same partner with 0-2 verbal prompts.

Feedback (≤30s): brief behavior-specific praise and one tweak.

Success for the session = client completes steps 1-5 and demonstrates the target social response chain in at least one of the two rehearsals with ≤2 verbal prompts and without unsafe behavior.
Non-examples: refusing to participate, off-topic scripts throughout rehearsal, arguing about the scenario, or needing >2 verbal prompts in both rehearsals.
Measurement (per session): Scenario name/skill, prompts used in Rehearsal #1 and #2 (0/1/2), Success (Y/N), notes.
Mastery criterion: 1 successful scenario per session on 4 of 5 consecutive sessions. (Then increase to 2 scenarios/session or fade to ≤1 verbal prompt.)
Probable Function

Role-play targets usually replace behaviors maintained by attention, tangible access, or escape/avoidance. Confirm via brief ABC notes to pick scenarios with best functional match.
Proactive Strategies

Teaching Method: BST (Behavioral Skills Training)

Instruction → Modeling → Rehearsal → Feedback, then a quick re-rehearsal if time allows to contact reinforcement.

Set-Up & Materials

Scenario bank (pick one per session):
Greeting/starting a conversation

Asking to join a game

Asking for help

Politely declining/accepting "no"

Waiting and taking turns

Disagreeing respectfully

Coping with teasing/mean comments

Requesting a break/cool-down

Visuals/AAC: sentence stems, "PAUSE-ACKNOWLEDGE-I-statement," "Ask-Comment-Ask," First/Then.

Role cards/props to make expectations concrete.

Prompts (fade quickly)

Gesture → visual → verbal prompt 1 (e.g., "Use your stem") → verbal prompt 2 (e.g., "Try it now"). A 3rd verbal prompt ends that rehearsal as not met.

Reinforcement

Immediate behavior-specific praise after each correct attempt.

Token/points for each successful rehearsal; bonus for completing the session's scenario.

Natural reinforcement: brief preferred social interaction or choice of next topic.

Generalization

Rotate partners, settings, and topics across sessions.

After success in role-play, plan one in-vivo opportunity the same day to use the skill (e.g., real greeting at dismissal) and praise.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (pick 1-2 per scenario)

Appropriate Request: "Can I join you?" / AAC equivalent—within 5-10s, ≤2 prompts, 80% across role-play opportunities.

Accepting "No"/Alternative: "Okay—maybe later," or "Can we set a timer?"—≤2 prompts, 80%.

Respectful Disagreement: Pause ≥3s → acknowledge → I-statement ("I see it differently because…")—≤2 prompts, 80%.

Help/Break Request: "Help please," or "Short break please"—≤2 prompts, 80%.

Turn-taking Entry: Wait for pause → signal → speak briefly—≤2 prompts, 80%.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Low-profile coaching: gesture to visual → concise cue ("Use your stem") → model briefly if needed.

If resistance/frustration appears: offer binary choice ("Practice with me or with Peer A"), or a 30-second reset; return to the scenario quickly.

No lengthy debates about the scenario; restate goal once and proceed.

Repair trial: After any miss, run a short do-over (10-20s) to contact success and praise.

Honesty

The Client Will Discuss the Consequences of Dishonesty and Identify Alternatives in 1 Scenario Per Session

Target Behavior

Increase discussing the consequences of dishonesty and identifying at least one alternative response in 1 scenario per session with ≤2 verbal prompts.
Operational Definition

Given a contrived or naturalistic scenario involving dishonesty (e.g., lying about work, cheating, taking without permission, hiding a mistake), the client:
Names at least two realistic consequences of the dishonest act
one immediate/personal (e.g., loss of privilege, guilt) and

one social/longer-term (e.g., damaged trust, fewer opportunities), and

Identifies at least one honest alternative they could use instead (e.g., ask for help, admit mistake, request more time),
 within 45 seconds of the prompt and with ≤2 verbal prompts.

Counts as success when: both parts (consequences + alternative) are stated and fit the scenario.
 Does not include: vague moralizing ("lying is bad") without linking to the scenario, consequences unrelated to the act, or alternatives that are also dishonest/avoidant.
Measurement
Primary: Session success (Yes/No) = completed 1 full analysis (≥2 consequences + 1 alternative) with ≤2 prompts.

Secondary: prompts used (0/1/2), latency (s), and quality rating (0-3):
0 = incomplete/incorrect; 1 = one consequence only; 2 = two consequences or one consequence + one alternative; 3 = two consequences and one alternative that clearly fits.
 Onset/offset: Onset at the end of the scenario prompt; offset when analysis is complete or 45s elapse after the last allowed prompt.

Probable Function

Desired behavior is maintained by insight, problem-solving, and improved social outcomes. Interfering behaviors (defensiveness, "I don't know," changing the subject) may function for escape from accountability, anxiety reduction, or control/autonomy, and can reflect language/processing demands.
Proactive Strategies

Antecedent supports
Use a simple visual framework: "2C + 1A + Repair"
Consequences—Now & Relationships/Future

Alternative—honest, workable next time

Repair—quick fix-it step if the dishonest act already happened

Prime with neutral, third-person scenarios first to reduce defensiveness.

Provide sentence starters:
"If I lie, then ___ (now) and ___ (later/with others). Instead, I could ___."

"To fix it, I will ___."

Offer choice of modality (speech/AAC/written→read aloud).

Right-size complexity; start with concrete school/home examples and scale up.

Teaching & prompting (least → most)
Point to the 2C+1A visual; wait 5-10s.

Prompt 1: "Name one thing that could happen now and one with others/later."

Prompt 2 (scaffold): "Pick one: lose trust / consequence at school / feel guilty … Now choose one honest alternative: ask for help / admit mistake / ask for more time."

Reinforcement
DRA: specific praise for accurate links ("You tied it to trust—nice insight") + token/point/access.

Bonus for independent (0-1 prompt) or quality=3 responses.

Thin reinforcement as fluency increases; generalize to real events.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Teach alternatives the client can name (and later use) instead of dishonesty:
Ask for help/extension: "I need help / more time."

Admit & plan: "I made a mistake. I will ___ to fix it."

Offer partial progress: "I finished part A; I can do part B by __."

Clarify/renegotiate: "Can we do it a different way?"

Use a pause: "I need a minute to think."

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Early signs (shrugging, "IDK," defensiveness):
Keep tone neutral; switch to third-person version of the same scenario; offer A/B choices for consequences; allow think time.

Reduce language load: use yes/no checks to build to full statements.

If avoidance persists after Prompt 2:
Provide a minimal success path: therapist states one consequence; client chooses a second from two options and selects one alternative from two choices. Reinforce, then retry a similar scenario later for independence.

Manding

Manding for Information

Manding for Needs Met

The Client Will Identify Personal Need for a Break and Communicate It With a Verbal Prompt

Target Behavior: Identify Need for a Break & Communicate It (≤1 verbal prompt)

Operational Definition

Break requesting is defined as the client recognizing rising dysregulation (e.g., frustration, fidgeting, off-task, faster breathing, clenched hands, repeating "I can't") or a pre-identified trigger (long task, noise, social demand) and communicating a need for a break using a taught method (card/hand signal/AAC/verbal script) before problem behavior occurs.
 A response counts when the client:
Uses one of the approved requests (e.g., shows Break Card, taps AAC "Break please," or says "I need a break") within 5 seconds of noticing signs or within 10 seconds of one brief verbal prompt ("Use your break request").

Transitions to the designated break area/strategy and engages in non-disruptive regulation for 30-120 seconds (as preset).

Returns to the task within 10 seconds of the timer and re-engages as agreed (First-Then).

Includes (examples):
Showing the Break Card during rising frustration and walking to the calm corner.

Saying, "I need 1 minute" when noise increases, using noise-canceling headphones, then returning.

AAC press "Break" followed by standing stretch, then back to seat.

Does not include:
Leaving area without communicating; arguing or refusing instead of requesting.

Using "break" to access unrelated items/locations not on the menu.

Break requests during safety-critical moments (e.g., crossing street)—use after safety is secured (score N/A).

Measurement
Opportunities: Any time early signs of dysregulation/trigger are observed or the client self-identifies a need.

Score each: Independent (no prompt), Prompted (≤1 verbal prompt), Not met (>1 prompt/no request/problem behavior first).

Track: request method used, break duration, return latency ≤10 s, and task completion post-break.

Session goal: ≥X successful break requests (set by team) with ≥80% return compliance.

Probable Function

Primary: Escape/avoidance of aversive stimuli (effort, noise, social demand) and/or self-regulation. Appropriate break requesting functions as FCT (functional communication) to replace problem behavior.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine — "Notice → Ask → Regulate → Return"

Notice: identify 2-3 personal signs (choose from a visual list).

Ask: use one approved request (card/hand signal/AAC/verbal).

Regulate: do menu item(s) for 30-120 s (breathing, stretch, water, headphones, fidget).

Return: timer ends → back within 10 s → resume First-Then task.

Use modeling → role-play → in-the-moment practice with immediate feedback.

Post a Break Menu (icons) and a 2-5 scale (1=calm, 5=overwhelmed) for self-checks.

Environment & Antecedents

Establish a clear break space, timer, and pre-agreed frequency/duration (e.g., up to 3 brief breaks per 30 minutes; flexible for need).

Use First-Then visuals; preview high-demand blocks and invite planned micro-breaks.

Provide noise/seat options, fidgets, and water access.

Prompting & Fading (max 1 verbal prompt)

Nonverbal first: point to Break Card/scale (doesn't use the verbal prompt).

If still struggling, Verbal (single): "Use your break request." Then wait 5-10 s silently.

After a prompted success, schedule a transfer trial (next trigger, aim for independence).

Reinforcement

DRA: Labeled praise + token/point for independent requests; reinforce prompted requests slightly less.

Bonus for quick return ≤10 s and task re-engagement.

Exchange menu: choice time, preferred activity after completing post-break work chunk.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Primary—Break Request (≤1 prompt): Upon early signs/trigger, client uses the approved request, takes a 30-120 s regulated break, and returns within 10 s to resume the task, in ≥80% of opportunities with ≤1 verbal prompt across 3 sessions.

Self-Monitor & Plan: Client identifies current level (1-5) and selects a Break Menu item independently, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Micro-Break Alternate: If a full break isn't possible, client requests a micro-break (30-60 s) or adjustment ("Can I stand while working?"), ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Help-then-Break Option: Client requests help or clarification first; if still dysregulated, requests break appropriately, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Brief cue → wait: point to card/scale; if needed, one verbal: "Use your break request."

When-Then: "When you ask, then you can take 1 minute."

Planned ignore of minor refusal/complaints; immediately reinforce the pivot to a request.

Start timer as soon as request is honored; avoid debate about fairness.

If escalation continues, offer guided choice: "30-second stretch or 2-minute quiet corner?"

Safety first: if behavior escalates, follow site plan; debrief privately and reset the routine.

The Client will appropriately ask for a break when he needs one, such as saying "I need a break", with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior: Appropriately request a break (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

An appropriate break request occurs when the client, while remaining safe and calm, communicates a clear request for a short break using a pre-taught modality (speech, AAC button, "BREAK" card, ASL sign, or hand-raise + "Break please") within 10 seconds of (a) an adult prompt or (b) early signs of frustration during a task.
Counts as 1 success when all are met:
Request is clear and directed to staff (audible/visible).

Occurs before or in the absence of maladaptive behavior (e.g., yelling, aggression, elopement, property destruction, self-injury).

Requires ≤2 verbal prompts total (e.g., Prompt 1: "Ask for a break." Prompt 2: "Use your break words.").

Client transitions to the break area/activity within 10 seconds after permission and returns when the break timer ends.

Non-examples: vague complaints ("This is dumb") without the break word/signal; requesting after engaging in maladaptive behavior; negotiating repeatedly after a decision.
Measurement: Each demand or observed escalation = 1 opportunity. Record Success (Y/N), prompts (0/1/2), latency to request (s), and maladaptive behavior (Y/N).
Mastery criterion: ≥80% successful opportunities per session across 4 of 5 consecutive sessions (then fade to ≤1 prompt, then independent).
Probable Function

Primary: Escape/avoidance from task demands or stimulation; secondary: attention. Confirm via ABC data.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine (FCT: Functional Communication Training)

Script: Notice hard → Say/Signal "Break please" → Go to break spot → Timer → Return & resume.

Model → Role-play → In-the-moment coaching across tasks.

Modality menu: speech ("I need a break"), AAC "Break" button, BREAK card, ASL sign BREAK.

Antecedent Supports

First-Then board ("Work 2 mins → Break 1 min").

Visible timer for both work and break.

Choice within work: order, materials, seating.

Task shaping: chunk tasks; provide examples; reduce initial response effort.

Pre-teach before high-demand blocks: "If it's tough, use your break words."

Break Parameters (make explicit)

Length: 1-3 minutes (start at 1; adjust based on data).

Where: designated calm area/spot.

What: quiet, non-competing options (water, stretch, fidget, breathing card).

Return cue: timer + "Back to work" card; client resumes within 10 seconds.

Reinforcement

Immediate honor of appropriate requests (deliver the break fast).

Behavior-specific praise: "Great job asking for a break calmly."

Tokens/points for each successful request + return.

Thin extra rewards gradually; continue to honor the break itself.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Break Request (primary): Client uses pre-taught modality to request a break within 10s of need or prompt, ≤2 verbal prompts, in 80% of opportunities.

Help Instead of Break (optional): If the issue is difficulty, client requests help ("Help please") in 70-80% of opportunities.

Time Extension/Count-to-10: Client asks for a brief delay ("30 more seconds?") or uses a coping action (3 breaths, squeeze fidget) while preparing to request, ≤1 prompt, 80%.

Return to Task: After break, client resumes the task within 10s in 80% of trials.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Prompting hierarchy: gesture to BREAK card/timer → Verbal Prompt 1 ("Ask for a break") → wait 5-10s → Verbal Prompt 2 ("Use your break words").

Do not grant a break because of maladaptive behavior. Wait for brief calm (5-10s), prompt the request, then honor it—keep voice neutral.

Binary choice if rising: "Break now or help now?"

Reset & repair: After any miss, run a quick do-over (prompt → request → 30-60s break → timely return), then praise.

The Client will ask to shorten skill coaching discussions to a reasonable time, such as 3 minutes, with 2 verbal prompts

Target Behavior

Increase asking to shorten a skill-coaching discussion to a reasonable time (e.g., 3 minutes) with ≤2 verbal prompts, and then participating for the agreed time.
Operational Definition

When a coaching conversation is proposed or underway, the client:
Requests a time-bound version using a polite, clear phrase (speech/AAC/sign), e.g.,
"Can we keep it to 3 minutes?"

"Short version, please—3 minutes."

"Two tips in 3 minutes, then practice?"

Waits for acknowledgment, and

Engages in the discussion for the agreed duration (timer running), without arguing or leaving.

Counts as success when all are true:
The request occurs before refusal/arguing and within 10 seconds of the coaching cue.

Requires no more than 2 verbal prompts (e.g., "Use your short-coaching request," "Choose a time.").

The client stays engaged until the timer ends (±15 seconds), then transitions as planned.

Does not include: refusing without a request, bargaining repeatedly after agreement, asking to shorten and disengaging before the timer, or using the request to avoid all coaching.
Measurement
Primary: % of relevant coaching opportunities with a qualifying short-coaching request + full participation.

Secondary: prompts used (0/1/2), latency to request (sec), agreed duration, completion (Y/N), and any arguing/refusal (Y/N).
 Onset/offset: Onset at coach's invitation to discuss skills; offset when timer ends and the agreed wrap-up step is completed.

Probable Function

Typically Escape/Avoidance of long discussions and/or Autonomy/Control over time; sometimes Attention (being heard about preferences). (Verify via ABC data.)
Proactive Strategies

Antecedent supports
Establish a menu of durations (e.g., 3-minute Short, 6-minute Standard, 10-minute Deep Dive) with icons.

Post a Short-Coaching Routine: Ask → Agree → Timer → Two tips → Plan/Next step.

Pre-teach that short ≠ skip: we still cover essentials, then practice.

Prime before high-demand blocks: "Coaching later—short or standard?"

Ensure AAC has quick buttons: Short coaching 3 min • Two tips only • Timer please • Standard later.

Teaching & prompting (least → most)
Point to the duration menu / timer (nonverbal cue).

Prompt 1: "If you want short coaching, ask for 3 minutes."

Prompt 2 (carrier): "Say: 'Short version, 3 minutes, please.'"

Start with contrived trials; use model-lead-test and role-plays.

Always start a visible timer after agreement; show the wrap-up cue (e.g., "One sentence plan").

Reinforcement
DRA: specific praise + token/point/access for (a) making the request appropriately and (b) staying engaged through the timer.

Bonus for independent (0-1 prompt) requests and on-time wrap-up.

Thin reinforcement as consistency improves.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Teach and accept these polite, workable alternatives:
Request delay: "Can we do it after math?" / "Put me on deck for coaching."

Request format: "Two tips + one example," "Bullet points only," "Show me, then I try."

Request help to tolerate: "I need one-minute break, then 3-minute coaching."

Split session: "Do 2 minutes now, 1 minute after practice."

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Early signs (sighing, stalling, rising tone):
Offer the bounded choice immediately: "3-minute short now or 6-minute standard after centers?"

Point to the routine; keep voice low; reduce language.

If arguing/refusal starts:
Neutral reset: "Use your short-coaching request." (counts as a prompt)

Once requested, honor promptly and run the timer to contact success.

If client seeks to skip entirely, offer a micro-dose: 90 seconds + one practice rep; then revisit.

After timer ends:
Quick wrap-up: "Say your 1-sentence plan," then transition to practice/next activity. Reinforce completion.

The Client will mand for attention appropriately from the technician with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior: Mand (Request) Attention Appropriately from Technician (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

Appropriate attention manding is defined as the client requesting the technician's attention using a taught method and waiting for acknowledgement before speaking further. A response counts when, within 5 seconds of needing attention (help, comment, share), the client does one of the following and then waits quietly up to the agreed wait time:
Verbal: "Excuse me, [Name]," or "Can I tell/ask you something?"

Name + Function: "Ms. Lee, I need help."

Gestural: light two-finger shoulder tap, raised hand, or Attention Card shown.

AAC: taps programmed button (e.g., "Excuse me," "I need help").

The client pauses until the technician gives a handoff cue (eye contact, "Yes?"/"Go ahead") or a wait cue with an expected time ("One minute"). If asked to wait, the client maintains quiet waiting (hands to self, voice off) until the cue or timer, without problem behaviors.
Does not count:
Calling out over others, repeating the name >2 times in a row, hovering in personal space, tugging clothing, snapping, clapping, or touching face/hair.

Using loud voice, profanity, or interrupting active instruction without urgency.

Walking away when told to wait, or continuing to talk without acknowledgement.

Safety/urgent alerts (e.g., "He's hurt!") are handled immediately and scored N/A.

Prompt allowance: Up to two brief verbal prompts total (e.g., "How can you ask?" "Use your 'excuse me'."). Nonverbal/visual cues do not use up the two prompts.
Measurement
Opportunities: Any time the client seeks access to the technician's attention (technician is busy or attention is not already on the client).

Score each opportunity: Independent, Prompted (≤2), or Not met (>2 prompts/problem behavior/no wait).

Track method used, latency to mand (goal ≤5 s), and wait tolerance when asked to wait.

Primary metric: % of opportunities with appropriate attention mand (independent + prompted). Secondary: % independent and average wait duration completed.

Probable Function

Primary attention (social interaction, help). Inappropriate attention-seeking (calling out, touching) is replaced by functional communication (FCT).
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine — "Get-Ask-Wait-Speak"

Get proximity (1-2 steps away, hands to self).

Ask using the taught script/card/AAC.

Wait for "Yes?" or a wait cue + timer.

Speak briefly (≤10 s) when cued; if asked to wait, continue waiting.

Model → Role-play → Practice during low-stakes times; then embed in natural routines.

Create a visual (icons: hand up / "Excuse me" bubble / clock / talk).

Antecedent Modifications

Establish attention windows (e.g., "After I finish the direction, I'll check in").

Technician consistently gives a clear acknowledgement: "Yes?" or "One minute," and sets a visible timer if waiting.

Pre-correct: "If you need me, say 'Excuse me, [Name]' and wait."

Prompting & Fading (max 2 verbals)

Nonverbal first: point to Attention Card/hand signal (no prompt used).

Verbal 1: "How can you ask?"

Verbal 2: "Use 'Excuse me, [Name].'"

After success, run a transfer trial later (create an opportunity; longer pause before cues) to build independence.

Reinforcement

DRA/FCT: Deliver attention quickly (and labeled praise) when the client uses the appropriate mand.

Start FR1 (every correct mand), then thin to VR2-3 while keeping problem behavior on extinction (no extra attention).

Bonus for independent mands and for successful waiting when cued ("Thanks for waiting—your turn now.").

Wait Tolerance (if needed)

Teach "Wait Card" with brief timer (15-30 s → fade up gradually).

Provide quiet wait activities (fidget, "Save-It" note) that do not compete with attention.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Primary—Appropriate Attention Mand: Client uses "Excuse me, [Name]" (or card/AAC) within 5 s of needing attention and waits for acknowledgement, achieving this in ≥80% of opportunities with ≤2 verbal prompts across 3 sessions.

Wait on Cue: When given a wait cue, client waits quietly until timer/acknowledgment, then speaks, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Two-Finger Tap Alternative: If verbal is hard, client uses a light shoulder tap + waits, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Save-It Strategy: If wait >30-60 s, client writes/draws the message on a Save-It card and continues waiting, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Planned ignore of minor calling out; immediately reinforce the pivot to "Excuse me."

When-Then: "When you ask appropriately, then I can help you."

Keep prompts brief/neutral; avoid lecturing.

If urgency rises, offer two choices: "Use your card or say 'Excuse me'—I'll cue you next."

Safety first: if behavior escalates (grabbing/pushing), increase space, remove audience, and re-teach once calm.

The Client will mand for attention appropriately from the technician with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior: Appropriately mand (request) attention from the technician — ≤2 verbal prompts

Operational Definition

An appropriate attention mand occurs when the client, while remaining safe and calm, uses a pre-taught modality to get the technician's attention and waits for acknowledgment before speaking further. Acceptable modalities include:
Speech: "Excuse me, [Name]," "Can I have your attention?," "Can you talk with me?"

AAC/ASL/Attention card: single-tap "Attention" button/sign or handing an ATTENTION card.

Optional, if pre-approved: a light shoulder tap paired with the phrase.

A response counts as successful when all are met:
Directed to the technician (name use/eye orientation/device output toward tech).

Occurs independently or with ≤2 verbal prompts total (e.g., Prompt 1: "Use your attention words," Prompt 2: "Say 'Excuse me, [Name]'.").

Client waits quietly ≤5 seconds for acknowledgment after the request (no repeating/raising volume).

No maladaptive behavior before or during the request (e.g., yelling, grabbing, arguing, property destruction).

Opportunity definition: The technician is visibly occupied (speaking/writing) for ≥5s and the client seeks engagement. Create 3-6 opportunities per session.
Non-examples: calling out "hey/yo," repeating requests rapidly, tugging clothes, interrupting without acknowledgment, requesting after engaging in problem behavior.
Measurement (each opportunity): Success (Y/N), # verbal prompts (0/1/2), latency to request (s), waited ≤5s (Y/N), maladaptive (Y/N), notes.
Mastery criterion: ≥80% successful opportunities per session across 4 of 5 consecutive sessions (then fade to ≤1 prompt, then independent; later add short wait tolerance).
Probable Function

Access to adult attention/social interaction (primary). May also contact tangible or escape functions depending on context—confirm with brief ABC notes.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Skill (FCT: Functional Communication Training)

Model → role-play → in-the-moment coaching for the sequence:
 Approach/Orient → Mand ("Excuse me, [Name]") → Wait ≤5s → Acknowledgment → Speak/Request.

Provide sentence stems/AAC buttons: "Excuse me, [Name]." / "Can you help me?" / "I want to tell you something."

Two-step retry rule: If no acknowledgment after 5s, repeat once with the same polite phrase; then continue waiting.

Antecedent Supports

Visual cue card: 👋 "Excuse me, [Name] → Wait."

Designated waiting spot next to technician's desk to reduce crowding.

Contrived practice: Technician withholds attention for 10-15s to create EO, then differentially reinforces the taught mand.

NCR (noncontingent attention): brief check-ins every 5-7 minutes to reduce motivation for problem attention-seeking.

Reinforcement

Immediate social attention (within ~2-3s) for appropriate mands: eye contact, name use, warm tone, 10-30s of engagement/help.

Behavior-specific praise: "You said 'Excuse me, [Name]' and waited—great asking for attention!"

Token/point for each success; exchange at session end.

Withhold attention following inappropriate bids (planned ignore when safe), then prompt and reinforce the correct mand.

Generalization & Thinning

Practice with multiple staff/peers, different distances, noise levels.

Once stable, introduce brief wait tolerance (e.g., "One moment" + visible 5-10s timer) while still reinforcing the polite mand.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Primary FERB—Attention Mand: Client uses "Excuse me, [Name]" (or AAC/ASL/card) to obtain attention within 10s of need, ≤2 verbal prompts, in 80% of opportunities.

Wait After Mand: Client waits quietly ≤5s for acknowledgment (one polite repeat allowed) in 80% of opportunities.

Alternative if Busy: Client accepts "One moment" and waits the indicated time (start with 5-10s) with ≤1 prompt in 80% of trials.

Help vs. Attention Discrimination: If the true goal is assistance, client follows the attention mand with a clear help request ("Help with ___, please") in 80% of successes.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Low-profile prompts: point to the cue card/AAC → Prompt 1 ("Use your attention words") → wait up to 5-10s → Prompt 2 ("Say 'Excuse me, [Name]'.").

Planned ignore of inappropriate bids (e.g., calling out) when safe; immediately attend after the next correct mand.

Neutral redirect if escalation signs appear: "Try your attention words," offer brief binary: "Ask now or write it down—your choice."

Safety first: If maladaptive behavior occurs, follow site plan; after calm, run a repair trial (prompt → correct mand → quick attention) to contact reinforcement.

The Client will mand for attention appropriately from peers with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior

Increase asking peers for attention appropriately (i.e., manding for attention) with ≤2 verbal prompts.
Operational Definition

An appropriate attention mand to a peer is a brief, directed request for a peer's attention (speech, sign, or AAC) produced before speaking the main message and followed by a brief wait for acknowledgment. A qualifying response includes both:
A polite cue directed to the peer (e.g., "[Name], excuse me," "Can I tell you something?" "Can I play?"), and

Waiting up to 10 seconds for the peer's acknowledgment or a natural pause before delivering the message.

Appropriate opportunities (non-exhaustive):
Wanting to join play/group, show something, ask a question, request help/materials from a peer, or respond to a peer who is currently engaged elsewhere.

Does not include:
Calling out over others, talking while the peer is speaking, grabbing/tugging, repeated "hey/hey/hey," touching face/hair, or manding to adults (this goal is peer-focused).

Saying the message first and adding "excuse me" afterward.

Measurement
Primary: % of opportunities with a qualifying cue + wait using ≤2 verbal prompts.

Secondary: number of independent vs. prompted mands, latency from opportunity to cue (sec), latency from cue to speaking (sec), and errors (call-out/grab/interrupt).

Onset/offset: Onset when the client seeks access to a peer's attention (moves toward/looks at peer or a contrived opportunity is presented). Offset when the client delivers the message after acknowledgment/pause or after 10s without response.

Probable Function

Primarily access to social attention/interaction; may also involve escape from waiting or autonomy/control (wanting the floor). (Confirm with brief ABC notes.)
Proactive Strategies

Antecedent supports
Post a 3-step visual: Approach → Say cue ("Excuse me, [Name]") → Wait (count 5-10).

Pre-correct before high-risk times: "If you need Sam's attention, cue then wait."

Seat/pair with supportive peers; reduce noise; create predictable check-in moments.

Ensure AAC has quick buttons: Excuse me • Can I talk to you? • Can I play? • Your name + I have something to say.

Prime peers to respond within ~3 seconds to appropriate cues ("I'll listen in a sec," "One moment—then your turn").

Teaching & prompting (least → most; max 2 verbal prompts)
Nonverbal: point to visual/gesture "wait."

Verbal Prompt 1: "Use your attention cue."

Verbal Prompt 2: "Now wait for a pause—then talk."

Use model-lead-test with quick role-plays (joining a game, asking for a turn, showing work).

Time delay (5-10s) after cues to build waiting tolerance.

Coach brief entries (≤10 seconds or 1-2 sentences) and a yield phrase ("Your turn.").

Reinforcement
DRA: immediate, labeled praise + natural peer attention for cue+wait; add token/point/access early (FR1), then thin.

Bonus for independent mands and for waiting the full interval without calling out.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Priority Card (limit 1-2 per activity) for time-sensitive comments: show card and say "Excuse me."

Hold-That-Thought notepad/AAC note while waiting.

Request order: "Can I go next?" / "Put me on deck?"

If peer is busy: "When can you talk?" or accept "2 minutes" with a timer.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Early signs (leaning in, mouth opening to cut in, hand reaching):
Silent palm "wait," point to visual; move closer and acknowledge ("I see you—you're next.")

If client calls out or grabs:
Brief, neutral reset: "Pause—cue first." (counts as a prompt)

Immediately reinforce the first appropriate cue that follows; avoid giving the conversation floor after an error.

If waiting is hard:
Offer a bounded choice: "Quick 10-second add now after your cue, or full turn next?"

If escalation continues, assign a speaking token for the next turn; reinforce when used.

The client will ask for help with a difficult task with 2 verbal prompts

Eye Contact

Eye Contact During Conversations

Maintaining appropriate eye contact for 80% of the duration of a conversation

Target Behavior: 
 Maintaining appropriate eye contact for 80% of the duration of a conversation.
&nbsp;
Motivating Operation:
 Encouraging effective communication skills, fostering social engagement, and building rapport with others.
Antecedent Procedures:
 1. Use a naturalistic setting such as a classroom, therapy room, or home where conversations typically occur.
 2. Inform the client that the goal is to maintain eye contact while talking.
 3. Practice eye contact through role-playing or other drills in controlled settings before moving to naturalistic ones.
&nbsp;
Discriminative Stimulus:
 Another individual initiates or engages in a conversation with the client.
Appropriate Response:
 The client will maintain eye contact with the conversation partner for at least 80% of the conversation's duration to be considered a successful trial.
&nbsp;
Error Correction Procedures:
 1. **Modeling**: If the client fails to maintain eye contact, the therapist models appropriate eye contact.
 2. **Prompting**: Use non-verbal cues like tapping or visual prompts like moving into the client's line of sight to encourage eye contact.
 3. **Immediate Feedback**: Provide specific feedback about the duration and appropriateness of eye contact.
 4. **Reinforcement**: Positive reinforcement is provided when the client successfully maintains eye contact for the target percentage of time.
&nbsp;
Criteria for Mastery: 
 The client will maintain appropriate eye contact for 80% of the conversation's duration in 8 out of 10 trials across 3 consecutive data collection sessions.
&nbsp;
This ABA goal is designed to improve the client's social communication skills by focusing on one of its fundamental aspects: eye contact. Achieving this goal would be beneficial for social interactions and building meaningful relationships.
Instructional Notes

MO:
 Encouraging effective communication skills, fostering social engagement, and building rapport with others.
 
 Antecedent Procedures:
Use a naturalistic setting such as a classroom, therapy room, or home where conversations typically occur.
 2. Inform the client that the goal is to maintain eye contact while talking.
 3. Practice eye contact through role-playing or other drills in controlled settings before moving to naturalistic ones.

 
 SD:
 Another individual initiates or engages in a conversation with the client.
 
 Response:
 The client will maintain eye contact with the conversation partner for at least 80% of the conversation's duration to be considered a successful trial.
 
 Prompting:
 visual prompt,verbal prompt,gestural prompt,modeling
 
 Error Correction:
 1. **Modeling**: If the client fails to maintain eye contact, the therapist models appropriate eye contact. 2. **Prompting**: Use non-verbal cues like tapping or visual prompts like moving into the client's line of sight to encourage eye contact. 3. **Immediate Feedback**: Provide specific feedback about the duration and appropriateness of eye contact. 4. **Reinforcement**: Positive reinforcement is provided when the client successfully maintains eye contact for the target percentage of time.
 
 Reinforcement:
 differential social reinforcement,socially mediated reinforcement

Following Directions

Following Multi Step Directions

The client will follow 2 step directives that involve distance

Retell

Retell Information

The client will Independently retell one activity or discussion that occurred during the therapy session

Target Behavior: 
 Independently retelling one activity or discussion that occurred during the therapy session.
&nbsp;
Motivating Operation:
 Enhancement of memory recall and comprehension, thereby increasing engagement and the utility of therapeutic activities or discussions.
Antecedent Procedures:
 1. Conduct a typical therapy session involving various activities or discussions.
 2. Towards the end of the session, inform the client that they will be asked to recall an event or discussion.
 3. Make sure to provide a quiet and comfortable environment to facilitate focus and recall.
&nbsp;
Discriminative Stimulus:
 The therapist asks, "Can you tell me about one activity or discussion we had today?" or a similar prompt designed to elicit recall.
Appropriate Response:
 The client will independently retell one activity or discussion from the session within 1 minute of the SD. The retelling should be coherent and include key details to be considered a successful trial.
&nbsp;
Error Correction Procedures:
 1. **Modeling**: If the client struggles to retell the activity or discussion, the therapist will provide a model or example.
 2. **Prompting**: Use verbal or gestural prompts to help the client begin their response or to recall specific details.
 3. **Immediate Feedback**: Give immediate feedback if the retelling is incomplete or incorrect, specifying what needs improvement.
 4. **Repetition**: Re-prompt the SD, providing another opportunity for the client to practice the correct behavior.
&nbsp;
Criteria for Mastery: 
 The client will independently retell one activity or discussion from the session within 1 minute of the SD in 8 out of 10 trials across 3 consecutive data collection sessions. Mastery should be generalized across multiple types of activities and discussions.
&nbsp;
This goal aims to help the client improve their ability to recall and retell events, which is an essential skill both for maximizing the benefits of therapy and for day-to-day life.
Instructional Notes

MO:
 Enhancement of memory recall and comprehension, thereby increasing engagement and the utility of therapeutic activities or discussions.
 
 Antecedent Procedures:
Conduct a typical therapy session involving various activities or discussions.
 2. Towards the end of the session, inform the client that they will be asked to recall an event or discussion.
 3. Make sure to provide a quiet and comfortable environment to facilitate focus and recall.
&nbsp;

 
 SD:
 The therapist asks, "Can you tell me about one activity or discussion we had today?" or a similar prompt designed to elicit recall.
 
 Response:
 The client will independently retell one activity or discussion from the session within 1 minute of the SD. The retelling should be coherent and include key details to be considered a successful trial.
 
 Prompting:
 visual prompt,verbal prompt,gestural prompt,modeling
 
 Error Correction:
 1. **Modeling**: If the client struggles to retell the activity or discussion, the therapist will provide a model or example. 2. **Prompting**: Use verbal or gestural prompts to help the client begin their response or to recall specific details. 3. **Immediate Feedback**: Give immediate feedback if the retelling is incomplete or incorrect, specifying what needs improvement. 4. **Repetition**: Re-prompt the SD, providing another opportunity for the client to practice the correct behavior.
 
 Reinforcement:
 differential social reinforcement,socially mediated reinforcement

The Client will use necessary details when retelling an activity or discussion from the therapy session

Target Behavior: 
 Using necessary details when retelling an activity or discussion from the therapy session.
&nbsp;
Motivating Operation:
 Improving the richness of recall and the capacity to convey information accurately, thereby fostering effective communication and deeper understanding of the session's content.
Antecedent Procedures:
 1. Conduct a standard therapy session incorporating various activities and discussions that may be topics for retelling.
 2. Signal to the client towards the end of the session that they will be asked to recall and detail one or more events.
 3. Ensure an environment conducive to focus and clear articulation, minimizing distractions.
&nbsp;
Discriminative Stimulus:
 The therapist will prompt, "Can you tell me about something we discussed or did today? Please include important details."
Appropriate Response:
 Within 2 minutes of the SD, the client will retell an activity or discussion from the session, incorporating necessary details such as who, what, where, when, and why to be considered a successful trial.
&nbsp;
Error Correction Procedures:
 1. **Modeling**: If the client omits crucial details, the therapist models a detailed retelling.
 2. **Prompting**: Use verbal prompts like, "Can you tell me more about that?" to encourage the inclusion of missing details.
 3. **Immediate Feedback**: Provide specific feedback about what details were omitted and how to include them in future retellings.
 4. **Repetition**: Redeliver the SD to allow the client another opportunity to include the necessary details.
&nbsp;
Criteria for Mastery: 
 The client will include all necessary details when retelling an activity or discussion within 2 minutes of the SD in 8 out of 10 trials across 3 consecutive data collection sessions. Generalization should occur across multiple sessions and various activities or discussions.
&nbsp;
This ABA goal aims to cultivate the client's ability to include essential details when retelling events, which is critical for effective communication and comprehension.
Instructional Notes

MO:
 Improving the richness of recall and the capacity to convey information accurately, thereby fostering effective communication and deeper understanding of the session's content.
 
 Antecedent Procedures:
Conduct a standard therapy session incorporating various activities and discussions that may be topics for retelling.
 2. Signal to the client towards the end of the session that they will be asked to recall and detail one or more events.
 3. Ensure an environment conducive to focus and clear articulation, minimizing distractions.

 
 SD:
 The therapist will prompt, "Can you tell me about something we discussed or did today? Please include important details."
 
 Response:
 Within 2 minutes of the SD, the client will retell an activity or discussion from the session, incorporating necessary details such as who, what, where, when, and why to be considered a successful trial.
 
 Prompting:
 visual prompt,verbal prompt,gestural prompt,modeling
 
 Error Correction:
 1. **Modeling**: If the client omits crucial details, the therapist models a detailed retelling. 2. **Prompting**: Use verbal prompts like, "Can you tell me more about that?" to encourage the inclusion of missing details. 3. **Immediate Feedback**: Provide specific feedback about what details were omitted and how to include them in future retellings. 4. **Repetition**: Redeliver the SD to allow the client another opportunity to include the necessary details.
 
 Reinforcement:
