 differential social reinforcement,socially mediated reinforcement

The Client will retell a daily experience including 2 expanded details and one personal connection, with 1 Verbal Prompt, Once Per Session

Target Behavior: Retell a Daily Experience with Details & Personal Connection (≤1 verbal prompt, once per session)

Operational Definition

Daily-experience retell is defined as the client orally recounting one real event from that day (school/home/community) and including:
Two expanded details, and

One personal connection,
 with no more than one brief verbal prompt from staff during the retell.

Expanded details (any two qualify): a specific who/where/when, a sequence marker (first/then/finally), a reason ("because…"), a feeling word, a sensory or descriptive detail, a quantity/time detail, proper name of a person/place, or a brief dialogue snippet.
Personal connection (any one qualifies): a statement linking the event to self (past experience — "It reminded me of…", present feeling/reaction — "I felt proud…", preference/opinion — "I liked it because…", or future plan — "Next time I will…").
Acceptable prompt (max = 1): one brief verbal cue such as "Tell me more about it." or "What did it remind you of?"
 Nonverbal cues (pointing to icons/visuals) are allowed and do not count as the verbal prompt. Modeling full sentences or supplying content does not count and is disallowed during the probe.
Start/Stop rules
Start when the adult gives a clear cue (e.g., "Tell me something you did today."). Client begins within 10 seconds.

Retell window: up to ~90 seconds (finish earlier if all elements are met).

Terminate/mark "not met" if: more than 1 verbal prompt is needed, the client goes off-topic >10s, refuses, or adult must model content.

Measurement / Criterion
Event-based checklist (Y/N). Criterion met when the retell:
Begins ≤10s after the cue,

Contains ≥2 expanded details,

Contains ≥1 personal connection,

Uses ≤1 verbal prompt.

Probable Function

Primary: escape/avoidance (narrative language demand, retrieval/organization).
 Secondary: attention (seeking adult scaffolding) and/or tangibles (delaying to keep preferred activity).
Proactive Strategies

Teach the routine (outside the probe)
Introduce a 3-part visual: Event → 2 Stars (details) → 1 Heart (connection).

Provide sentence frames:
Event: "Today I ___ at ___."

Details: "First… Then…", "Because…", "It was ___ (feeling)."

Connection: "It reminded me of ___," "I like/don't like it because ___," "Next time I will ___."

Rehearse with photo prompts or quick memory log cards during the day (jot 3 keywords).

Set up the context
Give the cue after a meaningful activity; have visual icons/word bank ready (feelings, places, time words).

Allow 5-10s planning time to glance at the visual before speaking.

Use Think-Pair-Share rehearsal (peer first), then independent retell during the probe.

Reinforcement
Labeled praise for target elements ("You added two details and a connection!").

Small contingent reinforcer for 0-1 prompt completions; thin as independence grows.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Plan request: "Give me 5 seconds to think," then begin within 10s.

Word/clarification request (once): "What's the word for ___?" or "Can you repeat the cue?" then continue.

Self-prompting with visuals: Point to 2 Stars/1 Heart icons and produce the elements without adult content.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Use the single allowed prompt ("Tell me more about it / What did it remind you of?"), then pause 5-10s.

If stuck, offer a content-neutral choice: "School or home event?" (does not add content).

Keep language brief/neutral; avoid leading questions that supply details.

If frustration rises, allow a 20-30s reset, then re-offer the cue once (still only 1 verbal prompt total).

Inferencing

Inferencing answers to questions asked

The Client will answer 1 inferencing question after hearing or reading a short story with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior: Answer 1 Inferencing Question After a Short Story (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

Answering an inference question is defined as the client, after hearing or reading a short story, providing a plausible inference that is not explicitly stated in the text and citing at least one text clue (or background-knowledge clue) to support the answer.
 A response counts when the client:
Gives a spoken or AAC answer using a taught stem (e.g., "I think ___ because ___"),

Within 30-60 seconds of the question (or within the agreed think-time), and

Requires no more than two brief verbal prompts.

Short story = a narrative or passage appropriate to level (≈80-250 words; or an equivalent read-aloud ~1-2 minutes).
Includes (examples):
"I think the boy is nervous because he is sweating and keeps looking at the clock."

"The party is a surprise because they hide and whisper."

Does not include:
Literal recall ("The dog ran home") without an inferred idea.

Off-topic guesses with no story-based clue.

Answers given after >2 verbal prompts, or outside the time window.

Measurement
Opportunities: each short story with an inference question (≥1 per session).

Scoring per opportunity:
Independent: accurate inference + 1+ clue, 0 prompts.

Prompted: accurate inference + 1+ clue with ≤2 verbal prompts.

Not met: inaccurate/no inference, no evidence, >2 prompts, or out of time.

Optional accuracy rubric (0-2 points):
2 = correct inference + relevant clue(s);

1 = partially correct or weak clue;

0 = incorrect/no clue.

Probable Function (when the skill breaks down)

Most commonly skill deficit (doesn't know how to infer/evidence), escape/avoidance of demanding language tasks, or performance anxiety. Confirm with brief ABC/data.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine — "Clues + Me = Inference"

Clues: underline/highlight 1-2 key details.

Me: add background knowledge ("People sweat when nervous").

Inference: combine with a stem: "I think ___ because ___ and ___."

Use think-aloud modeling with 2-3 examples.

Provide a mini graphic organizer (C + BK → I).

Pre-teach 3-5 question stems: What can you figure out about…? How do you know…? What's the author hinting…?

Text/Task Supports

Right-size text length and vocabulary; preview critical words.

Offer read-aloud/TTS as needed; allow picture supports for younger learners.

Provide sentence frames: "I infer ___ because the story says ___, and I know ___."

Prompting & Fading (max 2 verbals)

Nonverbal first (doesn't count toward two): point to organizer/sentence frame; gesture to highlighted clues.

Verbal Prompt 1: "Use your inference frame."

Verbal Prompt 2: "What clue tells you that?"

After any prompted success, run a transfer trial on the next passage (longer silent wait, fewer cues).

Reinforcement

DRA: Labeled praise + token/point for a complete inference (bonus for independent).

Start FR1, thin to VR2-3 as consistent; exchange for small, quick motivators.

Celebrate evidence language ("because," "the story says…") with extra points.

Antecedent Modifications

Begin with high-interest or familiar-topic stories; increase complexity gradually.

Use First-Then visuals ("First inference, then game").

Provide think-time (e.g., 10-15 s) and a note strip for quick clue jotting.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB) / Skill Targets

Primary—Complete Inference (≤2 prompts): After the story, the client produces one inference + one clue using a frame within 30-60 s, in ≥80% of opportunities with ≤2 verbal prompts across 3 sessions.

Use the C+BK→I Organizer: Client identifies 1-2 clues, states background knowledge, and combines into an inference, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Clarify-Then-Infer: If unsure, client asks one clarifying question ("Does ___ mean ___?"), then answers with an inference, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Self-Monitor Language: Client uses a because statement or "the text says…" in answers, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Brief cue, then wait: "Use your frame," (point to organizer); allow 10-15 s.

Two-choice support: "Pick one clue or ask one clarifying question."

Reduce load if stuck: offer two answer choices and ask, "Which fits the clue?" then fade choices next trial.

Micro-break option (30-60 s) if frustration rises; resume with a shorter passage.

Planned ignore of avoidance talk; immediately reinforce the pivot to using the frame or citing a clue.

Inferring Logical Next Steps

The Client will identify the logical next step in a short written or spoken scenario

The Client will predict the expected answer or outcome in a short written prompt

Language

Categorization

The client will categorize 2 objects and situations with a verbal prompt

Target Behavior: Categorize 2 items (objects or situations) with ≤1 verbal prompt

Operational Definition

During a structured teaching block, when presented with a categorization task, the client will correctly name or sort the category for two targets (any mix of objects or situations) using speech or an approved modality (AAC/pointing to label) with no more than one verbal prompt.
What counts as a trial
Object trial: Technician presents 2-3 pictures/objects and asks, "What category are these?" or "Where does this go?"
 Correct example: "Animals," "Food," "Things you wear," "Red things," "Things that cut."

Situation trial: Technician presents a picture/story card and asks, "What kind of situation is this?"
 Correct example: "School rule," "Emergency," "Sharing/turn-taking," "Unexpected," "Home activity," "Safe vs. unsafe," "Big vs. small problem," etc. (choose one scheme per session and teach it).

Prompt allowance: ≤ 1 brief verbal prompt total per trial (e.g., "Think about what they have in common," or "Category?"). Gestural/visual prompts (pointing to headers) don't count.
Non-examples: labeling single items without naming the category; sorting by preference ("I like it") instead of the taught rule; needing ≥2 verbal prompts; incorrect category.
Measurement (per session):
Provide at least 4 opportunities (e.g., 2 object trials + 2 situation trials).

For each trial record: Correct (Y/N), Prompts (0/1), Latency (s), Category set used.

Session success: Client completes ≥2 correct trials (any mix of object/situation) with ≤1 verbal prompt each.
 Mastery criterion: Meets session success in 4 of 5 consecutive sessions (then raise to ≥3-4 correct and/or fade to 0 prompts).
Probable Skill Domain

Language/cognition—feature-function-class (FFC) and social situation classification. If errors cluster during high demand, consider escape-maintained behaviors and increase supports.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Skill (errorless → transfer of control)

Model: "These are dogs and cats—animals."

Immediate guided practice: Client echoes/points to category label; deliver quick praise.

Prompted trial: Present a new but similar set; give visual/gestural cue, then one verbal prompt if needed.

Transfer trial (no prompt): Rotate with mastered sets; ask "What category?"

Mixed & varied practice: Interleave objects and situations; shuffle categories.

Materials

Sorting mats/bins with headers (e.g., Animals, Foods, Clothes; or Safe/Unsafe; Home/School; Expected/Unexpected).

Picture cards (objects) + situation cards (short scenarios/illustrations).

AAC page/word bank: common category labels.

Visual rule card: "Look → Think together → Name the group."

Antecedent Supports

Start with clear, concrete categories (animals, foods) then move to abstract/social (expected vs. unexpected).

Limit distractors; teach one situation framework per session.

Use themewise sets tied to the day's activities for natural reinforcement.

Reinforcement

Behavior-specific praise every correct: "Nice—'things you wear'!"

Token/points per correct trial; exchange at end.

Natural reinforcement: use the category in a quick game (e.g., pick any "animal" card for show-and-tell).

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Clarification request: If unsure, client uses "Can you show an example?" or "What are my choices?" with ≤1 prompt in 80% of uncertain moments.

Explain the rule: Client states a feature/function link once per session (e.g., "They're tools because they cut").

Self-correction: If wrong, client tries a new category within 5 s after a neutral prompt ("Try again—think alike").

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Low-profile cues: point to header → brief cue ("Think together") → if needed, single verbal prompt ("Category?").

If frustration shows: offer binary choice ("Animals or clothes?") or a 30-60s micro-break, then resume with an easier set.

No over-prompting: If a second verbal prompt would be needed, end the trial neutrally, model the answer, and run a quick errorless repair trial to contact reinforcement.

Compare and Contrast

The client will compare and contrast by finding similarities and differences between 2 objects and 2 situations with a verbal prompt

Target Behavior

Increase comparing and contrasting by finding at least one similarity and one difference between a pair of objects and a pair of situations in the same session, with ≤1 verbal prompt per pair.
Operational Definition

Given:
Object pair (photos or real items), and

Situation pair (brief stories, pictures, or videos),

the client produces two statements per pair:
Similarity using a frame like "Both ___ …," and

Difference using a frame like "___ but ___ …,"

via speech/AAC/sign within 15 seconds of the cue and with no more than 1 verbal prompt (e.g., "Tell one thing the same and one thing different.").
Acceptable similarity sources
Objects: category, function, parts, material, color/shape/size, where found/used.

Situations: setting, people/roles, goals, rules, steps, feelings.

Acceptable difference sources
Objects: function, size/shape/color/material, cost, where/when used.

Situations: cause, consequence, rule, setting, time/order, emotion, perspective.

Does not include: off-topic features, repeats ("both are things"), or only one of the two required statements.
Measurement

Primary: Session success = completes both pairs (objects + situations) with a similarity AND difference each, and ≤1 prompt per pair.

Secondary: prompts used (0/1), latency (sec), modality, and quality score (0-3) per pair:
0 = inaccurate/none; 1 = only similarity or only difference; 2 = one accurate of each; 3 = adds a second accurate similarity or difference.
 Onset/offset: Onset at end of the therapist's instruction; offset after required statements or 60s (per pair).

Probable Function

This is a skill-acquisition goal; barriers (IDK, silence, off-topic) often function as escape from language load or reflect retrieval/organization demands.
Proactive Strategies

Visual scaffold: Venn diagram or "Same / Different" mat with icon prompts (category, function, parts, setting, cause/consequence).

Sentence starters: "Both ___," "They are different because ___."

Word banks: common categories (animals, tools, places), situation cues (who/where/goal/rule).

Priming: preview pairs; start concrete → move abstract; allow modality choice (speech/AAC/sign).

Think time: quiet 5-10s before any prompt.

Teaching & Prompting

Model-Lead-Test quickly: therapist models one pair → practice together → client solo on new pairs.

Least-to-most (cap at 1 verbal prompt):
Point to Venn/mat (nonverbal).

Single verbal prompt: "One thing the same and one thing different." (Any additional verbal help counts as coaching but exceeds mastery criterion.)

If still stuck (for teaching, not mastery): provide category cue ("Both are tools—what's one difference?") or a forced choice; note as prompted >1.

Reinforcement

Specific praise for accurate pair of statements; tokens/points/brief access early (FR1), then thin.

Bonus for independent (0 prompts) or quality=3 responses.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Within the task, teach the client to request support instead of saying "I don't know":
"More time," "Give me choices," "Repeat/rephrase," "What category is it?"

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

If latency rises or "IDK" appears: point to the mat; offer A/B choice for similarity source (category vs. function); keep tone neutral.

Provide a minimal success path after the single prompt: therapist states one similarity; client supplies the difference (or vice versa). Reinforce, then try a fresh pair for independence.

Tacting Emotions

The client will identify their emotions without using profane language when they are frustrated, angry, sad etc. with 2 verbal prompts

Target Behavior: Identify Emotions Without Profanity (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

Emotion labeling without profanity is defined as the client naming their current feeling (e.g., "I'm frustrated/sad/angry/anxious") and, if needed, adding a brief context or request (e.g., "because the work is hard—can I get help?") without using profane language.
 Count a successful response when, within 60 seconds of a visible trigger/sign of distress or within 10 seconds of a prompt, the client:
States an emotion word (or selects it via AAC/emotion card), and

Avoids any words on the team's "no-swear list" (defined by caregivers/setting), and

Keeps the statement brief and calm (≤10 seconds; voice at classroom level 2-3).

Includes (examples):
"I'm mad and need a minute."

Pointing to "angry" on an emotion chart + showing a Break card.

"I'm disappointed I can't go first."

Does not include:
Using profanity, slurs, or insulting labels ("This is [expletive]," "You're [expletive]").

Yelling, threats, or property aggression (score separately).

Quoting profanity in reading/performances is N/A if context-appropriate and pre-taught.

Measurement
Opportunities: Any observable sign of strong emotion (tense voice, tearing up, clenched fists, refusal talk) or when the client indicates upset.

Score each opportunity: Independent (0 prompts), Prompted (response after ≤2 brief verbal prompts), Not met (>2 prompts, profanity used, or no label).

Track: emotion named, presence/absence of profanity, latency to label, and any follow-up request.

Probable Function (of profanity)

Usually automatic expression/venting, attention, or escape/control during frustration. Replacement skill = functional communication of emotion and need.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine — "F-B-N" (Feel → Because → Need)

Feel: "I feel ___."

Because (optional): "because ___."

Need (one request): "I need ___ (help/break/clarify/water)."
 Practice via modeling → role-play → real moments, using the same three-part script. Offer a wallet card/AAC button for F-B-N.

Visuals & Tools

Emotion scale (1-5) or feelings chart with 6-10 core emotions.

Request Menu (Help, Break 1-2 min, Choice, Clarify, Water, Quiet space).

Voice meter (0-5); "calm voice" = 2-3.

"No-swear words" visual (private) + acceptable substitutes ("Ugh," "Shoot," "Dang," squeeze ball).

Antecedent Modifications

Pre-correct before known triggers: "If it's tough, use F-B-N."

First-Then, timers, chunking tasks; right-size difficulty.

Provide fidgets/noise reduction, seat options, and choice within tasks.

Prompting & Fading (max 2 verbals)

Nonverbal first (doesn't count): point to emotion chart/voice meter, tap the script card.

Verbal 1: "Use your feeling word."

Verbal 2: "Finish with what you need."
 Then wait 5-10 seconds silently. After any prompted success, run a transfer trial later with longer wait time for independence.

Reinforcement

DRA/FCT: Immediate, labeled praise + token/point for clean (no-profanity) emotion labeling and F-B-N use (bonus for independent, quick ≤5 s).

Keep reinforcement for replacements stronger than attention to profanity.

Exchange: brief choice time, preferred activity, helper role, or "problem-solve ticket."

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Primary—F-B-N without profanity (≤2 prompts): Upon frustration/anger/sadness indicators, the client states an emotion and (optionally) a brief reason + request, with no profanity, in ≥80% of opportunities with ≤2 verbal prompts across 3 sessions.

Quick Label + Break: "I'm overwhelmed—break please," transitions to a 30-120 s break and returns within 10 s of timer, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Substitute Phrase: Uses preapproved exclamations ("Shoot," "Ugh") instead of swears while still labeling the feeling, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Clarify-Then-Label: If confused, asks one clarifying question, then labels feeling ("I'm confused and frustrated"), ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Validate → Guide (≤7 words): "I hear you—use your feeling words."

When-Then: "When you label it clean, then we can problem-solve."

Planned ignore of mild profanity; immediately reinforce the pivot to clean labeling.

Offer brief regulation options (water, wall push-ups, breathe x4) if voice rises; re-cue F-B-N.

Keep language short/neutral; avoid debate during high arousal.

Safety first: if escalation continues, follow site plan; debrief privately using F-B-N once calm.

Sorting

Conflict

Conflict Resolution

The Client Will Use Basic Conflict Resolution Strategies, Like Asking for Help from an Adult, in a Disagreement with Peers With a Verbal Prompt

Target Behavior: Use basic conflict-resolution strategies (e.g., ask an adult for help) with ≤1 verbal prompt

Operational Definition

During a disagreement with one or more peers (e.g., over turns, rules, space, or opinions), the client will, within 10 seconds of the disagreement becoming apparent or an adult cue, use at least one pre-taught conflict-resolution strategy calmly and with no more than one verbal prompt. Accepted strategies:
Acknowledge + I-statement: "I feel ___ because ___; I want ___."

Offer a solution: propose turn-taking, timer, trade, rock-paper-scissors, or "agree to disagree."

Ask for adult help: "Excuse me, can you help us solve this?"

Respectful exit: "I'm taking a short break," then step away safely.

Success = the client uses any one of the above strategies with a calm voice/body (no yelling, insults, threats, aggression, property destruction, elopement), accepts the outcome or adult direction, and does not return to arguing for ≥30 seconds after the exchange.
Prompts allowed: ≤ 1 brief verbal prompt total (e.g., "Use your conflict steps."). Gestural/visual cues (pointing to card) do not count toward the one prompt.
Non-examples: arguing, speaking over peers, name-calling, repeated bargaining after a decision, seeking peers to "take sides," refusing to let the topic end, needing ≥2 verbal prompts.
Measurement: Each peer disagreement (natural or contrived) = 1 opportunity. Record Success (Y/N), verbal prompts (0/1), strategy used (I-statement / solution / ask adult / exit), and brief notes. Aim for 3-5 opportunities per session; contrive if none occur.
Mastery criterion: ≥80% successful opportunities per session across 4 of 5 consecutive sessions. Then fade to independent (0 verbal prompts) or raise criterion to demonstrate two strategies before seeking adult help.
Probable Function

Problem responses in conflict often contact attention/control, tangible access, or escape from uncomfortable interactions. Verify via brief ABC notes to tailor supports.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Skill (BST: Instruction → Modeling → Rehearsal → Feedback)

Conflict script ("PACE-S") card:
 Pause (3 breaths) → Acknowledge ("I hear you…") → Communicate (I-statement) → Explore solutions (timer/trade/turns) → Seek adult help if needed.

Sentence stems (post on card/AAC):
"I feel ___ because ___."

"Can we use the timer/trade/turns?"

"Let's agree to disagree."

"Excuse me, can you help us solve this?"

Role-play 2-3 short scenarios per day; rotate peers/topics; reinforce immediately.

Antecedent Supports

Visuals: pocket PACE-S card; solution menu icons (timer, turns, trade, RPS).

Clear routines: shared rules for games/materials; visible timers.

Noncontingent attention: quick check-ins to reduce attention-seeking via arguing.

Priming: before high-risk blocks, review the steps and point out the solution menu.

Reinforcement

DRA: Praise and deliver natural outcomes for using a strategy (e.g., start timer, adult mediates right away).

Behavior-specific praise: "You used an I-statement and suggested turns—great problem solving."

Token/points: 1 token per successful opportunity; bonus for independent (0-prompt) use.

Group contingency (optional): team earns a bonus when all members use one strategy in an activity.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Ask for Help (primary example): Client says, "Excuse me, can you help us?" within 10 s of a stuck disagreement, ≤1 verbal prompt, in 80% of opportunities.

I-Statement + Solution: Client uses an I-statement and proposes one solution within 10 s, ≤1 prompt, in 80% of opportunities.

Respectful Exit: Client requests/announces a brief break from the interaction instead of arguing, ≤1 prompt, in 80% of need moments.

Acceptance/Closure: After adult/peer decision, client says "Okay" or "Let's agree to disagree," and does not re-engage in arguing for ≥30 s in 80% of opportunities.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Low-profile cueing: point to PACE-S card → single verbal prompt ("Use your conflict steps") → wait up to 10 s.

Limit attention to arguing; reinforce the first use of a strategy.

Binary choice if rising: "Offer a solution or ask for help."

If unsafe behavior emerges: follow the site safety plan; after calm, run a brief repair trial (model → client uses one step → immediate praise/natural outcome).

Consistency: Access to attention/materials follows strategy use, not arguing.

Social

Interacting with Others

Initiating

The client will initiate a game or activity with a peer by approaching peer and asking "do you want to play?" in various social settings with 1 verbal prompt 

Target Behavior: 
 Initiating a game or activity with a peer in various social settings.
&nbsp;
Motivating Operation:
 Promoting social inclusion, peer engagement, and cooperation through initiating shared activities.
Antecedent Procedures:
 1. Place the client in a setting where games or activities are readily available, such as a playground, classroom, or social skills group.
 2. Ensure a peer is present and available for interaction.
 3. Inform the client that the aim is to initiate a game or activity with a peer during the session or activity.
&nbsp;
Discriminative Stimulus:
 A peer is present and not actively engaged in another game or activity, creating a natural opportunity for initiation.
Appropriate Response:
 Within 30 seconds of the SD, the client will initiate a game or activity with a peer by making an appropriate suggestion, such as "Do you want to play tag?" or "Let's build something with these blocks," to be considered a successful trial.
&nbsp;
Error Correction Procedures:
 1. **Modeling**: If the client does not initiate, the therapist models an appropriate way to suggest a game or activity.
 2. **Prompting**: Use subtle verbal or non-verbal cues like a nudge or a whisper, "You can ask them to play," to encourage initiation.
 3. **Immediate Feedback**: Provide specific feedback on the appropriateness or timing of the initiation.
 4. **Repetition**: Offer another opportunity to initiate a game or activity with the same or a different peer.
&nbsp;
Criteria for Mastery: 
 The client will initiate a game or activity within 30 seconds of the SD in 8 out of 10 trials across 3 consecutive data collection sessions. Mastery should be generalized across different peers and social settings.
&nbsp;
This ABA goal aims to improve the client's social skills by encouraging them to initiate games or activities with peers, which is essential for developing friendships and social interaction.
Instructional Notes

MO:
 Promoting social inclusion, peer engagement, and cooperation through initiating shared activities.
 
 Antecedent Procedures:
Place the client in a setting where games or activities are readily available, such as a playground, classroom, or social skills group.
 2. Ensure a peer is present and available for interaction.
 3. Inform the client that the aim is to initiate a game or activity with a peer during the session or activity.
&nbsp;

 
 SD:
 A peer is present and not actively engaged in another game or activity, creating a natural opportunity for initiation.
 
 Response:
 Within 30 seconds of the SD, the client will initiate a game or activity with a peer by making an appropriate suggestion, such as "Do you want to play tag?" or "Let's build something with these blocks," to be considered a successful trial.
 
 Prompting:
 visual prompt,verbal prompt,gestural prompt,modeling
 
 Error Correction:
 1. **Modeling**: If the client does not initiate, the therapist models an appropriate way to suggest a game or activity. 2. **Prompting**: Use subtle verbal or non-verbal cues like a nudge or a whisper, "You can ask them to play," to encourage initiation. 3. **Immediate Feedback**: Provide specific feedback on the appropriateness or timing of the initiation. 4. **Repetition**: Offer another opportunity to initiate a game or activity with the same or a different peer.
 
 Reinforcement:
 differential social reinforcement,socially mediated reinforcement

The Client Will Identify 2 Ways to Respectfully initiate conversations with peers with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior

Increase identifying two respectful ways to initiate a conversation with a peer with ≤2 verbal prompts.
Operational Definition

Given a prompt (e.g., "Name two respectful ways to start talking to a peer right now," or a picture/brief scenario), the client states or selects two distinct initiation strategies (speech/AAC/sign/choice cards) within 20-30 seconds.
 A respectful initiation strategy must include a polite attention cue (e.g., name + "excuse me") or a readiness check ("Is now a good time?").
Examples that count (any two, distinct):
Greeting + Name + Topic/Question: "Hi, Maya—how was art?"

Ask to Join/Sit/Play: "Can I join your game?" / "Is it okay if I sit here?"

Compliment + Question: "Nice drawing—what are you making?"

Shared-Activity Comment + Question: "I'm reading that book too—who's your favorite character?"

Offer/Request Help to Connect: "Need a hand with that?" / "Can you show me how you did that?"

Readiness Check + Topic: "Do you have a minute to talk about the project?"

Does not include: calling out from across the room, interrupting without an attention cue, criticisms/sarcasm, one-word "hey" with no follow-up, or adult-directed initiations (this goal is peer-focused).
Measurement

Primary: Session success (Yes/No) = client identifies two respectful ways for at least one trial per session with ≤2 verbal prompts.

Secondary: prompts used (0/1/2), latency to produce each way (sec), modality (speech/AAC/sign), and context (classwork, recess, group).
 Onset/offset: Onset when the prompt/scenario ends; offset when two qualifying ways are given or 30s elapse after the last allowed prompt.

Probable Function

This is a skill-acquisition task; non-responding/"I don't know" often functions as escape from language/social demand or reflects retrieval/organization needs.
Proactive Strategies

Antecedent supports
Post a 2-Step Starter: Attention cue → Opener (e.g., "Excuse me, [Name]… Can I join?").

Provide a Peer Opener Menu (icons for the six examples above).

Prime likely contexts ("At recess, which two could you use?").

Ensure AAC has quick buttons: Excuse me [Name] • Is now a good time? • Can I join? • Nice ___ • Can you help me?

Teaching & Prompting

Model-Lead-Test: therapist models two ways for a scenario → rehearse together → client names two new ways.

Least-to-most (max 2 verbal prompts):
Point to Opener Menu / 2-Step Starter (nonverbal).

Verbal Prompt 1: "Name two respectful ways."

Verbal Prompt 2 (scaffold): "Start with an attention cue, then your opener."

Vary contexts (quiet work, group play, transition) to promote generalization.

Reinforcement

Specific praise for two accurate, distinct strategies; brief token/point/access early (FR1), then thin.

Bonus for independent (0-1 prompt) responses and for tailoring to the scenario (e.g., using a readiness check when peer looks busy).

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

When stuck, teach the client to request support instead of "I don't know":
"More time, please." / "Choices?" / "Repeat the scenario."

Use the menu to point to two options and then say them.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

If latency rises or avoidance appears, keep tone neutral; offer A/B choice ("Join or Compliment?"), then ask for a second way.

Provide a minimal success path if still stuck: therapist supplies one correct way; client selects a second from two options (note as prompted).

The client will offer a compliment to a peer with a verbal prompt

Target Behavior: Offer a Compliment to a Peer (≤1 verbal prompt)

Operational Definition

Peer complimenting is defined as the client facing a peer, using a brief, positive statement about the peer's work/effort/appearance (non-sensitive)/behavior, and pausing for a response. A response counts when the client:
Uses a taught script (e.g., "[Name], I like your __," "Great job on __," "Thanks for helping with __"),

Speaks in a calm, friendly tone (voice level 2-3),

Is specific to something present or recent, and

Occurs independently or within 10 seconds of one brief verbal prompt (e.g., "Give a compliment").

Includes (examples):
"Liam, I like your drawing—your shading looks cool."

"Nice teamwork on the puzzle, Ava."

"Thanks for passing the ball, Noah."

Does not include:
Generic or mechanical phrases repeated back-to-back ("Nice. Nice. Nice.").

Sarcasm/teasing; comments on private body features/sensitive topics.

Compliments directed to adults (target is peers) or delivered while interrupting.

Measurement
Opportunities: Natural social windows (arrival, centers, transitions, group work, recess).

Event recording per session: mark Independent, Prompted (≤1), or Not met.

Track: peer name, topic of compliment, latency from prompt (goal ≤10 s).

Probable Function

Desired outcome = social attention/connection and positive peer engagement. Inappropriate or absent compliments may reflect skill deficits, anxiety, or competing attention-seeking behaviors.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine — "N-S-P" (Name → Specific compliment → Pause)

Name the peer ("Hi, Maya—").

Specific compliment (work/effort/kindness/fashion non-sensitive/item).

Pause and smile; wait ≥3 seconds for response.

Use modeling → role-play → practice with peers; provide video/self-modeling if available.
Visuals & Tools

Compliment Menu card with safe categories: Work/Effort, Teamwork/Kindness, Accessories/Non-sensitive clothing, Skills/Play.

Sentence frames: "I like your __ because __," "Great job on __," "Thanks for __."

AAC buttons for "Nice job," "I like your __," "Thanks for __."

Antecedent Modifications

Arrange peer partners/buddies and shared tasks that make compliments natural (art share, center rotations).

Pre-load 2-3 contextual compliments before social windows ("What could you say about Diego's Lego build?").

Signal green-light times: "Now's a good time for compliments."

Prompting & Fading (max 1 verbal prompt)

Nonverbal first: point to the Compliment Menu or give a thumbs-up (doesn't use the verbal prompt).

Verbal (single): "Give a compliment." Then wait 5-10 s.

After a prompted success, run a transfer trial later (new peer/opportunity, aim for independence).

Reinforcement

DRA: Labeled praise + token/point for appropriate compliments (bonus for independent).

Start FR1, thin to VR2-3 as consistent.

Exchange: pick next game/partner, line leader, sticker.

Boundaries & Safety

Teach respectful topics (avoid body/weight/private features).

If a peer doesn't respond, client uses a graceful exit: "Okay, see you," then moves on (reinforce).

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB) / Skill Targets

Primary—N-S-P with ≤1 prompt: Client delivers one specific peer compliment and pauses for response within 10 s of identification/prompt, in ≥80% of opportunities across 3 sessions.

Independent Compliment: By mastery phase, ≥50% of compliments occur without any prompt.

Thank-You Response: When complimented back, client replies "Thanks" or smiles/nods within 3 s, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Repair Strategy: If the compliment lands awkwardly, client uses "I meant that kindly" or changes topic, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

If the client freezes, offer two-choice cue: "Work or kindness compliment?"

Planned ignore of off-topic/sarcastic comments; immediately reinforce the pivot to a genuine compliment.

When-Then: "When you share one compliment, then you pick the game."

If a peer reacts negatively, coach graceful exit and privately praise the attempt.

The client will utilize a shared interest to initiate a conversation with a peer with 2 verbal prompts

Target Behavior: Use a shared interest to initiate a conversation with a peer (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

When within conversation distance of a peer, the client will initiate by doing all of the following within 10 seconds and with no more than two verbal prompts total:
Gain attention appropriately (e.g., "Hi [Name]," light wave, or AAC attention button).

Reference a shared interest (pre-identified topic/activity/item both enjoy; e.g., "We both like Minecraft/Roblox/soccer/dinosaurs").

Add a connected question or inviting comment (e.g., "What level are you on?" "Want to build a goal together?").

An initiation counts as successful when the three-step chain occurs with calm body/voice, directed to the peer, and without maladaptive behavior (e.g., yelling, grabbing, insults). One initiation per opportunity.
Prompts allowed (max 2 verbal total): e.g., Prompt 1: "Use a shared interest," Prompt 2: "Ask a question." Gestural/visual prompts don't count toward the two.
Non-examples: generic "hi" with no topic; off-topic monologue; asking unrelated for tangibles; repeated prompts (>2); initiating after a disruptive behavior.
Measurement: Provide 3-5 opportunities per session (natural or contrived). Record Success (Y/N), # verbal prompts (0/1/2), latency (s), topic used, notes.
Mastery criterion: ≥80% successful opportunities per session across 4 of 5 consecutive sessions (then fade to ≤1 prompt, then independent; increase complexity/novel peers).
Probable Function

Builds social attention/connection and access to joint interaction; replaces attention-seeking through blurting or intrusive bids.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Skill (BST: Instruction → Modeling → Rehearsal → Feedback)

3-Step script (card/AAC page): 👋 Greet → ⭐ Shared interest → ❓ Question/invite.

Sentence stems:
Greet: "Hi [Name]!"

Shared interest: "We both like ___ / I saw you play ___."

Question/invite: "What's your favorite ___?" "Want to ___ together?"

Role-play 2-3 quick scenarios/day; rotate peers and topics; immediate praise.

Antecedent Supports

Shared-Interest Map: brief list/icons of classmates' interests; review before recess/centers.

Conversation starters ring or AAC quick-phrases.

Prime before target blocks: "Pick one person + one shared topic."

Set up contrived opportunities: staff cues a peer to display the shared item/topic.

Reinforcement

Natural social access right after successful initiation (peer engages).

Behavior-specific praise: "You greeted, mentioned soccer, and asked a question—great start!"

Tokens/points per success; bonus for independent initiations.

Thin tokens gradually as natural peer reinforcement maintains behavior.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Initiation Chain (primary): Greet → Shared interest → Question/invite within 10s, ≤2 prompts, in 80% of opportunities.

If peer is busy: Use attention mand first ("Excuse me, [Name]") then the initiation, ≤2 prompts, 80%.

If unsure: Client uses a clarification request ("What do you like to play?") ≤1 prompt, 80%.

Sustain brief exchange: After the peer responds, client adds one relevant follow-up ("Cool—me too") in 70-80% of successes.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Low-profile cues: point to starter card → brief cue ("Shared interest… now ask").

If off-topic/monologue starts: gesture + 3 words max ("Tie to shared topic"). Reinforce the next on-topic bid.

If rejection occurs: coach a respectful exit ("Okay, maybe later") and praise coping; offer a new peer/topic.

Repair trial: Immediately run a short, successful redo (10-20s) after any miss to contact reinforcement.

Interacting with Peers

The client will accept a compliment from a peer/adult graciously such as with a thank you with a verbal prompt

Target Behavior

Increase accepting a compliment from a peer/adult graciously (e.g., saying "Thank you") with ≤1 verbal prompt.
Operational Definition

When a peer/adult offers a genuine compliment (about work, behavior, appearance, effort, etc.), the client:
Produces a brief, polite response (speech/AAC/sign) such as "Thank you," "Thanks," "I appreciate it," optionally adding a short comment ("Thanks—I worked hard on it") within 5-7 seconds, and

Directs it to the giver (turns body/eyes or uses name).

Counts as success when: response occurs within the time window with ≤1 verbal prompt (e.g., "Say 'thank you'").
 Does not include: ignoring, arguing/rejecting ("No I didn't," "It's not good"), self-putdowns, changing the subject to avoid responding, sarcasm, or whispering inaudibly without repair.
Measurement

Primary: Daily/session success (Yes/No) — at least 1 qualifying acceptance.

Secondary: Prompt level (0/1), latency (s), modality (speech/AAC/sign), context (peer/adult; setting), and quality (0-3):
0 = no/negative response; 1 = polite word only; 2 = polite word + name/eye contact; 3 = polite word + brief positive comment/reciprocal ("Thank you—nice job on yours too").
 Onset/offset: Onset at end of compliment; offset at client response or after 7s post-prompt.

Probable Function

Desired behavior is maintained by positive social attention/affiliation. Rejections/deflections may function for escape from social attention, anxiety reduction, or reflect skill/processing needs.
Proactive Strategies

Post the S²T Routine: See the compliment → Smile (or neutral face) → Thank ("Thank you, [Name]").

Prime likely moments (work check-ins, hand-offs).

Ensure AAC quick buttons: Thank you • Thanks • I appreciate it • Thanks for noticing.

Provide a phrase menu card with 3-4 acceptable responses.

Pair with supportive peers; reduce noise; practice first with contrived compliments.

Teaching & Prompting

Model-Lead-Test: therapist models → practice together → client solo.

Least-to-most prompts (max 1 verbal): gesture to card → single verbal ("Say 'thank you'").

Allow 5-7s think time before prompting.

Reinforcement: labeled praise ("Nice, quick 'thank you' to Alex") + token/brief access early; thin over time.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

AAC/Sign + brief nod if speech is hard.

Repair after missed window: "I forgot to say it—thank you."

If uncomfortable with topic: "Thanks—let's talk about something else," (still counts if polite).

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

If client argues/puts self down, neutral repair: "Try the polite response: 'Thank you.'"

If freezing/avoidance: point to S²T card; offer A/B choice ("'Thank you' or 'I appreciate it'?"); reduce audience.

The Client Will Acknowledge a Peer's Comment or Action with a Nod or Brief Verbal Response with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior: Acknowledge a Peer's Comment/Action (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

Peer acknowledgment is defined as the client giving a brief, respectful signal that they received a peer's comment or action within 3-5 seconds. An acknowledgment is any one of the following:
Nonverbal: a single nod, thumbs-up, brief smile with eye orientation toward the peer.

Brief verbal (≤5 words, level 2-3 voice): "Okay," "Thanks," "Got it," "Cool," "Nice pass," "I see."

A response counts when it occurs after:
A peer speaks directly to the client (greeting, comment, request, question), or

A peer does something for/with the client (hands materials, invites to play, assists).

Does not count:
Ignoring for >5 seconds, talking over the peer, sarcastic tone, or repeating back the peer's words without meaning.

Long replies (>10 seconds) that shift to a different topic before acknowledging.

Situations where the client clearly did not hear/see the peer or during safety-critical moments (score N/A).

Prompt allowance: Up to two brief verbal prompts (e.g., "Acknowledge," "Say 'okay'/'thanks'"). Nonverbal cues (point to cue card) do not use up the two prompts.
Measurement

Opportunity: Each time a peer directs a comment/action to the client during social/working time.

Scoring per opportunity:
Independent = acknowledgment within 3-5 s, 0 prompts.

Prompted = acknowledgment after ≤2 verbal prompts.

Not met = no acknowledgment, >2 prompts, or negative response.

Primary metric: % of opportunities with acknowledgment (independent + prompted).

Secondary: % independent; latency (s) to acknowledgment.

Probable Function (when acknowledgment is missing)

Often skill/fluency deficit, attention to task (competing), anxiety/uncertainty, or escape from social exchange. Replacing with a quick, low-demand acknowledgment supports access to peer attention/connection appropriately.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine — "Look → Signal → Return"

Look at the peer (orient head/eyes).

Signal one acknowledgment (nod or ≤5-word phrase).

Return to task or continue the brief exchange as appropriate.

Model → Role-play → Practice across typical moments (greetings, hand-offs, invites).

Provide a mini "Ack Menu" card with icons (nod 👍, "Okay," "Thanks," "Got it").

Antecedent Supports

Seat/proximity that makes peers easy to see/hear.

Preload likely acknowledgments before transitions ("If Sam hands you the marker, try 'Thanks'").

Peers taught to pause 3 seconds after their comment to allow acknowledgment.

Prompting & Fading (max 2 verbals)

Nonverbal first: point to the Ack Menu / gesture a nod (doesn't use a prompt).

Verbal 1: "Acknowledge."

Verbal 2: "Say 'okay' or nod."

After any prompted success, run a transfer trial later with a longer silent wait for independence.

Reinforcement

DRA: Labeled praise + token/point for timely nod/brief response (bonus for independent or <3 s latency).

Start FR1, thin to VR2-3 as consistent.

Small exchanges: choose partner, first turn in a game, sticker.

Boundaries & Alternatives

Teach "Didn't hear—please repeat?" when uncertain.

Teach a polite defer if busy: "One sec," (hold up 1 finger) then acknowledge.

Limit overuse: one signal per peer comment/action is sufficient.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Primary—Timely Acknowledgment (≤2 prompts): Following a peer's directed comment/action, the client orients and gives one nod or ≤5-word response within 3-5 s, in ≥80% of opportunities with ≤2 verbal prompts across 3 sessions.

Clarify-Then-Acknowledge: If unclear, the client asks, "Can you repeat?" then acknowledges, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Polite Defer: Client signals "One sec" (gesture/phrase) and then acknowledges within 10 s, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Brief cue, then wait (3-5 s): "Acknowledge."

Two-choice redirect: "Nod or say 'okay.'"

Planned ignore of minor off-topic replies; immediately reinforce the pivot to a correct acknowledgment.

If dysregulated, allow a 30-60 s micro-break and practice a quick repair ("Sorry, thanks").

The client will identify one interest he shares with a peer with 2 verbal prompts

Target Behavior: Identify one shared interest with a peer (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

When within conversation distance of a peer, the client will state one accurate shared interest (topic, activity, item, team, game, show, hobby) using speech or an approved modality (AAC/sign/point to label) within 10 seconds of an adult cue or a natural opportunity, with no more than two verbal prompts total.
A response counts as successful when all are met:
Accuracy: The identified interest is something the peer actually likes, confirmed by prior inventory/observation or the peer's immediate agreement (e.g., "Yeah, I like soccer").

Clarity & direction: Statement is clear and directed toward the peer (e.g., "We both like Minecraft," "You like drawing—I do too").

Calm body/voice and no maladaptive behavior (e.g., grabbing, teasing).

Prompt limit: Occurs independently or with ≤2 brief verbal prompts (gestural/visual cues don't count).

Non-examples: guessing multiple unrelated interests; listing the client's interests without linking to the peer; requiring ≥3 verbal prompts; inaccurate match that the peer rejects.
Opportunities & Measurement: Provide 3-5 opportunities per session (natural or contrived). For each, record Success (Y/N), # verbal prompts (0/1/2), latency to respond (s), and topic named.
Mastery criterion: ≥80% successful opportunities per session across 4 of 5 consecutive sessions. (Then fade to ≤1 prompt, then independent.)
Probable Function

Builds social connection/attention and initiates peer interaction; replaces attention-seeking or awkward bids with a skillful opener.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Micro-Skill (BST: Instruction → Modeling → Rehearsal → Feedback)

Mini-script:
Notice peer's interest (from list/clues).

Say the match: "We both like ___."

(Optional bridge) Ask one related question: "What's your favorite ___?"

Model with quick examples, then role-play 2-3 times using real classmates' interests; give behavior-specific praise.

Antecedent Supports

Classmate Interest Map: simple chart (name → 2-3 interests) or photo icons; review before recess/centers.

Visual starters ring / AAC page: "We both like ___," "I saw you ___," "You like ___—me too."

Environmental clues: jerseys, stickers, books, club gear—teach the client to scan for clues.

Priming: before social blocks, cue: "Pick one person + one shared topic."

Prompting (fade quickly)

Gesture to interest map/icon → visual cue card → Verbal Prompt 1 ("Find one thing you both like") → wait up to 10 s → Verbal Prompt 2 ("Say 'We both like ___.'").

Reinforcement

Natural social access: immediate peer engagement when possible.

Praise: "Nice match—'We both like Legos.'"

Tokens/points per success; bonus for independent matches.

Generalization games: quick scavenger hunt—"Find someone who likes ___ too."

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Shared-Interest Identification (primary): Client names one accurate shared interest within 10 s, ≤2 verbal prompts, in 80% of opportunities.

If unsure: Client asks a clarifying question—"Do you like ___?"—then states the match, ≤1 extra prompt, 80%.

If peer is busy: Client first gains attention appropriately ("Excuse me, [Name]") before stating the match, ≤2 prompts, 80%.

Follow-up bridge: After the match, client adds one related question/comment ("What set do you build?") in 70-80% of successes.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Low-profile coaching: point to map → whisper cue ("Find one thing in common") → brief second cue ("Say 'We both like…'").

If the guess is wrong: keep it neutral—"Try again; check the list or ask." Run a quick repair trial to contact success.

If peer declines/rejects: coach respectful coping ("Okay, maybe later") and offer a new peer/topic; praise coping.

Avoid over-prompting: If a 3rd verbal prompt would be needed, end the trial, model a correct example, then run a short errorless redo.

The Client will play fairly and follow the rules in a game

Target Behavior

Increase playing fairly and following the rules during games with ≤2 verbal prompts.
Operational Definition

During a structured game (board, card, playground, or digital), the client:
Follows posted rules and turn order (uses the correct number of moves/rolls/spins; uses materials as intended).

Respects outcomes (accepts wins/losses, points, penalties) and uses polite language (e.g., "good game," "nice move").

Avoids cheating behaviors (e.g., moving extra spaces, changing rules mid-game, peeking at hidden info, taking extra turns, interfering with others' turns).

Counts as success for a session/game when all are true:
No more than 1 minor rule reminder needed per round/period and ≤2 verbal prompts overall.

No cheating behaviors; disputes are handled using the taught rule-check routine.

Client completes the game or designated portion without leaving due to frustration.

Does not include: rule errors caused by unclear/complex rules, missing materials, peers cheating, or accommodations not yet taught (these should be clarified first).
Measurement

Primary: % of turns with full rule-following (goal ≥80%).

Secondary: number of prompts used (0/1/2), rule violations (minor vs. major), sportsmanship acts (e.g., "good game," compliment), and if a dispute occurred/was resolved via routine.

Intensity (highest per game):
1 - Minor: needs a brief reminder; game continues.

2 - Moderate: disputes/arguing ≥10s, brief reset needed.

3 - Severe: quits game, aggression, or repeated cheating → stop/reset per plan.
 Onset/offset: Onset at game start; offset at game end or designated time/rounds.

Probable Function

Rule-breaking/disputes may serve Access to tangibles/winning, Attention, Escape from waiting/losing, or Control/Autonomy. (Confirm with ABC data.)
Proactive Strategies

Set up for success
Rule Preview Card (one-page visual): Goal, How to win, Turn order, 3 key rules, What if a problem?

Model & practice 1-2 sample turns before real play.

Choose right-fit games (short rounds, simple rules) and increase complexity gradually.

Pre-correction: "Play by the card, use 'rule-check' if unsure, and say 'good game' at the end."

Structure turns: visible My Turn/Your Turn marker; timers for turn length (30-60s).

Roles: assign scorekeeper/referee (rotates) to reduce power struggles.

Reinforcement menu: points/tokens for each fully fair turn; bonus for independent sportsmanship.

Teaching & Prompting

Core routines to teach (and post visually)
Start Routine: Read rules → Name goal → Do 1 practice turn.

Fair Turn Routine: Wait → Take your turn by the rules → Move the marker → Say "Your turn."

Rule-Check Routine: Pause → Ask, "What's the rule?" → Check card → Agree and play on.

Finish Routine: Say "Good game" → Quick debrief (what went well).

Prompting hierarchy (max 2 verbal prompts)
Nonverbal cue (point to rule card/turn marker) → Verbal 1 ("Use the rule-check") → Verbal 2 ("Take your turn by the card, then 'Your turn'").

Fade prompts quickly; aim for gesture-only cues.

Reinforcement
DRA: token/point for each fair turn; specific praise ("Nice rule-check and quick 'Your turn'").

Jackpot for full game with 0-1 prompts and positive closing.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Teach these instead of cheating/arguing:
Ask for clarification: "What's the rule?" / "Can we check the card?"

Request a pause/break: "I need 1 minute; I'll be back."

Request a do-over token (limit 1 per game) for honest mistakes.

Express feelings respectfully: "I'm frustrated—I still play by the rule."

Compromise option: coin flip or draw card when rules are ambiguous.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Early signs (grabbing pieces, rushing turns, voice rising):
Calm, brief cue: point to marker; "Use your rule-check." Offer A/B choice (continue now vs. 1-min pause).

Reduce stimulation (fewer observers, quieter table), shorten remaining round.

If a violation/dispute occurs:
Neutral reset: "Pause. Rule-check." (counts as prompt) → read rule → apply it → resume.

If arguing persists: time-limited statement ("10 seconds to state your view, then we follow the card").

If escalation continues: take a brief reset break, return with a do-over or simplified rule.

The client will identify a realistic reaction to a contrived social initiation with 2 verbal prompts

Target Behavior: Identify a Realistic Reaction to a Contrived Social Initiation (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

Identifying a realistic reaction is defined as the client, when the technician delivers a contrived social initiation (scripted greeting, invitation, request, correction, tease, or boundary test), stating out loud what an age-appropriate, respectful response would be or briefly demonstrating it in role-play within 10 seconds, with no more than two brief verbal prompts.
A response counts when it:
Matches the situation's intent (e.g., invitation → accept/decline politely; request → comply/offer alternative).

Uses a calm tone (voice level 2-3) and brief phrasing (≤10 seconds).

Is safe, respectful, and context-appropriate (school/clinic norms).

Does not count:
Responses that are off-topic, disrespectful, escalatory, or unsafe.

Needing >2 verbal prompts, or latency >10 seconds after the final prompt.

Literal reading of options without selecting/owning a choice.

Measurement
Each scripted initiation = 1 opportunity/trial (aim 5-10 per session across types).

Score: Independent, Prompted (≤2), or Not met.

Optional accuracy rubric (0-2):
2 = realistic + respectful + matched to intent;

1 = partly realistic (minor mismatch/wording);

0 = unrealistic/off-topic.

Track scenario type, latency, and whether the client stated vs demonstrated the reaction.

Probable Function

Skill acquisition (social problem-solving/interpretation). Failures may reflect uncertainty, anxiety, or escape from social demands. Replacement = rapid select-and-say of a matched response.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine — "See → Intent → Choose"

See the initiation (What happened?).

Intent (What do they want? invite/help/feedback/joke/boundary?).

Choose one realistic response from the menu; say or demo it.

Use modeling → think-aloud → role-play → feedback with increasing variability.
Response Menu (visual)

Greeting → "Hi, [name]!" / "Nice to see you."

Invitation → "Yes, I'll join," or "No thanks, maybe later."

Request to share/help → "Sure, here you go," or "In two minutes, please."

Correction/feedback → "Okay, got it—thanks."

Accidental bump/apology → "Are you okay?" / "Thanks for saying sorry."

Tease/minor bother → Ignore + move, or "Please stop," or "I'm going to play over there."

Boundary test → "No thanks; I'm not comfortable with that."

Sentence Frames

"I would say, '___.'"

"A realistic reaction is to __ because __."

Antecedent Supports

Present clear, brief scenarios (picture/video/one-sentence).

Start with high-predictability initiations, then mix types.

Pre-teach tone and length expectations; provide a small choice of responses.

Prompting & Fading (max 2 verbals)

Nonverbal first (point to menu/intent icon; does not use a prompt).

Verbal 1: "What's a realistic response?"

Verbal 2: "Pick from the menu and say it."
 After success, run a transfer trial (new scenario; longer quiet wait) to build independence.

Reinforcement

DRA: Labeled praise + token/point for realistic, respectful match (bonus for independent or <5 s).

Start FR1, thin to VR2-3 as fluent.

Exchange: choose next scenario/partner, brief preferred activity.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB) / Skill Targets

Primary—Identify Reaction (≤2 prompts): Given a contrived initiation, client states or demonstrates an appropriate reaction within 10 s in ≥80% of trials with ≤2 verbal prompts across 3 sessions.

Explain the Match: After responding, client gives a one-line reason ("They invited me, so I said yes/no politely"), ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Two-Choice Rescue: When stuck, client selects between two staff-offered options, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt (then fade options).

Generalize to Peer Role-play: With a peer actor, client identifies/demos the reaction, ≥70% of trials with ≤2 prompts (advance criterion).

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

If a scenario triggers frustration, offer Pause Card (30-60 s) → resume with an easier type.

Brief cue, then wait (3-5 s): "Realistic response."

Two-choice redirect: "Accept or decline politely?"

Planned ignore of joking/off-topic answers; reinforce the pivot to a realistic response.

Keep feedback specific & neutral: "Matched the invite—nice."

The client will identify and acknowledge a contribution made by a peer to a game or group project with 2 verbal prompts

Target Behavior: Identify and acknowledge a peer's contribution (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

During a cooperative activity (e.g., small-group project, lab, team game), when a peer makes a contribution—an action, idea, resource, fix, or effort—the client will, within 10 seconds, do both of the following:
Identify the specific contribution (name what the peer did), and

Acknowledge it with a brief, respectful statement or gesture directed to the peer.

Acceptable responses (any modality):
Speech/AAC: "Thanks for passing the ball," "Nice idea to add headings," "Good job finding the piece we lost," "I appreciate you checking our work."

Gesture + label: thumbs-up while saying/pressing "Nice pass," nod + AAC "Good idea."

Prompts allowed: Up to two brief verbal prompts total (e.g., Prompt 1: "Name what they did," Prompt 2: "Say thanks."). Gestural/visual cues don't count toward the two.
Non-examples: vague praise without identifying the contribution ("Good job" only); acknowledging oneself; sarcasm/teasing; delayed >10 s; needing ≥3 verbal prompts; acknowledging while engaging in maladaptive behavior (e.g., mocking, eye-rolling).
Opportunities & Measurement

Create 3-5 opportunities per session (natural or contrived).

Record per opportunity: Success (Y/N), Prompts (0/1/2), Latency to respond (s), Context (game/project), Peer contribution named.

Session success goal: ≥80% of opportunities successful.

Mastery criterion: Meets session goal on 4 of 5 consecutive sessions (then fade to ≤1 prompt, then independent).

Probable Function

Builds prosocial communication and peer connection; replaces attention-seeking/competitive talk with prosocial acknowledgment. May also support task engagement and cooperation.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Micro-Skill (model → role-play → in vivo)

Three-step script (card/AAC page):
 NOTICE → NAME the contribution → APPRECIATE
 ("I saw you ___ → That helped because ___ → Thanks!")

Sentence stems:
"Nice [contribution]."

"Thanks for [contribution]—that helped us [result]."

"Good call on [idea]." / "Great [effort]."

Context practice:
Games/Sports: "Nice pass/defense/assist."

Projects: "Thanks for formatting the slides / checking spelling / sharing sources."

STEM/Labs: "Good measurement," "Smart hypothesis."

Run 2-3 quick role-plays per day; rotate peers/topics; give behavior-specific praise.

Antecedent Supports

Contribution menu (icons by setting: pass, idea, fix, share, lead, check).

Prime before activities: "Watch for one thing a teammate does and use your 'Name + Thanks'."

Spotlight moments: Adults briefly narrate peer contributions to seed examples.

Seating/structure: Clear roles (recorder, runner, checker) to make contributions visible.

Reinforcement

Natural social reinforcement: positive peer response; team progress.

Behavior-specific praise: "You named Sam's idea and thanked him—excellent teamwork."

Tokens/points: 1 token per successful acknowledgment; exchange at session end.

Group bonus (optional): extra point when each team member gives one specific acknowledgment.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Name + Thanks (primary): Client identifies a peer's specific contribution and acknowledges it within 10 s, ≤2 verbal prompts, in 80% of opportunities.

If unsure what to name: Client asks a quick clarifier—"What did you change?"—then delivers Name + Thanks, ≤2 prompts, in 80%.

If peer is busy/no eye contact: Client uses an attention mand first ("Excuse me, [Name]"), then Name + Thanks, ≤2 prompts, in 80%.

Link to task: Client adds a short impact statement ("That helped us finish faster") in 60-80% of successes (shape up).

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Low-profile cueing: point to contribution menu → Prompt 1 ("Name what they did") → wait up to 5-10 s → Prompt 2 ("Say thanks").

If client gives vague praise: brief transfer cue—"What exactly did they do?" then reinforce the corrected specific acknowledgment.

If competitiveness/teasing appears: redirect to script, offer binary choice ("Name their help or take a 30-sec reset"), then run a quick repair trial.

Consistency: Reinforce specific, accurate acknowledgments; avoid giving extra attention to non-examples or sarcasm.

Joining Peers

The Client will identify 1 activity that peers may welcome him to join, like a game or conversation with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior

Increase identifying one activity that peers may welcome the client to join (e.g., a game, conversation, group task) with ≤2 verbal prompts.
Operational Definition

Given a natural or contrived social setting (class, recess, group work), the client names or selects one specific peer activity that appears open to joining within 15-20 seconds. A qualifying response includes:
Saying/pointing to one activity (e.g., "four-square," "the Lego table," "talking with Ben and Maya"), and

Indicating it is joinable using at least one readiness cue (e.g., "there's space," "they're looking around," "they said 'who wants to play?'").

Counts as success when: the client identifies one appropriate, joinable activity with ≤2 verbal prompts.
 Does not include: activities that are clearly closed (teacher-led, testing), exclusive/private conversations, or vague answers ("something over there").
Measurement
Primary: Session success (Yes/No) = names one joinable activity with ≤2 prompts.

Secondary: prompts (0/1/2), latency (s), accuracy of readiness cue (accurate/inaccurate), context (class/recess/group).
 Onset/offset: Onset at instruction ("Find something peers might welcome you to join"); offset when one qualifying activity is identified or 30s elapse after the last allowed prompt.

Probable Function

Skill acquisition (scanning/social judgment). Non-responding often functions as escape from social demand or reflects processing/anxiety.
Proactive Strategies

Teach a quick scan routine (S.L.O.T.)
See the room (5-second sweep)

Listen for invites/laughter/neutral chatter

Openings: look for space, open circle, free seat, extra controller/equipment

Target one activity and name it

Visuals & tools
Joinability Cues poster: Open space • Smiles/eye contact • "Wanna play?" • Extra materials • Waiting for turn.

Activity Menu icons (games, conversations, building, drawing, partner work).

AAC quick buttons for common activities and cues ("There's space," "They're inviting people").

Teaching & Prompting

Model-Lead-Test: adult models S.L.O.T. aloud → practice together → client tries in the live setting.

Prompting hierarchy (max 2 verbal):
Gesture/point to visual (nonverbal).

Verbal 1: "Use S.L.O.T.—name one activity you could join."

Verbal 2 (scaffold): "Pick one with space or an invite—which one?"

Start with obvious "green-light" activities; gradually add ambiguous scenes.

Reinforcement

Immediate, specific praise for correct identification ("You spotted open seats at Uno—nice scan!") + token/brief access early; thin over time.

Bonus for independent (0-1 prompt) identifications and accurate cue naming.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

If unsure, teach the client to:
Ask for help: "Point out two options?" / "Is that group open?"

Request choices: "Give me two joinable options."

Use a readiness check script with peers: "Is it okay if I join?" (for later generalization).

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

If the client freezes/IDKs: quiet 5-10s think time, then A/B choice ("Game or conversation?").

If overwhelmed: shrink the field (look at one side of the room), or use photos of the setting to practice first.

The Client Will Suggest or Join a Structured Play Activity with Peers, Like a Sports Game or Art Project, for 2 minutes per opportunity With 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior: Suggest or Join a Structured Play Activity for 2 Minutes (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

Structured play participation is defined as the client initiating or joining a peer-led or adult-arranged structured activity (e.g., board game, sports game, building challenge, art project) and remaining engaged for at least 2 consecutive minutes with peers. A response counts when the client:
Suggests an activity using a taught script (e.g., "Want to play Uno or Connect 4?" "Let's play catch—first to 5 points?") or joins with a taught script ("Can I join?" "Is there a spot on your team?").

Follows basic activity rules/roles, shares materials/turns, and stays within the play space.

Occurs independently or within 10 seconds of no more than two brief verbal prompts (e.g., "Invite someone," "Ask to join").

Maintains on-task engagement for ≥2 minutes (measured by timer), with no aggressive/disruptive behavior.

Includes (examples):
Inviting two peers to start Jenga; playing for 2+ minutes.

Asking to join soccer; takes a position and plays for 2+ minutes.

Joining an art station; collaborates on a shared piece for 2+ minutes.

Does not include:
Hovering without inviting/joining; side-watching.

Starting but leaving the game/area before 2 minutes without a taught exit.

Unstructured wandering or solitary play away from peers during the interval.

Activities not permitted or unsafe; rule-breaking that stops play.

Measurement
Opportunity: Each green-light social time with at least one accessible structured activity (e.g., recess, centers, indoor games).

Score per opportunity:
Independent = initiates/joins and sustains 2 minutes; 0 prompts.

Prompted = initiates/joins and sustains 2 minutes after ≤2 verbal prompts.

Not met = no initiation/join, >2 prompts required, or <2 minutes engagement.

Track: activity type, peers involved, prompt level, any repairs needed, and actual duration.

Probable Function

Desired: peer attention/connection and access to preferred activities. Non-participation may be maintained by escape (from social/skill demands), anxiety/uncertainty, or competing interests. Replacement focuses on quick, low-effort functional communication + rule-following.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine — "G-O-R-E-2"

Greet/Approach a peer/group.

Offer/Join using a script: "Play __ or __?" / "Can I join?"

Review rules/roles quickly ("Your turn then mine," "I'll be goalie").

Engage: start play immediately.

2-Minute sustain: stay in the activity until the timer ends.

Model → Role-play → Practice with peers; fade adult scaffolds.

Use visuals: small Play Menu (Games, Sports, Art), Join scripts, and Rules icons (turns, share, space).

Antecedent Modifications

Pre-arrange 2-3 clearly defined stations with materials and posted mini-rules.

Assign buddy/peer leaders; prime them to accept joins and offer simple roles.

Offer two pre-loaded choices the client already knows how to play.

Signal green-light windows ("Play time—find or start a game").

For sports: set small-sided versions (2v2, quick rotations) to reduce wait time.

Prompting & Fading (max 2 verbals)

Nonverbal first (doesn't count): point to Play Menu or Join card; gesture to a peer/group.

Verbal 1: "Invite someone / Ask to join."

Verbal 2: "State the rule or your role, then start."

After a prompted success, run a transfer trial later (new activity/peer; longer wait for independence).

Reinforcement

DRA: Labeled praise + token/point for initiating/joining and completing the 2-minute sustain (bonus for independent).

Start FR1, thin to VR2-3 as consistent.

Exchange: choose next game, be team captain/dealer, extra art supplies, or first pick.

Use group reinforcement periodically (whole table earns a star when everyone sustains 2 minutes).

Safety & Boundaries

Pre-teach stop/space signals, fair play, and polite decline ("No thanks, maybe later").

If a peer says no, client uses a repair: invite someone else or pick another activity.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Suggest Activity (primary): Client delivers a two-choice invitation and begins play, sustaining ≥2 minutes, ≥80% of opportunities with ≤2 verbal prompts across 3 sessions.

Join Script: Client says "Can I join?"/"Is there a spot for me?"; on "yes," states a role and engages ≥2 minutes, ≥80% with ≤2 prompts.

Rule/Role Statement: Before play, client states one rule or role ("We take turns / I'll be pitcher") and follows it, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Polite Decline/Redirect: If a group is full or says no, client uses "Okay—maybe later" and invites another peer within 30 s, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Brief cue, then wait (5-10 s): "Invite or join."

Two-choice redirect: "Board game or art?"

If the client leaves early, offer repair: "Rejoin or switch to [second option] for 2 minutes."

Planned ignore for minor hesitations; immediately reinforce the pivot to invite/join.

If frustration rises, offer micro-break (30-60 s) + quick restart with the simplest station.

Responding Politely to Compliments During Social Interactions

The Client will respond to compliments with a clear, polite verbal reply e.g., "thank you," "I appreciate that" during social interactions with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior: Respond to compliments with a clear, polite verbal reply (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

During a social interaction in which the client receives a direct compliment from a peer or adult (e.g., "Nice drawing," "Great pass," "I like your shoes"), the client will, within 5-10 seconds, provide a clear, polite verbal reply directed to the speaker, such as:
"Thank you."

"I appreciate that."

"Thanks, that means a lot."

Optional brief add-on: "Thanks, I worked hard on it."

Acceptable modalities include speech or a pre-approved AAC phrase (e.g., "Thank you," "That's kind"). A smile or thumbs-up may accompany but does not replace the verbal/AAC reply unless the team has agreed that AAC is the client's primary modality.
Prompts allowed: Up to two brief verbal prompts total (e.g., Prompt 1: "Say 'thank you,'" Prompt 2: "Use your polite words."). Gestural/visual cues (pointing to a cue card/AAC button) don't count toward the two.
Counts as success when the client:
Produces a polite verbal/AAC reply within 5-10s,

Directs it to the person who gave the compliment, and

Maintains a calm/neutral body and tone (no sarcasm/hostility).

Non-examples: ignoring the compliment; arguing/denying ("No, it's terrible"); deflecting with self-criticism; responding with unrelated requests; needing ≥3 verbal prompts; replying while engaging in maladaptive behavior (e.g., yelling, mockery).
Opportunities & Measurement: Provide 3-5 compliment opportunities per session (natural or contrived). For each, record Success (Y/N), # verbal prompts (0/1/2), latency to respond (s), and brief notes (context, who complimented).
Mastery criterion: ≥80% successful opportunities per session across 4 of 5 consecutive sessions (then fade to ≤1 prompt, then independent; optionally add a brief follow-up comment goal).
Probable Function

Skill builds prosocial attention/connection and replaces arguing/deflecting (often maintained by attention or avoidance of social focus).
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Micro-Skill (model → role-play → in vivo)

3-step script: Hear → Say "Thank you" → (Optional) Short add-on.

Model several examples; role-play with quick rotations (peer/adult compliments).

Provide AAC buttons or a cue card with 2-4 phrases: "Thank you." / "I appreciate that." / "Thanks, that's kind." / "Thanks, I worked hard."

Antecedent Supports

Prime before social blocks: "If someone compliments you, use your 'thank you' words."

Visuals: pocket-sized "Compliment Replies" card/AAC page; place near common compliment contexts (art wall, PE, centers).

Sentence stem bank for optional add-ons: "I worked hard," "My friend helped," "I got it for my birthday."

Reinforcement

Natural reinforcement: positive social response from the giver.

Behavior-specific praise from staff immediately after correct replies ("You said 'Thank you' right away—polite and clear.").

Token/points for each success; exchange at session end.

Thin extra tokens over time as natural social reinforcement maintains behavior.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Polite Reply (primary): After a compliment, client says "Thank you/I appreciate that" within 5-10s, ≤2 prompts, in 80% of opportunities.

If urge to deny/deflect: Client uses the Accept-then-Add format ("Thank you… I'm still practicing") instead of arguing, ≤2 prompts, 80%.

If unsure what to say (AAC/visual support): Client selects an approved AAC button or reads from the cue card within 10s, ≤2 prompts, 80%.

Optional bridge skill: After the reply, client adds one brief related comment ("Do you like drawing too?") in 60-80% of successes (shape up).

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Low-profile prompts: gesture to card/AAC → Prompt 1 ("Say 'thank you'") → wait 5-10s → Prompt 2 ("Use your polite words").

If client argues/denies: neutral redirect—"Accept first," model: "Thank you," then optionally add "I'm still learning." Run a quick repair trial immediately.

Planned ignore of minor awkwardness; reinforce the first correct reply that follows.

If frustration rises: offer binary choice—"Say 'thank you' or use the AAC button," then praise success.

Social Skills

Social Cues

The Client will demonstrate recognition of facial and gestural cues made by an adult with 2 verbal prompts

Target Behavior

Increase recognition of facial and gestural cues made by an adult with ≤2 verbal prompts.
Operational Definition

When an adult produces a common facial or hand/arm cue, the client either:
Labels the cue/meaning (e.g., "You mean quiet," "That's stop"), or

Responds appropriately to the cue (e.g., stops, comes here, lowers voice),

within 5-7 seconds of cue presentation and with no more than 2 verbal prompts (e.g., "What does this mean?" / "Show me what to do").
Cue set (teach & probe at minimum 8):
Facial: smile (approval), frown (disapproval), raised eyebrows (check/attention), head nod = yes, head shake = no.

Gestural: finger-to-lips = quiet, open palm out = stop, beckon = come here, point = look/come to item, raised hand = wait, thumbs-up = okay/good, wave = hello/bye.

Does not include: cues delivered simultaneously with explicit verbal instructions (track as compliance), ambiguous/novel gestures not yet taught, or responses after the window without repair.
Measurement

Primary: Accuracy per trial (Correct label or correct action = success). Aim ≥10 trials/session.

Secondary: prompts used (0/1/2), latency (s), response type (label vs. action), error type (no response/mislabel).

Onset/offset: Onset at cue presentation; offset at correct label/action or after 7s post-final prompt.

Probable Function

This is a skill-acquisition goal; non-responding/misreading may function as escape from social demands or reflect processing, attention, or language needs.
Proactive Strategies

Routine: Look → Label → Do (LLD)
Look at face/hands, Label the cue/meaning (aloud or AAC), Do the matching action.

Visual supports: cue ring/cards with photos/icons; LLD mini-card.

Priming: preview the day's cue set; model meanings in a mirror/video before live practice.

Environment: reduce noise/visual clutter during teaching; ensure line-of-sight to adult.

AAC readiness: quick buttons for "yes/no/stop/quiet/wait/come/okay/look."

Teaching & Prompting

Model-Lead-Test per cue: adult demonstrates → practice together (client labels/does) → independent trial.
