Least-to-most prompting (max 2 verbal prompts):
Gesture/point to eyes/LLD card (nonverbal).

Verbal 1: "What does this mean?" or "Show me what to do."

Verbal 2 (scaffold): "Is it stop or come here?" / "Do the action."

Mix label and do responses so the client learns both mapping and functional use.

Use error correction: brief model ("This means quiet"), immediate lead with the client, then retest the same cue later.

Reinforcement

Specific praise for quick, accurate LLD ("You saw 'stop' and you stopped—nice reading!").

Token/point for each independent or first-prompt success; thin as accuracy stabilizes.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Teach the client to ask for clarity instead of guessing/avoiding:
"Please repeat/show again."

"Say the word."

"I didn't get it—what does that mean?"

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

If the client freezes or mislabels: keep tone neutral, point to LLD visual, give Verbal 1; if needed, provide a forced choice on Verbal 2, then immediately reinforce correct mapping.

If attention drifts, use proximity and a brief "eyes on" gesture before re-presenting the cue.

The Client will demonstrate recognition of facial and gestural cues made by a peer with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior: Recognize Peer Facial & Gestural Cues (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

Recognition of peer cues is defined as the client accurately interpreting a peer's facial expression or gesture and showing that recognition by either:
Labeling it (verbally/AAC)—e.g., "She looks confused," "He's telling me to stop," or

Acting appropriately—e.g., stepping back when a peer puts up a stop-hand, joining when waved over, speaking up when a peer cups their ear.

A response counts when it occurs within 5 seconds of the peer cue (or within 10 seconds of a prompt) and requires no more than two brief verbal prompts total (e.g., "What does that mean?" / "Show what you'd do.").
Cues taught/tracked (examples):
Facial: happy, sad, angry, worried, confused, surprised, bored.

Gestural/body: nod/head shake, wave in/away, thumbs up, stop hand, "come here," shoulder turn/step back (needs space/not now), point to item/seat, palm-up offer, ear-cup (speak louder).

Does not count:
Ignoring for >5 s, guessing wildly, or choosing a response that conflicts with the cue (e.g., approaching when the peer shows stop).

Needing >2 verbal prompts or requiring the adult to tell the exact answer.

Situations with ambiguous/sarcastic cues beyond the learner's taught set (score N/A).

Measurement
Opportunities: Each time a peer (or role-play peer) shows a taught facial/gestural cue. Aim 8-12 trials/session.

Score per trial: Independent, Prompted (≤2), or Not met.

Optional accuracy rubric (0-2): 2 = correct label and/or matched action; 1 = partly correct (right valence, wrong action); 0 = incorrect/no response.

Track latency (goal ≤5 s), cue type, and whether the client labeled or acted.

Probable Function

This is a social interpretation skill. Misses often come from skill deficits, anxiety/overload, or inattention. Teaching recognition reduces conflicts and improves peer engagement.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine — "SEE → MEAN → ACT"

SEE: Look at face-hands-body (3-point scan).

MEAN: Name the cue's meaning (happy? stop? invite?).

ACT: Do the matched response (join, give space, speak louder, offer help).

Use modeling → video clips/freeze-frames → role-play with peers.

Provide a Cue Card set (icons for face/gestures) and an Emotion Wheel (6-8 core feelings).

Start with high-contrast cues (big smile, clear stop-hand), then fade to subtler/hybrid cues.

Prompting & Fading (max 2 verbals)

Nonverbal first (doesn't count): point to the cue card or gesture "look."

Verbal 1: "What does that mean?"

Verbal 2: "Show what you'd do."
 Then wait 5-10 s silently. After success, run a transfer trial with a longer quiet wait.

Reinforcement

DRA: Labeled praise + token/point for correct label or matched action (bonus for independent or <3 s latency).

Start FR1, thin to VR2-3 as fluent.

Menu: pick next game/partner, help-leader role, sticker.

Antecedent Supports

Seat/proximity where faces/hands are visible; reduce noise during practice.

Pre-load the likely cues for upcoming contexts ("During kickoff, watch for a thumbs-up to start").

Teach peers to hold the gesture for ~2 s to allow recognition.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB) / Skill Targets

Primary—Recognize & Respond (≤2 prompts): Given a peer facial/gestural cue, the client labels it or shows a matched action within 5 s, in ≥80% of trials with ≤2 verbal prompts across 3 sessions.

Clarify-If-Unsure: Client asks a brief check ("Do you need space?" "Is that a stop?") when uncertain, then acts, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Dual Response: Client both labels and acts for at least 50% of trials by mastery phase.

Generalize: In natural play/class moments (non-contrived), client responds correctly to 3+ spontaneous cues per session, ≥70% success with ≤2 prompts.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

If a misread causes friction, cue a quick repair: "Oops—try: 'Sorry, I'll give space.'"

Brief cue, then wait: "What does that mean?" (5 s).

Two-choice redirect: "Invite or give space?"

If overwhelmed, offer a 30-60 s micro-break, then retry with a clearer cue.

Eye Contact

Following Environmental Expectations

The Client will follow the expectations of his environment

Target Behavior: Follow the expectations of the environment (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

"Following the expectations of the environment" means the client demonstrates the pre-taught, setting-specific behaviors listed on an Expectations Matrix (below) during a target routine.
A success is scored when, during the designated routine, the client:
Meets all listed expectations for that setting in ≥80% of 30-second whole intervals, and

Requires ≤2 verbal prompts total (gestural/visual prompts don't count), and

Shows no maladaptive behavior (e.g., yelling, aggression, elopement, property destruction).

Non-examples: off-task >10s; ignoring directions; unsafe hands/feet; arguing; refusal; leaving area; misusing materials; more than 2 verbal prompts.
Expectations Matrix (examples—customize to your setting)

Work/Desk:
Start within 10s of the direction, 2) Stay in seat/area, 3) Use materials safely, 4) Ask for help/break appropriately.

Small Group/Carpet:
Mouth quiet while others speak, 2) Body facing group, 3) Hand up/signal for turn, 4) Follow group directions.

Transitions/Hallway:
Walk in area, 2) Stay with group/route, 3) Voice level 0-1, 4) Hands/feet to self.

Cafeteria/Playground/Community:
Stay in designated space, 2) Share/take turns, 3) Respect equipment/people, 4) Use polite words.

Measurement

Select 1 target routine per session (5-10 minutes). Use 30-second whole-interval recording for "on-expectation," plus tally verbal prompts (0-2) and any maladaptive behavior (Y/N).

Mastery: ≥80% on-expectation intervals with ≤2 prompts in 4 of 5 consecutive sessions. Then (a) fade to ≤1 prompt or (b) add a second routine per session.

Probable Function

Interfering behaviors often contact escape (from tasks), tangibles, attention, or automatic reinforcement. Confirm via ABC data and adjust supports accordingly.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Expectations (BST)

Explain → Model → Role-play → Feedback for each setting using the matrix (show examples/non-examples).

Pre-correction: Before each routine, preview 2-3 expectations and the prompt limit ("Remember: start in 10 seconds; hand up to talk").

Visuals: First-Then board, mini "Expectations" card, visual schedule, voice-level chart.

Antecedent Supports

Clear directions (1-2 steps), check for understanding, "show me" starts.

Task design: break work into chunks, provide exemplars, set up materials.

Behavioral momentum: 2-3 easy "high-P" actions before harder tasks.

Choices: order, tools, seating, partners (where appropriate).

Transition aids: 2-minute/30-second warnings, timers, countdowns.

NCR attention: brief check-ins to reduce attention-seeking behavior.

Reinforcement

DRA for on-expectation behavior (specific praise tied to the matrix: "You started in 10 seconds—nice!").

DRO: deliver a small reward for intervals with zero problem behavior.

Tokens/points: 1 token per on-expectation minute; exchange after session.

Thin gradually as behavior stabilizes.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Teach and reinforce these quick alternatives so the client can still meet expectations:
Help Request: "Help please," or AAC help within 10s of difficulty, ≤2 prompts.

Break Request: "Break please" (1-3 min), return within 10s of timer, ≤2 prompts.

Ask for More Time/Choice: "30 more seconds?" or "Can I start with ___?" ≤2 prompts.

Check the Rule: "What are the expectations?" / point to card, then comply.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Prompting hierarchy: gesture to visual → 1-3 word cue ("Start now," "Hands to self") → Verbal Prompt 2 if needed → brief model. Fade quickly after success.

Match consequence to function:
Escape-maintained: don't end tasks after problem behavior; honor break only after an appropriate request.

Tangible-maintained: withhold item when off-expectation; provide after on-expectation or FCR.

Attention-maintained: minimize attention to off-expectation; deliver immediate praise for returning to expectations.

Neutral reset: "Let's reset—First-Then," restart the interval.

Safety plan for any unsafe behavior; after calm, run a repair trial (10-30s on-expectation) and reinforce.

Personal Space

The client will demonstrate an understanding and respect of personal space by maintaining appropriate physical distance with 2 verbal prompts

Target Behavior

Increase maintaining appropriate physical distance from others during everyday interactions with ≤2 verbal prompts.
Operational Definition

In social/learning settings, when the client is near another person (peer/adult), the client:
Positions their body at an appropriate distance for the context (see guides below),

Keeps hands/objects to self unless consent is given, and

Maintains that distance for the interaction or for ≥30 seconds (whichever is shorter),

with no more than 2 verbal prompts (e.g., "Use your space bubble," "Take one step back").
Context distance guides (teach as visuals, not exact legal rules):
Talking / small group: about one arm's length between torsos ("space bubble").

Lines / waiting: at least one big step or one floor tile behind the person in front.

Desk/table work: hips against back of chair, feet under own chair, materials on own mat/half of table.

Walking/passing: say "Excuse me," keep shoulders from touching; no brushing or leaning.

Does not include: touching with consent (high-five, handshake), staff-directed proximity (e.g., first aid), crowded safety situations where spacing isn't possible, or culturally typical greetings that are explicitly agreed to.
Onset/offset: Onset when the client enters within the taught "bubble" or initiates contact without consent; offset when they step back to the appropriate distance and maintain it for ≥10 consecutive seconds (or the interaction ends).
Measurement

Primary: % of identified opportunities in which the client maintains appropriate distance with ≤2 prompts.

Secondary: average prompts used (0/1/2), latency to adjust after cue (seconds), and incidents of unconsented touch (count).

Intensity (highest per session):
1—Minor: brief encroachment; steps back after gesture/first prompt.

2—Moderate: needs second verbal prompt or makes brief contact (e.g., bump/lean).

3—Severe: repeated crowding after 2 prompts or sustained contact despite cues.

Probable Function

Crowding/touching can be maintained by attention/affection, escape/anxiety relief (seeking closeness), automatic/sensory input (deep pressure), or task access/control (reaching for items). (Verify with ABC notes.)
Proactive Strategies

Environment & antecedents
Space visuals: floor dots/footprints; "one arm's length" posters; desk mats for zones.

Pre-correct before transitions/lines: "Remember—one big step between people."

Teach consent script: "Can I give you a high-five/hug?" Accept "no" gracefully.

Predictable routines: assigned line spots, clear entry/exit paths, defined seat boundaries.

Sensory alternatives: offer movement/deep-pressure options (wall push-ups, chair push-downs, fidget) so closeness isn't used for regulation.

Noncontingent attention: brief check-ins reduce seeking attention via proximity.

Skills instruction (model-lead-test)
Space Bubble Routine (S.B.R.): Scan → Stretch one arm to measure → Step to adjust → Stay.

Practice with hula-hoops/string "bubbles," mirrors, and role-plays (talking, lining up, passing).

Reading cues: show photos/videos of facial/gestural "back up" signals (leaning away, palm-out, step back) and practice labeling/adjusting.

Prompting hierarchy (cap at 2 verbal prompts)
Nonverbal: palm-out "stop/space" sign or point to floor dot.

Verbal Prompt 1: "Space bubble." / "One step back."

Verbal Prompt 2: "Arm's length—adjust and stay." (Then praise when achieved.)
 Fade toward gesture-only cues as mastery grows.

Reinforcement
DRA: labeled praise + token/points for independent spacing and quick adjustments (<3s).

Bonus for 0-1 prompt interactions and for using consent language.

Optionally use DRO for short intervals with zero encroachment (if it doesn't suppress appropriate bids for attention/affection).

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Teach what to do instead of crowding/touching:
Get attention first: "Excuse me, [Name], can I tell you something?" (while staying at arm's length).

Ask for contact: "High-five?" / "Hug?" (accepts "no").

Request proximity alternative: "Can I sit next to you?" / "Can we move to a bigger space?"

Request regulation: "I need deep pressure/a squeeze tool," not a person.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Early signs (leaning in, reaching, stepping too close):
Move slightly to the side, give nonverbal space cue, point to floor dot—keep voice low.

Offer an immediate FERB: "High-five or fist bump?" / "Use your fidget."

If encroachment continues after prompts:
Neutral reset: "Pause—Space Bubble Routine." Guide a single step back, then praise as soon as they hold the distance.

If sensory-seeking is evident, swap to a planned sensory option; if anxiety-driven, offer brief coping ("two breaths," then resume).

The Client will maintain appropriate distance from adult when conversing

Target Behavior: Maintain Appropriate Conversational Distance from an Adult (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

Appropriate conversational distance is defined as the client positioning their body so they are about one arm's length (≈2-3 feet / 0.6-0.9 m) from the adult while conversing, unless the activity requires closer spacing (e.g., showing a small item at the table) or increased spacing (e.g., hallway safety).
 A response counts when the client:
Initiates and maintains the target distance for the duration of the exchange or

Adjusts within 5 seconds after a cue (see prompting) and then maintains the distance for the rest of the exchange.

Includes (examples):
Standing or sitting with toes on a floor marker or "footprints" about an arm's length from the adult.

Briefly leaning in to hear and returning to target distance within 3 seconds.

Asking, "Is this a good distance?" and adjusting.

Does not include:
Standing too close (e.g., <12 inches, touching, leaning on the adult) for >3 seconds without a legitimate task reason.

Standing too far (e.g., >6 feet) so the adult asks the client to come closer for typical-volume conversation.

Repeatedly drifting in/out of range or circling behind the adult.

Situations with safety/medical needs (score N/A).

Measurement
Opportunities: Each conversational exchange (≥10 seconds) during session.

Score per opportunity:
Independent = client maintains target distance with 0 prompts.

Prompted = maintains after ≤2 brief verbal prompts (nonverbals don't count toward the two).

Not met = >2 prompts, fails to adjust within 5 s, or remains too close/too far.

Optional MTS: 10-s momentary checks during a conversation; record % intervals at target distance.

Probable Function

Mis-distance often reflects skill/awareness deficits, sensory seeking/avoidance, attention seeking, or anxiety/uncertainty about social rules. Replacement focuses on self-check and quick adjust.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine — "Check → Adjust → Maintain"

Check: Do a quick arm-length check (extend forearm without touching) or stand on floor footprints/spot.

Adjust: Take one small step forward/back if needed.

Maintain: Keep distance while talking; re-check after movements.

Use modeling → role-play → real chats with immediate feedback.

Provide visuals: floor dots/footprints, "arm's length" poster, "personal bubble" card.

Antecedent Modifications

Place floor markers where conversations usually occur.

Arrange seating at a table corner or across the table to naturally set distance.

Pre-teach context rules (table work can be closer, hallway/line may be a bit farther).

Offer hearing supports (quieter space, face speaker) so closeness isn't needed to hear.

Prompting & Fading (max 2 verbals)

Nonverbal first (doesn't use a prompt): tap the floor spot, palm-down "step back/forward" gesture, point to "bubble" card.

Verbal 1: "Arm's length." or "Take one step back/forward."

Verbal 2: "Hold this distance."
 After any prompted success, run a transfer trial later (new location, longer wait for independence).

Reinforcement

DRA: Labeled praise + token/point when the client is at/returns to target distance (bonus for independent and quick ≤5 s adjusts).

Start FR1, thin to VR2-3 as consistent.

Exchange ideas: pick conversation topic, quick preferred activity, helper role.

Sensory/Preference Supports

Offer a fidget or seat band if closeness is sensory-seeking.

Teach the client to self-advocate: "I need more space," or "I can't hear—can we move here?"

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Primary—Self-Check Distance: Client performs an arm-length check and maintains ≈2-3 ft during a conversation, or adjusts within 5 s of a cue, in ≥80% of opportunities with ≤2 verbal prompts across 3 sessions.

Quick Adjust Step: On noticing drift or a cue, client takes one step forward/back and re-centers, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Preference Statement: If uncomfortable, client says, "This distance is better for me," and positions accordingly (still within agreed range), ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Brief cue, then wait (3-5 s): "Arm's length."

Two-choice redirect: "Step back one or stand on the blue dot."

Planned ignore of minor, momentary drifts; immediately reinforce the pivot to correct distance.

If the client keeps closing in, change position (angle your body, use table edge) and offer a micro-break if regulation seems low.

Keep language neutral/brief; avoid shaming. Prioritize safety and dignity.

The Client will maintain appropriate distance from peer when conversing

Target Behavior: Maintain appropriate distance from a peer when conversing (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

During a face-to-face conversation with a peer, the client will keep appropriate personal space for the setting and adjust as needed using pre-taught cues.
Appropriate distance (default, standing):
About 1-2 arm lengths from the peer (~2-4 feet / 60-120 cm), facing or slight angle.

Seated at a table: hips within own chair space; bodies not touching; materials stay on own work area.
 In line / walking: keep one arm length from the person ahead; no body contact.
Counts as a success during a conversation trial when the client:
Maintains the distance range above (or a teacher-set alternative for the context),

Responds to cues (visual/gestural or verbal) by adjusting within 5-10 seconds,

Has no unwanted contact (leaning on, bumping, touching personal items) for the duration, and

Requires ≤2 verbal prompts total (gestural/visual prompts don't count).

Non-examples: standing closer than one arm length without consent; hovering over materials; leaning into face/ear; following so closely that the peer has to move away; moving so far (>6 ft) that the partner repeatedly asks to speak up; needing ≥3 verbal prompts.
Measurement

Run 2-4 conversation opportunities per session (natural or contrived), 1-2 minutes each.

Use 10-second momentary time sampling: mark "on-distance" at each beep. Record verbal prompts (0/1/2) and any contact intrusions (Y/N).

Trial success: on-distance in ≥80% of samples, ≤2 verbal prompts, 0 intrusions.

Mastery: ≥80% successful trials per session across 4 of 5 consecutive sessions. Then fade to ≤1 prompt or increase duration/novel peers.

Probable Function

Too-close/too-far positioning may be driven by attention seeking, sensory/comfort needs, anxiety/impulsivity, or escape (standing far to avoid talk). Confirm with ABC notes to fine-tune supports.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Skill (model → role-play → in vivo)

"Space Bubble" rule: Arm out = good distance. Practice with floor dots/tape or hoops.

Adjust phrases: "I'll take a step back so we can both see," "Is this a good distance?"

Partner perspective: quick demo of how distance affects comfort/hearing.

Antecedent Supports

Visuals: small "space bubble" card; floor dots at centers; line markers.

Prime before social blocks: "Remember—arm length apart. If I show the card, take one step back."

Seating plans that naturally separate (staggered chairs, name tags).

Noise/context adjustments: Teach exceptions (crowded hallway, whispering with permission, team huddle with consent).

Prompting (fade quickly)

Gestural/visual cue first (tap card / hand signal).

Verbal Prompt 1: "Check your space."

Verbal Prompt 2: "Take one step back/forward."

If a 3rd verbal prompt would be needed, end trial, model, then run a quick repair trial.

Reinforcement

Specific praise: "Nice—arm-length space and quick adjustment."

Tokens/points for each successful trial; exchange at session end.

Natural reinforcement: smoother conversation/peer acceptance; let the peer choose topic/game after a success.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Self-check & adjust: On cue, client looks at distance and steps back/forward once within 5-10s, ≤2 prompts, in 80% of opportunities.

Consent/clarify: Client asks, "Is this a good distance?" or "Can I sit closer?" ≤1 prompt, in 80% of opportunities.

Attention mand (if peer is far/busy): "Excuse me, [Name]" + move to appropriate distance, ≤2 prompts, 80%.

Coping alternative (if urge to lean): Use a quiet fidget or shift stance instead of touching, ≤1 prompt, 80%.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Low-profile correction: model by stepping to the spot; gesture to card; then a brief cue ("Check space").

If peer expresses discomfort: coach a quick repair—client apologizes ("Sorry—better now?") and adjusts; reinforce immediately.

Minimize attention to repeated crowding; reinforce the first correct adjustment that follows.

Safety: If pushing/arguing occurs, follow site plan; after calm, run a short repair trial (20-30s at correct distance) and praise.

Leisure Skills

Independent Engagement

The Client Will Choose and Engage in a Simple Activity, Like Reading or Drawing, during unstructured time when prompted to do so

Target Behavior

Increase self-directed engagement in a simple activity (e.g., reading, drawing, puzzle, journaling, quiet game) once per session with ≤2 verbal prompts.
Operational Definition

When the client is bored (self-reports or shows indicators like sighing, roaming, "there's nothing to do") or when the therapist cues a boredom check, the client:
Selects one pre-taught activity from an Activity Menu within 30 seconds, and

Begins and stays engaged with that activity for ≥5 consecutive minutes (quietly, materials used as intended),

with no more than 2 verbal prompts (e.g., "Choose an activity," "Start now with the timer").
Counts as success: one full cycle (choose + engage ≥5 min) during the session with ≤2 prompts.
 Does not include: screen time if not on the menu, wandering, talking to peers/staff to avoid engagement, switching activities before 5 minutes without using the taught "switch" request.
Onset/offset: Onset at boredom indicator or boredom-check cue; offset after 5+ minutes of continuous engagement (or upon approved switch after 2 minutes during teaching phase).
Measurement

Primary: Session success (Yes/No) = one qualifying cycle completed.

Secondary: prompts used (0/1/2), latency to choose (s), engagement duration (min), switches requested (Y/N), brief notes on activity chosen.

Probable Function

This is a skill-acquisition/regulation goal. Boredom behaviors may function for attention, escape from task demands, or automatic stimulation. Teaching a quick self-activation routine provides autonomy and structure.
Proactive Strategies

Set up the environment
Create a personal Activity Menu (5-8 options) with visuals/icons (e.g., book basket, sketch kit, maze/puzzle, journaling, LEGO/free build, word search, mindfulness coloring).

Keep materials ready-to-grab in labeled bins or a "Boredom Box."

Post a Boredom-to-Action (B2A) card: Notice → Pick → Start → Stick (5 min).

Use a quiet timer (5 minutes), choice board, and a small "Switch" card (1 allowed switch per cycle after 2 minutes).

Prime at session start: "If you feel bored, use B2A."

Teaching & Prompting

Model-Lead-Test (rapid)
Model: therapist thinks aloud: "I'm bored → I pick drawing → start → stick for 5."

Lead: client picks with support; start timer together.

Test: client runs B2A solo on a new boredom cue.

Prompting hierarchy (max 2 verbal prompts total)
Nonverbal: point to Activity Menu/B2A card → wait 5-10s.

Verbal 1: "Choose one."

Verbal 2: "Start now; 5-minute timer."
 Fade quickly toward gesture-only cues.

Engagement micro-starts (if needed)
Give a 10-30 second "first step" (open book to page, draw one shape, start first puzzle row), then the 5-minute timer.

Reinforcement

DRA: specific praise for quick choose/start and for completing 5 minutes ("Nice job choosing quickly and sticking with it!").

Token/point/access to preferred activity after the 5-minute block; thin over time.

Bonus for independent (0-1 prompt) cycles and for requesting a single permitted switch appropriately.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Teach the client to ask skillfully instead of avoiding/complaining:
"More time to choose (30s)?"

"Can I switch once?" (after 2 minutes)

"I need help getting started."

"Can I do it with headphones/quiet corner?"

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Early signs (restless, "this is boring"):
Neutral redirection to B2A + A/B choice: "Pick reading or drawing."

Reduce decision load: offer 2-item mini menu from the larger list.

If agitation rises, do a micro-start + timer; praise first 30-60s, then fade praise.

If the client quits before 5 minutes:
Calm reset: "Use your switch card or return to your pick." Start a shorter 3-minute timer once, then work back to 5 minutes next trial.

Perspective Taking

Emotion Recognition

The Client Will Identify Basic Emotions in Himself or Others, Such as Happiness or Frustration, in Contrived Scenarios With a Verbal Prompt

Target Behavior: Identify Basic Emotions (Self/Other) in Contrived Scenarios (≤1 verbal prompt)

Operational Definition

Emotion identification is defined as the client labeling a basic emotion (e.g., happy, sad, angry/mad, frustrated, scared, worried, surprised, calm) in himself or another person when presented with a contrived scenario (photo card, short video, acted role-play, or brief story).
 A response counts when the client:
Says or selects one correct emotion word (or AAC symbol) within 5-10 seconds of the cue or within 10 seconds of a single brief verbal prompt (e.g., "Name the feeling").

For "other" targets, pairs the label with one clue (face, body, situation) when asked, using a stem like "because ___."

Includes (examples):
"He feels frustrated because his tower fell."

Points to "happy" on AAC after a picture of a child opening a gift.

"I feel worried before the test."

Does not include:
Giving an emotion not on the taught list for that session unless clearly justified by cues.

Describing actions without a feeling ("She's running") or giving only a cause without a label.

Needing >1 verbal prompt, or exceeding the time window.

Measurement

Opportunities: 8-12 contrived trials per session (mix self/other; pictures, role-play, stories).

Score each trial:
Independent = correct label (and clue if requested), 0 prompts.

Prompted = correct label after ≤1 verbal prompt.

Not met = incorrect/no response, >1 prompt, or out of time.

Optional 2-point rubric: 2 = correct label + relevant clue; 1 = correct label only/weak clue; 0 = incorrect/no label.

Track latency (goal ≤10 s), modality (voice/AAC/point), and scenario type.

Probable Function (when errors occur)

Most often a skill/knowledge gap (limited emotion vocabulary or mapping cues to feelings), language formulation difficulty, avoidance of demanding tasks, or anxiety. Replacement = fast, low-effort label (and brief clue).
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine — "SEE → FEEL → BECAUSE"

SEE: Scan face-body-situation (3-point check).

FEEL: Choose the best match from the Emotion List.

BECAUSE (if asked): Give 1 clue: "because ___ (tears/eyebrows up/blocks fell)."

Model → Think-aloud → Guided practice → Independent, rotating through the same emotions with new pictures/stories.

Start with high-contrast cues (big smile, obvious tears), then fade to subtler cues.

Visuals & Tools

Emotion List (6-8 icons/words) posted or on a ring.

Cue frame card: "Face? Body? Situation?"

Sentence frames / AAC stems: "(He/She/I) feels __ (because __)."

Antecedent Modifications

Right-size language level; pre-teach key vocabulary (frustrated, worried).

Reduce competing noise; ensure clear view of faces/hands.

Preview session structure: "We'll do 10 feelings pictures—use SEE → FEEL → BECAUSE."

Prompting & Fading (max 1 verbal prompt)

Nonverbal first (doesn't consume the prompt): point to Emotion List/cue frame, gesture toward face/body clue.

Verbal (single): "Name the feeling." (If needed after the label: "What clue tells you that?")

After any prompted success, run a transfer trial later (new image; wait silently) to build independence.

Reinforcement

DRA: Labeled praise + token/point for correct labels (bonus for independent or <5 s latency).

Begin FR1, thin to VR2-3 as performance stabilizes.

Exchange options: pick next picture/story, brief preferred activity, sticker.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB) / Skill Targets

Primary—Correct Label (≤1 prompt): Given a contrived scenario, client labels a target emotion within 10 s in ≥80% of trials with ≤1 verbal prompt across 3 sessions.

Add One Clue (others' emotions): When asked "How do you know?", client gives one relevant cue (face/body/situation) in ≥80% of "other" trials with ≤1 prompt.

Self-Label: For "self" prompts ("How would you feel?"), client states one emotion (or AAC) and, if prompted, one brief reason, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Generalize Set: Expand from core 4 (happy, sad, angry, scared) → + worried, frustrated, surprised, calm.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

If stuck, offer a two-choice rescue ("happy or frustrated?"), then fade choices next trial.

Keep cues brief/neutral; avoid over-talking.

Provide a 30-60 s micro-break if signs of frustration appear, then resume with an easier cue.

For near-miss answers (e.g., "mad" vs "frustrated"), validate valence and shape to the target on the next trial.

The client will identify and verbalize the feelings of others when presented with a contrived or hypothetical scenario

Target Behavior: 
 Identifying and verbalizing the feelings of others when presented with a contrived or hypothetical scenario.
&nbsp;
Motivating Operation:
 Promoting empathy, emotional understanding, and social comprehension skills.
Antecedent Procedures:
 1. Utilize a controlled setting like a therapy room or classroom for the exercise.
 2. Prepare scenarios that depict characters experiencing different emotions.
 3. Inform the client that the objective is to identify how others might feel in the given scenario.
&nbsp;
Discriminative Stimulus:
 The therapist or educator presents a contrived scenario that involves characters experiencing emotions, e.g., "Sarah looks sad because her ice cream fell on the ground."
Appropriate Response:
 Within 1 minute of hearing the scenario, the client will identify and verbalize the emotions experienced by each character involved, like saying, "Sarah probably feels sad because her ice cream fell," to be considered a successful trial.
&nbsp;
Error Correction Procedures:
 1. **Modeling**: If the client doesn't identify the emotions, the therapist models how to articulate the feelings of the characters involved.
 2. **Prompting**: Use verbal prompts such as, "How do you think Sarah feels?" to facilitate emotional identification.
 3. **Immediate Feedback**: Provide specific feedback on the accuracy of the client's emotional identification.
 4. **Review**: Go over the scenario again to give the client another chance to identify emotions.
&nbsp;
Criteria for Mastery: 
 The client will correctly identify and verbalize the emotions of others in 8 out of 10 contrived scenarios across 3 consecutive data collection sessions.
&nbsp;
This ABA goal is designed to build the client's emotional intelligence by enhancing their ability to recognize and articulate the emotions of others, which is crucial for successful social interactions and relationships.
Instructional Notes

MO:
 Promoting empathy, emotional understanding, and social comprehension skills.
 
 Antecedent Procedures:
Utilize a controlled setting like a therapy room or classroom for the exercise.
 2. Prepare scenarios that depict characters experiencing different emotions.
 3. Inform the client that the objective is to identify how others might feel in the given scenario.

 
 SD:
 The therapist or educator presents a contrived scenario that involves characters experiencing emotions, e.g., "Sarah looks sad because her ice cream fell on the ground."
 
 Response:
 Within 1 minute of hearing the scenario, the client will identify and verbalize the emotions experienced by each character involved, like saying, "Sarah probably feels sad because her ice cream fell," to be considered a successful trial.
 
 Prompting:
 visual prompt,verbal prompt,gestural prompt,modeling
 
 Error Correction:
 1. **Modeling**: If the client doesn't identify the emotions, the therapist models how to articulate the feelings of the characters involved. 2. **Prompting**: Use verbal prompts such as, "How do you think Sarah feels?" to facilitate emotional identification. 3. **Immediate Feedback**: Provide specific feedback on the accuracy of the client's emotional identification. 4. **Review**: Go over the scenario again to give the client another chance to identify emotions.
 
 Reinforcement:
 differential social reinforcement,socially mediated reinforcement

The client will identify and verbalize the feelings of others in natural, real-world scenarios

Target Behavior: Identify and verbalize others' feelings in natural, real-world scenarios (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

When another person (peer/adult) displays observable emotional cues during everyday activities (e.g., recess, small group work, transitions, games, problem solving), the client will, within 10 seconds, do both of the following using speech or an approved modality (AAC/sign):
Identify one plausible emotion that fits the situation and cues (e.g., "happy," "proud," "frustrated," "worried," "bored," "embarrassed").

Verbalize it in a respectful, person-directed sentence (e.g., "You look frustrated," "I think you're excited," "It seems like you're worried.").

Prompts allowed: up to two brief verbal prompts total (e.g., Prompt 1: "Check feelings." Prompt 2: "Say what you notice."). Gestural/visual cues (point to feelings chart) don't count toward the two.
Counts as success when:
The label is reasonable for the available cues or the client uses a respectful check-in question ("Are you feeling upset?") and accepts the answer.

Tone/body are calm and respectful (no teasing/mocking).

Occurs within the 10-second window and ≤2 verbal prompts.

Non-examples: saying nothing; incorrect/teasing labels ("You're being dramatic"); asking repeatedly after an answer; needing ≥3 verbal prompts; labeling while engaging in maladaptive behavior.
Measurement

Create 3-5 natural opportunities per session (or brief contrived moments if none occur).

Record: Success (Y/N), emotion named, context, prompts (0/1/2), latency (s), and whether a confirming response occurred (Y/N).

Mastery criterion: ≥80% successful opportunities per session across 4 of 5 consecutive sessions. Then fade to ≤1 prompt, then independent; expand to more nuanced emotions.

Probable Skill Domain / Function

Perspective-taking & social cognition (feature-function-class of facial/body/voice/situation cues). Errors can be shaped by escape (avoiding social engagement) or attention (saying provocative labels). Confirm via ABC notes.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Micro-Skill (BST: Instruction → Modeling → Rehearsal → Feedback)

Four Cue Lens:
 Face (brow, mouth, eyes) → Body (posture, movement) → Voice (volume, pace) → Situation (what just happened?).

Sentence stems (speech/AAC page):
"You look [feeling]."

"It seems like you're [feeling]."

"Are you feeling [feeling]?" (check-in)

"Do you want help?" / "Can I do anything?" (optional follow-up)

Practice set: brief photo/video clips, emoji cards, and 10-second role-plays; rotate easy → subtle emotions.

Model think-aloud: "Your shoulders dropped (body) and voice got quiet (voice) after losing the piece (situation)—that looks disappointed."

Antecedent Supports

Feelings ring/visual scale (green-yellow-red) with 8-12 emotions.

Prime before high-yield times (group work, games): "Spot one feeling and name it kindly."

Environment: reduce noise during practice; provide a respectful phrasing card.

Perspective warm-ups: "How would you feel if…?" quick rounds.

Reinforcement

Behavior-specific praise: "Nice job noticing the quiet voice and saying 'You look worried.'"

Natural reinforcement: positive peer engagement/help offered.

Token/points for each success; bonus for independent labels and accurate check-ins.

Gradually thin tokens as natural social responses maintain the skill.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Primary skill: Client identifies and verbalizes one reasonable feeling within 10 s, ≤2 prompts, in 80% of opportunities.

If unsure: Client uses a check-in question ("Are you feeling ___?" or "How are you feeling?") and reflects the answer back, ≤2 prompts, 80%.

If peer declines sharing: Client responds, "Okay—thanks," and does not persist (no more than one follow-up), ≤1 prompt, 80%.

Optional bridge: Offer a brief supportive statement ("Do you want help?" / "I can wait with you"), ≤1 prompt, 70-80%.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Low-profile cueing: gesture to feelings card → Prompt 1 ("Check feelings") → wait up to 10 s → Prompt 2 ("Say what you notice").

If mislabeling upsets the peer: coach a quick repair—"Sorry. I meant to help. How are you feeling?"—then praise the repair.

Avoid over-probing: if the person declines, reinforce the client's respectful back-off.

If teasing/provocative labels occur: provide minimal attention; immediately reinforce the next respectful identification/check-in.

Understanding others Perspectives

The client will identify and verbalize the perspective of another person when presented with a contrived or hypothetical scenario

Target Behavior: 
 Identifying and verbalizing the perspectives of others when presented with a contrived or hypothetical scenario.
&nbsp;
Motivating Operation:
 Promoting empathy, social understanding, and perspective-taking skills.
Antecedent Procedures:
 1. Utilize a structured setting like a therapy room or classroom to conduct the exercise.
 2. Prepare scenarios that involve multiple perspectives on an issue or situation.
 3. Inform the client that the aim is to identify how others might feel or think in the given scenario.
&nbsp;
Discriminative Stimulus:
 The therapist or educator presents a contrived scenario that includes multiple characters with differing perspectives, e.g., "Sara wants to play outside, but Tim wants to stay indoors because it's too hot."
Appropriate Response:
 Within 1 minute of hearing the scenario, the client will identify and verbalize the perspective of each character involved, like saying, "Sara probably wants to enjoy the outdoors, while Tim might be thinking it's too uncomfortable outside," to be considered a successful trial.
&nbsp;
Error Correction Procedures:
 1. **Modeling**: If the client doesn't identify the perspectives, the therapist models how to articulate the viewpoints of those involved.
 2. **Prompting**: Use verbal prompts like, "How do you think Sara feels?" to facilitate perspective-taking.
 3. **Immediate Feedback**: Offer specific feedback on the client's ability or inability to grasp the perspectives involved.
 4. **Review**: Go over the scenario again to give the client another chance to identify perspectives.
&nbsp;
Criteria for Mastery: 
 The client will correctly identify and verbalize the perspectives of others in 8 out of 10 contrived scenarios across 3 consecutive data collection sessions.
&nbsp;
This ABA goal is designed to enhance the client's social cognition by developing the ability to understand and articulate the perspectives of others, a crucial skill for social interactions and relationships.
Instructional Notes

MO:
 Promoting empathy, social understanding, and perspective-taking skills.
 
 Antecedent Procedures:
Utilize a structured setting like a therapy room or classroom to conduct the exercise.
 2. Prepare scenarios that involve multiple perspectives on an issue or situation.
 3. Inform the client that the aim is to identify how others might feel or think in the given scenario.

 
 SD:
 The therapist or educator presents a contrived scenario that includes multiple characters with differing perspectives, e.g., "Sara wants to play outside, but Tim wants to stay indoors because it's too hot."
 
 Response:
 Within 1 minute of hearing the scenario, the client will identify and verbalize the perspective of each character involved, like saying, "Sara probably wants to enjoy the outdoors, while Tim might be thinking it's too uncomfortable outside," to be considered a successful trial.
 
 Prompting:
 visual prompt,verbal prompt,gestural prompt,modeling
 
 Error Correction:
 1. **Modeling**: If the client doesn't identify the perspectives, the therapist models how to articulate the viewpoints of those involved. 2. **Prompting**: Use verbal prompts like, "How do you think Sara feels?" to facilitate perspective-taking. 3. **Immediate Feedback**: Offer specific feedback on the client's ability or inability to grasp the perspectives involved. 4. **Review**: Go over the scenario again to give the client another chance to identify perspectives.
 
 Reinforcement:
 differential social reinforcement,socially mediated reinforcement

The client will identify and verbalize the perspective of another person in natural, real world scenarios

Target Behavior: 
 Identifying and verbalizing the perspectives of others in natural, real-world scenarios.
&nbsp;
Motivating Operation:
 Enhancing real-world empathy, social comprehension, and perspective-taking skills.
Antecedent Procedures:
 1. Position the client in natural settings where interactions with others are likely, such as school, home, or community events.
 2. Provide foundational training on perspective-taking using contrived scenarios, before attempting in natural settings.
 3. Inform the client that the objective is to understand and express how others may feel or think in the given situation.
&nbsp;
Discriminative Stimulus:
 An interaction or event occurs involving multiple people with differing viewpoints, such as a disagreement between friends or a decision-making situation in a group.
Appropriate Response:
 Within 2 minutes of witnessing the natural scenario, the client will identify and verbalize the perspective of each person involved, like saying, "Mom might be worried about us, that's why she's saying no to going out late," to be considered a successful trial.
&nbsp;
Error Correction Procedures:
 1. **Prompting**: If the client does not identify the perspectives, offer subtle verbal prompts like, "What do you think they're feeling?"
 2. **Immediate Feedback**: Give specific feedback on whether the client accurately captured the perspectives involved.
 3. **Discussion**: Use a one-on-one discussion to dissect the scenario, focusing on the different viewpoints.
 4. **Practice**: Provide additional natural occurring scenarios to practice perspective-taking.
&nbsp;
Criteria for Mastery: 
 The client will correctly identify and verbalize the perspectives of others in 8 out of 10 natural occurring scenarios across 3 consecutive data collection sessions.
&nbsp;
This ABA goal aims to build on foundational perspective-taking skills by applying them to real-world, naturally occurring situations, a vital step for meaningful social interactions and emotional understanding.
Instructional Notes

MO:
 Enhancing real-world empathy, social comprehension, and perspective-taking skills.
 
 Antecedent Procedures:
Position the client in natural settings where interactions with others are likely, such as school, home, or community events.
 2. Provide foundational training on perspective-taking using contrived scenarios, before attempting in natural settings.
 3. Inform the client that the objective is to understand and express how others may feel or think in the given situation.
&nbsp;

 
 SD:
 An interaction or event occurs involving multiple people with differing viewpoints, such as a disagreement between friends or a decision-making situation in a group.
 
 Response:
 Within 2 minutes of witnessing the natural scenario, the client will identify and verbalize the perspective of each person involved, like saying, "Mom might be worried about us, that's why she's saying no to going out late," to be considered a successful trial.
 
 Prompting:
 visual prompt,verbal prompt,gestural prompt,modeling
 
 Error Correction:
 1. **Prompting**: If the client does not identify the perspectives, offer subtle verbal prompts like, "What do you think they're feeling?" 2. **Immediate Feedback**: Give specific feedback on whether the client accurately captured the perspectives involved. 3. **Discussion**: Use a one-on-one discussion to dissect the scenario, focusing on the different viewpoints. 4. **Practice**: Provide additional natural occurring scenarios to practice perspective-taking.
 
 Reinforcement:
 differential social reinforcement,socially mediated reinforcement

The client will accept another person's opinion or view with one verbal prompt

Target Behavior

Increase accepting another person's opinion/view with ≤1 verbal prompt.
Operational Definition

When a peer/adult shares an opinion or perspective that may differ from the client's, the client shows acceptance by giving a brief, respectful acknowledgment and not arguing. A qualifying response includes one of the following within 10 seconds:
Acknowledgment phrase: "Okay," "I hear you," "Thanks for sharing," "That makes sense," "I see your point."

Reflect/summary: "You think ___ because ___."

Agree-to-disagree/close: "We see it differently, and that's okay."

Counts as success when:
Occurs within the time window, directed to the speaker, and

Requires no more than 1 verbal prompt (e.g., "Use your acceptance phrase"), and

Client does not argue, insult, or attempt to change the other person's view in that moment.

Does not include: eye-rolling, sarcasm, interrupting, debating after acceptance, or dismissive statements ("Whatever," "I don't care").
Onset/offset: Onset at the end of the other person's opinion; offset after the client gives a qualifying acceptance or 10s pass post-prompt.
Measurement

Primary: % of opinion opportunities with a qualifying acceptance (goal ≥1 per session initially).

Secondary: prompt level (0/1), latency (sec), and quality rating (0-3):
0 = no/negative response; 1 = brief neutral ("okay"); 2 = clear acceptance ("I hear you") or accurate summary; 3 = acceptance + respectful close ("agree to disagree" / gratitude).

Probable Function

Arguing/rejecting often serves attention, control/autonomy, or escape from cognitive effort/dissonance; acceptance can be maintained by positive social attention and smoother interactions. (Verify with brief ABC notes.)
Proactive Strategies

Antecedents & setup
Post a 2-Step Acceptance Script: Hear → Acknowledge (e.g., "I hear you," "Thanks for sharing").

Pre-correct before discussions: "When someone shares a view, use your acknowledge phrase."

Provide sentence starters card (I hear you • That's your view • Thanks for the idea • We can agree to disagree).

Prime topics that tend to trigger debate; choose low-stakes examples first.

Ensure AAC has quick buttons for acceptance phrases.

Teaching (model-lead-test)
Model short examples (therapist states an opinion → therapist/client uses acceptance phrase).

Role-play 3-5 trials (easy → slightly conflicting views).

Prompting hierarchy (max 1 verbal prompt)
Nonverbal: point to the script card/gesture "acknowledge."

Single verbal prompt: "Use your acceptance phrase."
 (If additional coaching is needed during teaching—not for mastery—offer a carrier: "Say: 'I hear you.'")

Reinforcement
Specific praise for calm, on-time acceptance ("Nice 'I hear you'—that kept the convo moving").

Token/point/brief access early (FR1), then thin; bonus for independent (0 prompts) or quality=3 responses.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Teach what to do instead of arguing:
Summarize first: "You think ___."

Agree to disagree: "We see it differently; that's okay."

Ask for turn later: "Can I share my view after you finish?"

Request time/space: "I need a minute," then return and use the script.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Early signs (voice rising, interrupting, repeating point):
Calm cue to card; soft voice: "Acceptance phrase."

Offer bounded choice: "Acknowledge now, or take 30 seconds and then acknowledge."

If arguing begins:
Neutral reset: "Pause—give your one acceptance line, then we'll move on."

Do not enter debate; deliver rich attention to the acceptance that follows.

The Client will compromise or apologize for what he did when given another's perspective with prompt

Target Behavior: Compromise or Apologize After Hearing Another's Perspective (≤1 verbal prompt)

Operational Definition

Compromise/Apologize after perspective is defined as the client, after an adult/peer states their perspective about a conflict or impact, doing both of the following within 30 seconds (or after a ≤2-minute micro-break if taught) and with no more than one brief verbal prompt:
Reflects the perspective in 1 short line (e.g., "You felt left out when I grabbed the marker."), and

Repairs via either:
a brief apology with a repair action (e.g., "I'm sorry I grabbed. I'll give it back and wait my turn."), or

a compromise proposal that is reasonable and specific (e.g., "How about you use it for 2 minutes, then me?").

Counts as success (examples):
"You were frustrated I talked over you. I'm sorry—your turn, then I'll go."

"You wanted a turn. Let's set a 2-minute timer and switch."

"You needed quiet. I'll lower my voice; can we sit apart?"

Does not count:
Arguing, blaming, or minimizing ("It wasn't a big deal"), sarcasm, or conditional apologies ("Sorry, but…").

Proposals that ignore the other's concern, or refusal to follow through on the agreed repair.

Needing >1 verbal prompt or exceeding the time limit (unless safety/regulation requires pause—score N/A).

Measurement

Opportunities: Any time an adult/peer states how the client's action affected them (contrived or natural).

Score each opportunity:
Independent = reflect + apology/compromise within 30 s, 0 prompts.

Prompted = same, after ≤1 brief verbal prompt (e.g., "Use your repair script.").

Not met = argues, no repair, >1 prompt, or >30 s without action.

Track: latency, whether apology or compromise was used, and follow-through on repair.

Probable Function (for arguing/refusal)

Commonly autonomy/control and face-saving, sometimes escape from accountability. Replacement is a fast, predictable reflect-then-repair routine that still preserves some control (choice of apology or compromise).
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine — "Hear → Reflect → Repair"

Hear: Listen without interrupting (listening body).

Reflect (1 line): "You felt/wanted ___ when I ___."

Repair:
Apologize + Action: "I'm sorry for __. I'll __ now."

Compromise: "How about __ then __ (timer/turns/space/role)?"

Practice via model → role-play → feedback using common classroom/clinic scenarios.

Visuals & Tools

Repair Card with two columns: Apologize + Action / Compromise plan (timer, turns, space, order, split).

Sentence frames on a small card:
Reflect: "You felt __ when I __."

Apology: "I'm sorry for __. I will __."

Compromise: "How about __ then __?"

Antecedent Supports

Adults/peers give clear, brief perspective: "I felt rushed when you grabbed the marker."

Provide timers and shared-materials visuals (turns/first-then).

Allow a micro-break card (≤2 min) if emotions spike before repairing.

Prompting & Fading (max 1 verbal prompt)

Nonverbal first (doesn't use the prompt): point to Repair Card or timer.

Verbal (single): "Use your repair—reflect, then apologize or compromise."

After a prompted success, schedule a transfer trial later (new scenario; longer silent wait) to build independence.

Reinforcement

DRA: Immediate, labeled praise + token/point for reflect + repair (bonus for independent and quick ≤15 s).

Keep reinforcement stronger than any attention gained from arguing.

Exchange menu: pick partner/spot, first turn, brief preferred activity.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Primary—Reflect + Repair (≤1 prompt): After another's perspective is stated, client gives one-line reflection and an apology+action or compromise, within 30 s, in ≥80% of opportunities with ≤1 verbal prompt across 3 sessions.

Follow-Through: Client completes the agreed repair (return item, timer/turns, move seats) within 10 s of agreement, ≥90% of successes.

Micro-Break Then Repair: If dysregulated, client uses a ≤2 min pause card, then completes Reflect + Repair, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Agree-to-Disagree + Act: If no compromise is accepted, client says, "Okay, I'll do it your way," and acts within 10 s, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Validate → Guide (≤7 words): "I hear you—reflect, then repair."

When-Then: "When you repair, then we move on/you pick the next step."

One-and-done prompting; avoid debate.

Planned ignore for minor defensiveness; reinforce the pivot to reflection/repair.

If escalation rises, offer micro-break, reduce audience, and restart with the Repair Card.

The client will describe one alternative point of view of a contrived social situation with 2 verbal prompts

Target Behavior: Describe one alternative point of view in a contrived social situation (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

Given a contrived social scenario (picture, 1-3 sentence vignette, short role-play), the client will state one plausible point of view different from their own for a named character within 15 seconds, using speech or an approved modality (AAC/sign).
A response counts as successful when it includes:
Whose view (names the other person),

Feeling or thought that person might have, and

Reason/evidence tied to the situation (because ___ / based on ___).

Example: "From Alex's point of view, he might feel left out because no one passed to him."
Prompts allowed: Up to two brief verbal prompts total (e.g., Prompt 1: "Take their point of view," Prompt 2: "Say the feeling and because."). Gestural/visual cues (point to sentence stem/feelings chart) don't count.
Non-examples: repeating own opinion; describing events without naming the other person's feeling/thought; implausible/teasing attributions; needing ≥3 verbal prompts.
Measurement

Present 3-5 scenarios per session (naturalistic or scripted).

Record for each: Success (Y/N), Prompts (0/1/2), Latency (s), and a quick accuracy rubric (0-3):
Names person (0/1) • Feeling/thought (0/1) • Reason/evidence (0/1).

Mastery criterion: ≥80% successful scenarios per session across 4 of 5 consecutive sessions (then fade to ≤1 prompt, then independent; increase scenario complexity).

Probable Function

Skill acquisition in perspective-taking/theory of mind; errors may be influenced by rigidity or escape from language demand. Confirm with brief ABC notes.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Skill (BST: Instruction → Modeling → Rehearsal → Feedback)

Sentence stem (card/AAC page):
 "From [Name]'s view, he/she/they might feel/think ___ because ___."

4-Cue Scan: Face • Body • Voice • Situation (what just happened?).

Model 1-2 examples per scenario → guided rehearsal → independent probe.

Use thought/feeling bubbles on pictures and highlight evidence.

Antecedent Supports

Start with clear, concrete scenarios (obvious cues), then shift to subtle/ambiguous ones.

Provide feelings chart (8-12 emotions) and a why-words list (because, after, when).

First-Then: "One perspective → sticker/choice."

Pre-teach contrast language: "another view," "one possibility is…"

Prompting (fade quickly)

Gesture to stem/feelings chart → Verbal Prompt 1 ("Take their view") → wait up to 10-15 s → Verbal Prompt 2 ("Feeling and because"). A third verbal prompt ends the trial as not met; then model and redo quickly.

Reinforcement

Behavior-specific praise: "You named Maya, said 'worried,' and gave a reason—great perspective."

Tokens/points per success; exchange at session end.

Natural reinforcement: brief discussion/choice of next scenario.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Primary skill: Provide one alternative POV with person + feeling/thought + reason within 15 s, ≤2 prompts, in 80% of scenarios.

If unsure: Ask a quick clarifier—"What just happened to [Name]?" or "How is [Name] feeling?"—then give the POV, ≤2 prompts, 80%.

Evidence check: State one observed cue ("His shoulders dropped, so he might feel disappointed"), ≤2 prompts, 70-80%.

Coping alternative (if stuck/frustrated): Request help or a 30-sec break instead of refusing, ≤1 prompt, 80%.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Low-profile coaching: point to stem → Prompt 1 ("Their view") → wait → Prompt 2 ("Feeling and because").

If rigidly defending own view: use neutral limit + reframe: "We're practicing possibilities. Give one possible feeling for their view."

Repair trial: If a trial is missed, model a correct example, then run a 10-20 s redo to contact reinforcement.

Keep tone brief/neutral; avoid debates about "right" vs "wrong" when multiple answers are plausible—reinforce evidence-based reasoning.

The client will figure out someone else's perspective in a contrived scenario

Target Behavior

Increase identifying another person's perspective (thoughts/feelings/goals/knowledge) in a contrived scenario with ≤2 verbal prompts.
Operational Definition

Given a short story, picture, comic, video clip, or brief role-play depicting a character, the client:
States the character's perspective in at least two domains:
Think (belief/idea) • Feel (emotion) • Want (goal/need) • Know (what they have/lack in info)

Provides one piece of evidence from the scenario ("because ___"), and

(Optional bonus) predicts a likely next action consistent with that perspective.

Counts as success when the client gives ≥2 domains + one evidence statement within 30-45 seconds and with ≤2 verbal prompts.
 Does not include: restating facts only, projecting own feelings without scenario evidence, or "I don't know" without using a taught support.
Measurement
Primary: Trials correct per session (e.g., 3-5 scenarios).

Quality score (0-3): 0 = off/none; 1 = one domain only; 2 = ≥2 domains or 1 domain + evidence; 3 = ≥2 domains + evidence (+/- prediction).

Onset/offset: Onset at end of scenario; offset after response or 45s post-last prompt.

Probable Function

Skill-acquisition target; nonresponse/deflection may function as escape from language load or reflect retrieval/processing needs. Success is maintained by attention, competence, and smoother social interactions.
Proactive Strategies

Visuals & setup
Perspective Map card: Think • Feel • Want • Know → Do Next.

Emotion wheel + "evidence stems" ("because I see/hear ___").

Keep early scenarios simple (1-2 characters); increase ambiguity gradually.

Ensure AAC has mental-state verbs ("thinks," "feels," "wants," "knows," "because").

Teaching (model-lead-test)
Therapist think-aloud on one example → guided practice → client solo.

Use contrast pairs (two characters with different info) to highlight knowledge differences (false-belief later).

Prompting (least → most; max 2 verbal prompts)
Point to Perspective Map (nonverbal).

Prompt 1: "Tell me two: think/feel/want/know."

Prompt 2 (scaffold): "Start: 'He feels ___ because ___.' Then add one more (think/want/know)."

Reinforcement
Labeled praise for domains + evidence; tokens/points/access early, then thin.

Bonus for independent (0-1 prompt) or quality = 3 responses.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Teach the client to request supports instead of "I don't know":
"More time / repeat / slower."

"Choices? (happy or worried?)"

"Show the picture again / pause video."

Sentence starters: "I think they feel ___ because ___."

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

If stuck: shrink task—one domain + evidence first, then add a second.

Provide minimal success path: therapist supplies domain, client supplies evidence (or vice versa).

Keep tone neutral; avoid quizzing loops—rotate to an easier scenario and return.

The Client will identify possible reasons for another's actions

Target Behavior: Identify Possible Reasons for Another's Actions (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

Reason inference is defined as the client stating at least one plausible reason (hypothesis) for why another person acted a certain way, based on observable clues (facial/body cues, context, past events, rules) in a brief scenario (picture, video clip, role-play, or real moment).
 A response counts when the client:
Uses uncertainty language (e.g., "maybe," "could be," "might") and a sentence frame such as "They might ___ because ___."

Cites at least one clue (e.g., "because the bell rang," "because he looks worried").

Responds within 10-15 seconds of the prompt or within 10 seconds after no more than two brief verbal prompts (e.g., "What might be the reason?" "Use your frame").

Does not count:
Literal restatements of the action without a reason ("He ran").

Mind-reading as a fact without uncertainty ("He hates me") without a clue.

Needing >2 verbal prompts or taking longer than the time window.

Measurement
Opportunities: 8-12 trials per session across mixed scenarios.

Score each trial:
Independent = plausible reason + 1 clue, 0 prompts.

Prompted = plausible reason + 1 clue with ≤2 verbal prompts.

Not met = no reason, no clue, implausible given evidence, >2 prompts.

Optional 2-point rubric: 2 = plausible + clear clue; 1 = somewhat plausible or vague clue; 0 = off-base/no clue.

Track latency, scenario type (school rule, emotion/need, accident/mistake, goal/plan), and number of hypotheses offered.

Probable Function (when the skill breaks down)

Likely skill/interpretation deficit, limited emotion vocabulary, inattention to context clues, or anxiety/rigid thinking. Replacement focuses on a quick, structured hypothesis-with-evidence response.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine — "SEE → CLUE → MAYBE"

SEE the action and context (face-body-situation scan).

Find one CLUE (emotion, rule, time cue, consequence, past event).

Say a MAYBE reason using the frame: "They might ___ because ___(clue)."

Provide modeling → guided practice → independent with increasing scenario complexity.
Reason Categories (visual "Reason Wheel")

Goal/Plan (they want to finish/get/avoid ___)

Emotion/Need (tired, hungry, worried, excited)

Rule/Expectation (it's the rule/bell rang/line starts)

Constraint (no time/materials, blocked path)

Mistake/Accident (dropped, forgot, misunderstood)

Sentence Frames / AAC

"They might ___ because ___."

"Maybe they feel ___; that's why they ___."

"It could be a rule—since ___, they ___."

AAC buttons: maybe / because / feels / rule / needs + emotion icons.

Task Design & Supports

Start with high-contrast pictures (obvious clues), then move to short videos and live role-plays.

Pre-teach 6-8 emotion words and key context words (late, bell, broken, hungry).

Use think-alouds and highlight clues (underline, circle).

Prompting & Fading (max 2 verbals)

Nonverbal first (doesn't count): point to the Reason Wheel or the clue.

Verbal 1: "What might be the reason?"

Verbal 2: "Use your 'might… because…' frame."
 Then wait 5-10 s silently. After prompted success, run a transfer trial with fewer cues.

Reinforcement

DRA: Labeled praise + token/point for hypothesis + clue (bonus for independent or <5 s latency).

Start FR1, thin to VR2-3 as fluent.

Exchange: choose next scenario/video, brief preferred activity.

Antecedent Modifications

Reduce noise/visual clutter during trials.

Provide choice of modality (picture vs. acted skit).

Preview session structure and Reason Wheel categories.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB) / Skill Targets

Primary—Hypothesis + Clue (≤2 prompts): Given a scenario, client states one plausible reason with one clue using uncertainty language within 10-15 s, in ≥80% of trials with ≤2 verbal prompts across 3 sessions.

Two Hypotheses Option: Client generates two different plausible reasons when asked, ≥60% of trials with ≤2 prompts (to reduce cognitive rigidity).

Clarify-Then-Reason: If unsure, client asks one clarifying question ("Was he late for class?") and then gives a reason, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Generalize to Natural Moments: In live interactions, client offers one hypothesis about a peer/teacher action at least 3 times per session, ≥70% success with ≤2 prompts.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

If the client fixates on a negative, steer to two-choice rescue: "Is it a rule reason or a feeling reason?"

Keep feedback brief/neutral ("Plausible—your clue matches.").

Offer a 30-60 s micro-break if frustration rises; resume with an easier, high-clue scenario.

Validate near-misses by affirming the clue and shaping the reason on the next trial.

The Client will demonstrate social awareness by observing peer reactions to a situation and adjusting their own response accordingly Once Per Session with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior: Focused Attention (5 minutes with ≤2 verbal prompts, once per session)

Operational Definition

Focused attention is defined as the client maintaining task-directed engagement with a non-preferred work task or structured play task for 5 consecutive minutes after a clear start directive, with no more than two brief verbal prompts from staff during that interval.
Counts as focused attention (examples):
Work tasks: eyes oriented to materials, writing/typing, reading assigned text, solving items, sorting/manipulating task items, answering brief task-related questions, or asking a short on-topic clarification (≤10s).

Play tasks: manipulating play items consistent with the activity goal (e.g., building with blocks to match a model, turn-taking in a board game, following a pretend-play role), orienting to partner/materials, giving/receiving brief play turns.

Does NOT count as focused attention:
Looking away from materials/partner >10 seconds, unrelated talking/noises, playing with off-task items, off-topic wandering, out-of-seat without materials, refusal statements, elopement, or behaviors that require staff to remove the demand.

Assistance beyond two brief verbal prompts (e.g., modeling, physical guidance, step-by-step coaching, or additional verbal prompting).

Start/Stop rules:
Start timing at the first observable task/play action after the start directive and when all materials are available.

Pause/terminate and mark "not met" if: a) any single off-task period >10s occurs, b) more than two verbal prompts are needed, c) safety concerns arise.

One opportunity per session: Score the first eligible non-preferred or structured play task presented that session (e.g., independent worksheet, Lego model build, board game with rules). "Non-preferred" determined by recent preference data/staff report within the past two weeks.

Measurement/criterion: Duration with integrity checks. Criterion met when the client maintains focused attention for 5:00 with ≤2 verbal prompts and no disqualifying assistance.
Probable Function

Primary: escape/avoidance of sustained effort or rule-bound play.
 Secondary: access to attention or preferred tangibles/activities obtained by delaying/ending the task/play.
Proactive Strategies

Antecedent setup
Deliver a concise start directive; preview expectations ("5 minutes of focused play/work").

Task/play shaping & chunking: break into 1-3 minute sub-goals with a visible checklist or "play plan" card.

Choice within task/play: order, materials, seating, role in play, or color/tool choice.

Interspersal/high-p: start with 2-3 easy/brief actions before moderate ones.

Environmental supports: minimize distractions; have all materials ready; use a visual timer and a first-then card ("First 5 minutes focused, then ___").

Rules for support: clarify that only two brief verbal prompts will be used during timing.

Reinforcement
Labeled praise every 60-90s of continuous attention ("Nice focus—sticking with your build!").

Terminal reinforcer at 5:00 (token, sticker, brief preferred activity). Thin interval praise gradually as success increases.

Instructional supports
Teach self-monitoring with a 5-box minute tracker (check one box per minute).

Teach a brief help script ("I need a hint") and a next-step self-talk ("Look → Do → Check").

Use neutral micro-prompts only when needed (max two): "Keep going," "What's next?"

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Appropriate Help Request: When stuck, client uses a 3-5 word help script and resumes within 10s after support.

Timed Micro-Break Request (outside the timed 5-min trial): One 30-60s break per chunk using a break card; returns when timer signals.

Self-Monitoring of Attention: Client marks each minute of focus and quietly re-orients using the card instead of seeking escape.

Role/Material Swap within Play: Client requests a role/material change (e.g., "Can I be the builder now?") instead of disengaging.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Brief, neutral redirection to FERB: "Use your help card." Avoid debates/lectures.

Prompt-then-pause: give one prompt, allow up to 10s; if needed, deliver the second and final prompt.

Contingent choice: "Focus for 2 more minutes or take a 30s reset, then finish."

Modify without removing: reduce to the next step or offer a single hint, then fade back to independence.

Protect contingencies: do not allow access to preferred items/attention for off-task behavior; reinforce any return-to-focus and FERBs.

Safety first: if escalation occurs, stop timing, follow the safety plan, and re-offer with a smaller chunk later.

Transitions

Preferred to Non Preferred Activity

The Client will use a verbal or visual signal to ask for an additional 2 minutes to finish his current activity with 2 verbal prompts

Target Behavior

Increase using a verbal or visual signal to ask for an additional 2 minutes to finish the current activity, with ≤2 verbal prompts, and then transitioning when the timer ends.
Operational Definition

When a transition cue is given (e.g., "Time to clean up," timer chime, visual "Next"), the client:
Requests a 2-minute extension using a verbal phrase (e.g., "Two more minutes, please") or a visual/AAC signal (e.g., 2-minute card/button, holding up two fingers), within 5-10 seconds,

Waits for acknowledgment, starts a visible 2-minute timer, and uses the time to wrap up (not to start new tasks), and

Stops and transitions at the timer's end with no arguing.

Counts as success when all are true:
Request made within the window with ≤2 verbal prompts (e.g., "Use your 2-minute request," "Say it now").

Client stays engaged in appropriate wrap-up during the 2 minutes.

Client transitions within 15 seconds of timer end.

Does not include: refusing without requesting, bargaining for longer after 2 minutes, starting new activities, or repeated requests in the same transition if limit has been reached.
Onset/offset: Onset at the transition cue; offset after the client has transitioned following the 2-minute timer (or immediately if request is not made).
Probable Function

Extension requests meet needs for escape/avoidance of abrupt stopping, autonomy/control over time, and task completion; problem behaviors at transitions often function for the same reasons. This goal teaches a functional communication alternative.
Proactive Strategies

Environment & antecedents
Post a Transition Plan: Warning → Choice → 2-Minute Option → Timer → Stop/Go.

Provide predictable warnings (e.g., 5-min and 1-min) and a visual schedule/first-then.

Create 2-Minute Tickets (1-2 per block/day) and a 2-minute AAC button.

Use a highly visible timer (sand timer or on-screen circle).

Define wrap-up actions: save, label, put away, note "where to start next time."

Teaching & Prompting

Model-Lead-Test (quick)
Model: Adult hears cue → says "Two more minutes, please" → starts timer → wraps up → stops.

Lead: Client echoes with support; adult fades help.

Test: New transition, client initiates request.

Prompting hierarchy (cap at 2 verbal prompts)
Nonverbal: point to 2-Minute Ticket/AAC/timer.

Verbal Prompt 1: "Use your 2-minute request."

Verbal Prompt 2 (if needed): "Say: 'Two more minutes, please.'"
 Then: "Start timer… wrap up… stop at beep."

Adult response script
If available: "Yes—2 minutes. Start now."

If limit used: "No more tickets. Stop now or 30-second wrap-up—you choose."

Reinforcement

DRA: specific praise for timely request and on-time stop ("Nice ask and clean stop!") + token/point/brief choice of next activity.

Bonus for independent (0-1 prompt) requests and zero bargaining.

Thin gradually (e.g., praise only; tokens every second success).

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Teach polite alternatives when a 2-minute extension isn't available:
Request a micro-wrap-up: "30 seconds to put this away?"

Save-and-return plan: "Can I save and finish after lunch?"

Request help to stop: "Help me end," "Mark my place."

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Early signs (sighing, clutching materials):
Neutral cue to ticket/AAC; offer bounded choice: "2 minutes now, or stop and pick it later."

Start timer immediately after request to contact success quickly.

The Client Will Prepare for and Move from one Activity to another, within 2 Minutes of a Verbal Prompt

Target Behavior: Prepare for and Move to the Next Activity Within 2 Minutes of a Verbal Prompt

Operational Definition

Transition success is defined as the client, after a single verbal prompt to change activities (e.g., "Time for math—put art away and come to Table 3"), doing all of the following within 2 minutes:
Prepare/Close current task (e.g., stop, put materials in the bin/folder, tidy area).

Move to the assigned location (walk directly, safe body).

Start the next activity's first step (sit/stand in spot, take out needed materials, or begin item #1).

Counts as success: brief questions ("Where do I sit?"), a quick micro-break already in the plan, or one short reminder without resetting the 2-minute clock.
Does not count: arguing/refusal, wandering/off-route stops >5 s, continuing the old activity, or requiring >1 verbal prompt. Exempt (score N/A) when directions are unclear, materials unavailable, or safety/medical issues occur.
Measurement
Start timer at verbal prompt; stop when the first step of the new activity begins.

Score each transition: Independent, Prompted (≤1), or Not met (>2 min or >1 prompt).

Optional: note latency to prepare and latency to start; track locations most difficult.

Probable Function (when delays occur)

Commonly escape/avoidance (ending a preferred task or starting a hard one), attention (seeking negotiation), or need for predictability/closure. Plan targets a quick, predictable close-move-start routine.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine — "Close → Carry → Go → Start"

Close: stop, put away, wipe/stack (use a 3-step Clean-Up Card).

Carry: take required item (folder/water) if applicable.

Go: straight line to spot (follow floor dots/arrows).

Start: do the posted first step (visual cue).

Practice with modeling → role-play → real transitions; use a visible 2-min timer during teaching.
Antecedent Supports

Visual schedule + First-Then for upcoming change.

Warnings (2 min → 1 min → 30 s) and finish options ("Choose a stopping point").

Pack-&-Check list (pencil, folder, water).

Clear landmarks (colored table signs, floor footprints).

Right-size difficulty on arrival (begin with an easy first item).

Prompting & Fading (max 1 verbal prompt)

Nonverbal first: point to schedule/timer/arrow (doesn't use the verbal allowance).

Verbal (single): "Close-Go-Start." (or the full instruction once).

If needed, one brief neutral reminder inside the 2 min ("Keep going") is allowed but doesn't restart time.

Reinforcement

DRA: Labeled praise + token/point for on-time transitions (bonus for <90 s or independent).

Transition menu: choose seat/partner, be materials helper, brief preferred activity after arrival.

Thin from FR1 to VR2-3 as consistency improves.

Environment & Regulation

Offer carry-over choice (bring a small item/fidget) if leaving a preferred area.

Provide noise/space options at destination.

Teach a 30-60 s micro-break on arrival (if needed) that still meets the 2-min window.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Primary—Close-Go-Start (≤1 prompt): Following one verbal prompt, client completes close, move, and begin first step within 2 minutes in ≥80% of transitions across 3 sessions.

Request for Help/Time: Within 10 s of the prompt, client says, "One minute to finish?" or "Where do I go?" then transitions within the agreed time, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Use of Visuals: Client references schedule/arrow/first-step card instead of asking repeatedly, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Reactive / De-escalation Strategies

When-Then: "When you're at Table 3 in 2 minutes, then you choose the warm-up."

Two-choice redirect (brief): "Blue dots or green path."

Reduce demand intensity if stuck: allow 1 carry-over item or shorten first task chunk.

Planned ignore of negotiation; reinforce the pivot the moment movement starts.

If time hits ~90 s with little progress, offer guided first step: "Walk with me—sit here—start #1."

Group Skills

Group Discussions
