The Client will actively participate in the group discussion by asking/answering a question posed by the group leader 2 times per session with a verbal prompt

Target Behavior: Actively participate in group discussion by asking/answering a question (2× per session, ≤1 verbal prompt)

Operational Definition

During a structured group discussion, the client will produce two on-topic contributions per session by either:
Answering a question posed by the group leader, or

Asking the group leader one relevant question about the topic.

A contribution counts when it:
Is audible/visible, directed to the leader (or via approved modality: AAC/sign).

Is on-topic and complete (≥2-word utterance or clear AAC equivalent).

Occurs within 10 seconds of the leader's cue/opportunity or the client's own hand-raise being acknowledged.

Requires ≤1 verbal prompt (e.g., "Ask your question," or "Answer now"). Gestural/visual cues don't count toward the one prompt.

Non-examples: off-topic comments; talking over peers; single-word fillers ("uh"); repeating others' answers; needing ≥2 verbal prompts; refusal/"I don't know" without an attempt after a cue.
Measurement

Provide 4-6 opportunities per session.

Record per opportunity: Type (Ask/Answer), Success (Y/N), Prompts (0/1), Latency (s), brief notes.

Session criterion: ≥2 successful contributions with ≤1 verbal prompt each.

Mastery: Meets session criterion on 4 of 5 consecutive sessions (then fade to 0 prompts or increase to 3-4 contributions).

Probable Function

Skill acquisition in group participation; competing behaviors may be maintained by escape (from performance demands) or attention (blurting). Confirm via brief ABC notes.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Micro-Skill (BST)

Script card / AAC quick phrases:
Answer stems: "I think…," "It means…," "The answer is…."

Ask stems: "Can you explain…?," "Why does…?," "What happens if…?"

Model → role-play → in vivo practice with rapid feedback.

Antecedent Supports

Prime before group: "Today you'll make two contributions—one answer and/or one question."

Visuals: "2 Checks" card (two boxes to tick), hand-raise icon, topic word bank.

Think time: 5-10 seconds after each question; allow jot notes.

Turn structure: name sticks or talk tokens to signal fair turns.

Choice: allow the client to pick which two opportunities they'll take (early/late).

Prompting (fade quickly)

Gesture/visual cue → single verbal prompt if needed ("Ask now" / "Answer now").

If a 2nd verbal prompt would be needed, end that opportunity, model a correct response, then run a quick repair trial later.

Reinforcement

Behavior-specific praise: "Nice—on-topic answer right after the cue."

Token/points: 1 per successful contribution; bonus for completing both early in the session.

Natural reinforcement: leader response, peer attention, choice of topic for a follow-up question.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Answer on cue: After the leader's question, client gives an on-topic answer within 10 s, ≤1 prompt, in 80% of opportunities.

Ask one relevant question: Client asks the leader a topic-related question within 10 s of a cue or natural pause, ≤1 prompt, in 80% of opportunities.

Clarification request (if unsure): "Can you repeat?" / "What does ___ mean?" ≤1 prompt, 80%.

Respectful pass (once/session max): "I'll pass on this one," while still meeting the 2 contributions later.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Low-profile cueing: point to the "2 Checks" card → brief cue ("Your turn") → single prompt if needed.

If off-topic/blurting: planned ignore (when safe) and immediately reinforce the next correct on-topic contribution.

If anxiety rises: offer binary choice—"Ask now or answer the next one," or a brief 30-60s reset; return quickly to an easier opportunity.

Repair trial: After a miss, model a good ask/answer, then run a short redo to contact success.

The Client Will Engage in an Activity Common among Peers, Like Discussing a Popular Toy or Playing a Trending Game, Once Per Session

Target Behavior

Increase engaging in an activity common among peers (e.g., discussing a popular toy/show, playing a trending game/sport/card game) once per session with ≤2 verbal prompts.
Operational Definition

Given a natural or contrived peer opportunity, the client:
Joins or starts a peer-common activity (game, conversation, shared build/drawing), and

Stays appropriately engaged by either
contributing ≥2 on-topic conversational turns (Answer/Comment/Ask), or

completing one full round/turn cycle of a game/activity,
 within the activity's norms, and with no more than 2 verbal prompts (e.g., "Pick one to join," "Ask to join").

Counts as success: one qualifying participation per session (conversation or game).
 Does not include: off-topic monologues, rule-breaking, hovering without joining, or quitting immediately (<60s) without using a taught strategy.
Onset/offset: Onset at invitation/cue or when peers are available; offset after the client meets the turn/round criterion or 5 minutes elapse (whichever comes first).
Measurement

Primary: Session success (Yes/No) = engaged in one peer-common activity as defined.

Secondary: prompts used (0/1/2), context (conversation/game/sport), duration or turns completed, rule reminders (count), quality rating (0-3):
0 = no/brief off-topic; 1 = minimal (1 turn or partial round); 2 = meets criterion; 3 = exceeds (e.g., +1 extra turn or adds a question/compliment).

Probable Function

Skill acquisition + attention/affiliation, autonomy/choice; competing behaviors (avoidance, solitary play, perseveration) may serve escape from social demands or high-interest fixation.
Proactive Strategies

Setup
Create a Peer-Common Menu (5-8 items) reflecting current class/recess trends (e.g., four-square, Uno, LEGO build, popular show chat, drawing swap).

Teach/review basic rules for 2-3 quick games and topic cards for 2-3 popular discussions.

Post Join Steps: Scan → Choose → Ask to join → Participate → Say "Your turn."

Pair with supportive peers; schedule a prime window (e.g., first 10 minutes of recess/center time).

Ensure AAC has: Can I join? • What are the rules? • My turn/Your turn • I like ___ • What do you think?

Teaching & Prompting

Model-Lead-Test
Model (adult + peer): join script and quick round or 2-turn chat.

Lead: client rehearses with light scaffolds.

Test: live opportunity with fading prompts.

Prompting hierarchy (max 2 verbal)
Nonverbal: point to Peer-Common Menu / Join Steps.

Verbal 1: "Pick one and ask to join."

Verbal 2: "Do one round / give two turns."

Micro-skills
Conversation (AAA): Answer → Add detail → Ask a question.

Games: Rule card → My turn → Your turn marker; keep entries ≤10-15s.

Exit script: "Good game/Thanks, I'm done for now."

Reinforcement

DRA: immediate, labeled praise + token/point/access after the qualifying join (and on-time "good game/thanks").

Bonus for independent (0-1 prompt) participation, following rules, or using AAA.

Thin reinforcement as consistency improves.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Teach to ask skillfully instead of avoiding:
"Choices? Two I can join?"

"What are the rules? / Quick version?"

"Short turn first?" (one round)

"On deck next?" (wait with timer/marker)

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Early signs (hovering, "boring," rigid topic):
Offer A/B choice from the menu; cue join script.

If fixated off-topic: link & pivot ("You like cars—want to draw cars at the art table with them?").

If overwhelm/frustration:
Brief reset break (1 min), then re-enter with short turn goal or easier activity.

The client will initiate a verbal interaction with a peer in the social setting

Target Behavior: 
 Initiating verbal interaction with peers in social settings.
&nbsp;
Motivating Operation:
 Fostering social skills, increasing self-confidence, and enhancing peer relationships.
Antecedent Procedures:
 1. Place the client in social settings where peer interactions are encouraged, such as playdates, school, or community events.
 2. Pre-teach skills related to initiating conversations, such as greeting phrases or opening questions.
 3. Make the client aware that the goal is to initiate verbal interactions with peers.
&nbsp;
Discriminative Stimulus:
 The presence of peers in a social setting where interaction is possible and encouraged.
Appropriate Response:
 The client will independently initiate a verbal interaction with a peer, such as saying "Hi, how are you?" or "Do you want to play?" to be considered a successful trial.
&nbsp;
Error Correction Procedures:
 1. **Modeling**: If the client does not initiate, the therapist or caregiver models how to start a conversation.
 2. **Prompting**: Use verbal or visual prompts to encourage the client to initiate a conversation.
 3. **Immediate Feedback**: Provide specific feedback about the appropriateness and effectiveness of the initiation attempt.
 4. **Reinforcement**: Positive reinforcement is provided when the client successfully initiates a conversation.
&nbsp;
Criteria for Mastery: 
 The client will successfully initiate verbal interactions with peers in 8 out of 10 opportunities across 3 consecutive data collection sessions.
&nbsp;
This ABA goal is designed to help the client become more proactive in social settings by taking the initiative to start conversations. This is an important skill for forming and maintaining social relationships.
Instructional Notes

MO:
 Fostering social skills, increasing self-confidence, and enhancing peer relationships.
 
 Antecedent Procedures:
Place the client in social settings where peer interactions are encouraged, such as playdates, school, or community events.
 2. Pre-teach skills related to initiating conversations, such as greeting phrases or opening questions.
 3. Make the client aware that the goal is to initiate verbal interactions with peers.
&nbsp;

 
 SD:
 The presence of peers in a social setting where interaction is possible and encouraged.
 
 Response:
 The client will independently initiate a verbal interaction with a peer, such as saying "Hi, how are you?" or "Do you want to play?" to be considered a successful trial.
 
 Prompting:
 visual prompt,verbal prompt,gestural prompt,modeling
 
 Error Correction:
 1. **Modeling**: If the client does not initiate, the therapist or caregiver models how to start a conversation. 2. **Prompting**: Use verbal or visual prompts to encourage the client to initiate a conversation. 3. **Immediate Feedback**: Provide specific feedback about the appropriateness and effectiveness of the initiation attempt. 4. **Reinforcement**: Positive reinforcement is provided when the client successfully initiates a conversation.
 
 Reinforcement:
 differential social reinforcement,socially mediated reinforcemen

The client will join in group discussions with 2 verbal prompts

Target Behavior: Join Group Discussions (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

Joining a group discussion is defined as the client contributing at least one on-topic verbal turn during a designated discussion block (whole group or small group). A contribution counts when the client:
Gets the floor appropriately (raises hand/waits for pause or is called on),

Gives a brief, relevant comment, answer, or question (≤15 seconds, level 2-3 voice),

Acknowledges others (e.g., "I agree with Maya because…," "To add to what you said…"), and

Yields the floor when finished.

Prompt allowance: Up to two brief verbal prompts (e.g., "Ask a related question," "Share one idea"). Nonverbal cues/visual taps don't use the two prompts.
Does not count:
Off-topic remarks, interrupting, side conversations, monologues >15 seconds, or contributions given after >2 verbal prompts.

Safety/medical issues or unclear expectations (score N/A).

Measurement
Opportunity: Each scheduled discussion block (e.g., morning meeting, literature circle, science talk).

Score per opportunity:
Independent = at least one appropriate contribution, 0 prompts.

Prompted = contribution after ≤2 verbal prompts.

Not met = no contribution or >2 prompts.

Optional: also tally # of contributions, latency from prompt to speak, and quality (0-2: off-topic/weak/strong).

Probable Function

Nonparticipation often reflects skill/fluency deficits, performance anxiety, uncertainty about when to speak, or escape from language demands. Replacement is a clear, low-effort turn routine and sentence starters.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine — "R-W-S-B" (Raise → Wait → Speak → Brief)

Raise hand or make eye contact when invited.

Wait to be called / for a pause.

Speak on-topic (comment/answer/question).

Brief: ≤15 seconds, then yield.

Pair with C-Q-C content frame: Comment → Question → Connect (optional).
Visuals & Tools

Discussion Starters card: "I think…," "I noticed…," "Why do you think…?" "I agree/disagree because…," "To add…."

Turn token/talking chip (one chip = one turn).

Topic word on a sticky to anchor relevance.

Antecedent Modifications

Preload think time (10-15 s) and first question the client can answer.

Arrange seating for easy eye contact with the facilitator.

Establish a turn order or call list so the client knows when they're up.

Start with high-interest topics; gradually increase complexity.

Offer a pass once option with a planned re-entry later in the same discussion.

Prompting & Fading (max 2 verbals)

Nonverbal first: point to starter card/hand-raise gesture (no prompt counted).

Verbal 1: "Share one idea."

Verbal 2: "Ask a related question or add on."
 Then wait 5-10 s silently. After a prompted success, run a transfer trial in the next discussion with longer wait for independence.

Reinforcement

DRA: Labeled praise + token/point for each appropriate contribution (bonus for independent or first-try).

Exchange: pick next topic, be discussion helper, brief preferred activity.

Periodic group reinforcement (table/star) when everyone contributes once.

Boundaries & Repairs

If off-topic or too long, cue two-choice redirect: "Keep it to one sentence or ask a question."

Teach graceful repair: "Let me try again—My question is…"

If anxious, allow written first: jot your sentence, then read it.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Primary—One Appropriate Turn (≤2 prompts): During a discussion, the client produces ≥1 on-topic contribution with correct turn-taking and ≤15 s length, in ≥80% of discussion opportunities with ≤2 verbal prompts across 3 sessions.

Independent Turn: Increase to ≥50% of discussions with 0 prompts by mastery phase.

Ask-One Option: If unsure what to say, the client uses a starter question from the card, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Agree/Disagree Frame: "I (agree/disagree) because __," ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Brief cue → wait: "Share one sentence." (5-10 s)

Two-choice rescue: "Comment or question?"

Planned ignore of side comments; reinforce the pivot to an appropriate contribution.

Offer a 30-60 s micro-break if stress rises; re-enter with an easy prompt.

Group Instruction

The client will join group activities during group instruction with 2 verbal prompts

Target Behavior: Join group activities during group instruction (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

When a group activity/instruction begins (e.g., morning meeting, centers, small-group lesson, team game), the client will complete the Join Chain:
Move to the group spot (assigned seat/area) within 30 seconds of the adult cue or activity start.

Orient body to the leader/materials (seated/standing as expected; materials ready).

Follow the first direction (e.g., "eyes on speaker," "open book," "sit crisscross").

Remain with the group and participate for at least 5 minutes or until the natural transition, whichever comes first.

A trial is successful when all steps occur with calm/safe behavior and ≤2 brief verbal prompts total (e.g., Prompt 1: "Join the group," Prompt 2: "Take your spot"). Gestural/visual cues do not count toward the two prompts.
Non-examples: ignoring/arguing; lingering outside the group area; joining but leaving within 2 minutes; requiring ≥3 verbal prompts; joining only after problem behavior is reinforced; unsafe behavior (yelling, aggression, property disruption).
Measurement

Opportunities: Each new group block = 1 opportunity. Aim for 2-4 opportunities per session.

Record: Success (Y/N), # verbal prompts (0/1/2), latency to take spot (s), time remained (min), and brief notes.

Optional quality check during the 5-minute window: 30s whole-interval for "on-expectation" (attending, following directions).

Mastery criterion: ≥80% successful opportunities per session across 4 of 5 consecutive sessions (then fade to ≤1 prompt or increase required duration).

Probable Function

Non-joining often contacts escape/avoidance (from task/peer density), attention (through negotiation), tangibles (staying with preferred items), or automatic reinforcement (sensory). Confirm with ABC data.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Join Chain (model → role-play → in vivo)

Mini-script: "Join → Spot → Orient → First direction → Stay." Practice outside instruction first.

Start ritual: consistent cue (chime/hand signal + "Join now").

Behavioral momentum: two easy actions before the join cue ("Touch your name, bring your pencil").

Priming: preview the plan ("In 2 minutes we'll join the group—your spot is blue dot.").

Antecedent Supports

Visuals: First-Then board ("Join group → Choice"), mini "Join Chain" card, visual schedule, timer.

Transition aids: 2-min/30-sec countdown, clear path to seating, materials pre-staged at the spot.

Choice architecture: pick seat (row/edge/close to leader), pick fidget (quiet), pick role (materials helper, note-taker).

Task design: open with high-success tasks or brief interactive warm-up.

Reinforcement

Immediate, specific praise on arrival ("You joined in 10 seconds—great start").

DRA: tokens/points for completing the Join Chain; bonus for quick join (≤10s) or 0 prompts.

Natural reinforcement: access to group game/leader attention/peer interaction.

Thin token density as success stabilizes; keep natural reinforcement.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Appropriate Delay/Break Request: "30 seconds then I'll join?" or "Short break, then group," honored via First-Then, ≤2 prompts, 80% of need moments.

Help/Support Request: "Can you walk with me?" / "Where's my spot?" within 10s of cue, ≤2 prompts, 80%.

Seat/Tool Choice: Client asks for preferred seat or quiet fidget instead of refusing, ≤2 prompts, 80%.

Return After Brief Exit: If the client steps out (with permission), returns within 10s of timer/ cue in 80% of instances.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Low-profile prompting: gesture to spot/visual → Prompt 1 ("Join the group") → wait up to 10s → Prompt 2 ("Take your spot").

Binary choice if hesitation: "Walk with me or go on your own." / "Blue dot or green chair."

Do not let avoidance access preferred items/attention; deliver attention and choices after the Join Chain.

Neutral reset if refusal: restate First-Then; use a brief guided start ("I'll walk with you"), fade support quickly.

If escalation/unsafe behavior: follow site plan; after calm, run a repair trial (short successful join) and reinforce.

Task Completion

Completing Non Preferred Tasks

The Client will initiate a non preferred task without getting distracted by other activity or task, upon deciding to do an activity or being given instructions to work on a given task

Target Behavior

Increase starting a non-preferred task promptly and staying focused through the initial work period after deciding to work or receiving an instruction, with ≤2 verbal prompts.
Operational Definition

Following either (a) the client's decision to start an activity or (b) a clear adult instruction to begin a specific task, the client:
Initiates the task by performing the first required step within 10 seconds, and

Maintains focus for an initial "Focus Start" period of 2 minutes (or until Step 1-2 are completed), without shifting to another task/activity.

Off-task/distraction (track as errors):
Looking away from task materials or talking about unrelated topics for >3 consecutive seconds,

Handling unrelated items/devices, opening a different app/page/activity,

Leaving seat/area (without permission) or starting a different task.

Does not include: waiting for missing materials, clarification requests, brief regulation strategies (e.g., one deep breath) ≤3s, or teacher-directed transitions.
Onset/offset: Onset at instruction/decision ("Start math now") or when the client states intent to begin; offset after 2 minutes of continuous on-task behavior (or completion of the first 1-2 steps), or upon a qualifying off-task error.
Measurement

Primary:
Latency to start (sec) from cue → first step (goal ≤10s).

Focus Start success (Yes/No): 2 minutes with no off-task >3s and no task-switching.

Secondary: prompts used (0/1/2), number/type of off-task errors, need for help/materials, setting.

Intensity (highest per trial): 1 = brief glance off-task; 2 = handles unrelated item/switches window; 3 = leaves area or refuses.

Probable Function

Competing behaviors may be maintained by escape/avoidance (hard/long tasks), attention, access to tangibles/screens, or automatic (boredom/novelty seeking). (Confirm with ABC data.)
Proactive Strategies

Set up for success
Task Preview Card (What / How much / How to know it's done / What's next).

First-Then: Task → Preferred 2-3 min.

Right-size: break into micro-steps; provide worked examples or checklists.

Choice within boundaries: where to sit, which tool, order of sub-tasks.

Environment: clear desk, put unrelated items away, notifications off, headphones/quiet corner if helpful.

Noncontingent attention at start blocks (brief check-in) to reduce attention-seeking detours.

Teaching & Prompting

Start Routine (S.T.A.R.T.)
See the cue, Tell yourself "Start now," Act on first step, Run 2-min timer, Tell next step if finished early.
 Post this as a mini-card.

Prompting hierarchy (cap at 2 verbal prompts)
Gesture/point to START card/checklist/timer (wait 5-10s).

Verbal 1: "Begin the first step now."

Verbal 2: "Start and stay with it for 2 minutes."
 If materials/clarity are the barrier, deliver brief setup (≤15s) then re-cue.

Micro-start (5-15s) if needed: model the first action (write name, open doc, place the first block) → client continues while 2-min timer runs.
Reinforcement

DRA: labeled praise + token/point/access to the Then item for (a) ≤10s start and (b) 2-min Focus Start success.

Bonus for independent (0-1 prompt) initiation and zero off-task.

Thin gradually: extend Focus Start to 3-5 minutes over time before access.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Within 10 seconds of the cue, allow:
"Help, please" (specific: "Which step first?"),

"More time?" (30-second countdown, then start),

"Different order?" (swap sub-tasks),

"Timer please" (client requests the 2-min Focus Start timer).
 These do not replace starting; they enable starting.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Early signs (stalling, scanning room, reaching for unrelated item):
Move closer, point to START card/timer, keep language brief; offer A/B choice: "Start with #1 or #2?"

If off-task begins: neutral reset—"Back to task for 2 minutes," start/point to timer. Do not engage in debate.

If refusal or leaving area:
Provide one bounded choice (start micro-step now or 30-sec countdown then start).

After compliance, deliver quick reinforcement for the first 30-60s on-task to re-contact success

The Client will clean up around his spot after eating with 1 verbal prompt

Target Behavior: Clean Up Eating Area (≤1 verbal prompt)

Operational Definition

Post-meal clean-up is defined as the client completing a brief, clearly posted clean-up routine within 2 minutes of the end-of-meal cue or within 10 seconds of one brief verbal prompt (e.g., "Time to clean up"). Success requires all of the following for the client's spot:
Clear & Trash: Put all disposable items in the trash/recycling; place tray/dishes in the bin.

Wipe: Wipe table area (and chair edge if needed) with provided wipe/cloth.

Floor Check: Pick up visible items/crumbs at feet; sweep to dustpan if provided.

Reset: Return caddy/napkins, push in chair, sanitize/handwash, and proceed to the next activity.

Does not count:
Needing more than one verbal prompt, exceeding 2 minutes, or leaving the area messy.

Situations with missing supplies, blocked trash access, unclear cue, or safety concerns (score N/A).

Measurement
Opportunity: Each meal/snack where supplies and the routine are available.

Score per opportunity:
Independent = completes all steps ≤2 min with 0 prompts (nonverbal gesturing allowed).

Prompted = completes ≤2 min after ≤1 verbal prompt.

Not met = >1 prompt, >2 min, or steps incomplete.

Optional 0-2 quality rubric: 2 = all steps/clean; 1 = minor miss; 0 = incomplete.

Probable Function (for non-clean-up)

Commonly escape/avoidance of an unpreferred chore, attention (negotiating), or skill/organization deficits. Replacement is a quick, predictable checklist routine with clear end point.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine — "Trash → Wipe → Floor → Reset"

Model → Guided practice → Timed practice using the exact supplies/location.

Post a 4-step picture card at the table and on a portable ring.

Antecedent Supports

Clear end cue ("All done—clean-up time") + 2-minute visual timer.

Supplies ready: wipes/cloths, caddy, dustpan, visible trash can.

Assign zones/footprints so the client knows what "my spot" includes.

For big messes, break into micro-steps ("Table first, then floor").

Prompting & Fading (max 1 verbal prompt)

Nonverbal first (doesn't count): point to the card/timer, tap the trash icon.

Verbal (single): "Clean up now." Then wait quietly up to 10 s.

After prompted success, run a transfer trial next snack with a longer silent wait.

Reinforcement

DRA: Labeled praise + token/point for on-time, complete clean-up (bonus for independent or <90 s).

Small exchanges: line leader/first choice in the next activity, sticker, or minute of preferred task.

Use group reinforcement (table star) when everyone resets their area.

Environment & Safety

Use food-safe wipes/cloths; store chemicals out of reach.

Provide gloves if needed for sensory concerns; offer a scraper for sticky spots.

Address allergies and disposal rules per site policy.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Primary—Complete Clean-Up (≤1 prompt): Following the cue, client completes Trash → Wipe → Floor → Reset in ≤2 minutes with ≤1 verbal prompt, ≥80% of opportunities across 3 sessions.

Ask for Help/Supplies: If stuck, client requests needed item or brief help within 10 s, then resumes, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Self-Timing: Client starts timer independently and races it to finish, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Reactive / De-escalation Strategies

Two-choice redirect: "Table first or floor first?"

When-Then: "When you finish clean-up, then you pick the next center."

Planned ignore of minor complaints; reinforce the pivot the moment cleaning begins.

If stalling at ~90 s, provide brief guided step ("I'll hold the bin—trash now") and fade next time.

Staying on Task

The Client will stay focused on task/activity until the task/activity is complete

Target Behavior: Stay focused on a task/activity until it is complete (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

During a designated work activity (e.g., seatwork, worksheet, center task, chore, project), the client will remain on-task from the start cue until the task is completed.
On-task means the client is:
Oriented to materials/teacher (eyes/body toward work).

Actively responding (writing, reading, manipulating materials, following steps) or appropriately waiting for the next step.

Using an approved strategy when stuck (help/break/clarify).

Staying in the assigned area unless given permission to move.

Completion is defined before starting (e.g., finish all 10 problems at ≥80% accuracy, copy notes to the end of the page, assemble parts per checklist, or work until the teacher ends the activity).
Success for a trial (one task/activity) is scored when the client:
Is on-task in ≥85% of 30-second whole intervals from start to completion,

Completes the task as defined,

Requires ≤2 brief verbal prompts total (gestural/visual cues don't count), and

Shows no maladaptive behavior (e.g., yelling, aggression, elopement, property destruction).

Non-examples: looking away or engaging with unrelated items for >5 seconds, off-topic talk, head on desk, using unassigned electronics, leaving area without permission, refusing/arguing, needing ≥3 verbal prompts.
Measurement

Mark one target task per opportunity (aim for 2-3 per session).

Record: % on-task (30s whole-interval), completion (Y/N), latency to start (s), prompts (0/1/2), maladaptive (Y/N), time to completion (min).

Mastery criterion: Meets success on ≥80% of tasks per session across 4 of 5 consecutive sessions. Then raise to ≥90% on-task and/or fade to ≤1 prompt.

Probable Function

Off-task behavior frequently contacts escape/avoidance (hard/long tasks), attention (peer/adult engagement), tangibles (competing items), or automatic reinforcement (sensory/self-stim). Confirm with brief ABC data.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the "Work-Ready" Routine (model → role-play → in vivo)

Start in 10s after cue.

Materials check (pencil, paper, tools).

Goal strip: write the task goal ("Do #1-10 at 80%").

Plan if stuck: Help / Break / Clarify.

Begin (first small step).

Task Design & Supports

Chunking: break tasks into small steps; use checklists.

Exemplars: provide a worked model or sample product.

Choice: order of problems, tool, seat, partner when appropriate.

Timers & visuals: visual schedule, First-Then ("Finish → Choice"), quiet fidget if needed.

Behavioral momentum: start with 2-3 easy items.

Clear end signal: checklist box, "Done" bin, or teacher stamp.

Prompting (fade quickly)

Gesture/visual cue → Verbal Prompt 1 ("Back to work") → wait up to 10 s → Verbal Prompt 2 ("Use your plan: help/break/clarify").

If a 3rd verbal prompt would be needed, pause, model the next step, and run a brief repair trial.

Reinforcement

DRA: behavior-specific praise for on-task and step completions.

Task-completion payoff: immediate access to a preferred mini-choice/activity.

Tokens/points: earn by minute or by chunk; exchange at session end.

Thin token density as independence increases; keep natural reinforcement (grades, finished product, praise).

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Help Request: "Help please," or AAC help within 10 s of difficulty, ≤2 prompts, in 80% of need moments.

Brief Break Request: "Break please (1-2 min)," returns within 10 s of timer, ≤2 prompts, in 80%.

Clarification Request: "What's step 1?" / "Can you show an example?" ≤2 prompts, 80%.

Self-monitor & reset: Uses a self-check card ("On task? Next step? Timer set?") every 2-3 minutes, ≤1 prompt, in 80%.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Low-profile redirection: point to goal strip/timer → brief cue ("Back to step #__").

Match consequence to function:
Escape-maintained: don't end the task after off-task behavior; do honor a break immediately after a proper request.

Attention-maintained: minimize attention to off-task; give rich attention for on-task.

Tangible-maintained: withhold competing items during off-task; deliver after completion or FCR.

Neutral reset: reduce the next chunk, model one step, then fade support.

After escalation: follow safety plan; once calm, run a short repair trial (1-2 minutes on-task) to contact reinforcement.

Hygiene

Personal Appearance

The Client will make sure his face is clean after finishing a meal or snack with 1 verbal prompt

Target Behavior

Increase making sure the face is clean after finishing a meal/snack with ≤1 verbal prompt.
Operational Definition

After the client finishes eating or drinking (last bite/sip set down), the client:
Initiates a face-cleaning routine within 10 seconds, and

Removes visible food/liquid residue from the lips, corners of mouth, chin, cheeks, and around the nose,

Completes the routine and disposes of the napkin/wipe and washes or sanitizes hands,
 within 2 minutes, with no more than 1 verbal prompt (e.g., "Clean your face.").

Counts as clean: no visible residue on the specified areas by mirror check or one-tissue test (tissue stays clean after a light dab).
 Does not include: grooming beyond face (e.g., hands—tracked under handwashing), spills on clothing, or touch-ups outside the 2-minute window unless using the taught repair.
Onset/offset: Onset at the end of eating; offset when face is clean and hands are washed/sanitized (or 2 minutes elapse).
Measurement

Primary: Opportunity success (Yes/No) per meal/snack = initiated ≤10s, face clean ≤2 min, ≤1 verbal prompt.

Secondary: prompt level (0/1), latency to start (s), completion time (s), quality (0-3)
0 = not completed/refused; 1 = partial (missed area); 2 = all areas clean; 3 = clean + independent (0 prompts).

Probable Function

Skill/independence goal; noncompliance often functions as escape from sensory feel (wet/rough wipes), attention (joking/avoidance), or automatic (inattention/forgetting).
Proactive Strategies

Materials & setup
Place napkin/wipe and small mirror at the eating area; add a "Clean Face" visual on lunchbox/tray.

Offer sensory options: soft napkin, damp paper towel, hypoallergenic wipe, patting instead of rubbing.

Pre-teach a single routine and practice with washable marker "food dots" on face for fun accuracy checks.

Teach the 4-Step "CHeCK-Wash" routine (card/poster):
Check in mirror (look at lips, corners, chin, cheeks, nose).

Help yourself with napkin/wipe (wipe front-to-back; pat if sensitive).

Check again (quick scan / tissue test).

Klean-up: trash the wipe/napkin → Wash/Sanitize hands.

Prompting hierarchy (max 1 verbal)
Nonverbal: point to "Clean Face" card/mirror/napkin (does not count toward the verbal limit).

Single verbal prompt: "Clean your face." (no additional verbal coaching for mastery trials).

If still learning (teaching phase only), you may model one stroke silently, then fade.

Reinforcement
Labeled praise ("Nice quick clean—corners and chin too!").

Token/point/privilege early; thin to praise/natural feedback (peers/comfort).

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Teach acceptable supports instead of refusal/avoidance:
"Different wipe, please," "It's too scratchy," "Help with the corners," or "I'll pat, not rub."

"Did I miss a spot?" (adult points silently; client fixes).

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Early signs (joking, stalling, pushing wipe away):
Keep tone neutral; use the nonverbal point to card/mirror; offer sensory choice ("napkin or damp towel?").

If the single verbal prompt is used and client resists, allow patting and reduce pressure; reinforce first correct swipe.

Responsibility

Responsibility for Belongings

The Client Will Organize His personal belongings Once Per Session with a Reminder

Target Behavior: Organize Personal Belongings Once Per Session (≤1 reminder)

Operational Definition

Belongings organization is defined as the client completing a brief, posted pack-up routine for his own items once per session within 3 minutes of the cue, with no more than one reminder (verbal or visual). A successful routine includes all of the following:
Gather: collect all personal items from work area/common areas (backpack, folder, device, jacket, lunchbox, water bottle).

Sort & Stow: place items in their labeled spots (backpack pockets/cubby/device case/charging station).

Toss/Return: throw away trash/recycling; return borrowed materials to the correct bin.

Secure: zip/close backpack/cases; hang jacket; cap water bottle.

Spot Check: use the posted 2-point check—"Desk clear?" "All items packed?"—then move to the next activity/exit.

Does not count: needing >1 reminder, exceeding 3 minutes, rummaging in others' belongings, leaving area messy/missing required items. Score N/A if supplies/labels are missing or a safety issue interrupts.
Measurement

Opportunity: the scheduled pack-up time (once per session).

Scoring:
Independent = completes all steps ≤3 min with 0 reminders.

Prompted = completes ≤3 min after ≤1 brief reminder ("Organize your belongings now" or a single visual cue).

Not met = >1 reminder, >3 min, or steps incomplete.

Optional quality rubric (0-2): 2 = all steps & area clean; 1 = minor miss; 0 = incomplete. Track missing-item repairs and actual duration.

Probable Function

Delays/avoidance often serve escape from a low-interest chore, attention (negotiation), or reflect organization skill deficits. Replacement is a fast, predictable checklist routine with clear end point.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine — "Gather → Stow → Toss → Secure → Check"

Model → Guided practice → Timed practice using the client's real items.

Post a 5-step picture card on desk/cubby and a mini card on the backpack.

Environment & Antecedents

Label bins/pockets with icons/colors; add name labels to all personal items.

Use floor dots leading to backpack/cubby; keep trash/recycling visible.

Set a 3-minute visual timer when the pack-up cue is given.

Provide a Pack List (icons) for consistent items (folder, device, water, jacket).

Reminder (max 1)

One brief reminder only (verbal or visual): "Pack up now," or tap the picture card/timer once. Then allow 10-15 s of quiet work time.

Reinforcement

DRA: Labeled praise + token/point for completing the routine on time (bonus for independent or <2 minutes).

Exchange ideas: first choice of next activity/seat, line leader, sticker.

Occasional group bonus when the whole table meets the routine.

Troubleshooting Supports

Missing-Item Protocol: 30-second search → ask, "Where is the ___?" → check last location → mark on Missing Item Log if not found.

Offer sensory aids (gloves/wipes) if mess textures are a barrier.

Keep a small lost-and-found bin at the station.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Primary—Complete Pack-Up (≤1 reminder): After the cue, client finishes Gather → Stow → Toss → Secure → Check within 3 minutes with ≤1 reminder, in ≥80% of sessions across 3 consecutive weeks.

Pack List Self-Check: Client uses the picture Pack List and points/touches each icon while packing, ≥80% with ≤1 reminder.

Ask-for-Help Briefly: If stuck or missing an item, client asks a single specific question ("Where does the tablet go?") and resumes, ≥80% with ≤1 reminder.

Reactive / De-escalation Strategies

Two-choice redirect: "Start with desk or backpack?"

When-Then: "When you finish pack-up, then you pick the next activity."

Planned ignore of minor complaints; reinforce the pivot as soon as packing begins.

If lagging near 2 minutes, provide one guided step ("Zip the backpack; I'll hold it") and fade this support next session.

The Client will organize personal belongings by placing items in labeled areas at session end Once Per Session with 1 Verbal Prompt.

Target Behavior: Organize personal belongings by placing items in labeled areas at session end (1×/session, ≤1 verbal prompt)

Operational Definition

At the end-of-session cue (e.g., "Pack up," bell, timer), the client will complete the Pack-Up Routine:
Gather assigned belongings (e.g., folder/device/water bottle/jacket).

Sort & place each item into its labeled area (picture/word labels; e.g., backpack pocket, cubby bin, hook, device cart slot).

Check off a pack-up checklist and signal "done."

Success (for the one daily opportunity) = the client completes all required steps within 3 minutes of the cue (or before leaving the area, whichever comes first), with ≤1 verbal prompt total (gestural/visual prompts allowed), and no maladaptive behavior (e.g., arguing, throwing items, refusal, property misuse).
 If an item is missing, the client reports it calmly ("My bottle is missing") and marks it—this still counts.
Non-examples: leaving items on desk/floor, stuffing items in wrong places, needing ≥2 verbal prompts, ignoring labels, unsafe handling.
Measurement

Opportunities: 1 pack-up window per session.

Data to record: Success (Y/N), verbal prompts (0/1), time to complete (min:sec), % items correctly placed (# correct / total on checklist), notes/missing items.

Mastery criterion: Success on 4 of 5 consecutive sessions. Then fade to 0 verbal prompts or shorten time to ≤2 minutes.

Probable Function

Difficulties often reflect escape/avoidance (transition/task demand), attention (negotiating at dismissal), or executive-skill challenges (organization/planning). Address with clear routines, visuals, and quick reinforcement.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Pack-Up Routine (BST)

Instruction → Model → Rehearsal → Feedback outside transition time.

Task analysis posted:
Gather → 2) Sort by label → 3) Place → 4) Check list → 5) "Done."

Color/shape coding (blue dot = backpack area; hanger icon = hook).

Pack-Up Staging: 5 minutes before end, place belongings in a staging zone on desk to reduce search time.

Antecedent Supports

Visual checklist (words/pictures/AAC) with 4-8 items max.

Labeled areas are obvious & consistent (large, contrasting labels).

Timer visible for the 3-minute window.

First-Then: "Pack up → Choice time/line leader."

Materials audit once weekly (teach where each item lives).

Prompting (fade quickly)

Gestural/visual cue to checklist/labels → single verbal prompt if needed ("Pack-up routine").

If a 2nd verbal prompt would be needed, mark not met, then model one item and run a quick repair trial (still deliver natural end-of-session routine).

Reinforcement

Immediate, behavior-specific praise: "Nice job matching items to labels."

Natural payoff: earlier access to dismissal/choice/line role.

Token/points for each successful pack-up; bonus for 0 verbal prompts or ≤2:00 completion.

Periodically spotlight organization ("Organizer of the Day").

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Location check: "Where does this go?" / point to label—≤1 prompt, in 80% of need moments.

Missing-item report: "My ___ is missing," then mark it on the list—≤1 prompt, 80%.

Help request: "Can you help with the zipper/shelf?"—≤1 prompt, 80%.

Time request (brief): "30 more seconds?"—≤1 prompt, 80%.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Low-profile redirection: point to checklist/label → (if needed) single cue: "Pack-up routine."

Neutral reset if off-task: "Start with the first item," model one placement, then fade.

Match consequence to function: attention only after progress; no extended negotiation about keeping out items—use First-Then.

Repair trial after any escalation: 1-2 items placed correctly → praise → finish pack-up.

The Client will check for and secure personal belongings before leaving an area during transitions Once Per Session with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior

Increase checking for and securing personal belongings before leaving an area once per session with ≤2 verbal prompts.
Operational Definition

At a transition cue (e.g., "pack up," bell, timer), the client:
Checks for required items on a posted Essentials List (e.g., backpack, folder/homework, device, water bottle, jacket),

Secures items (zipped/closed, strapped/shouldered, device in case), and

Leaves the area with all items (nothing left on desk/cubby/floor),
 within 2 minutes and with no more than 2 verbal prompts (e.g., "Do your bag check," "Zip and go").

Counts as success: one full cycle (check + secure + exit with items) in the session.
 Does not include: materials still in use by staff, missing items not present in the room, or tasks beyond the 2-minute window when an item is truly lost.
Onset/offset: Onset = transition cue; offset = client exits area with items secured or 2 minutes elapse.
Measurement

Primary: Session success (Yes/No) = completed cycle with ≤2 prompts.

Secondary: prompts used (0/1/2), completion time (s), # items forgotten (0-n), and quality (0-3)
0 = incomplete/forgot item; 1 = gathered but not secured; 2 = gathered & secured; 3 = independent (0 prompts) and ≤90s.

Proactive Strategies

Environment & setup
Create a personal Essentials List (icons) for the typical setting.

Label/colour-code items; attach a tag/clip for quick grab.

Keep belongings in a single home base (bin/cubby/hook); clear the work area 1 minute before pack-up.

Use a visible 2-minute pack timer and First-Then (Pack → Next activity).

Teach the 4-step "B.A.G.S." routine (mini-card):
Belongings list: point & name each item.

Area sweep: 360° glance (desk, floor, chair, cubby).

Grab & Guard: put in bag, zip/close, shoulder/hold.

Signal ready: stand by door, say "Ready."

Teaching & Prompting

Model-Lead-Test two quick practice runs, then live transitions.
Prompting hierarchy (cap at 2 verbals):
Nonverbal: point to B.A.G.S. card/timer/home base.

Verbal 1: "Do your bag check."

Verbal 2: "Zip and go."
 Fade to gesture-only as soon as possible.

Reinforcement

Specific praise for fast, complete pack-ups ("Nice area sweep—nothing left behind").

Token/point or choice of line spot/first turn; thin once consistent.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Teach to ask skillfully instead of stalling/searching chaotically:
"More time (30s) to pack?"

"Help find ___?"

"May I leave a note to pick up ___ later?"

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

If the client lingers or chats: point to card, start the 2-minute timer, offer A/B choice ("Water bottle or folder first?").

If an item is missing: brief lost-item plan (check home base → quick scan → log on list), then exit; retrieve later.

The Client will identify the primary function of common items with 1 Verbal Prompt.

Target Behavior: Identify the Primary Function of Common Items (≤1 verbal prompt)

Operational Definition

Function identification is defined as the client stating or selecting the primary purpose/use of a familiar object (picture, real item, or word card). A response counts when the client:
Gives a brief, accurate function (spoken/AAC/point-to-function icon) using a frame like
 "You use a/an ___ to ___" or "It's for ___,"

Within 5-10 seconds of presentation or within 10 seconds of one brief verbal prompt (e.g., "What is it used for?").

Includes (examples):
"Toothbrush — for brushing teeth," "Scissors — for cutting," "Microwave — for heating food," "Umbrella — for keeping dry."

Does not include:
Naming the item without the function ("It's a toothbrush").

Giving a non-primary/unsafe use ("Scissors are for poking").

Requiring >1 verbal prompt or exceeding the time window.

Measurement
Opportunities: 10-20 trials/session using mixed items (real/pictures).

Scoring per trial:
Independent = correct function, 0 prompts.

Prompted = correct function after ≤1 verbal prompt.

Not met = incorrect/no response, >1 prompt, or out of time.

Optional 2-point rubric: 2 = accurate, generalizable function; 1 = partially correct/vague; 0 = incorrect. Track latency and modality (voice/AAC).

Probable Function (when errors occur)

Typically skill/label deficit or retrieval difficulty; sometimes avoidance of demand or inattention. Replacement = fast, consistent function statement anchored to visuals.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine — "LOOK → NAME → FUNCTION"

LOOK at the picture/object.

NAME it (optional quick step for retrieval).

Say/select the FUNCTION: "It's for ___ / You use it to ___."

Model → Guided practice → Independent with immediate, specific feedback.

Start with high-frequency items the client regularly uses.

Materials & Visuals

Function frames (sentence starters): "It's for ___." "You use a ___ to ___."

Category boards (Kitchen, School, Hygiene, Clothing/Weather, Tools, Leisure).

Function icon set (cut, write, clean, cook, play, protect, carry, measure).

AAC: program buttons for "It's for…", "You use it to…", plus common verbs.

Item Set (examples)

Hygiene: toothbrush (brush teeth), soap (wash hands), towel (dry).

School: pencil (write), scissors (cut), glue (stick), ruler (measure).

Kitchen: fork (eat), cup (drink), microwave (heat), fridge (keep food cold).

Clothing/Weather: hat (keep head warm), umbrella (keep dry), sunglasses (block sun).

Community/Tools: key (unlock), money (buy), broom (sweep), tape (stick).

Prompting & Fading (max 1 verbal prompt)

Nonverbal first (doesn't count): point to the function frame/icon; gesture "use."

Verbal (single): "What is it used for?" or "Tell me its job."

After a prompted success, run a transfer trial (new item; silent wait) to build independence.

Reinforcement

DRA: Labeled praise + token/point for accurate, quick function statements (bonus for independent or <5 s).

Start FR1, thin to VR2-3 as accuracy stabilizes.

Exchange: quick preferred activity, choose next item category, sticker.

Antecedent Modifications

Present visually clear images/real objects; reduce background noise.

Block by category first, then mix for generalization.

Pre-teach or review common action verbs (cut, carry, protect, measure).

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB) / Skill Targets

Primary—Correct Function (≤1 prompt): Given a common item, client provides the primary function within 10 s, in ≥80% of trials with ≤1 verbal prompt across 3 sessions.

Independent Function Statements: Increase to ≥50% independent by mastery phase.

Category Cue Use: When unsure, client uses a category clue ("It's from kitchen… used to ___"), ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Two-Choice Rescue: If stuck, technician offers two verbs ("cut or write?"); client selects correctly, ≥80% (then fade choices).

Generalization & Maintenance

Embed naturalistic probes during routines (lunch, art, clean-up): "What's this for?"

Add novel but familiar items weekly; rotate pictures and real objects.

Check carryover with caregivers/teachers (home/school lists).

Reactive / De-escalation Strategies

Brief cue, then wait (5-10 s): "Use your function frame."

Two-choice redirect: "Is it for eating or cleaning?" (fade quickly).

Offer a 30-60 s micro-break if frustration appears; resume with an easier item.

Reinforce the pivot immediately when the client attempts a function statement.

The Client will explain the cause-and-effect of discarding a useful item by naming one realistic consequence (e.g., "then I can't use it") for 3 different items with 1 Verbal Prompt

Target Behavior: Explain the cause-and-effect of discarding a useful item by naming one realistic consequence for 3 different items (≤1 verbal prompt)

Operational Definition

Given a useful item (real or pictured) and the prompt "What happens if you throw this away?", the client will, within 10 seconds, state one realistic consequence tied to the item's function.
Realistic consequence = a plausible, functional result that would likely occur the same day or soon (e.g., "Then I can't use it," "I won't be able to charge my tablet," "I won't have water at PE").

Trial structure: Present one item at a time; run 3 different items per session.

Prompt allowance: At most one brief verbal prompt total per item (e.g., "Say what would happen."). Gestural/visual cues (pointing to an "If…then…" strip) don't count.

Counts as success (per item) when the client:
Names a consequence that matches the item's function (accuracy).

States it within 10 s of the prompt/cue.

Uses calm, appropriate language.

Requires ≤1 verbal prompt.

Non-examples: vague/irrelevant outcomes (e.g., "It disappears"), magical thinking, moral judgments without function ("That's bad"), needing ≥2 verbal prompts, or refusing.
Measurement

Opportunities: 3 items/session (e.g., pencil, water bottle, bus pass/ID, coat, charger, homework, library book, notebook).

Record per item: Item named | Consequence accuracy (Y/N) | Verbal prompt (0/1) | Latency (s) | Notes.

Session success: All 3 items correct with ≤1 verbal prompt each.

Mastery criterion: Session success on 4 of 5 consecutive sessions. (Then fade to 0 prompts and/or increase to 4-5 items.)

Probable Function

This is primarily a skill acquisition/executive-function task (linking item function → outcome). Misses may reflect impulsivity, escape from language demand, or limited rule-governed behavior.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Micro-Skill (BST: Instruction → Modeling → Rehearsal → Feedback)

Rule: "If I discard ___, then ___ happens."

Model 2-3 examples, then guided practice → independent probe.

Use a visual If → then strip or sentence stem card (AAC-ready).

Materials & Sequencing

Start concrete/high-frequency items: pencil, water bottle, charger.

Advance to social/academic: homework, bus pass/ID, library book, then coat/hat (seasonal).

Use real items or clear photos; rotate across days.

Prompting (fade quickly)

Gesture/point to the If→then strip → wait up to 10 s → single verbal prompt ("Say what would happen.").

If a 2nd verbal prompt would be needed, stop the probe, model a correct answer, then run a quick repair trial (not scored).

Reinforcement

Behavior-specific praise after each correct consequence ("Yes—if you throw away your charger, your tablet can't charge.").

Tokens/points per correct item; exchange at session end.

Natural reinforcement: brief access to the item (e.g., use water bottle/charger), or a quick choice.

Generalization

Embed in the moment: before transitions ("Where does the ID go so we don't lose bus access?").

Quick daily "What if…?" lightning round with different contexts (school, home, community).

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Teach alternatives to discarding/losing items:
Storage statement: "This goes in my labeled spot/cubby."

Repair/replace request: "This is broken—can we fix or replace it?"

Check-in question: "Is this trash or do we still need it?"

Plan-ahead statement: "I'll keep my pass in my wallet so I can ride home."

Criteria for FERB: Use the statement/request within 10 s of handling the item, ≤1 verbal prompt, in 80% of relevant moments.
De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Low-profile cueing: point to the item → tap the If→then strip → (if needed) single cue: "Say what would happen."

If client argues/jokes: acknowledge, then restate function and ask for one realistic result.

If frustration rises: offer binary choice—"Tell me the consequence or use the visual to read it," or a 20-30 s micro-break; resume with an easier item.

Repair trial after any miss: model one correct consequence, then have the client say a different one for a new item to contact reinforcement.

Sample Consequences Bank (use for modeling & accuracy)

Pencil: "Then I can't write my answers."

Water bottle: "Then I won't have water during PE."

Charger: "Then my tablet/phone will die and I can't use it."

Homework: "Then I'd get a zero and can't show what I know."

Bus pass/ID: "Then I can't ride the bus and need another ride."

Library book: "Then I can't finish reading and might owe a fee."

Coat (winter): "Then I'll be cold at recess/walk home."

Notebook/planner: "Then I won't know my assignments."

The client will state an appropriate alternative action to throwing out useful items (e.g., "put it in my backpack/return it to the bin/label it") for 3 different items with 1 Verbal Prompt

Target Behavior

The client will state an appropriate alternative action (e.g., "put it in my backpack/return it to the bin/label it") for 3 different useful items per session with ≤1 verbal prompt.
Operational Definition

When presented with—or about to discard—a useful item (e.g., pencil, worksheet, library book, charger, reusable container), the client will, within 10 seconds, state a specific, accurate action + location that preserves/organizes the item (e.g., "Label it, then put it in my red homework folder," "Return it to the class supply bin," "Put it in my backpack front pocket").
Counts as success (per item):
Response includes a clear action + where (keep/return/label/organize),

Occurs within 10s, with ≤1 verbal prompt (e.g., "What should you do instead of throwing it away?").

Does not include: vague "keep it" without location, choosing trash for a useful item, or incorrect/vague placement.
Probable Function

Throwing out useful items may be maintained by escape from organizing effort/clutter, inattention/habit, or sensory/cleanliness preferences. Appropriate alternatives contact predictability, efficiency, and social approval.
Proactive Strategies

Visuals: post a K.R.L.R. card—Keep (where?) • Return (to whom/where?) • Label (name/date) • Recycle (paper).

Environment: color-coded bins/folders (Backpack bin, Return tray, Label station with marker), clear "Trash vs. Keep" examples.

Teaching: brief model-lead-test with mixed trials (useful vs. true trash) before live opportunities.

Prompting rule: use nonverbal cues first (point to K.R.L.R.), allow only one verbal prompt on mastery trials.

Reinforcement: specific praise for fast, specific alternatives; token/point early, then thin.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

"Is this trash or keep?"

"Where should this go—backpack or return tray?"

"Can I label this and file it?"

De-escalation Measures/reactive strategies

Neutral block & point to K.R.L.R. if client moves toward trash → deliver the single verbal prompt once.

If uncertain, allow a brief forced choice during teaching only ("Backpack or Return tray?"), then fade.

If item already tossed (and clean), use rescue & repair: retrieve → client states correct action → completes it; reinforce the repair, not the toss.

Given pictures or real items, the Client will sort each into Keep / Fix / Trash and state what would happen if they discarded the item (one consequence) with a Verbal Prompt

Target Behavior: Sort to Keep / Fix / Trash & State One Discard Consequence (≤1 verbal prompt)

Operational Definition

When shown a real item or picture, the client will:
Sort it onto a labeled Keep, Fix, or Trash bin/mat, and

Say one logical consequence of discarding it using a frame like:
 "If I throw it away, then ___."

A trial counts when both steps are completed within 10-15 seconds of presentation or within 10 seconds of one brief verbal prompt (e.g., "Sort it and tell me what would happen if you tossed it").
Sorting Rules (teach explicitly)

KEEP = Works now and needed/used soon.

FIX = Broken/low but repairable/refillable/chargeable and worth it.

TRASH = Used up, spoiled, unsafe, or not repairable.

Examples
Keep: full water bottle; working calculator.

Fix: pencil to sharpen; dead tablet (charge); loose-sole shoe (glue).

Trash: empty wrapper; dried marker; cracked cup that leaks.

Consequence examples (for the sentence frame)
If Keep item were trashed → "I won't have it when I need it / I'd waste money buying another."

If Fix item were trashed → "I'd throw away something fixable / waste resources."

If Trash item were trashed → "The area stays clean / there's no smell/clutter."

Measurement

Opportunities: 10-20 mixed items per session.

Score each trial:
Independent = correct bin and logical consequence, 0 prompts.

Prompted = correct bin and consequence after ≤1 verbal prompt.

Not met = wrong bin, no/illogical consequence, >1 prompt, or out of time.

Optional 2-point rubric: 2 = correct bin + clear consequence; 1 = correct bin + vague consequence; 0 = incorrect.

Teaching Routine — "CHECK → DECIDE → PLACE → EXPLAIN"

CHECK: Is it working? Needed? Repairable?

DECIDE: Match to rule (Keep/Fix/Trash).

PLACE: Put it on the correct mat/bin.

EXPLAIN: Say "If I throw it away, then ___."

Use model → guided practice → independent, starting with easy contrasts (obvious trash vs obvious keep), then add "Fix" and gray areas.
Materials & Visuals

Three color-coded mats/bins (Keep=green, Fix=yellow, Trash=red).

Rule cards with icons (✅ works/needed, 🛠 fix/refill/charge, 🗑 used-up/unsafe).

Sentence strip/AAC: "If I throw it away, then ___."

Prompting & Fading (max 1 verbal prompt)

Nonverbal first (doesn't use the allowance): point to rule card or sentence strip.

Verbal (single): "Sort it and tell what happens if you threw it away."

Then wait 5-10 s silently; after success, run a transfer trial with only nonverbal cues.

Reinforcement

DRA: Labeled praise + token/point for correct sort + consequence (bonus for independent or <7 s).

Thin from FR1 to VR2-3 as accuracy stabilizes.

Quick exchanges: choose next item set, line leader, sticker.

Safety/Boundaries

Adults handle sharps/soiled/hazardous items; client still labels/sorts via pictures.

Respect ownership—teach ask first before trashing real belongings.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Primary—Correct Sort + Consequence (≤1 prompt): Client sorts into Keep/Fix/Trash and states one logical discard consequence within 10-15 s in ≥80% of trials across 3 sessions.

Rule Reference: Client points to the rule that justified the choice, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Gray-Area Justification: For ambiguous items, client adds a brief "because" (e.g., "Fix because it can be taped"), ≥70% with ≤1 prompt.

Generalize: In natural routines (end of art/lunch), client correctly sorts 3+ items and states a consequence, ≥70% success.

Responsibility for Actions

The Client Will Acknowledge Responsibility for a Personal Action with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior: Acknowledge responsibility for a personal action (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

After a personal action that breaks a rule or causes a problem (e.g., mess, interruption, rough tone), the client will complete the Responsibility Chain within 10 seconds of an adult cue or self-awareness:
Own it: "I [what I did]."

Acknowledge impact/apologize: "I'm sorry / I see that caused a problem."

Repair/next step: "I'll fix it by ___ / Next time I'll ___."

Prompts allowed: up to two brief verbal prompts total (e.g., "Responsibility words," then "Say 'I…' and how you'll fix it"). Gestural/visual cues don't count toward the two.
Counts as success when the client completes all 3 steps with calm tone, within 10s, and ≤2 verbal prompts.
Non-examples: blaming/denying, arguing, sarcasm, "sorry" without ownership/repair, needing ≥3 verbal prompts.
Measurement & Mastery

Each incident = 1 opportunity. Record Success (Y/N), prompts (0/1/2), latency to start (s), repair completed (Y/N), notes.

Mastery: ≥80% successful opportunities per session across 4 of 5 consecutive sessions; then fade to ≤1 prompt, then independent.

Probable Function

Avoidance of consequences/attention, anxiety about mistakes, or control. Use brief ABC notes to confirm.
Proactive Strategies (Teach & Prevent)

BST: Instruction → Modeling → Role-play → Feedback using common scenarios (spilled items, blurting, late start).

Script card/AAC page: "I [did]. I'm sorry. I will [repair]."

Pre-correction: "If a mistake happens, use your responsibility words."

Choice in repair: offer two fix options (pick up/replace; redo/apologize).

Predictable adult response: "Fix-it first" (prioritize quick repair over lectures).

Reinforcement

Behavior-specific praise: "You owned it and fixed it—responsible."

Natural outcomes: quick re-entry to activity, restored privileges.

Tokens/points for completed chain; bonus for 0 prompts or ≤30s repair.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Primary chain: "I ___; I'm sorry; I'll ___" within 10s, ≤2 prompts, in 80% of opportunities.

If unsure/anxious: "I might have ___—how can I fix it?" ≤2 prompts, 80%.

Brief reset: "30 seconds to calm, then I'll explain," returns and completes chain ≤60s, ≤1 prompt, 80%.

Peer impact check (when relevant): "Are you okay? Anything else to fix?" ≤1 prompt, 70-80%.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Neutral cue → Prompt 1 → wait 5-10s → Prompt 2.

Avoid debates about intent; focus on repair.

Binary choice: "Own it now or after a 30-sec reset."

After escalation, run a brief repair trial (model → client repeats script → completes micro-fix), then praise.

Personal Life Skills

Waiting

The Client will hold back for minimum of 5 minutes when told not to do something

Target Behavior

Increase delaying the restricted action for at least 5 minutes after being told "no," "not now," or "don't do that," with ≤2 verbal prompts.
Operational Definition

When an adult delivers a clear prohibition/delay cue (e.g., "Not now—don't touch the iPad"), the client:
Refrains from engaging in the restricted action for ≥5 consecutive minutes, and

Uses an appropriate alternative (wait quietly, choose an approved activity, or use a taught request), and

Avoids protest behaviors (arguing, grabbing, sneaking, tantrum).

Includes (count as violations): touching/activating the restricted item/area, crossing a set boundary, repeated bargaining (>2 requests within the 5 minutes), or leaving supervision to access it.
Measurement
Primary: Success (Y/N) per opportunity = waited ≥5 min with ≤2 verbal prompts and no violations.

Secondary: prompts used (0/1/2), any protest (Y/N/type), latency to settle (s), use of alternative (Y/N).

Onset/offset: Onset = end of "not now/don't do that" statement; offset = 5:00 on visible timer without violations (or a violation occurs).

Probable Function

Problem responses to "no/not now" often function for access to tangibles/activities, attention, or autonomy/control; sometimes automatic/sensory. Waiting offers access to predictable control (timer), attention for compliance, and planned access later.
Proactive Strategies

Set the stage
Post a Wait Routine: Hear "Not now" → Acknowledge ("Okay") → Pick a Plan → Timer 5:00 → Check back.

Use a visible timer and a "Wait/On-Deck" card (or token).

Create a Wait Plan Menu (quiet choices for 5 minutes): reading, drawing, puzzle, fidget, chair push-downs.

Use First-Then/When-Then language: "When the 5 minutes are up, then you may ask again/earn 2 minutes."

Pre-correct before high-risk moments: "If it's a 'not now,' show me Wait Routine."

Teaching (model-lead-test)
Role-play easy scenarios first (shorter delays), then build to full 5 minutes.

Teach acknowledgment response: "Okay" + choose from the menu.

Define boundaries (e.g., tape line, shelf sign) and practice looking-not-touching.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

During the 5-minute window, allow and reinforce:
"Okay— I'll wait." (verbal/AAC)

Request to schedule: "On deck—can I have it after timer?"

Request info: "When can I have it?" (adult gives time or condition).

Ask for alternative/help: "What can I do while I wait?"

Reinforcement

DRA: immediate, specific praise for starting wait + bonus at 5:00 ("You waited the whole time—nice job.").

Deliver promised Then (brief access/turn, or next-step option) when criteria met.

Extra reinforcement for independent waits (0-1 prompt) and calm body/voice.

De-escalation Measures / Reactive Strategies

At the "not now" moment
Keep it brief & neutral: "Not now. When timer ends, you can ask again." Start timer; show Wait Menu.

If leaning toward the item: nonverbal block + point to timer/menu (no lecture).

If protesting/bargaining starts
One concise reminder (counts as a prompt): "Use your Wait Routine."

Offer a bounded choice: "Wait with drawing or puzzle?"

If escalation continues: reduce language, add space, and reinforce any move toward an alternative.

If a violation occurs
Neutral reset: remove access, restate boundary: "Not during timer." Restart a shorter practice interval (e.g., 2 minutes), then build back to 5.

Avoid accidental reinforcement (no debating); deliver attention to return-to-wait behavior

The Client will wait until the timer rings without continuously asking to gain access to reinforcing activity

Target Behavior: Wait for Timer Without Repeated Requests (≤1 verbal prompt)

Operational Definition

Waiting is defined as the client refraining from asking for or attempting to access the reinforcer until a visible/audible timer rings after the contingency is stated ("When the timer rings, then ___"). A response counts when, for the entire interval, the client:
Makes 0 additional requests for the reinforcer (polite one-time "I'll wait" is allowed if pre-taught),

Keeps hands off the reinforcer/materials, uses a calm voice/body, and

Accesses only after the timer rings—with no more than 1 verbal prompt ("Use your wait plan").

Probable Function

Problem requests typically contact attention, escape from waiting, and/or immediate access to the reinforcer. Replacement focuses on functional delay tolerance with clear cues and competing activities.
Proactive Strategies

Teach a Wait Routine (Set → Say → Do → Ring):
Set visible timer + state contingency.

Client says/shows "I can wait" (card/AAC).

Client engages in Wait Menu (book, puzzle, coloring, fidget, brief task).

Deliver access immediately at ring.

Antecedents: First-Then visual; reinforcer out of reach; start with short waits (e.g., 20-30 s) and increase gradually; post Wait Menu; provide clear access duration after ring.

Prompting (max 1 verbal): Use nonverbal point to timer/First-Then first; if needed give one cue: "Use your wait plan," then wait silently 5-10 s.

Reinforcement: Labeled praise + token for clean waits; immediate access at ring; bonus for 0 requests and for meeting longer waits.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Wait Statement/Signal: At interval start, client says "I can wait" or shows Wait Card, ≥80% of opportunities with ≤1 prompt.

Timer Check (Nonverbal): Client looks at timer instead of asking, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Wait Menu Engagement: Client selects and engages in a wait activity within 10 s and continues until ring, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Clean Access at Ring: Client begins reinforcer only after ring with calm body/voice, ≥90% independence.

De-escalation Measures / Reactive Strategies

Planned ignore of extra requests; silently point to timer/First-Then.

When-Then (brief): "When it rings, then iPad."

Block early access neutrally; restate contingency; consider shorter next interval to re-establish success.

Offer neutral regulation (breathing card/fidget) if arousal rises—no extra talk; resume waiting.

Self Image

The Client Will Share 1 Personal Achievement, With a Verbal Prompt

Target Behavior: Share one personal achievement (≤1 verbal prompt)

Operational Definition

During a check-in, circle time, small group, or 1:1 conversation, the client will state one personal achievement using speech or an approved modality (AAC/sign) within 10 seconds of an opportunity or cue.
Counts as success when the client:
States one clear achievement (something accomplished, improved, or completed recently),

Uses a complete, audience-directed statement (≥3 words or AAC equivalent),

Maintains calm, respectful tone, and

Requires ≤1 brief verbal prompt (e.g., "Share one achievement"). Gestural/visual cues don't count.

Examples:
"I finished my math worksheet."

"I scored a goal at recess."

"I read two chapters last night."

"I used my coping card during writing."

Non-examples: off-topic stories, bragging/put-downs, fabrications, needing ≥2 verbal prompts, or refusing.
Opportunities & Measurement

Provide 1-3 planned opportunities per session (e.g., morning meeting, end-of-session reflection).

Record per opportunity: Success (Y/N), Prompt (0/1), Latency to respond (s), Topic category (school/home/sports/peers/skills), brief notes.

Session criterion: At least 1 successful share with ≤1 verbal prompt.

Mastery: Meets session criterion on 4 of 5 consecutive sessions (then fade to 0 prompts or increase to 2 shares/session).

Probable Function

Skill acquisition for self-advocacy/social participation; may compete with escape (from speaking), attention-seeking (off-topic), or anxiety about performance.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Micro-Skill (BST)

Model → Role-play → In vivo.

Sentence stems / AAC buttons:
"I'm proud that I [did]."

"This week I [finished/practiced]."

"I worked hard on [task]."

Achievement Menu (visual ring): schoolwork, kindness/helping, self-regulation, hobbies/sports, chores/responsibility.

Antecedent Supports

Prime before the sharing block: "Think of one thing you accomplished."

Achievement journal/card: jot one win during the session to use later.

Choice of format: speak, read from card, or tap AAC phrase.

Time cue: 10-second share rule to keep it brief and reduce pressure.

Prompting (fade quickly)

Gesture to menu/journal → single verbal prompt if needed ("Share one achievement") → wait up to 10 s.

If a 2nd prompt would be needed, end that trial, model a correct example, and try a brief repair trial later.

Reinforcement

Behavior-specific praise: "Nice—clear and quick: 'I finished my notes.'"

Natural reinforcement: peer acknowledgment, leader follow-up question.

Token/points for each successful share; bonus for independent (0 prompt).

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Primary skill: Share one achievement within 10 s, ≤1 prompt, in 80% of opportunities.

If unsure: Use a help cue—"Can I see the menu?" or "Can I read my card?"—then share, ≤1 prompt, 80%.

If anxious: Request a brief delay—"Can I go last?"—then complete the share when called, ≤1 prompt, 80%.

Respectful phrasing: Use "I'm proud that…" without comparisons or put-downs, ≤1 prompt, 80%.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Low-profile cueing: point to menu/journal → single cue ("Share one achievement").

Binary choices: "Speak or read from your card." / "Share now or at the end."

If off-topic starts: brief redirect—"One achievement"—then reinforce the corrected share.

Repair trial after a miss: model a short example, have the client deliver theirs immediately, and praise.

Setting and Achieving Goals

The Client Will Set a Personal Goal and Take 1 Step Towards Achieving It Per Session

Target Behavior

Increase setting one personal goal and completing one concrete step toward it once per session with ≤2 verbal prompts.
Operational Definition

During a designated goal time (start/middle of session), the client:
States a specific, doable goal for today using a stem like: "By the end of session, I will ___."

Names one concrete step that can be done now (≤10 minutes or a single action).

Completes that step within the session window.

Counts as success: goal stated + step identified + step completed, all within the session, with ≤2 verbal prompts total.
 Does not include: vague goals ("be better"), steps unrelated to the goal, or planning without action.
Probable Function

Desired behavior is maintained by autonomy/competence and positive attention. Interference (stalling, "IDK," perfectionism) often serves escape from effort/uncertainty or control over demands.
Proactive Strategies

Visuals/tools:
Goal card: "Today I will ___."

Step menu (examples): write 3 sentences, sort 5 papers, ask 1 question, practice greeting 2 times.

Barrier→Fix map: Too big → Make it smaller; Not sure → Ask for help; No time → 2-minute version.

SMART-ish rule: specific, small, finish-today.

Micro-step rule: if stuck, make the step so small it's easy (2-3 minutes or one action).

First-Then: Step → Preferred choice (2-3 min).

Prime at start: "We'll set one goal and do one step."

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Teach/allow instead of avoidance:
"More time/choices?"

"Help me make it smaller."

"Swap to a similar goal?"

"Timer please (2-3 min focus)."

Teaching & Prompting

Model-Lead-Test: adult models goal+step → do one together → client does a new one.

Prompting (max 2 verbals):
"State your goal for today."

"Name one step you can do now."

If still stuck (teaching phase only), offer A/B choices for steps; fade quickly.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Early hesitation/perfectionism: shrink the step (cut in half), set a 2-minute timer, give A/B choice.

Negative self-talk/avoidance: neutral redirect to Barrier→Fix; praise first attempt.

If off-task: brief reset—point to goal card, start a 90-second micro-step, then expand.

Self Monitoring

The Client will utilize a self-monitoring tool to manage his age-appropriate behaviors

Target Behavior: Use a self-monitoring tool to manage age-appropriate behaviors (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

During a defined work or group block (e.g., 10-20 minutes), the client will use a self-monitoring tool (card/app/checklist/AAC) to track 3-5 age-appropriate behaviors and review results at the end of the block.
Age-appropriate behavior targets (pick 3-5):
On-task/working (eyes/materials, following steps)

Respectful voice (0-2 volume, polite words)

Hands/feet to self & safe materials use

Staying in assigned area/with group

Following directions within 10 seconds

Waiting/turn-taking during talk time

Using the tool = completing all steps with ≤2 verbal prompts total:
Start-up (within 30s of cue): Get the tool, set the interval timer (2-5 min), read today's goal.

Interval checks: At each timer signal, mark Yes/No for each target for the previous interval.

End-of-block: Total points, compare with adult score, circle goal met/not yet, and state one next step.

Success for a block =
Client completes ≥80% of planned interval checks,

Agreement with adult ratings ≥80% (or within 1 check on each target),

Behavior performance ≥80% Yes (or shows improvement vs. baseline),

≤2 verbal prompts used to run the system (gestures/visuals don't count).

Non-examples: skipping checks; guessing without looking at behavior; arguing scores; needing ≥3 verbal prompts; unsafe behavior during checks.
Measurement & Mastery

Record per block: intervals planned/completed, % agreement, % Yes, prompts (0/1/2), time on task (min), notes.

Mastery criterion: Meets "Success for a block" on 4 of 5 consecutive sessions. Then: (a) fade to ≤1 verbal prompt, (b) lengthen blocks, (c) thin adult checks to random 50% → 25% of intervals.

Probable Function

Primary: skill acquisition in self-management/executive function. Off-task/competing behaviors may be maintained by escape, attention, tangibles, or automatic stimulation—use brief ABC notes to tailor supports.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine (BST: Instruction → Modeling → Rehearsal → Feedback)

Define each target with examples/non-examples (e.g., "On-task = pencil moving, reading, or asking for help; Not on-task = head down, phone").

Model a full block (start-up → two interval checks → end review).

Guided practice with immediate feedback, then independent probes.

Materials & Setup

Self-monitor card with 3-5 targets × 4-8 intervals (Yes/No boxes).

Timer (visual/vibe) set to 2-5 minutes per interval (start short; grow).

Anchor sheet with icons for each target; goal line (e.g., "Aim for 80% Yes").

Reinforcer menu + token/point tracker.

Antecedent Supports

Pre-correction before block: "Run your system—timer on, check each beep."

First-Then: "Self-monitor block → Choice for 2 minutes."

Reduce response effort: tool stays on desk; marks are checkmarks or stickers.

Behavioral momentum: 1-2 easy tasks before starting.

Prompting (fade quickly)

Gesture to card/timer → Verbal Prompt 1 ("Start your system") → wait 5-10s → Verbal Prompt 2 ("Mark the check").

If a 3rd prompt would be needed, pause scoring, model one interval, then resume (repair trial).

Reinforcement Plan

Two-path reinforcement:
 A. Process (using the tool): reward for ≥80% checks completed and ≥80% agreement (even if behavior % is still growing).
 B. Outcome (behavior): additional reward for ≥80% Yes or beating last session by +10 percentage points.

Behavior-specific praise every interval or two ("You checked right at the beep—great self-management").

Thin rewards as independence rises; keep natural reinforcement (finishing work faster, teacher trust).

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)
