Help/clarify request: "What does 'respectful voice' mean right now?" within 10s, ≤1 prompt, in 80% of need moments.

Missed-beep repair: "I missed that check—marking it now," resets timer within 10s, ≤1 prompt, 80%.

Brief break request: "30-second reset, then I'll keep checking," returns on time ≤1 prompt, 80%.

Self-cue statement: "Next beep I'll check hands to self," independent in 70-80% of intervals (shape up).

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Low-profile cueing: tap card/timer → single cue ("Run your system").

If client disputes a rating: use Problem-Solving Rule—state evidence once, then move on; offer to average scores or re-observe next interval.

If two checks are missed in a row: stop, rehearse the routine quickly (10-20s), restart with a shorter interval.

Match consequence to function: honor break/help requests made appropriately; do not let refusal earn escape from the monitoring routine.

Repair trial after escalation: one short interval successfully checked → praise → continue.

ADL Skills

Flexibility

Peer Tolerance

The Client will tolerate another peer when he feels the game is not fair with prompt

Target Behavior

Increase tolerating another peer when the client feels the game is not fair with ≤2 verbal prompts.
Operational Definition

During a game (board, card, sport, playground, digital), when the client perceives unfairness (e.g., "bad call," unclear rule, uneven teams, lucky roll), the client:
Stays in the game through the current round/turn (or ≥2 minutes) without quitting, arguing, or retaliating, and

Uses one taught coping/repair step, such as:
"Rule-check" (ask to read the rule card),

Request a referee/teacher decision,

Accept a do-over/coin flip/randomizer,

Take a 30-60s calm pause and then resume,

Keeps a safe/neutral voice and body (no insults, threats, grabbing pieces).

Counts as success: any fairness trigger occurs and the client completes one coping step and continues play for the round/2 minutes with ≤2 verbal prompts (e.g., "Use your fairness plan," "Rule-check.").
Does not include: repeated arguing after a decision, name-calling, leaving the area, changing rules unilaterally, or refusing to resume after the pause.
Onset/offset: Onset at the moment the client indicates "unfair" (verbally or behaviorally). Offset after the client completes a coping step and continues play through the round/2 minutes.
Probable Function

Perceived unfairness often triggers behavior maintained by access to winning/tangibles, attention, escape from losing/waiting, and control/autonomy. Teaching tolerance provides predictable control (routine/choices), fair dispute paths, and positive attention for coping.
Proactive Strategies

Game setup
Post a one-page Rule Preview Card (goal, turn order, top 3 rules, tie-break method).

Assign/rotate a referee/scorekeeper role; keep a randomizer handy (coin, die, spinner).

Provide Do-Over Tokens (1 per player per game) and a 30-60s Calm Pass option.

Use right-fit games first (short rounds, clear rules), build difficulty gradually.

Pre-correct: "If something feels unfair, use your Fairness Plan."

Teach the S.T.A.Y. Fair routine (mini-card)
Stop & breathe (2 breaths).

Tell the plan: "Rule-check or Referee or Do-over?"

Accept the decision/randomizer.

Your turn—play on (say, "Your turn.")

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Rule-check: "What's the rule? Let's see the card."

Referee request: "Can the ref decide?"

Randomizer/Do-over: "Let's flip a coin."

Calm pause: "I need 30 seconds, then I'll play."

Compromise: "Mix both rules this round?" (if allowed)

Teaching & Prompting

Model-Lead-Test a quick fairness scenario before live play.

Prompting hierarchy (max 2 verbals): gesture to S.T.A.Y. card → Verbal 1: "Use your fairness plan." → Verbal 2: "Rule-check or do-over?"

Keep adult tone neutral; avoid debating mid-round.

Reinforcement

DRA: labeled praise + point/token for (a) using one coping step and (b) staying in for the round/2 min.

Bonus for independent (0-1 prompt) tolerance and for positive sportsmanship ("Nice play," "Your turn").

De-escalation Measures / Reactive Strategies

Early signs (voice rise, grabbing piece, repeating "unfair"):
Move closer; quiet cue to S.T.A.Y. card; offer bounded choice: "Rule-check or 30-second pause?"

If arguing persists after a decision:
Neutral reset: "Decision made—play on. Use your Calm Pass if needed."

If escalation continues, pause game briefly; re-enter with Do-Over Token or randomizer; reinforce return to play.

If client attempts to quit:
Offer a micro-goal: "Finish this round, then choose: continue or switch." Reinforce completion of the round.

Sharing Personal Information

The Client will tolerate discussion and identify his personal likes and dislikes

Target Behavior: Tolerate discussion and identify personal likes/dislikes (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

During a preference discussion window (2 minutes) with an adult or peer, the client will:
Tolerate the discussion: stay in place, calm body/voice, no maladaptive behavior (e.g., yelling, refusal, leaving area, insults).

Identify one like and one dislike about the topic (or any everyday domain: activities, foods, games, classes, peers' suggestions) using speech or an approved modality (AAC/sign/choice board).

Prompts allowed: up to two brief verbal prompts total per opportunity (e.g., Prompt 1: "Share one thing you like," Prompt 2: "Share one you don't like"). Gestural/visual cues (feelings or preference chart) don't count toward the two.
Counts as success when all are true:
Client remains engaged for the 2-minute window without maladaptive behavior,

States ≥1 like and ≥1 dislike (clear, directed to partner),

Occurs independently or with ≤2 verbal prompts.

Non-examples: repeated "I don't care," silence >10s after a prompt, topic change to avoid answering, arguing/teasing others' preferences, leaving area, or needing ≥3 verbal prompts.
Measurement & Mastery

Opportunities: Plan 2-4 preference discussions per session (natural or contrived).

Record for each: Success (Y/N), prompts (0/1/2), maladaptive (Y/N), like named, dislike named, latency to first response (s).

Mastery criterion: ≥80% successful opportunities per session across 4 of 5 consecutive sessions. Then fade to ≤1 prompt, increase duration to 3-5 minutes, or add a reason ("because…") requirement.

Probable Function

Avoidance of disclosure or conversation demand (escape), seeking attention/control, or anxiety/uncertainty about preferences. Confirm with brief ABC notes.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Micro-Skill (BST: Instruction → Modeling → Rehearsal → Feedback)

Sentence stems / AAC buttons:
Like: "I like ___ (because ___)."

Dislike: "I don't like ___ (because ___)."

Unsure but cooperative: "I'm not sure—maybe ___ / can I think for 10 seconds?"

Model quick examples; role-play with safe topics first (snacks, games), then expand.

Use a Preference Menu (icons for foods, activities, subjects, people, settings).

Antecedent Supports

Prime before blocks: "We'll talk preferences for 2 minutes—share one like and one dislike."

Visuals: Like/Dislike cards, 1-5 "preference scale," topic picture ring.

Choice of topic (offer 2-3 options) to increase buy-in.

Think time: allow 5-10 seconds before expecting a reply.

Prompting (fade quickly)

Gesture to menu → Verbal Prompt 1 ("Share one thing you like") → wait up to 10s → Verbal Prompt 2 ("Now one you don't like").

If a 3rd prompt would be needed, end the probe neutrally, model an example, and run a brief repair trial later.

Reinforcement

Behavior-specific praise: "You stayed with the conversation and shared a like and a dislike—nice job."

Natural reinforcement: brief time talking about the named like or accessing a small related choice.

Token/points for each success; bonus for 0 prompts or adding a reason.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Preference statements (primary): Client states one like and one dislike within the 2-minute window, ≤2 prompts, in 80% of opportunities.

If anxious/unsure: Client uses a brief delay/clarifier ("Give me 10 seconds," "Can I see the menu?") then answers, ≤2 prompts, 80%.

If overwhelmed by topic: Client requests a topic swap politely ("Can we talk about sports instead?") once, ≤1 prompt, 80%.

If near escalation: Client requests a short break ("30-second break, then I'll answer") and returns on time, ≤1 prompt, 80%.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Low-profile cues first: point to Like/Dislike cards → brief cue ("One you like… now one you don't").

Binary choices: "Share now or after a 30-second think time."

Avoid debates about the client's opinions; acknowledge and move on.

If refusal/arguing starts: neutral reset ("We'll do one quick like, then we're done"), keep language brief; reinforce completion.

After any miss, run a repair trial (short, easy topic) to contact success.

The Client will tolerate discussion and reflect on how his own personal likes and dislikes are similar or different from a peer or adult

Target Behavior: Tolerate Preference Discussion & Reflect Similarities/Differences (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

Preference reflection is defined as the client participating calmly in a short discussion about personal likes/dislikes and stating at least one similarity or difference between his preferences and a peer's/adult's. A response counts when, during a 1-2 minute exchange or within 30 seconds of a direct prompt to reflect, the client:
Maintains a listening body (faces partner, voice level 2-3, no interrupting >2 sec),

Uses a taught sentence frame to state one similarity or one difference, e.g.,
Similarity: "We both like __."

Difference: "I like __, but you prefer __—that's okay."

Requires no more than 2 brief verbal prompts (e.g., "Use a 'both' or 'different' sentence").

Counts as success (examples):
"We both like soccer." / "I like drawing; you like building. That's okay."

"You don't like spicy foods; I do. We're different."

Does not count:
Arguing, persuading, or teasing about differences ("Mine is better").

Off-topic comments or refusal to listen.

Needing >2 verbal prompts or escalating tone.

Measurement

Opportunity: Any structured preference chat or natural moment where a partner shares a like/dislike.

Score each opportunity:
Independent = calm participation + ≥1 accurate similarity/difference, 0 prompts.

Prompted = same after ≤2 verbal prompts.

Not met = no reflection, off-topic/argument, or >2 prompts.

Track latency to reflection (goal ≤30 s), duration engaged (goal 1-2 min), and # of reflections (stretch: aim for 2—one "both," one "different").

Probable Function (when breakdown occurs)

Barriers often include rigidity/need for being "right," anxiety, skill deficits in perspective-taking, or attention-seeking debate. Replacement = quick, neutral compare-and-accept statements.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine — "L-A-C-E"

Listen (body facing, quiet mouth).

Acknowledge ("I hear you like __").

Compare (use frame: both or different sentence).

End respectfully ("That's okay / Thanks for sharing").

Model → Role-play → Practice with high-interest, low-stakes topics first.

Use a mini Venn card (Mine / Both / Yours) and a sentence-frame strip.

Visuals & Tools

Venn pocket card with icons and stems:
Both: "We both like __."

Different: "I like __; you prefer __."

Respect close: "That's okay / We're different."

Optional Feelings meter + micro-break card for regulation if strong feelings arise.

Antecedent Modifications

Start with safe categories (games, foods, animals, activities) before values/sensitive topics.

Preload two topic choices; set a 1-2 minute timer for the chat.

Establish turn order ("You share, then I share").

Prompting & Fading (max 2 verbals)

Nonverbal first (doesn't count): point to Venn card/sentence strip.

Verbal 1: "Say a 'both' or 'different' sentence."

Verbal 2: "Use the frame: 'I like __; you prefer __.'"

After a prompted success, run a transfer trial later with longer quiet wait for independence.

Reinforcement

DRA: Labeled praise + token/point for calm participation and accurate reflection (bonus for independent or for giving both a similarity and a difference).

Exchange: choose next topic/partner, brief preferred activity.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Primary—Reflect Once (≤2 prompts): During a preference discussion, client states ≥1 accurate similarity or difference with calm body/voice, in ≥80% of opportunities with ≤2 verbal prompts across 3 sessions.

Stretch—Both + Different: Client provides one "both" and one "different" statement in the same chat, ≥60% of opportunities with ≤2 prompts.

Acknowledge-Close Script: Client uses a respectful close ("That's okay / Thanks for telling me") after differing views, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Regulate-Then-Reflect: If upset, client requests a ≤2-min pause and then completes one reflection, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Validate → Guide (≤7 words): "I hear you—use a compare sentence."

Two-choice redirect: "Say a 'both' or a 'different' sentence."

When-Then: "When you reflect once, then you pick the next topic."

If debating starts, planned ignore the argument and reinforce the pivot to a compare statement.

Offer micro-break if arousal rises; resume with an easier topic.

Respecting Boundaries

Property

The Client will ask before touching or using another person's belongings during activities Once Per Session with a Verbal Prompt

Target Behavior

Increase asking permission before touching/using another person's belongings during activities once per session with ≤1 verbal prompt.
Operational Definition

When the client wants/appears to want to use or move an item that belongs to another person (peer/adult; non-communal), the client:
Requests permission directed to the owner (or supervising adult if owner unavailable) with a clear phrase/AAC/sign:
"Is this yours? May I use it?" / "Can I borrow your scissors for 2 minutes?"

Waits for an answer (up to 10 seconds) without touching the item, and

Respects the response: uses and returns as agreed if "yes," or chooses an alternative if "no," ideally adding "Thanks" or "Okay."

Counts as success: one full cycle (ask → wait → respect/return) within the session, produced within 5-10 seconds of the intent/opportunity, with ≤1 verbal prompt (e.g., "Ask first.").
Does not include: grabbing/handling before asking; asking after taking; moving personal items "out of the way" without permission; communal/supply items clearly labeled as shared (track separately); emergencies (first-aid/safety).
Onset/offset: Onset when the client reaches toward/looks for/mentions the other's item or a need for it; offset when the client completes the ask-wait-respect sequence (and returns item if used).
Probable Function

Access to tangibles/activities; sometimes attention or autonomy/control; occasionally habit/inattention. Appropriate asking provides predictable access, clear boundaries, and positive social attention.
Proactive Strategies

Label spaces: "Personal" vs "Class/Shared" with icons; teach where alternatives are stored.

Post a mini-poster: Ask → Wait → Respect → Return/Thank.

Prepare alternatives (borrow bin/backup tools) so "no" still leads to success.

Prime before high-risk times: "If you need something that isn't yours, use your ask."

Ensure AAC has quick buttons: Is this yours? • May I use it? • For 2 minutes? • Thanks • Okay.

Teaching & Prompting

Model-Lead-Test (2-3 quick role-plays), then live opportunities.
Nonverbal cue (doesn't count toward verbal limit): point to the poster or item, palm-out "wait."

Single verbal prompt (max 1): "Ask first."

Coach concise, specific asks (what + how long): "May I borrow your marker for one problem?"

Teach return routine: place back/hand to owner + "Thanks."

Reinforcement

DRA: labeled praise for ask-wait-respect ("Nice job asking and waiting—thanks for returning it.").

Token/point/brief access early; thin as independent asking increases.

Bonus for independent (0 prompts) asks and for polite "thanks."

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

If access is limited/owner says no:
Request alternative: "Where's the borrow bin/extra?"

Request time/trade: "Can I use it after you?" / "Trade for my blue marker?"

Ask adult to page owner or confirm if item is personal vs communal.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Early signs (reaching/hovering): gentle nonverbal block + point to poster; if needed, the one verbal prompt.
 If denied access: coach a brief coping line—"Okay, I'll grab an extra," then immediately show alternative.
 If grabbing occurs: neutral reset—item back to owner, restate boundary, and have client redo the ask-wait sequence; reinforce the redo, not the grab.

Understanding Roles

Authority Structures

The Client will identify the next two levels of authority above self for a given setting and state "who answers to who and why" (one role - who they report to - one brief reason) with a verbal prompt.

Target Behavior: Identify the next two levels of authority above self and state "who answers to who and why" (≤1 verbal prompt)

Operational Definition

Given a setting (e.g., classroom, school bus, clinic, sports team, workplace) presented verbally or on a card, the client will, within 20-30 seconds, produce a two-step authority chain using speech or an approved modality (AAC/sign):
Self → Immediate authority + brief reason
"I am a student. I answer to the teacher because she runs the class and keeps us safe."

Immediate authority → Next authority + brief reason
"The teacher answers to the principal because the principal manages the whole school."

Success (per scenario) = both links above are stated clearly, directed to the listener, reasons are plausible for the setting, and the response occurs independently or with ≤1 brief verbal prompt (e.g., "Use your authority chain."). Gestural/visual cues don't count toward the prompt.
Non-examples: naming only one level; giving unrelated or value-judgment reasons ("because they're bossy"); inaccurate roles for the setting; needing ≥2 verbal prompts; refusal/arguing.
Opportunities & Measurement

Present 3-5 scenarios per session (mix settings).

Record per scenario: Accuracy (Self→Level 1, Level 1→Level 2, Reason quality Y/N each), Prompt (0/1), Latency (s), Notes.

Session success: ≥80% scenarios correct with ≤1 prompt.

Mastery criterion: Achieve session success on 4 of 5 consecutive sessions (then fade to 0 prompts and/or require two distinct reasons).

Probable Skill Domain

Executive function & social-systems understanding (organizational hierarchy, help-seeking pathways). Errors may be influenced by escape from language demand or control/rigidity about rules.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Micro-Skill (BST: Instruction → Modeling → Rehearsal → Feedback)

Sentence stem (card/AAC):
"I am a [role]. I answer to [role] because [reason]. [Role] answers to [role] because [reason]."

Authority Ladder visual: three rungs labeled ME → Level 1 → Level 2 with icons per setting.

Reason Bank (post on card): safety • rules/policies • resources/permission • scheduling • expertise/training • program leadership.

Antecedent Supports

Setting cards (Classroom, Bus, Clinic, Team, Workplace, Community program).

Role rings for each setting (e.g., Student, Teacher, Principal).

Think time: 5-10 s before expecting a response.

First-Then: "Name the chain → choose the next scenario."

Prompting (fade quickly)

Point to ME → L1 → L2 visual → (if needed) single verbal prompt: "Use your authority chain."

If a 2nd prompt would be needed, end the probe, model a correct chain, then run a quick repair trial later (not scored).

Reinforcement

Behavior-specific praise: "You named teacher → principal and gave safety and leadership reasons—nice job."

Tokens/points per correct scenario; exchange at session end.

Natural reinforcement: access to choose the next setting card or brief preferred activity.

Quick "Authority Ladder" Examples (for modeling)

Classroom: Me (Student) → Teacher (runs class, safety) → Principal (manages school, policies).

School Bus: Me (Rider) → Bus Driver (safety, bus rules) → Transportation Supervisor/Principal (routes, policy).

Clinic: Me (Client) → Technician/Therapist (implements plan) → BCBA/Clinical Supervisor (writes plan, oversight).

Sports: Me (Player) → Coach (practice/rules) → Athletic Director/Head Coach (program leadership).

Workplace: Me (Staff) → Supervisor (assigns tasks) → Manager/Director (department outcomes, policy).

Community Program: Me (Participant) → Group Leader (activity safety) → Program Coordinator (program rules/schedule).

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Primary chain: Produce both links + reasons within 20-30 s, ≤1 prompt, in 80% of scenarios.

Clarify when unsure: Ask a quick helper question—"Who is in charge of [setting]?" or "What's the principal's job?"—then complete chain, ≤1 prompt, 80%.

If titles are unknown: Use role description ("the person who runs the bus schedule") in place of the exact title, ≤1 prompt, 80%.

Help-seeking link: Add where to go for help in that chain (e.g., "If teacher isn't here, ask the office") in 60-80% of successes (shape up).

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Low-profile cueing: gesture to ladder → single cue ("Use your authority chain").

If client debates hierarchy: acknowledge ("Some places do it differently") and refocus: "Give one possible chain for this setting."

If stuck/anxious: offer binary support—"Use the sentence stem or pick from the reason bank."

Repair trial after a miss: model one accurate chain for that card, then have the client do a new card immediately to contact success.

The Client will follow the appropriate chain of command using a 3-step script: gain attention - state issue - request help/permission, and explain why that person is in charge. Once per session with a verbal prompts

Target Behavior: Follow Chain of Command With a 3-Step Script (≤1 verbal prompt)

Operational Definition

Chain-of-command request is defined as the client selecting the appropriate person in charge for a given issue and using the 3-step script to communicate—then adding a brief rationale for why that person is in charge. A response counts when, within 30 seconds of identifying an issue (or within 10 seconds of one brief verbal prompt), the client:
Gain attention: "Excuse me, [Title/Name]."

State the issue: "[Describe problem/need in one sentence]."

Request help/permission: "May I/Can we ___?"

Explain why that person is in charge: "You're in charge of ___ / You're the ___ for this."

Includes (examples):
"Excuse me, Ms. Lee. The tablet won't turn on. May I get a new one? You're in charge of classroom tech."

"Mr. Gomez, Sam and I had an argument. Can you help us solve it? You're our group leader."

Does not include:
Asking a peer or the wrong staff when a designated adult is available.

Skipping steps, arguing, or making multiple requests to different people ("shopping for yes").

Emergencies: nearest safe adult is acceptable (score N/A).

Measurement

Opportunity: Provide at least one scenario per session (natural or contrived).

Score each opportunity:
Independent = correct authority + full script + rationale, 0 prompts.

Prompted = same, after ≤1 verbal prompt (e.g., "Use your chain-of-command script").

Not met = wrong person, missing steps/rationale, >1 prompt, or >30 s.

Track: scenario type, authority chosen, latency, and whether assistance was secured.

Probable Function (when errors occur)

Common barriers: skill/role awareness deficit, impulsivity/attention seeking, or escape from expected process. Replacement gives a fast, respectful pathway to help.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine — "Get → Say → Ask → Why"

Get attention: "Excuse me, [Title/Name]."

Say the issue in one sentence.

Ask for help/permission.

Why this person: "You're in charge of __."

Use modeling → role-play → feedback with multiple scenarios (materials issue, permission request, peer conflict, schedule question, safety concern).
Visuals & Tools

Chain-of-Command Map (who handles: tech, behavior, schedule, health, facilities).

Wallet Script Card with blanks: "Excuse me, __. __ is happening. May I __? You're in charge of __."

Decision cue: "Is it tech / schedule / behavior / health? → see map."

Antecedent Modifications

Post titles on staff lanyards/doors; keep the map visible.

Provide two quick exemplars before high-risk times ("For laptop—Ms. Lee; for schedule—Mr. K.").

Clarify urgent exceptions: nearest adult.

Prompting & Fading (max 1 verbal prompt)

Nonverbal first (doesn't use the allowance): point to the map or script card.

Verbal (single): "Use your chain-of-command script."

Then wait 5-10 s silently. After success, run a transfer trial later (new scenario, no prompt).

Reinforcement

DRA: Labeled praise + token/point for correct authority + full script + rationale (bonus for independent or <15 s latency).

Provide the requested help/permission quickly when appropriate—this is natural reinforcement.

Thin from FR1 to VR2-3 as consistent.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Primary—Full Script to Correct Authority (≤1 prompt): Client selects the right person and completes Get → Say → Ask → Why within 30 s, ≥80% of sessions across 3 consecutive weeks.

If Unsure—Clarify, Then Script: Client asks, "Who's in charge of [issue]?" and, once identified, uses the script, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

One-and-Done Request: Client avoids "shopping for yes" (no second request to a different person) ≥90% of opportunities.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Brief redirect: "Who handles this? Check the map."

Two-choice rescue: "Tech or schedule?"

When-Then: "When you use the script with the right person, then we can solve it."

If the client asked the wrong person, coach a polite handoff: "Please ask Ms. Lee—she's in charge of tech."

Understanding Own Role

The Client will respond to questions demonstrating awareness of his role in the environment

Target Behavior

Increase responding to questions that demonstrate awareness of the client's role in the environment with ≤2 verbal prompts.
Operational Definition

When asked a role-awareness question (e.g., "What's your job right now?", "Where should you be and doing what?" "Who are you working with?"), the client gives a correct, on-topic answer within 10 seconds that identifies at least two role elements, such as:
Location (where to be)

Task/Action (what to do)

Partner/Supervision (with whom/for whom)

Rule/Safety (how to behave/any boundary)

Examples (count as success):
"I'm a reader at the quiet table, working with Ms. Lee."

"I'm on clean-up—put markers in the bin and push in chairs."

"I'm a player on defense; stay behind the line."

Does not include: silence >10s, "I don't know," off-topic answers, or incorrect role/location.
Measurement
Primary: % correct responses across 4-6 probes per session (goal initially ≥80%).

Secondary: prompts used (0/1/2), latency (s), quality (0-3)
0 = incorrect/none; 1 = one element only; 2 = two correct elements; 3 = ≥3 elements (e.g., task + place + rule).
 Onset/offset: Onset at end of question; offset at client response or 10s after the final allowed prompt.

Probable Function

Primarily skill acquisition (mapping roles to context); mis-responding may serve escape/avoidance (from expectations), attention, or reflect working-memory/executive or language demands.
Proactive Strategies

Role Map visual: Where • What • With whom • Rule (W-W-W-R).

Environment cues: labeled zones ("Quiet table," "Clean-up station"), lanyards/badges for roles, "Right-Now Role" card on desk.

Schedules: First-Then/visual schedule showing current role.

Pre-teach & preview: before transitions, state: "Your role: writer at table 2, with Sam, volume 2."

Minimize ambiguity: post 3-4 core roles for each setting with icons.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Teach the client to request support instead of guessing/avoiding:
"What's my role now?" / "Where should I be?"

"Show me on the map?" (points to visual)

"More time / repeat, please."

Teaching & Prompting

Model-Lead-Test using the Role Map: adult models a 2-3 element answer → guided practice → independent probes.

Prompting hierarchy (max 2 verbals):
Gesture to Role Map/zone sign.

Verbal 1: "Tell me where and what."

Verbal 2 (scaffold): "Start with: 'I am ___ at ___.'"

Fade to gesture-only cues quickly; keep language concise.

De-escalation Measures / reactive strategies

If off-topic/IDK: neutral redirect to two elements: "Where and what?" Offer A/B choice if needed (teaching phase).

If anxious/resistant: give 5-10s quiet think time; allow pointing to icons/AAC; acknowledge and restate role once.

If in wrong place: brief error correction—escort to correct zone, have client state role there, reinforce immediately.

Understanding Others Roles

The Client will respond to questions demonstrating awareness of others' roles in the environment

Target Behavior: Respond to questions showing awareness of others' roles in the environment (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

In everyday settings (e.g., classroom, clinic, cafeteria, office, bus, community), when asked a role-related question, the client will give a clear, accurate response within 10 seconds using speech or an approved modality (AAC/sign/pointing to a labeled photo).
Role-related question types (any count):
Identify the role/person - "Who is the school nurse?"

Describe role function - "What does the custodian do?"

Help-seeking route - "Who should you ask if you lose your schedule?"

Authority link - "Who does the therapist report to?"

Success (per opportunity) = correct answer + calm body/voice + ≤2 brief verbal prompts total (e.g., Prompt 1: "Answer the role question," Prompt 2: "Say the job or who to ask"). Gestural/visual cues don't count toward the two.
Non-examples: guessing unrelated people, saying "I don't know" without using supports, arguing, or needing ≥3 verbal prompts.
Measurement & Mastery

Provide 4-6 questions per session across at least two settings.

Record: Question type, Correct (Y/N), Prompts (0/1/2), Latency (s), Notes.

Mastery criterion: ≥80% correct with ≤2 prompts across 4 of 5 consecutive sessions. Then fade to ≤1 prompt and/or add "because" reason.

Probable Skill Domain

Social-cognition & executive functioning (understanding roles, responsibilities, and help pathways). Errors may be affected by escape from language demand or anxiety/rigidity.
Proactive Strategies

Teach & Practice (BST)

Instruction → Modeling → Role-play → Feedback using real photos/names from each setting.

Sentence stems / AAC buttons:
"The [role] is [name]."

"The [role] is responsible for [task] because [reason]."

"If I need [issue], I ask the [role]."

Role Map posters: pictures/names of staff with icons (nurse-health, custodian-clean/safety, counselor-feelings, admin-rules/schedule).

Antecedent Supports

Warm-up walkabouts: point out roles in the moment.

Reason bank (why): safety • rules/policies • supplies/repairs • learning/help • scheduling.

Choice of supports: Role Map, lanyard badges, area signs, or a quick "Who does what?" card.

Prompting (fade quickly)

Gesture to Role Map → Verbal Prompt 1 ("Answer the role question") → wait 5-10 s → Verbal Prompt 2 ("Say who and what they do"). If a 3rd would be needed, model once and run a brief repair trial later.

Reinforcement

Behavior-specific praise: "You said 'Ask the librarian for books'—exactly right."

Tokens/points for correct answers; bonus for 0 prompts.

Natural reinforcement: immediate access to the correct help route in real time.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Use supports when unsure: "Can I check the Role Map?" then answer, ≤2 prompts, in 80% of need moments.

Clarify the question: "Do you mean who or what they do?" ≤1 prompt, 80%.

Ask-for-help routing: "Who is in charge of [issue]?" then restate the answer, ≤2 prompts, 80%.

Reason add-on (advanced): Include a brief "because" in 60-80% of answers (shape up).

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Low-profile cues: point to Role Map → brief cue ("Who handles that?").

If the client says "I don't know," redirect to a support (map/badge/sign) and reinforce the use of supports.

Keep language brief; avoid debates about titles—accept accurate role descriptions when names are unknown.

Following Directions

Following Multi-Step Directions

Following Single-Step Directions

The Client Will Successfully Follow a One-Step Instruction, Such as "Please Hand Me the Book," Once Per Session

Target Behavior: Follow a One-Step Instruction (once per session)

Operational Definition

Following a one-step instruction is defined as the client starting within 10 seconds of a clear, single action direction (SD) and completing the action as specified when materials are available and the demand is developmentally appropriate.
 Examples of SDs: "Please hand me the book," "Sit in the blue chair," "Point to your name," "Turn off the light."
Counts as success:
Begins the action ≤10 s after the SD and completes it without pauses >20 s.

May include ≤1 minimal prompt (e.g., brief gesture) for the session goal; track prompt level.

Does not count:
Ignoring/arguing, doing a different action, starting only after >1 prompt, unclear SDs, missing materials, or safety/medical interference (score N/A).

Measurement
Provide at least one trial per session (natural or contrived).

Score: Independent (0 prompts), Prompted (≤1 minimal prompt), Not met (>1 prompt/no completion).

Record latency to start and type of instruction delivered.

Probable Function (when the response doesn't occur)

Most commonly escape/avoidance of demand, inattention/prompt dependency, or uncertainty about the SD. Plan focuses on clear cues, quick starts, and strong reinforcement.
Proactive Strategies

Delivering the SD

Gain attention first (name, eye-level), then give one concise action ("Hand me the book").

Ensure materials are present and the action is realistic.

Use neutral tone and avoid stacked instructions.

For early success, use behavioral momentum: 2-3 easy one-step requests before a harder task.

Visuals/Supports

Post a simple First-Then or "Do → Done" card.

For location-based SDs, use clear landmarks (colored chair, arrow to bin).

Prompting & Fading

Wait 3-5 s after the SD (silent).

If needed, give one least-intrusive prompt (gesture/model), then fade on next trial.

Use a quick transfer trial (re-present SD with no prompt) to shift control to the SD.

Reinforcement

DRC (Differential Reinforcement of Compliance): Immediate, labeled praise for starting within 10 s and finishing (bonus for independent).

Pair with a token/point or brief preferred access; start FR1, then thin to VR2-3 as consistent.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Clarify-Then-Do: If unsure, the client asks a brief question ("Which book?") within 5 s, then completes the SD; ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Start-First-Step: Client initiates the first movement toward the action within 10 s (stand up/reach/grasp) and completes without >20 s pause; ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Break/Help Request (if needed): Client says "Help" or shows a break card instead of refusing, then follows the agreed plan and completes the SD afterward; ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Neutral re-presentation (once): Restate the SD one time; wait quietly.

When-Then: "When you pass the book, then you earn ___."

Two-choice redirect: "Hand me the book or point to it and I'll grab it."

Minimize attention to refusal; reinforce the pivot the moment the client initiates the action.

If no start after ~2 minutes, defer and adjust (shorter step, stronger antecedents) for the next trial.

Coping Skills

Challenging Situations

The Client Will Practice Coping Strategies for Challenging Situations, in DTT with Verbal Prompt

Target Behavior

The client will select and perform an appropriate coping strategy in response to a contrived challenging-situation cue during Discrete Trial Training (DTT), with ≤2 verbal prompts.
Operational Definition

Within a DTT trial, when presented with an SD (e.g., "You just lost a turn" + visual), the client will begin and carry out a pre-taught coping strategy within 10 seconds, and sustain/complete it for 30-60 seconds (as appropriate to the strategy) or until the planned end signal.
 Coping strategy set (teach 4-6):
Regulate: 2-5 calm breaths (box/belly breath), 5-4-3-2-1 grounding, count to 10, stretch/squeeze tool.

Communicate: "Break please," "Help please," "More time," "I feel ___."

Cognitive: coping self-talk ("I can handle this"), reframe ("It's practice, not perfect").

Plan/Problem-solve: choose A/B option, first-then plan, ask for the rule check.

Success = starts within 10s, completes the strategy as taught, and keeps body/voice safe/neutral; ≤2 verbal prompts allowed (gesture/visuals don't count toward the limit).
Does not include: arguing/avoidance, incomplete/incorrect strategy, or starting after 10s without a prompt.
DTT Protocol (how to run trials)

Trial structure (10-12 trials/session):
SD (discriminative stimulus): brief scenario card/mini-skit (e.g., "Timer ended," "Peer got the last turn," "Work got harder").

Wait 5-10s for independent strategy.

Prompting (least→most; max 2 verbals): gesture to menu → Verbal 1 ("Choose a coping strategy now") → Verbal 2 (carrier: "Try breathing / Break please").

Consequence: reinforce correct performance (specific praise + token/access) and transfer trial (run a similar SD aiming for less prompt).

Intertrial interval: 5-10s neutral.

Error correction: quick model-lead-test, then immediate transfer trial with reduced prompt.

Generalization mini-block: 2-3 "naturalistic" probes at end with subtler cues, varied settings/partners.

Probable Function

Challenging moments often evoke behaviors maintained by escape/avoidance (from effort or emotion), access (to items/turns), attention, or control/autonomy. Coping strategies provide functionally equivalent ways to regulate, request, or plan—contacting reinforcement for calm, skillful responses.
Proactive Strategies

Teach first, then test: quick model-lead-test for each strategy; post a Coping Menu (icons).

Prime: "In DTT, when it's tough, pick one strategy fast."

Make it easy to pick: place fidget/visual timer within reach; AAC buttons for Break/Help/More time.

Task size: start with mild triggers; increase intensity gradually (noise, time pressure, loss, error).

Motivation: dense reinforcement at first (FR1 for independent or first-prompt responses), then thin.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

"Break please (2 minutes), then I'll return."

"Help please / Which step first?"

"More time (30s)?"

"A or B?" (choice to regain control).

"Rule check?" (in games/tasks).

De-escalation Measures / Reactive Strategies

Early signs (sighing, tense posture, "this is dumb"): point to Coping Menu; Verbal 1 prompt; keep voice low; offer A/B coping choice.

If escalation continues: Verbal 2 with carrier phrase; switch to regulate first (breath/grounding), then communicate.

If unsafe/near meltdown: pause DTT, run the pre-taught crisis plan (space, minimal language, safety); after calm, do a brief repair trial (very easy SD) to end on success.

Confusion

The client will utilize a confusion scale to identify how confused they are with the task in demand

Target Behavior: Use a "Confusion Scale" to report understanding of the current task (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

During an assigned task ("task in demand"), the client will rate their understanding using a pre-taught Confusion Scale and, when needed, take the matching action (e.g., ask for help/clarification).
 A check occurs at pre-set times (e.g., every 2-5 minutes or at step boundaries). A check is successful when the client:
Selects a scale level within 10 seconds of the check cue (timer/beep/teacher signal),

Shows the rating (card/AAC/gesture) clearly to staff,

Follows the matching action for that level (see below), and

Requires ≤2 brief verbal prompts total (gestural/visual cues don't count).

Non-examples: shrug/"I don't know," no rating after the cue, ratings that don't match behavior (e.g., selects "green" while not working), needing ≥3 verbal prompts, or escalation instead of using the scale.
The Confusion Scale (post on card/AAC)

0 - Green: I've got it. → Keep working.

1 - Yellow: A little unsure. → Keep working + re-read directions or check example.

2 - Orange: Confused. → Ask a specific question or request an example ("Can you show step 1?").

3 - Red: Very confused/blocked. → Ask for help now or a brief 1-2 min break, then resume.

(Icons/colors recommended; provide AAC buttons for 0/1/2/3 and matching action phrases.)
Measurement & Mastery

Plan 4-8 checks per work block (2-5 min intervals or step-based).

Record per check: Rating (0-3), Action taken (none/re-read/example/help/break), Prompts (0/1/2), Latency to rate (s), On-task next 1 min (Y/N).

Block success: client completes ≥80% of planned checks with ≤2 prompts and uses the matching action for all ratings ≥2.

Mastery: Achieve block success on 4 of 5 consecutive sessions. Then fade to ≤1 prompt and/or lengthen intervals.

Probable Function

Skill acquisition (self-awareness/self-advocacy). Competing behaviors (avoidance, calling out, shutdown) may be maintained by escape, attention, or automatic reinforcement—confirm with brief ABC notes.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine (BST: Instruction → Modeling → Rehearsal → Feedback)

Explain each level with examples/non-examples.

Model a check: timer beeps → show card → take matching action.

Guided practice with easy tasks → fade prompts → independent probes.

Setup & Supports

Visible scale (laminated card, desk strip, or AAC page) + matching action menu.

Timer (visual/vibrate) for checks.

Worked example and step checklist available for "1-2" ratings.

First-Then: "Run your scale → choice/minibreak."

Prompting (fade quickly)

Gesture to scale/timer → Verbal Prompt 1 ("Rate now") → wait up to 10 s → Verbal Prompt 2 ("Do the matching action").

If a 3rd prompt would be needed, pause scoring, model one full check, then run a quick repair check (not scored).

Reinforcement

Behavior-specific praise: "You rated '2' and asked for an example—great self-advocacy."

Tokens/points for each successful check; bonus for 0 prompts across a block.

Natural reinforcement: faster progress, immediate help when rated accurately.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Accurate rating on cue: Client selects 0-3 within 10 s, ≤2 prompts, in 80% of checks.

Action match: For 2-3, client uses a specific help/clarify/break request within 10 s of rating, ≤2 prompts, 80%.

Self-repair: If the client forgets a check, they state, "Missed check—rating now," and proceed, ≤1 prompt, 80%.

Clarifier when unsure: "What does level 2 mean again?" then rate, ≤1 prompt, 80%.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Low-profile cueing: tap scale/timer → brief cue ("Rate now") → brief cue ("Match your action").

Binary choice: "Ask a question or take a 1-minute break?" (for level 3).

No debates about the chosen level; accept good-faith ratings and support the matching action.

If escalation starts: neutral reset ("30-sec reset, then we rate"), then resume with an easier step and prompt the scale.

Stressful Conversations

The Client will utilize coping strategies such as using deep breathing or a stress ball during stressful conversations with 2 verbal prompts

Target Behavior: Use Coping Strategies During Stressful Conversations (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

Coping during conversation is defined as the client initiating and using a taught regulation strategy (e.g., deep breathing or stress-ball squeeze) while remaining in—or briefly pausing and returning to—the conversation when signs of stress appear (e.g., faster/louder voice, clenched hands, interrupting, rigid tone).
 A response counts when the client, within 10 seconds of a detectable stress cue or within 10 seconds of a prompt, does one or more of the following for 20-60 seconds and then continues the talk appropriately:
Deep breathing (e.g., box breath 4-4-4-4)

Stress ball/fidget squeeze (quiet, below the table if possible)

Grounding cue (feet flat, shoulders down)

Brief self-talk: "I can handle this; slow down."

Counts as success: calm/inside voice (level 2-3), no profanity/arguing spike, stays engaged or uses a pre-taught micro-break card (≤2 min) and returns within 10 s of timer.
Does not count: walking off without permission, escalating (yelling, insults), refusing to re-engage, or needing >2 verbal prompts.
Measurement
Opportunities: Any conversation likely to evoke stress (feedback, disagreement, limit-setting).

Score per opportunity:
Independent = used strategy within 10 s, 0 verbal prompts, stayed/returned to conversation.

Prompted = success after ≤2 brief verbal prompts.

Not met = no strategy, escalation, left without plan, or >2 prompts.

Track: strategy used, latency to start, duration of strategy use, whether conversation continued/returned.

Probable Function (of problem responses)

Often autonomy/control and escape from emotional discomfort; sometimes attention. Replacement is in-place regulation that lets the client keep access to the interaction without escalation.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine — "Notice → Do → Continue"

Notice: Spot a cue (heart fast, tight hands, urge to interrupt).

Do one strategy immediately (Breathe or Squeeze).

Continue: speak in level-2/3 voice, or use the Micro-Break card and return.

Practice with model → role-play → real conversations, starting with low-stakes topics.
Visuals & Tools

Coping Menu card (Breathe / Squeeze / Ground / Micro-Break).

Voice meter 0-5 (target 2-3).

Micro-Break card (≤2 min) + small timer.

Quiet fidget/stress ball available at seat.

Antecedent Modifications

Preview tough talks ("Feedback coming—have your coping card ready").

Set conversation rules: no interruptions, take turns, okay to pause.

Choose a quieter spot; minimize competing stimuli.

Prompting & Fading (max 2 verbals)

Nonverbal first (doesn't use the allowance): gesture breathing icon, point to Coping Menu/ball.

Verbal 1: "Use your coping strategy."

Verbal 2: "Breathe/squeeze now."
 Then wait 5-10 s silently. After prompted success, run a transfer trial later with longer quiet wait to build independence.

Reinforcement

DRA: Labeled praise + token/point for initiating a strategy and keeping/returning to the conversation (bonus for independent or <5 s latency).

Natural reinforcement: problem-solving continues; access to preferred activity after the talk.

Thin from FR1 to VR2-3 as consistent.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Primary—Coping Use (≤2 prompts): During a stressful conversation, client initiates deep breathing or stress-ball squeeze within 10 s of a cue (or prompt) and continues/returns to the conversation, in ≥80% of opportunities with ≤2 verbal prompts across 3 sessions.

Micro-Break Repair: If needed, client uses Micro-Break card (≤2 min) and returns within 10 s to resume, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Self-Advocacy Cue: Client says "Please slow down / give me 10 seconds" and then uses a strategy, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Voice Control: Maintains level-2/3 voice during/after strategy use, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Validate → Guide (≤7 words): "I hear you—use a coping strategy."

When-Then: "When you breathe, then we continue."

Planned ignore of minor sighs/complaints; reinforce the pivot to a strategy immediately.

If escalation rises, offer two-choice: "Breathe 4 counts or squeeze for 10."

For near-boil, allow Micro-Break; set timer; praise the return and finish the topic.

Intense Emotions

The client will identify a situation that will trigger intense emotions with 2 verbal prompts

Target Behavior

Increase stating a personal trigger situation (something likely to spark intense emotions) with ≤2 verbal prompts.
Operational Definition

Given a structured cue (e.g., prompt card, photo, short scenario) or a brief check-in about recent events, the client will name or select one specific situation that typically triggers intense emotion (client-rated ≥7/10 on a feelings scale) within 15 seconds.
 A qualifying response may include:
Situation only: "When someone laughs at me."

Situation + emotion: "When plans change last minute—I get anxious."

Situation + early sign: "Crowded lunchroom—I feel hot and my heart races."

Does not include: vague responses ("stuff at school"), off-topic comments, or situations the client says are neutral/low (≤3/10) without clarification.
Measurement

Primary: Trials correct per session (recommend 3-5 probes): named 1 specific trigger with ≤2 prompts and ≤15s latency.

Secondary: prompts used (0/1/2), latency (s), intensity rating (0-10), and quality (0-3):
0 = none/incorrect; 1 = generic ("school"); 2 = specific ("math tests"); 3 = specific + emotion or early sign.
 Onset/offset: Onset at end of the clinician's cue; offset at response or 15s after the final allowed prompt.

Probable Function

This is a skill-acquisition/insight target. Avoiding or giving vague answers can function as escape from uncomfortable feelings, control/autonomy, or attention redirection; accurate identification is maintained by competence, predictability, and effective coping planning.
Proactive Strategies

Define "intense." Use a Feelings Thermometer (0-10); "intense" = 7-10.

Trigger Map (visual): common categories with icons—Social (teasing, being left out), Performance (tests, being called on), Change (sudden plan shifts), Sensory (noise, crowds), Fairness/Loss, Rules/Corrections, Waiting/Delays.

Sentence stems / AAC buttons: "I get upset when ___," "A big trigger is ___," "I feel ___ when ___."

Prime safely: normalize the task ("Everyone has triggers; we're just spotting patterns").

Keep it concrete: photos/picture cards of typical school/home situations.

Teaching & Prompting

Model-Lead-Test (quick): clinician models ("A big trigger for me is loud sudden noises"), client practices with support, then responds independently.

Least→Most prompting (max 2 verbals):
Gesture to Trigger Map/Feelings Thermometer (no words).

Verbal Prompt 1: "Name one situation that really sets off big feelings."

Verbal Prompt 2 (scaffold): "Pick from a row: Social, Performance, Change, Sensory—which one? What situation there?"

Optional forced-choice only during teaching (not counted toward mastery): "Is it tests or crowds?"

Reinforcement

Specific praise for clear, specific triggers ("Good job picking 'when plans change suddenly'—very specific.").

Token/point/brief preferred activity early; thin as accuracy becomes consistent.

Bonus for independent (0-1 prompt) and quality=3 responses.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

If the question feels hard, teach the client to use:
"More time, please."

"Give me choices."

"Can I point to a picture?" / "Let me write/draw it."

"Pass—come back." (one pass per session; must answer later in the same session)

De-escalation Measures / Reactive Strategies

Early signs of distress (shrugging, "I don't know," visible tension):
Lower demand: offer A/B choice with pictures; allow pointing or rating first ("Circle anything 7-10").

Keep tone neutral; validate briefly ("Makes sense that this is tough—one clear example is enough").

If escalation rises:
Shift to a regulation micro-step (2 calm breaths) → retry with an easier category or a past, non-current example.

Avoid probing for details of traumatic events; keep focus on labeling the situation, not processing it.

Size of the Problem

The client will answer three questions to help him label the size of a problem accurately

Target Behavior: Answer three questions to label the size of a problem accurately (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

Given a real or contrived problem scenario (live, picture, or 1-3 sentence vignette), the client will:
Answer three size-of-problem questions,

State the problem size (Small / Medium / Big) using speech or an approved modality (AAC/sign), and

(Optional but recommended) Name one matching reaction for that size.

A trial is successful when the client completes the 3 answers + size label (+ reaction, if used) within 60 seconds, with ≤2 brief verbal prompts (gestural/visual cues don't count), and the size label matches the preset rubric for that scenario.
Non-examples: skipping questions; vague answers; inconsistent label (e.g., calls a broken pencil "big"); needing ≥3 verbal prompts; escalation/arguing.
The Three Questions (simple, repeatable)

Safety: "Is anyone in danger or hurt?"

Scope/Impact: "How many people/things are affected and how big is the problem?"

Fix/Effort: "Can I fix it myself quickly, or do I need adult/emergency help?"

Decision Rules

If Q1 = Yes (danger) → Big.

If Q1 = No:
Small = affects one/few, minor impact, client can fix fast (≈2 min) with simple strategies.

Medium = affects several / moderate impact or needs adult help/time (≈5-30 min).

Big = affects many/major impact or needs urgent/specialized help (can't fix today/emergency).

Matching Reactions (teach to "match the size")

Small: quiet coping (breath, sharpen pencil, ask for help, try again).

Medium: ask adult to problem-solve, use timer/repair/plan.

Big: get help now (safety first, follow crisis plan).

Measurement & Mastery

Opportunities: 4-6 scenarios per session (mix real + contrived).

Record per trial: Answered 3? (Y/N) • Correct size (Y/N) • Prompts (0/1/2) • Latency (s) • Reaction matched? (Y/N) • Notes.

Session success: ≥80% of trials with correct size and ≤2 prompts.

Mastery: Session success on 4 of 5 consecutive sessions. Then fade to ≤1 prompt and require reaction naming every time.

Probable Function

This is a skill-acquisition goal (judgment/perspective-taking). Mis-sizing may be influenced by escape (from demands), attention (big reactions), or anxiety/rigidity—monitor via ABC notes.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine (BST: Instruction → Modeling → Rehearsal → Feedback)

Post a Stoplight Visual (Green = Small, Yellow = Medium, Red = Big) with the three questions beside it.

Model with think-alouds: "No danger… one person… I can fix it—Small."

Role-play 3-5 quick scenarios/day; mix easy → ambiguous; rotate peers/contexts.

Add an AAC page or card with: "Small / Medium / Big" buttons and "Because…" stems.

Antecedent Supports

Sentence stems: "No danger / one person / I can fix = Small."

Reason bank: safety • number of people • time to fix • needs adult • cost/damage.

First-Then: "Label size → choose matching reaction/earn token."

Think time: allow 5-10 seconds before expecting an answer.

Prompting (fade quickly)

Gesture to visual → Verbal Prompt 1: "Answer the three questions." → wait → Verbal Prompt 2: "Label the size."

If a 3rd prompt would be needed, model once and run a repair trial (not scored).

Reinforcement

Behavior-specific praise: "You checked safety, impact, fix—labeled Medium. Nice match."

Tokens/points per correct label; bonus for 0 prompts or naming a matching reaction.

Natural reinforcement: immediate access to the appropriate fix/plan.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Three-Question Routine (primary): Client answers all 3 questions and labels size within 60 s, ≤2 prompts, in 80% of trials.

Clarifier when unsure: "Can I check the questions card?" then label, ≤1 additional prompt, 80%.

Coping instead of overreaction: Uses a size-matched reaction (small=quiet fix; medium=ask adult; big=get help) ≤2 prompts, 80%.

Self-correction: If label doesn't fit, client revises after a brief cue ("Check safety/scope/fix"), ≤1 prompt, 70-80%.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Low-profile cueing: point to stoplight → brief cue ("Safety? Scope? Fix? Label.").

If escalation starts: offer binary choice—"Use small reaction or take a 30-sec reset."

Avoid debate over ambiguous cases; reinforce evidence-based reasoning ("You picked Medium because it needs adult help—good justification").

Repair trial after a miss: present an easy scenario, guide through questions, label correctly, praise.

Scenario Starters (customize)

Small: pencil breaks; can't find eraser; tab closes on Chromebook.

Medium: group argument over rules; assignment missing instructions; cafeteria spill.

Big: injury/bleeding; someone elopes; fire alarm/smoke.

The Client will respond to a no-problem situation e.g., minor inconvenience, waiting, or change in plans by demonstrating calm and socially acceptable behavior Once Per Session with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior: Respond Calmly to "No-Problem" Situations (≤2 verbal prompts; once per session)

Operational Definition

A no-problem situation is a minor inconvenience (e.g., short wait, small change in plans, brief delay, item unavailable, minor error) that does not threaten safety or major outcomes.
 Calm, socially acceptable responding is defined as the client, within 30 seconds of the no-problem cue/event (or within 10 seconds of a prompt), doing all of the following and maintaining it for the brief period required (typically 1-2 minutes or until the situation resolves):
Voice at level 2-3 (inside voice); no profanity.

Body calm (hands to self, stays in area, no door slams/throwing).

Uses one taught replacement: e.g., "Okay," waits, asks one respectful question, or chooses an alternate from the plan menu.

No arguing/complaining cycle (≤1 short request, then accepts).

Includes (examples):
"Okay—I can wait," then quiet waiting until called.

"Got it—plan changed. Can I do math first?"

"They're using the red marker; I'll use blue."

Does not include:
Arguing, repeated complaints, refusal, loud sighs paired with refusal, leaving area without permission, or attempts to renegotiate after a clear "not now."

Situations that are not minor (safety/medical)—score N/A.

Prompt allowance: Up to two brief verbal prompts (e.g., "Use your small-problem plan," "Pick wait or alternate"). Nonverbal cues do not use the prompt allowance.
Measurement

Provide at least one opportunity per session (natural or contrived).

Score each opportunity:
Independent = meets criteria with 0 prompts.

Prompted = meets criteria after ≤2 verbal prompts.

Not met = escalation/refusal/>2 prompts.

Track: trigger type (wait/change/unavailable), latency to calm response (goal ≤30 s), whether a replacement was used, and duration of successful waiting/alternate.

Probable Function (of problem behavior in these moments)

Often attention (getting adult engagement), escape from waiting/uncertainty, and/or control/autonomy. Replacement teaches quick acceptance + practical next step.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine — "Size → Say → Choose → Do"

Size it: "Is this a small/no problem?" (use 0-1 on a problem scale).

Say a calm acknowledgment: "Okay," "No big deal," "I can wait."

Choose a tool: Wait, Alternate, Ask one question, or Micro-break.

Do it: follow through until it's your turn or the change is finished.

Practice with model → role-play → feedback across common triggers.

Visuals & Tools

Problem-Size Scale (0-5) with "0-1 = no/small problem → use plan."

Small-Problem Plan Menu: Wait quietly, pick alternate, ask one question, micro-break (30-60 s).

First-Then card; Wait Menu options (book, doodle, fidget).

One-Ask rule poster: "Ask once → accept."

Antecedent Modifications

Give advance warnings for likely changes ("Schedule switch after centers").

Always pair waits/changes with a clear endpoint (timer, order in line).

Offer bounded choices (order/material) to preserve autonomy.

Reinforcement

DRA/DRO: Labeled praise + token/point for calm response (bonus for independent and <10 s latency).

Natural reinforcement: faster access once the wait ends; first choice of next activity.

Consider DRO 5-10 min following the event for zero arguing/complaining.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Calm Acknowledgment: "Okay / I can wait / No big deal," ≥80% of opportunities with ≤2 prompts.

Wait Skill: Quiet wait using Wait Menu until timer/turn, ≥80% with ≤2 prompts.

Alternate Plan: Chooses an acceptable alternate (different material/order) and starts within 10 s, ≥80% with ≤2 prompts.

One-Ask Then Accept: Asks one respectful question ("When will it be my turn?"), accepts the answer, ≥80% with ≤2 prompts.

Prompting & Fading (max 2 verbals)

Nonverbal first: point to Problem-Size scale or Plan Menu (doesn't count).

Verbal 1: "Use your small-problem plan."

Verbal 2: "Pick wait or alternate now."
 Then wait 5-10 s silently. After prompted success, run a transfer trial later with longer silent wait to build independence.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Validate → Guide (≤7 words): "I hear you—small problem plan."

When-Then: "When you wait calmly, then you choose first."

Two-choice redirect: "Alternate or micro-break?"

Planned ignore of minor grumbling; reinforce the pivot to calm behavior immediately.

If arousal rises, offer 30-60 s micro-break; resume plan with a timer.

The Client will label a minor everyday problem and state or demonstrate an independent problem-solving strategy Once Per Session with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior

The client will label a minor everyday problem and state or demonstrate an independent problem-solving strategy once per session with ≤2 verbal prompts.
Operational Definition

A minor everyday problem = small, non-safety issue that can be solved quickly (≈1-3 minutes) without adult intervention.
 Examples: missing pencil, dull/broken pencil, small spill, worksheet misplaced, Chromebook low battery, glue dried up, partner absent, website tab closed, schedule tweak, need a ruler/scissors, line is long.
 Not included (handled by staff): injuries, bullying, property damage, major tech failure, lost valuables.
A qualifying response occurs when the client, within 15-20 seconds of the cue or noticing the problem:
Labels the problem specifically (e.g., "My pencil broke"), and

States or begins an independent strategy from the taught menu (e.g., "Sharpen it," "Grab a backup," "Wipe the spill," "Plug in and switch to paper," "Use the 'Return Tray'," "Open the rubric from Drive").

Completes the first step and proceeds until the problem is functionally resolved or a planned checkpoint (≤3 minutes).

Success (session): at least one full instance (label + strategy started) with ≤2 verbal prompts.
 Onset/offset: Onset at problem cue/recognition; offset when the strategy is started and carried through to resolution/checkpoint.
Probable Function

This is a skill-acquisition/exec-function goal. Problem behaviors at these moments (complaining, calling out "I can't," task refusal) often serve escape/avoidance, attention, or control/autonomy. Independent fixes contact competence, predictability, and positive attention for self-management.
Proactive Strategies

Fix-It Menu (post/portable):
Find/Replace: backup pencil, spare worksheet, extra headphones.

Repair/Reset: sharpen, tape/staple, undo/redo, reopen tab, restart timer.

Clean/Organize: wipe, trash, return to bin, label & file.

Wait/Work-around: pick next step, partner with neighbor, use paper instead of device.

(If needed after 2 independent steps: polite help request.)

4-Step Routine (S.T.E.P.): See it → Title it (name the problem) → Execute a fix → Peek/check result.

Environment: backup supplies bin, cleanup kit, labeled return tray, device chargers visible, quick-links bookmark bar.

Visuals: Problem Size Meter (1-2 = handle yourself; 3+ = get adult).

Priming: before work blocks: "If a small problem pops up, use S.T.E.P. and the Fix-It Menu."

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Teach these brief alternatives instead of complaining/refusing:
"I'll try a fix."

"More time (30s)?"

"Choice of two fixes?"

"After two tries, may I ask for help?"

Teaching & Prompting

Model-Lead-Test with 3-5 contrived minis (broken pencil, small spill, missing paper).

Prompting hierarchy (max 2 verbals total):
Nonverbal: point to S.T.E.P./Fix-It Menu.

Verbal Prompt 1: "Name the problem."

Verbal Prompt 2: "Pick a fix and start."

Use transfer trials (similar problem, fewer prompts) to build independence.

De-escalation Measures / reactive strategies

Early frustration (sighs, "I can't"): lower voice, point to menu; offer A/B fixes; start a 10-20s micro-start (hand them the backup, begin first wipe).

If stuck after Prompt 2: give a single forced choice (teaching phase only), then fade.

If problem is actually larger (size ≥3): validate and route to adult plan; do not insist on independence.

The Client will label a small problem in a natural setting and use a defined coping or problem-solving strategy Once Per Session with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior: Label a small problem in a natural setting and use a defined coping/problem-solving strategy — 1× per session (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

During everyday routines (classwork, transitions, games, community), when a minor setback occurs, the client will:
Label the problem as "small" (speech/AAC/sign) within 10 seconds of an adult cue or noticing it, and

Use one pre-taught coping or problem-solving strategy that matches a small problem, then

Return to the task/activity.

"Small problem" = no one is unsafe, impact is minor/short, and the client can fix or accept it quickly (≈2 minutes) with simple tools/help.
Small-problem Strategy Menu (teach these):
Replace/repair: sharpen pencil, get another, reopen tab, tape page, tidy small spill.

Clarify: re-read directions, check example, ask a specific question.

Coping: 3 breaths, count to 10, brief sip of water, quick stretch.

Plan: "I'll do #2 instead and come back," set a 1-min timer.

Prompts allowed: up to two brief verbal prompts total (e.g., Prompt 1: "Label the size." Prompt 2: "Pick a strategy."). Gestural/visual cues don't count toward the two.
Success (for the one required opportunity per session) = client clearly labels "small problem," uses one strategy from the menu, and resumes the task within 2 minutes, with ≤2 verbal prompts and no maladaptive behavior (yelling, refusal, elopement, property misuse).
Non-examples: calling it "big," debating, requesting to stop the whole activity, needing ≥3 verbal prompts, or escalating.
Measurement

Opportunities: track 1 natural instance per session (contrive if none occur).

Record: Success (Y/N) • Prompts (0/1/2) • Latency to label (s) • Strategy used • Return ≤2:00 (Y/N) • Notes/Context.

Mastery criterion: Session success on 4 of 5 consecutive sessions. Then fade to ≤1 prompt or require two small-problem repairs per session.

Probable Function

This is a skill-acquisition goal (emotion regulation/problem solving). Prior escalations may be maintained by escape, attention, or automatic reinforcement—use brief ABC notes to tailor supports.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine (BST: Instruction → Modeling → Rehearsal → Feedback)

Stoplight visual: Green = Small • Yellow = Medium • Red = Big.

Script card/AAC page:
"This is a small problem." → "I will [strategy]." → "Back to work."

Role-play 2-3 quick scenarios (broken tip, lost eraser, slow loading page), then practice in vivo.

Antecedent Supports

Strategy Menu posted on desk/device; tools ready (extra pencil, tape, example).

Pre-correction before high-demand blocks: "If a small problem pops up, say 'small problem' and use your menu."

First-Then: "Fix small problem → choice for 30-60 s."

Prompting (fade quickly)

Gesture to stoplight/menu → Verbal Prompt 1: "Label the size." → wait up to 10 s → Verbal Prompt 2: "Pick a strategy."

If a third prompt would be needed, model once, run a quick repair trial (not scored), then return to task.

Reinforcement

Behavior-specific praise: "You called it small and grabbed a new pencil—perfect fix."

Token/points for each successful session; bonus for 0 prompts or ≤60 s total repair time.

Natural reinforcement: smoother progress, immediate access to the next preferred step/role.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Size label + strategy (primary): Say "small problem" and use one menu strategy within 2:00, ≤2 prompts, in 80% of sessions.

Clarifier instead of complaining: "What's step 1?" / "Can I see the example?" ≤1 prompt, 80%.

Brief reset (if rising): "30-second reset, then I'll fix it," returns on time ≤1 prompt, 80%.

Self-statement: "It's small; I can fix this," independent in 60-80% of instances (shape up).

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Low-profile cues: point to stoplight → cue "Small" → point to menu.

Binary choices: "Sharpen or grab a new pencil?"

No debate: if client argues "It's big," acknowledge once ("Feels big; check safety/time—still small to fix"), then prompt strategy.

After any miss: run a repair trial (easy small problem → label → fix) to contact reinforcement.

The Client will label a medium-sized problem that requires adult help and use an appropriate help-seeking strategy Once Per Session with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior: Label a Medium-Sized Problem & Use Help-Seeking Strategy (≤2 verbal prompts; once per session)

Operational Definition

A medium-sized problem is an issue that is not an emergency and cannot be solved independently (needs adult assistance), e.g., tech won't turn on, missing materials the client can't access, peer conflict needing mediation, small spill, schedule confusion.
 A response counts when, within 30 seconds of noticing the problem (or within 10 seconds of a prompt), the client:
Labels the size: "This is a medium problem," or points to 2-3 on the Problem-Size Scale,

Uses a help-seeking script to the correct adult:
Gain attention ("Excuse me, [Name]"),

State the issue in one sentence,

Request help/permission ("Can you help me ___?"),

Waits calmly for the response (hands to self, voice level 2-3; no repeated asking).

Counts as success (examples):
"Medium problem—my Chromebook won't start. Ms. Lee, can you help me log in?" (waits).

"Medium problem—we're both using the red marker. Mr. K, can you help us take turns?"

Does not count:
Calling out to peers, arguing, or trying to fix unsafe issues; repeated nagging; walking off without permission; mislabeling a small ("no problem") or big (emergency) issue.

Safety/medical emergencies → follow safety plan (score N/A).

Measurement

Opportunity: Provide ≥1 scenario per session (natural or contrived).

Score each opportunity:
Independent = size label + full help script + calm wait, 0 prompts.

Prompted = same after ≤2 brief verbal prompts (e.g., "Size it," "Use your help script").

Not met = missing size label/script, >2 prompts, escalation, or leaves area.

Track: problem type, adult contacted, latency to label/request, and whether waiting remained calm.

Probable Function (when problems arise)

Breakdowns often reflect skill/interpretation deficits, control/attention seeking, or escape from uncertainty. Replacement = quick size → help routine that preserves autonomy and gets assistance.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine — "S-H-W" (Size → Help → Wait)

Size it: point to 2-3 (medium) on the scale or say "medium problem."

Help: use 3-step script—Gain attention → State issue → Request help.

Wait calmly for the plan.

Practice with model → role-play → feedback, then embed in real situations.
Visuals & Tools

Problem-Size Scale (0-5) with examples (0-1 small/no problem; 2-3 medium; 4-5 big/emergency).

Help Script card: "Excuse me, __. __ is happening. Can you help me __?"

Who-to-ask map (tech, materials, schedule, behavior).

Waiting menu (quiet hands, fidget, breathe 4-4-4 while waiting).

Antecedent Modifications

Preload likely medium scenarios before blocks (tech/materials/peer).

Make the right adult obvious (lanyards, posted roles).

Ensure access to quiet waiting tools; clarify big vs. medium rules.

Prompting & Fading (max 2 verbals)

Nonverbal first (doesn't count): point to size scale or script card.

Verbal 1: "Size it—what kind of problem?"

Verbal 2: "Use your help script."
 Then wait 5-10 s silently. After success, run a transfer trial later with longer silent wait to build independence.

Reinforcement

DRA: Labeled praise + token/point for size label + script + calm waiting (bonus for independent or <15 s latency).

Provide the requested help promptly when appropriate (natural reinforcement).

Thin from FR1 to VR2-3 as consistent.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Primary—Label + Help + Wait (≤2 prompts): Client labels medium, uses the 3-step help script, and waits calmly, ≥80% of sessions (once-per-session opportunity) with ≤2 verbal prompts across 3 consecutive weeks.

Correct Adult Selection: Chooses the appropriate person (per map) ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

One-and-Done Request: Makes one request and waits (no repeated asking) ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Clarify-if-Unsure: "Who should I ask?" then uses script, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Validate → Guide (≤7 words): "I hear you—size it, then ask."

Two-choice rescue: "Tech issue or materials?" (then point to the right adult).

When-Then: "When you use your help script, then I'll problem-solve with you."

If nagging starts, planned ignore + point to 'Wait'; reinforce the pivot to calm waiting immediately.

If it's actually a big problem, switch to safety protocol (score N/A) and reteach size rules later.

The Client will identify a big problem lasting weeks or months, requiring multiple adults and communicate an appropriate coping or help-seeking response Once Per Session with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior

The client will identify one "big problem" (lasting weeks/months and typically requiring involvement from multiple adults/services) and communicate an appropriate coping or help-seeking response, once per session with ≤2 verbal prompts.
Operational Definition

During a structured check-in or scenario prompt, the client will, within 30-45 seconds:
Label one big problem (specific situation that is ongoing—weeks/months—and needs multiple adults such as caregivers, teachers, counselor, administrators, doctors, case workers), and

State or demonstrate one appropriate coping/help-seeking response, such as:
"I want to meet with the school counselor,"

"Please call my parent/guardian so we can make a plan,"

"Can we set up weekly check-ins?"

"I need accommodations/supports for ___,"

"I will use calm breaths then write what I need,"

"I want a restorative meeting with adults present."

Examples of big-problem categories (non-exhaustive): prolonged peer conflict, ongoing academic failure in several classes, repeated housing/transportation instability, chronic health/family stress, long-term grief/adjustment, persistent safety concerns requiring adult coordination.
Does not include: small/temporary issues (1-2 incidents, simple fixes), emergencies requiring immediate safety response (handled by crisis plan on the spot).
 Onset/offset: Onset at end of the clinician's question/scenario; offset when client has named one big problem and stated one coping/help-seeking action or 45s elapse after the last allowed prompt.
Probable Function

Skill-acquisition (identifying problem size and selecting effective, collaborative responses). Avoidance, "IDK," or shutdown may function for escape from uncomfortable topics, control/autonomy, or attention modulation.
Proactive Strategies

Problem Size Meter (1-5): Small (fix now) → Medium (one adult) → Big (weeks/months, multiple adults) → Crisis (right now safety).

Support Pathway Ladder (visual): Trusted adult → Teacher → Counselor/Case manager → Admin/Team → Community/Medical.

Coping/Help Menu (icons/AAC): Ask for meeting • Weekly check-ins • Family call • Accommodation request • Restorative conference • Outside referral • Calm strategy before talking (breath/grounding).

Sentence stems / AAC buttons: "I have a big problem about ___," "I need help from (roles)," "Please schedule…," "My coping step is ___ while we wait."

Priming & privacy: do this during a predictable check-in; offer opt-out wording ("pass—come back later") and choice of modality (speak, write, point, AAC).

Teaching & Prompting

Model-Lead-Test (brief): clinician models identifying a big problem + one help action → practice together → client independent on a new scenario.

Prompting hierarchy (max 2 verbals):
Nonverbal: point to Problem Size Meter/Support Ladder (wait 5-10s).

Verbal Prompt 1: "Name one big problem you've seen (weeks/months, needs adults)."

Verbal Prompt 2 (scaffold): "Now pick one help action from the menu (who do we involve first?)."

If still stuck (teaching phase only), offer A/B choices for category and action; fade quickly.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Teach these instead of saying "I don't know" or avoiding:
"Give me choices," "More time," "I'll write it," "Point to the picture," "Pass—come back (one per session)."

De-escalation Measures / Reactive Strategies

Early distress (hesitation, shut-down): normalize ("Everyone has big problems sometimes"), offer A/B category choice or allow nonverbal selection.

If affect rises: brief regulation (two calm breaths, grounding 5-4-3-2-1), then return to picking one help action only.

Content boundaries: If disclosure suggests immediate danger (to self/others), stop lesson and follow the safety/crisis protocol; do not continue the routine.

The Client will identify a huge problem e.g., emergency requiring immediate group response and follow established safety procedures Once Per Session with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior: Identify a huge problem (emergency) and follow established safety procedures — 1×/session (≤2 verbal prompts)

Operational Definition

When an emergency/huge problem occurs or is simulated (e.g., fire alarm, evacuation drill, shelter-in-place/lockdown drill, medical emergency cue, staff directive), the client will complete the Safety Response Chain:
Identify it as a huge problem (say/acknowledge "This is an emergency/huge problem").

Stop & Quiet (cease ongoing activity, voices off, hands/feet safe).

Attend & Follow the site's established safety procedure for that scenario (e.g., line up/evacuate by posted route to assembly point, shelter-in-place/lockdown per classroom plan, notify adult/office as pre-taught).

Stay with group and maintain procedure until the all-clear.

Prompts allowed: Up to two brief verbal prompts total (e.g., Prompt 1: "Emergency—follow procedure," Prompt 2: "With the group/this way"). Gestural/visual cues (pointing to route/sign) don't count toward the two.
Counts as success (for the single daily opportunity) when the client:
Clearly identifies it as huge (verbally/AAC or by immediately initiating the Safety Response Chain),

Follows the correct, pre-taught procedure for that scenario,

Remains safe, with the group, and ≤2 verbal prompts,

Shows no maladaptive behavior (arguing, refusing, running off, unsafe use of materials).

Non-examples: calling it "small," debating instructions, freezing without moving after prompts, leaving the group, needing ≥3 verbal prompts, or unsafe behavior.
Measurement

Opportunities: 1 planned drill/contrived scenario per session (or a real event if one occurs).

Record: Scenario type (Evacuation / Shelter-in-Place / Lockdown / Medical assist role), Identified as huge (Y/N), Prompts (0/1/2), Stayed with group (Y/N), Followed procedure (Y/N), Latency to move (s), Notes.
