Mastery criterion: Session success on 4 of 5 consecutive sessions. Then fade to ≤1 prompt or increase complexity (new locations/routes).

Probable Function

This is a safety skill (rule-governed behavior under high arousal). Interfering responses (freeze, flee, argue) may be maintained by escape/avoidance, attention, or anxiety—address with explicit teaching and practice.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Safety Response Chain (BST)

Instruction → Modeling → Role-play → Feedback, using the site's actual safety plan.

Three-step cue to memorize: "Stop - Quiet - Follow (the plan/teacher)."

Visuals: posted route map, door card with icons (evacuate/shelter), desk mini-card: Huge? → Stop/Quiet → Follow.

Social narrative (1 page) describing what the client does during each type of drill in this setting.

Antecedent Supports

Pre-correction before transitions: "If we hear the alarm or my emergency words, we do Stop-Quiet-Follow."

Walkthroughs of routes/locations when calm (empty hallways, practice lining up).

Clear roles: where to stand, who to follow, what to carry (if any) per site plan.

Sensory prep: headphones available if alarms are loud; pre-teach "hands on ears but keep moving with the group."

Prompting (fade quickly)

Gesture to route/door → Verbal Prompt 1: "Emergency—follow procedure." → wait up to 5-10s → Verbal Prompt 2: "With the group/this way."

If a third prompt would be needed, model/guide physically as permitted by policy, then run a brief repair trial later.

Reinforcement

Immediate, behavior-specific praise once safe: "You identified it as huge and followed the plan quickly—safe and responsible."

Tokens/points for session success; bonus for 0-1 prompt or fast start (latency ≤5 s).

Natural reinforcement: quick return to preferred activity after the drill debrief.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Primary chain: Identify "huge," then Stop-Quiet-Follow the procedure within 10 s, ≤2 prompts, 1×/session.

If startled/anxious: Use a pre-taught calm cue ("Hands on ears + keep moving," or 3 belly breaths while walking) without breaking the chain, ≤1 prompt.

If unsure which procedure: Ask once, "Evacuate or shelter?" then follow the direction, ≤1 prompt.

After-action check-in: State one safety step completed ("I stayed with the group") during the debrief independently in 70-80% of sessions (shape up).

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Crisis-language rule: short, concrete phrases; model movement; avoid debate.

Proximity & pacing: adult walks slightly ahead/alongside; mirror calm breathing if the client shows panic.

Binary choices that keep safety first: "Stand here or next to me," "Front of line or middle."

If bolting/refusal risk: follow site safety plan; once calm, run a brief repair trial (door → route start → assembly point) to contact success and praise.

Scenario Menu (customize to your site)

Evacuation (fire alarm/smoke cue): line up, exit via posted route, go to assembly point, stay with group.

Shelter-in-Place/Lockdown (administrator directive): move to designated area and follow classroom plan.

Medical emergency near you: freeze work, Alert adult immediately, clear space as directed, stay with group.

Constructive Responses Errors

The Client will demonstrate a constructive response to an error by engaging in observable problem-solving e.g., correcting work, asking for help and using at least one positive statement e.g., "I'll try again" following a mistake, Once Per Session with 2 Verbal Prompts

Target Behavior: Constructive Response to Errors (≤2 verbal prompts; once per session)

Operational Definition

A constructive response to an error is defined as the client, after noticing or being told about a mistake, engaging in observable problem-solving and using at least one positive statement before resuming the task. A response counts when, within 30 seconds of the error/feedback (or within 10 seconds of a prompt), the client:
Acknowledges with a positive statement (e.g., "I'll try again," "I can fix this," "Mistakes help me learn"), and

Takes an observable problem-solving action, such as: correcting work (erase/rewrite), checking directions/model/key, re-reading, using a checklist/strategy, or asking for help with the taught script, and

Re-engages to finish or attempt the corrected step.

Does not count: arguing/blaming, negative self-talk without repair, giving up/avoidance, leaving area, or needing >2 verbal prompts.
Measurement

Opportunity: Provide ≥1 natural or contrived error per session.

Score each opportunity:
Independent = positive statement + problem-solving action + re-engage, 0 prompts.

Prompted = same sequence after ≤2 brief verbal prompts.

Not met = escalation, refusal, or >2 prompts.

Track latency to start (goal ≤30 s), type of action (correct/check/help), and completion status.

Probable Function (when problems occur)

Common barriers include escape/avoidance of difficult tasks, face-saving/attention, and skill deficits in strategy selection. Replacement teaches a fast, predictable fix-it sequence.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine — "OWN IT → FIX IT → FINISH IT"

Own it: Say one positive statement (card/AAC).

Fix it: Choose one action—correct, check, or ask for help.

Finish it: Resume and complete the step; show the correction.

Use modeling → guided practice → independent with real work samples and immediate feedback.
Visuals & Tools

Fix-It Menu (Correct / Check directions or model / Ask for help).

Positive self-talk list (I'll try again; I can fix this; One step at a time).

Help script card: "Excuse me, ___, I made a mistake with ___. Can you help me ___?"

Quick checklist: "Circle error → Fix → Check → Turn in."

Antecedent Supports

Normalize errors ("Mistakes = data") and model your own quick fix.

Provide erasers/colored pens, example problems, and answer checks at set points.

Break tasks into small chunks; start with high-probability items.

Prompting & Fading (max 2 verbals)

Nonverbal first (doesn't count): point to Fix-It Menu/positive-statement strip.

Verbal 1: "Use your fix-it routine."

Verbal 2: "Choose: correct it or ask for help."
 Then wait 5-10 s silently. After a prompted success, run a transfer trial later with longer quiet wait for independence.

Reinforcement

DRA: Labeled praise + token/point for positive statement + action + re-engage (bonus for independent or <15 s latency).

Natural reinforcement: progress/credit on corrected work.

Thin from FR1 to VR2-3 as consistent.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Primary—Constructive Fix (≤2 prompts): Following an error/feedback, client states ≥1 positive phrase, takes one problem-solving action, and re-engages, in ≥80% of sessions (once-per-session opportunity) with ≤2 verbal prompts across 3 consecutive weeks.

Help-Seeking: Uses the help script when stuck >20-30 s, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Strategy Check: Uses a Fix-It checklist/model before asking for help, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

Self-Monitor: Says "Fixed it" and shows the correction, ≥80% with ≤1 prompt.

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Validate → Guide (≤7 words): "I hear you—own it, then fix it."

Two-choice redirect: "Correct it or ask for help?"

When-Then: "When you try a fix, then we check it together."

Planned ignore of minor complaints; reinforce the pivot the moment a positive statement or fix action begins.

Offer a 30-60 s micro-break if arousal rises, then restart with the routine.

Problem Solving

Solving Functional Problems

The client will identify 1 solution when presented with a problem in a contrived setting

Target Behavior

The client will state one workable solution when presented with a contrived problem scenario, once per session, with ≤2 verbal prompts.
Operational Definition

During a brief, scripted problem (spoken, picture card, or role-play), the client will name one specific, feasible solution that matches the problem's constraints within 15 seconds of the cue.
 Examples:
Broken pencil → "Sharpen it" / "Get a backup from the bin."

Spilled water → "Get paper towels and wipe."

Link won't open → "Refresh/restart or ask for the paper version."

Partner absent → "Work independently on step 1, then check in."

Counts as success when:
The solution fits the problem and can be done immediately or with available resources,

Occurs within 15s and with ≤2 verbal prompts, and

Is actionable (verb + object/place), not just "get help" unless the scenario defines help-seeking as the appropriate first step.

Does not include: vague answers ("fix it"), unrelated solutions, or solutions that require unavailable resources.
Probable Function

Skill-acquisition/executive functioning. Non-responding or off-topic answers often function for escape from language/effort or attention redirection.
Proactive Strategies

Problem→Solution Menu (visual/AAC): Find/Replace • Repair/Reset • Clean/Organize • Wait/Work-around • Ask (after 1-2 tries).

S.T.E.P. prompt: See it → Title it (name problem) → Execute a solution → Peek/check.

Pre-teach 6-10 common school/home problems with quick models.

Keep materials handy (backup supplies bin, cleanup kit, charger station).

Use clear constraints in scenarios (time, tools available).

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Teach brief supports instead of "I don't know":
"More time, please." • "Choices?" • "Repeat/rephrase." • "Point options on the menu."

Teaching & Prompting

Model-Lead-Test: clinician models one scenario/solution → guided practice → independent trial.

Least→Most prompting (cap at 2 verbals):
Gesture to Menu/S.T.E.P. (no words).

Verbal 1: "Name one solution."

Verbal 2 (scaffold): "Choose: Repair or Replace?" (Teaching only: a single forced choice if still stuck; fade quickly.)

Run 4-6 quick scenarios; mastery for the session = at least one successful solution as defined.

De-escalation Measures / Reactive Strategies

Early hesitation/IDK: quiet 5-10s wait, then A/B choice from the Menu.

Rising frustration: switch to the easiest solution category (Clean/Organize), validate effort, reinforce first correct attempt.

Perseveration on impossible solutions: restate constraint ("No scissors available—pick a Replace solution").

Analyzing a Problem

The Client will verbalize their initial reaction to a problem and restate it in a constructive manner Once Per Session with 2 Verbal Prompts for 80% of opportunities across 10 consecutive sessions

Target Behavior: State initial reaction → restate it constructively — 1× per session (≤2 verbal prompts); performance target: ≥80% of opportunities across 10 consecutive sessions

Operational Definition

When the client encounters a problem (real or contrived) during routines (work, transitions, peer interactions), the client will complete the Reframe Routine:
Initial reaction (honest but safe): Within 10 seconds, say a short, appropriate reaction to the problem (e.g., "I'm frustrated," "This is hard," "I don't like that").

Constructive restatement: Within 10 more seconds, restate the reaction in a solution-focused way using one of the taught formats below (examples):
Feeling + need/request: "I'm frustrated; please show me step 1."

Problem + plan: "My pencil broke; I'll sharpen it."

Boundary + alternative: "I don't like that; let's agree to disagree."

Delay/cope: "I'm overwhelmed; 30-second break, then I'll finish."

Clarify: "I'm confused; what does this word mean?"

Prompts allowed: Up to two brief verbal prompts total (e.g., Prompt 1: "Say your first thought," Prompt 2: "Now restate it constructively."). Gestural/visual cues (point to card) don't count.
Non-examples: silence >10s; profanity/threats; arguing; repeating complaints without a constructive restatement; more than 2 verbal prompts.
Success Criteria (per opportunity & per session)

Per opportunity: Client completes both steps within 20 seconds total, uses safe language, and stays regulated, with ≤2 verbal prompts.

Per session: At least 1 successful routine is recorded ("Once Per Session").

Mastery target: Across all scored opportunities, the client meets success in ≥80% of opportunities for 10 consecutive sessions (while also meeting the "once per session" minimum).

Measurement

For each scored opportunity, record: Initial reaction given (Y/N) • Constructive restatement (Y/N) • Prompts (0/1/2) • Latency (s) • Strategy type used • Regulated (Y/N) • Notes.
 Session summary: # opportunities, # successes, met 1× minimum (Y/N).
Probable Function

Skill acquisition in emotion regulation + communication. Prior problematic reactions may be maintained by escape, attention, or automatic reinforcement—use brief ABC notes to tailor supports.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Reframe Routine (BST)

Instruction → Modeling → Role-play → In-vivo coaching using common triggers.

Post a "Flip-the-Script" card/AAC page with stems:
Initial: "I feel ___ / This is ___."

Reframe choices: "Please show me… / I will… / Can we… / I need 30 seconds / What does ___ mean?"

Practice 3-5 quick drills daily (easy → harder scenarios).

Antecedent Supports

Stoplight or Size-of-Problem visual to normalize small/medium/big.

Choice architecture: order of tasks, seat/tool options.

Think time: allow 3-5 seconds before expecting the initial reaction.

First-Then: "Reframe → choice for 30-60s."

Prompting (fade quickly)

Gesture to card → Verbal Prompt 1: "Say your first thought." → wait → Verbal Prompt 2: "Reframe it."

If a 3rd prompt is needed, stop scoring, model a correct example, then do a short repair trial immediately.

Reinforcement

Behavior-specific praise after each success ("You said 'I'm frustrated' and asked for help—great reframe.").

Tokens/points for each success; bonus for 0 prompts or ≤10 s total.

Natural reinforcement: access to help/solution immediately after the reframe.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (FERB)

Primary chain: Initial reaction → constructive restatement within 20 s, ≤2 prompts, in ≥80% of opportunities.

If stuck: Use a menu cue ("I'll pick: 'Please show me step 1'") ≤1 extra prompt, 80%.

Brief reset instead of escalating: "30-second reset, then I'll reframe," returns on time ≤1 prompt, 80%.

Self-monitor statement: "I'm flipping my script now," independent in 60-80% of successes (shape up).

De-escalation / Reactive Strategies

Low-profile cues only; keep language brief.

Acknowledge feeling once ("I hear you're frustrated") then immediately cue the reframe.

No debate about the problem; reinforce the first constructive language.

If escalation starts: offer binary choice ("Ask for help or take 30 seconds"), then return to the reframe.

Self Image

Expressing Positive Self-Statements in Social Interactions

The Client will independently use a positive self-statement about their appearance e.g., "I look nice today" during a social interaction across school and home settings, Once Per Session

Target Behavior: Independent Positive Self-Statement About Appearance (once per session; across school & home)

Operational Definition

Positive self-statement about appearance is defined as the client spontaneously saying (or using AAC to say) a brief, affirming "I" statement about their appearance during a live social interaction (greeting, small talk, peer/adult check-in), without any in-the-moment prompts.
 Counts when, within a natural exchange (start or middle of a conversation), the client:
Uses a neutral-to-positive tone (voice level 2-3), ≤10 seconds.

Uses an "I" statement (e.g., "I look nice today," "I like my haircut," "I feel good in this shirt," "I like my style").

Keeps it respectful (no comparisons/put-downs of others; no profanity).

Does not count:
Statements after a verbal/gestural prompt in that interaction ("Tell them you look nice").

Self-talk when no partner is present, or off-topic blurts that interrupt.

Negative body talk ("I look bad") or comparative boasting ("I look better than you").

Said in a shouty/sarcastic tone.

Measurement

Provide ≥1 probe opportunity per session at school and at home (natural or lightly contrived conversation).

Score the first occurrence each session:
Independent = meets criteria with 0 prompts.

Not met = absent, prompted, off-topic, or negative.

Record: partner, setting, sample statement, appropriateness (Y/N), and latency from start of interaction (goal ≤30 s).

Track variety (aim ≥3 unique statements across 2 weeks).

Probable Function (when absent)

Likely skill/fluency deficit, self-consciousness/anxiety, or fear of seeming boastful. Replacement focuses on brief, context-appropriate "I like ___" phrases that fit casual talk.
Proactive Strategies

Teach the Routine — "Notice → Share → Shift"

Notice something about your appearance you feel good about (hair, outfit, accessory).

Share one short "I" statement in conversation.

Shift back to the topic (ask a simple follow-up: "How's your morning?").

Practice via model → role-play → feedback with peers/family; then fade practice cues.
Visuals & Tools (outside probe moments)

Affirmation Bank (wallet card/AAC page) with 6-10 options:
 "I look nice today." "I like my hair." "This jacket suits me." "I feel confident in this shirt."

Context cue icons (arrival, after PE, before class meeting) to plan when it might fit.

Compliment exchange mini-card: if someone responds, "Thank you!"

Antecedent Modifications

Arrange low-stakes social windows (arrival, homeroom, family breakfast/dinner).

Pre-session priming is okay (review the Affirmation Bank before school/home time), but do not prompt during the interaction you plan to score.

Encourage partners to respond naturally (smile/"Nice!"), not to cue.

Prompting & Fading (for practice only; not during probes)

During teaching (non-scored), you may use least-to-most: point to card → whispered model.

For probe/scored moments, no prompts; if a prompt slips in, mark Not met and try a new probe later.

Reinforcement

Natural social reinforcement (peer/family acknowledgment) + brief labeled praise after the exchange ("I liked how you shared that confidently").

Optional token/point if you're using a system—credit only independent statements.

Fade tangible reinforcement as the behavior becomes habitual.

Functionally-Equivalent Replacement Behaviors (Skill Targets)

Primary—Independent Affirmation: Client produces ≥1 spontaneous, appropriate "I" appearance statement during a social interaction once per session at school and home, with 0 prompts, in ≥80% of sessions across 3 consecutive weeks.

Graceful Follow-Up: After sharing, client asks one neutral question ("How are you?"), ≥80% of successful trials.

Compliment Response: If complimented, client replies "Thank you," ≥80% of times.

De-escalation / Repairs

If negative self-talk appears, validate then redirect: "I hear that—try one kind thing about yourself."

If timing is awkward, teach a repair line: "Oops—let me try that again," then share a brief affirmation at the next pause.

Keep coaching private and outside scored interactions.

Parent Training

Implementation of Behavior Plans

Following the Behavior Intervention Plan (BIP)

Consistency with reinforcement schedules

Applying planned consequences for maladaptive behaviors

Caregiver will implement the Behavior Intervention Plan as written during target routines Once Per Session with 1 Verbal Prompt.

Caregiver will deliver programmed reinforcement according to the specified schedule 3 times per session with 1 Verbal Prompt.

Caregiver will apply the planned consequence procedure when a defined maladaptive behavior occurs Once Per Session with 1 Verbal Prompt.

Data Collection & Monitoring

Recording frequency/intensity of behaviors

Tracking antecedents and consequences

Providing ratings on progress/generalization

Caregiver will record frequency and intensity rating for the target behavior in identified home routines Once Per Session with a Visual Cue and 1 Verbal Prompt.

Caregiver will complete ABC entries for recorded incidents 3 times per session with 1 Verbal Prompt.

Caregiver will provide a brief progress/generalization rating with an example Once Per Session with 1 Verbal Prompt.

Reinforcement & Motivation

Delivering positive reinforcement as directed

Encouraging and reinforcing functional communication

Using token economies or reinforcement schedules correctly

Caregiver will deliver positive reinforcement contingent on the identified replacement behavior 3 times per session with 1 Verbal Prompt.

Caregiver will acknowledge, prompt as needed, and reinforce functional communication (FCT) Once Per Session with 2 Verbal Prompts.

Caregiver will operate the token economy with correct criteria and exchange for 10 minutes Per Session with 1 Verbal Prompt.

Skill Generalization at Home

Supporting generalization of social skills (e.g., turn taking, sharing)

Encouraging independence with ADLs (e.g., hygiene, dressing, mealtime skills)

Reinforcing communication skills outside of sessions

Caregiver will set up a play or activity to practice a targeted social skill and provide feedback Once Per Session with 1 Verbal Prompt.

Caregiver will follow the task analysis and prompting plan for an ADL routine Once Per Session with 1 Verbal Prompt.

Caregiver will prompt and reinforce the target communication skill across two natural routines 3 times per session with 1 Verbal Prompt

Behavior Management

Implementing proactive/preventive strategies (e.g., environmental modifications, giving choices)

Responding appropriately to maladaptive behavior (e.g., aggression, tantrums, noncompliance)

Maintaining consistency with discipline strategies

Caregiver will implement two antecedent strategies (e.g., visual schedule, choices) before target routines Once Per Session with a Visual Cue

Caregiver will follow the response flow during incidents (safety - neutral response - teach/reinforce replacement) Once Per Session with 1 Verbal Prompt

Caregivers in the home will apply the agreed discipline strategies as written Once Per Session with 1 Verbal Prompt.

Communication with Child

Using functional communication prompts (mands, tacts, etc.)

Following prompting hierarchies

Modeling appropriate language and social interaction

Caregiver will create motivating situations and use least-to-most prompts for the target mand 3 times per session with 2 Verbal Prompts.

Caregiver will apply the specified prompting hierarchy with a 3-5s wait time in a skill routine Once Per Session with 1 Verbal Prompt

Caregiver will model the target language and social behavior during a daily activity Once Per Session with 1 Verbal Prompt

Coping & Emotional Support Strategies

Encouraging child coping skills (deep breathing, break cards, etc.)

Supporting emotional regulation at home

Modeling calm responses during escalations

Caregiver will cue and reinforce a taught coping strategy (e.g., deep breaths, break card) at early signs of escalation Once Per Session with 2 Verbal Prompts

Caregiver will run the regulation routine (identify feeling ? choose tool ? reflect) for 2 minutes Per Session with a Visual Cue and 1 Verbal Prompt

Caregiver will use a calm voice, brief statements, and planned positioning during escalations Once Per Session with 1 Verbal Prompt

Parent Collaboration with BCBA/Team

Attending training sessions consistently

Accurately reporting on child's progress at home

Following through with BCBA recommendations between sessions

Caregiver will attend scheduled parent training and participate Once Per Session independently

Caregiver will provide a concise home progress summary with one example Once Per Session with a Reminder

Caregiver will implement the assigned home practice plan and submit a permanent product (photo/video/data) Once Per Session with a Reminder
